PREFACE 

"Our Home Territory and North Amer- | ditions of the various state groups in the ica" is the second of a new four-book United States, considered in the order series of geographies which are truly pro- that seems best. gressive in their content and in the order and method of presenting geographical facts and principles.

In this book the text is devoted to a study of the pupil's home territory, country, and North America. An introductory treatment of the early exploration of North America, enough to establish in the pupil's mind the European connections of our people, comes first. This is followed by a brief study of the major political divisions and the chief physical regions of the continent. The study of 

Hawaii is then taken up.

This treatment of the territory is presented in simple language, but it is nevertheless comprehensive enough to make its study basic to all future study of geography. It is given its proper place in the text instead of being relegated to a "supplement" of a few pages bound in the back of the book, as has been the almost universal custom for a long time. 

Throughout the study of the territory of Hawaii the emphasis is upon the fundamental geographic factors and relations that influence the lives of people. It is a study of geographic controls, whether physical or economic, rather than a study of facts as such.

The text on the territory, after an outline treatment of the natural regions of the United States, is followed by a study of the natural regions and economic con- 

Studies of the parts of the United

States, of our outlying possessions, of the other countries in North America north and south of the United States, and of the continent as a whole, follow in order, and complete the work for the year. 

The story of geography as told in this text is a continuous narrative, and as the area described enlarges, frequent comparisons are made with regions previously studied, for the purpose of making clear to the children common or contrasting conditions and characteristics. 

Care is taken to show how and why relief, climate, and products differ in different parts of our country and as between countries. The work of the ice sheet, briefly referred to in the treatment of the physical regions of North America, is again studied in other parts of our own country in connection with the results of its work. The formation of the Grand 

Canyon of the Colorado River is shown to be but a larger expression of forces whose activities and results may be seen today in other parts of our continent. 

Political divisions, such as states and countries, are recognized and located, but within any political unit the work of the people is largely identified with the chief natural regions within its borders. In this way it is possible to show the children that somewhat similar physical conditions lead to similar human activities

vi 

PREFACE 

and that these may at times extend over | kept in mind the fact that they are to be a large area. 

However, the larger natural regions of a great country like our own may cut across many smaller political divisions, such as our states, the location of which, as well as the relation of each to others, should be a matter of common knowledge. Political boundaries, therefore, are given full consideration, and the location of natural resources and great industries is set out in terms of cities, states, and countries. For example, it is not enough that the Blue Grass region should be known as a part of the Central Plains. 

It should also be known as part of the state of Kentucky, within whose borders a rich and fertile soil has made a farming region of remarkable productivity and individuality.

A full equipment of maps is provided. 

The basic maps are of the combined physical-political type, and are conveniently placed for reference throughout the text. The groups of states into which the United States is divided are based upon those used in the reports of the United States census. In addition to the basic maps described above, an abundance of black and white maps have been used to show the natural regions, routes of transportation, the distribution of rainfall, important crops, domestic animals, and mineral products, and other matters of importance. In the preparation of all the maps, the authors have used by young pupils, and have, therefore, deliberately excluded details that would only make for confusion.

Great care has been taken in the choice of the illustrations, both as to size and as to placement, so that they can be easily studied. Each one was chosen because it helps to interpret the text - not merely or primarily because it is a beautiful picture. However, taken as a whole, they constitute an unusually attractive as well as a valuable body of illustrative material.

The teaching material is one of the special features of the book. This material carefully avoids the formal map and review questions of an earlier day, and likewise avoids the lifeless summaries that frequently tempt children to take a short cut in their study. The interesting exercises here provided furnish abundant review material and emphasize the more important geographic relationships and principles. They have considerable variety in form, and thus avoid the monotony often found in checks and review exercises. Above all, they lead the learner to arrive at many conclusions for himself. 

The authors gratefully acknowledge their indebtedness to the many teachers who have tested teaching material, maps, and illustrations in their class rooms. 

ALBERT PERRY BRIGHAM CHARLES T. MCFARLANE

NORTH AMERICA 

you know begins | The next in size is made up of North 

America and South America. There are people living in all five of these continents and in the continent of Australia. have read interesting stories about the children living in these different lands. 

Great, wide, beautiful, wonderful World, With the wonderful water round you curled.

The more you learn about the earth on which you live, the more you will find it to be both wonderful and beautiful. 

You already know many interesting things about the earth. Look at a globe. 

You will see that it shows much more water than land. Most of the land is in four large pieces. See how the water curves around them. You know the names of the oceans into which this great body of water is divided, and you have taken imaginary journeys across these oceans.

The largest land mass is made up of the continents of Asia, Africa, and Europe. You 

You have taken imaginary journeys to hot lands and to cold lands, and you have learned how the children of such lands dress and live. One very cold region is the Antarctic continent. Find it on the globe. Most of it is always covered with ice and snow. There are no homes of people there. Only a few kinds of birds and other animals are found along the coast of that continent.

The United States is on the continent of North America. Find it on the globe and study its shape so that you will know it when you see it on a map.

DISCOVERY AND SETTLEMENT 

3 — - 

How to Look at a Map. A globe shows what part of the earth's surface is land and what part is water. It shows the shapes of the continents, but does not show the continents or countries or states as large as we sometimes need to see them. 

Any part of the earth's surface, large or small, can be shown on a map. The scale of miles on a map is a measure you can use to find how large is any part shown. Using that scale in the map on page 2, see about how wide North 

America is, in its widest part. (6 99 In this book you will find and learn how to use several different kinds of maps. A relief map, such as that of North America on page 2, shows lowlands, highlands, and valleys. The lowlands are shown in green, and the higher lands in buff and yellow. The highest lands are mountains over two miles high. These are in purple or they are left white to show that they are always covered with snow. 

If you could go up and up and up and see all North America at once as you can see a farm or park or city from an airplane, the view might be a little like this map. You would be looking at a land about 3000 miles wide and 5000 miles long. In such a view heights of one or two miles would not amount to much. 

But when you are standing on a plain and looking up at a mountain a mile high, it seems very high indeed. 

Look once more at North America on the globe. See that, except in the northwest, the oceans that separate it from other continents are very wide. 

Things to Do. - 1. Write out a list of the six continents on which people have homes. 

2. Add the name of the continent on which there are no people. 3. On which continent is the United States? 

A.An Indian chief.

DISCOVERY AND SETTLEMENT 

You are now to learn about the white people who came to North America in 1492 and a little later, and about the people who were then already here. 

How long ago was the year 1492? It was in that year that Columbus made his first famous voyage across the Atlantic 

Ocean. The only white people in the world at that time were living in or near Europe. They knew much about the continent of Europe and a little about the continents of Asia and Africa. They supposed that these were the only continents in all the world. 

The Indians. When, in 1492, Columbus reached some islands of North America, he did not know that he had found a new world. He thought that he had reached the "Indies," as the mainland and islands of southeastern Asia were called. It was for that reason that he called the people here Indians. This name has been used ever since that time, but they are also called red men, because of the reddish-brown color of their skin.

NORTH AMERICA 

Painting in the American Museum of Natural History, New York 

Notice the men with bow and pipe, the woman papoose" in a crib hung up on a birch tree.

A.-An Indian village. Notice the bark houses at the left, the baskets, corn, and squashes in the foreground, and the birch-bark canoes, the lake, and the cornfield in the distance. pounding corn, another woman weaving a blanket, and the baby or 

What else do you see in the picture?

In 1492 there were fewer than 500,000 Indians in our whole country; not so many Indians as there are people now living in the one city of Buffalo or Milwaukee. Many of the Indians lived chiefly by hunting wild animals, and so they needed much land to supply food for even a few. North of the Gulf of 

Mexico North America was almost an empty continent, waiting for the white men to come and fill it with people. 

Before the white men came, the Indians of our country had no domestic animals but the dog. On land they traveled on foot. On streams and lakes they used a light birch-bark canoe. This could be easily carried across country from one stream or lake to another. 66 In the eastern part of our country many of the Indians lived a settled life in villages (A). Some had tents made of skins or mats. Others built houses of bark or of wood, the largest of which were 50 or 60 feet long and were divided into rooms. 

Some of the Indians raised beans, squashes, and corn. They gathered wild berries, and in some parts of the country wild rice. Acorns, sunflower seeds, and potatoes also were used for food. Corn and the potato were not known to the rest of the world until after the discovery of 

America. Now great crops of them are raised in many countries.

In the Southwest there were many Indians who lived by farming. As they had little wood, their houses were made

DISCOVERY AND SETTLEMENT 

5 mostly of dried mud, or adobe. Each building, or pueblo, was large enough for a small village. Some were several stories high, the upper rooms being reached by ladders. 

Along the shores of the continent oysters and clams were found, and fish were caught in all waters. Along the northwest coast the salmon caught in the rivers were the chief food of some tribes. 

The Indians had nothing made of iron. 

Their fishhooks were made of bone.

They trapped wild animals, or hunted them with bows and arrows. They made good arrowheads, knives, and hammers of stone, and they also used copper that they found near Lake Superior. They made pottery from clay (A), and wove blankets and baskets of many different kinds.

Dances, ball games, foot races, and contests in throwing and shooting were common Indian sports. The boys played with tops, slings, and stilts, and the girls played "house" and had dolls of buckskin.

Many Indian words are still in use as names of states, cities, islands, rivers, and lakes in North America. Here are some examples: Connecticut, Chicago, Manhattan, Mississippi, and Michigan. English French

B.Early claims of three nations in North America. 

Spanish Explorers. Columbus was an Italian, but his voyage of 1492 was made for Spain. He was trying to find a new way to reach southeastern Asia. 

Trade between western Europe and eastern Asia had been carried on by way of the Indian Ocean, but Columbus thought the western route would be shorter.

When Columbus reached the Bahama 

Islands and Cuba, he thought he had found the new way to Asia for which he was looking. He made three later voyages to this part of the world, and other 

Spanish explorers followed him. Spanish settlers soon took possession of the lands about the Gulf of Mexico and the Caribbean Sea, including Florida, Mexico, and 

Central America (B), and most of South 

America. In what is now the United

States they settled not only Florida but also large parts of the Southwest.

7 Many Spanish names are still used in | Nearly all of the land at first claimed by the southwestern part of our country; the French was taken away from them by for example, Colorado, El Paso, Los the English. 

Angeles, and San Francisco. 

French Explorers. As early as 1504

French fishermen were catching fish near 

Newfoundland. French explorers soon discovered the St. Lawrence River and the Great Lakes. It was a French explorer who started the settlement that has become the city of Quebec. The lake between the states of New York and Vermont was named after him (map, page 154). What was his name?

French missionaries and traders followed lakes and streams and pushed through the forests to the central part of the continent. They followed the Mississippi River to its mouth and claimed much land for the French king. Many people living in eastern Canada and in the city of New Orleans still speak the French language. Detroit, St. Louis, and New 

Orleans are among the many French names that are still found on the map of 

North America. 

English Explorers. The first of the 

English explorers came as traders and to claim new lands for the English king.

Later Englishmen came to build homes and live here. Their first successful settlement was at Jamestown, in Virginia, in 1607, and the second was at Plymouth, in what is now Massachusetts, in 1620.

Other settlements followed along the middle Atlantic coast. The English also took away from the Dutch a settlement at the mouth of the Hudson River and gave it the name of New York. It is now the largest city in North America.

Explaining Things. — Explain clearly: 

1. Why we have a holiday on October 12. 

2. Why Columbus made his voyage. 

3. Why Columbus gave the name Indians to the people he found. 

4. Why so few Indians spread out over so much land.

COUNTRIES OF NORTH AMERICA 

Look at the map of North America on page 6. You will see that most of it is now divided between two great countries the United States and the Dominion of Canada. South of the United 

States you will see Mexico and a number of smaller countries.

The United States. Our country, the 

United States, was at first formed by the thirteen colonies which won their independence from England in the American Revolution. Now it is made up of fortyeight states, and stretches across the central part of the continent from the 

Atlantic Ocean on the east to the Pacific 

Ocean on the west. It takes in much land that was once held by the English and the French, and part of the land once The map on page 5 shows the parts of 

North America as they were first claimed by the Spanish, French, and English. | held by the Spanish.

NORTH AMERICA 

749 1902 RENEWED CANADA

TREATY 28 A.-Boundary marks. The mark at the left is on the boundary between the United States and Canada; that on the right, between the United States and Mexico. 

Follow the northern and southern boundaries of our country on the map. 

On the land itself there are no such lines as are used to show the boundaries on a map. Instead, the boundary is marked in places by a stone or a post (A and 9A). 

The boundary markers may be close enough together so that each one can be seen from the one next in line. In a river a boundary is usually in midstream; and in a lake, halfway between the two shores (9B). Through what lakes and what river does the northern boundary of the 

United States go? In what stream is part of the boundary between Mexico and the United States? 

Canada and the Northern Islands. 

Canada is the largest country in North 

America. It includes all that part of the continent north of the United States except Alaska and Labrador. Many islands, too, along the coasts of the mainland, are part of Canada. Labrador belongs to Newfoundland, which is a British colony separate from Canada. 

Alaska belongs to the United States. 

Greenland belongs to Denmark, a small country in Europe.

Countries South of the United States. 

- Mexico, like Canada and the United

States, extends across the continent. It narrows toward the south and has long coast lines on the Pacific Ocean and on the 

Gulf of Mexico. 

Southeast of Mexico is a group of seven small countries. Across one of these the 

United States built a ship canal to connect the Atlantic Ocean with the Pacific 

Ocean. What is its name? 

Southeast of the United States is a group of islands called the West Indies. In two of them, Cuba and Hispaniola, there are independent countries. Several of the other islands belong to the United States, and the rest belong to countries in Europe. 

Draw an Outline Map of North America.- 

You should know North America so well that you can draw a map of it from memory. But do not try to do that at first.

Take a large sheet of strong paper, use a pencil, and copy your map from the one on page 6. Sketch in the shore line. Do not try to show the small turns and all the "ins and outs." Show the main shapes in smooth lines. Unless you have practiced much map work or other drawing, you will often need to use your eraser. Do not mind if you have to use several sheets of paper before you get a shape that is like the printed map. Besides the main shore line, put in the shores of great bodies of water such as Hudson Bay, Gulf of St. Lawrence, Gulf of Mexico, and the 

Great Lakes. Add the large islands, Greenland, Newfoundland, and Cuba. Do not put in the names, unless you can do it in put in the names, unless you can do it in very small, neatly printed letters. Put in the boundaries of the larger countries, but let the small Central American countries all go in one piece. Do your map with care.

A. Part of the boundary between the United States and Mexico. This view is taken from Mexico looking across the boundary into the United States. The boundary line here crosses the street, and is marked by the pole where an officer is standing. He is inspecting the cars entering our country. On the left side are cars entering Mexico from the United States. B. The "Peace Bridge" crossing the boundary between the United States and Canada. 

The picture shows the Niagara River at its source in Lake Erie. The boundary runs in the middle of the lake and the river. Remember that the river flows north. You see here a very small part of Canada on the left, and part of the city of Buffalo, in the United States, on the right. The narrow strip of water between the city and the river is the Barge Canal, about which you will read later.

PHYSICAL REGIONS OF NORTH AMERICA 

11 

PHYSICAL REGIONS OF NORTH AMERICA 

You have just been looking at the map on page 6, which shows the countries of North America. Such a map is called a political map. 

You are now to look at another kind of map of North America on page 10, called a physical map. map. 

It shows the bordering oceans, the great rivers, and the large lakes. It shows plains and mountains. It shows what parts of the land are high and what parts are low.

Regions of North America. - The height of land is measured from the level of the sea. Find the color guide in the corner of the map. What color shows lands that are less than 1000 feet above sea level? Trace this region from Florida to Nova Scotia. It is the Atlantic 

Lowland. Some of it is nearly level, and some of it is hilly. It is all less than 

1000 feet above sea level. 

Notice that the same color is used to show a very large region reaching a long distance north of the Gulf of Mexico on both sides of the Mississippi River. It extends past the Great Lakes and all the way across Canada from south to north. 

Why is this region colored green? Find two routes by which you could go from the Gulf of Mexico to the Arctic Ocean, and never be more than 1000 feet above the sea. 

Between the two lowlands of our country that you have just studied there is a region of highlands. What is its name? Follow with your finger the parts of this highland that are between 

1000 feet and 2000 feet above the sea. 

Find parts that are between 2000 feet and 5000 feet high. Two peaks in these mountains are more than 6000 feet high. 

They are marked by small crosses. Mt. 

Washington is 6279 feet high, and Mt. 

Mitchell is 6711 feet high. Find them. 

Mt. Mitchell is the highest mountain in the eastern half of North America. 

Find, in the central part of the United 

States, the three rivers named Missouri, Arkansas, and Red. On each of these rivers, using your ruler to help, find a point that is halfway between the Atlantic 

Ocean and the Pacific. A line extending through these points would divide the

United States into an eastern half and a western half. Is this line east or west of the Mississippi River? 

Almost all of the western half of North 

America is highland. This highland region reaches from Central America, through Mexico, across the United States and Canada into Alaska. 

How high are the Great Plains? Find 

Pikes Peak. It is over twice as high as 

Mt. Mitchell. The map also gives you the names and locations of other mountain peaks and of several mountain ranges. In its widest part the western highland is more than 1000 miles wide. 

How long would it take you to fly across it in an airplane going 100 miles an hour?

There is not much lowland along the 

Pacific Ocean. On the map find several small lowland regions in the far West. 

It will be easy for you to remember that North America has an eastern lowland, an eastern highland, a great central lowland, and a great western highland. 

You know that the central lowland is much larger than the eastern lowland. 

You will also want to remember that the western highland is much wider, much higher, and much longer than the eastern highland.

Do you? Greenland is about one fourth as large as the United States.

On the map on this page, most of Greenland is shown in black. This part of the island is covered by a great glacier, or sheet of ice, several thousand feet thick. At its edge the ice flows slowly down valleys into the ocean (A). There large masses of ice break off and float away as icebergs. 

If all the snow that falls on Greenland melted, there would be no glacier. But it does not all melt. For a long time there has been more snow each winter than the summer sun can melt. This snow packs together until it makes ice. 

The glacial ice, growing thicker and thicker in the central part, presses out around the edges. This is a little like the way molasses candy will act when a big lump of it is left in a pan. A barrel of asphalt is almost always hard and brittle when it is first opened. If it is

GLACIERS 

13 

H. L. Fairchild 

In some places the melting of an old ice sheet has left earth and stones scattered over large fields. In other places earth and stones are heaped up in oval hills which the ice shaped that way as it passed over them. These are called drumlins (A).

A.-Drumlins, in New York state, near Lake Ontario. The nearer drumlin is seen in nearly its full length. left as a lump on the roadside or street of the glacier, in a ridge or hill called a for a time it will spread over the ground moraine. or pavement. These two examples will help you to understand how glaciers flow. 

There are smaller glaciers on the high mountains and in the mountain valleys of 

Alaska. Why? There are glaciers also in western Canada and in the western part of the United States. There are glaciers in the high mountains of some of the other continents. On the Antarctic continent there is an ice sheet larger than that of Greenland. 

Glaciers move only a few feet or only a few inches a day. As they move along they scrape up loose earth and stones.

These are held in the moving ice. They help to wear away the rocks over which the glacier moves. In this way glaciers may wear away the tops of hills, or dig a valley deeper.

As the glacier of a mountain valley moves down, it gets into a region that is not so cold. The ice melts more and more until it is melting faster than it is moving forward. When this happens the earth and stones are left at the end snow. 

At one time the climate of Canada and the northern part of the United States was colder than it is now. There was much 

Not all that fell in the winter was melted the next summer. The snow got deeper and deeper until it packed into ice. The ice sheet grew larger and larger until it covered more than half of North America, as shown by the map on page 12. 

Find two places on the map from which the arrows are pointing away in all directions. These two places are where the snow was heaviest. As it packed into ice, the ice moved in the ways the arrows point. Compare this map with the map of North America on page 6. Find three cities that are now near what was once the southern edge of this old ice sheet.

NORTH AMERICA 

The cold and the snow that formed the ice sheet of North America lasted a long time. Just how thick the ice was, no one knows. From the markings left on rocks when the ice melted, it is known that the ice moved as the arrows show. From the moraines left at its southern edge we know about how far into the United 

States it reached. 

Before the time of the ice sheet in 

North America there were hills and valleys and streams. There were also forests and shrubs and animals. Under the ice sheet, the hills and valleys were changed in shape. Streams were turned in new directions or blocked up by material that the glacier left, thus forming many lakes. When the ice at last melted off, plants and animals once more lived on the land that the ice had so long covered. 

A. Waterfall in Enfield Glen State Park, near Ithaca,

New York, as seen in the winter. The hills and valleys in this part of the state were greatly changed in shape by the ice sheet.

THE NEW WORLD AND THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT SPANISH, FRENCH, AND ENGLISH EXPLORATIONS AND SETTLEMENTS 

You have already learned some things about the early explorations of North America that were made by the Spanish, French, and English. You have learned, also, that the Dutch made some settlements in the northeastern part of our country. The most important of the 

Dutch settlements were made in the state of New York. Find this state on the map on page 87. Some of the Dutch settlements in New York were made at the mouth of the Hudson River, along its banks, and along the Mohawk River, which is the chief tributary of the Hudson. These and other Dutch settlements in the New World, as you have learned, were taken by the English.

WP- - 2 15 

What the Spanish Looked For in the 

New World. You will remember that when Columbus found the New World he was looking for a sea route to Asia. If you will take a globe and follow the route of his first voyage, you will see that he would have reached Asia if there had been no land in the way and if he had kept on in the direction he was going. He believed that he had reached islands near the coast of Asia, because he thought that the world was much smaller than it really is. He had an idea that Asia was much larger than it is, and that it was separated from Europe by one rather narrow ocean. He may have believed that there were undiscovered islands in this ocean, but he certainly did not expect to find the two great continents that were later discovered.

THE NEW WORLD AND THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT 

1 The ships (A) that Columbus used on this voyage when he discovered a New 

World would look very small to you if you could see them and compare them with the great ocean steamships of today. Columbus was very fortunate in the route that he followed on his voyage toward the west and the one that he followed on his voyage back to Spain. The map (page 

15) shows both of these routes. 

Columbus Helped by the Winds and the Movement of Ocean Waters. When 

Columbus left Spain he sailed southwest to some islands that are shown as tiny dots. From that point he turned his ships toward the west. In that part of our earth, at the season of the year when Columbus was making this voyage, there are winds that blow very steadily day after day. These winds blow from the northeast toward the southwest. But if the sails of a ship are turned just right and the ship is steered just right, it will move over the water toward the west.

The map (page 15) shows you that the surface waters of the ocean where these winds are blowing also move in the direction that Columbus wanted to sail. 

On his return voyage to Spain, Columbus crossed the ocean farther north. 

During the time of the year when he was crossing it, the winds there blow most of the time from the west. On both his outward and his homeward voyages the winds drove his ships in the direction he wanted them to go. Here again the map shows you that the surface waters of the ocean were moving in the direction A. The ships on which Columbus and his crew set out for the New 

World. that Columbus was sailing. 

Columbus made four voyages to the New World, and before he died he knew that he had not reached Asia as he had first believed. But so long as he continued his explorations he did not give up hope of finding a route to Asia. 

A Sea Route to Asia 

Is Found. - As the years passed, other European people took part in this search for a sea route to the 

Indies. Among them, as you know, were the French, the English, and the Dutch. 

For many years before the first voyage of Columbus, the sea captains of Portugal -a small country of Europe, west of Spain and facing the Atlantic were looking

ОСЕА N from north to south, and separated the Atlantic and 

Pacific oceans. Several

European countries began a search for a sea route across this mass of land. 

The search lasted a very long time and extended all along the Atlantic coast of the two continents that we now know as North America and South America. The routes followed by three Portuguese navigators who were successful in rounding the Cape of Good Hope. You will see that Diaz, although he reached the southern end of the continent, was forced to turn back be- A. fore he entered the Indian Ocean. Da Gama was more successful, and after making several landings on the west coast of Africa finally pushed on to the ancient city of Calicut in southwestern India. Cabral sailed so far west that he touched the coast of South America before turning southeast to pass the southern point of Africa. It was because Cabral reached the coast of South America that Portugal laid claim to the land we now know as Brazil. 

Only one sea route was found and this was across the narrow tip of South America. 

It was discovered by Fernando Magellan, a Portuguese, who was at the time in command of Spanish ships.

This water route, or strait, was given Magellan's name. 

See if you can find it on the map of South America on page 18. Then find it on a globe and notice how far it is from Spain and from any part of North America.

Exploring the New World Continents. 

It was not long before the search for a "western passage "western passage" to India led to attempts to explore the interior of the continents and to the making of settlements. Out of these grew the Spanish, French, and English claims already mentioned (pages 5 and 7). The native peoples, who had been called "Indians" by Columbus, were widely scattered over the two continents, and there were not many of them. All of the European explorers, and later the settlers who came to the New World, thought of them as savages, and often treated them with great cruelty. for a different route. They hoped to find that the Atlantic Ocean and the

Indian Ocean were connected south of 

Africa, and they were making voyages along the west coast of that continent. 

Such a route was finally found, by an expedition under the command of Vasco da Gama. He reached southern Asia after sailing around the southern end of

Africa (A). After that the Portuguese began to trade with the people of southern and eastern Asia and near-by islands.

Continents. In time it became known that the New World was a great mass of land that stretched a long, long distance

OCEAN 

It was not at first the intention of the Spanish explorers to raise crops or to make new homes for people.

The Spanish explorations, as you have learned, were carried on in the southern part of what is now the 

United States, and in Mexico, Central America, and 

South America. In Mexico, Central America, and northwestern South America natives were found working mines of gold and silver, and making dishes and ornaments from these precious metals. 

The Spaniards made war upon the natives, killed many of them, forced others to work as slaves in the mines, and took whatever they could find of value. 

You will not be surprised to learn that the natives fought back, and killed many of the Spaniards. But much of South America, Central

America that were early claimed by the 

Spanish, the French, and the English.

What did each of these countries try to do in these lands that they claimed as their own? 

Spain in the New World. The rulers of Spain told Spanish commanders who went to the New World that they must look for gold, silver, and jewels, and for spices, incense, and other things of value. 

The Indians were to be treated kindly and were to be converted to Christianity. 

Spanish rule.

The French in North America. The early Spanish explorations had been carried on in a part of the New World where it is warm. The native peoples there could live out-of-doors most of the time and did not need much clothing. 

The early French explorations were carried on in a part of the New World where, during the winter time, it is cold. 

The near-by ocean waters were filled with fish. In winter the animals in the forests had fine, thick furs.

Some of these fishermen traded with the Indians for furs that the Indians had taken from fur-bearing animals. When these furs were taken to France, they were sold for high prices. It was not long before the fur trade with Indians along along the 

St. Lawrence became very valuable, and fur-trading stations were built. At first

The first trappers were, of course, the Indians, but it was not long before some of the stronger and bolder of the Frenchmen took up the work. They joined with the Indians and they married

Indian women. They came out of the woods at the end of the season to sell their furs and buy supplies that they wanted. 

They then went back to their Indian wives. Their children, half French and half Indian, or "halfbreeds," grew up to follow the same kind of life.

As more and more of the fur-bearing animals were killed the trappers had to go farther north, farther west, and farther south. Behind the trapper went the trader, and along with the trader went the missionaries that were sent out from The Spaniards, who came early to the 

New World, came to find something of value that they could take away. They found it in the form of gold and silver and pearls. The making of permanent settlements was not then in their minds. The later spread of Spanish influence and power in the New World is part of another story.

The French came as fishers and fur traders. They made some settlements, but most of these were at first fur-trading posts and mission stations.

The English in North America. - Why did the English come to the New World?

During the time most of the English colonies along the Atlantic coast were settled, there was much religious trouble

A. The "Half Moon." How can you tell which way the ship is sailing? in Europe. In England, especially, this was true. Because they were very unhappy in their old -homes, some of these people came to the New World to live. Others came to search for gold, but most of those who came wanted to make their homes in the new land and make a new start in life. 

The Hudson's Bay Company. There was one part of North America that was claimed by the English for another reason. 

Turn to the map of North America on page 6, and find Hudson Bay. You have learned that when Henry Hudson, who was an Englishman, was sailing for the

Dutch, he visited the river in the eastern part of New York state that now bears his name. Later he was sailing for an

English company, trying, as so many were at that time, to find a water route across 

North America to the Indies.

While searching for this northwest passage he sailed through the straits and into the bay that were afterward given his name. He explored a part of the shores of the bay and spent the winter there. 

When spring came Hudson was anxious to continue the search for a northwest passage. But most of those who were with him were unwilling to do so. They wanted to return home at once. Hudson and eight members of his crew were put off the ship by other members of the crew, who then sailed for England. This was the last that was ever known about 

Hudson or those who were left with him. 

Because of the explorations made by 

Hudson in this bay, the English claimed all of the land drained by the streams that flow into it. Later, all of this region was given to a group of gentlemen who formed a company to trade for furs with the Indians in that part of North America.

This company became known as the 

Hudson's Bay Company, and it is still carrying on a fur trade in many parts of Canada.

Study the map on page 10 and notice how much of Canada drains into Hudson

Bay. The waters of the bay are shallow when the depths are compared with those of the ocean. But they are deep enough to float large ships during the four months of the year when the bay and Hudson 

Strait are free from ice. Much of northern Canada is a low plain that slopes gently toward this bay. Indeed, we may think of the bay itself as covering a part of this low, gently sloping plain. Much of this plain, at the time it was turned over to the Hudson's Bay Company, was covered with a forest. In the forest and along the streams lived the animals whose fine furs the trappers were seeking.

Something to Do. - Study the map of 

North America on page 5 showing the different parts of our continent that were at one time claimed by European countries. Then write on a clean sheet of paper statements that tell what parts of our own country were claimed at that time by Spain, France, and England. Make use of the map of the

Routes into the Interior. You must remember that in the early days, when these settlers in the eastern part of our country began to move toward the west, there were no roads for them to follow. 

Much of the land was covered with dense forests. There were pathways through the forests, but most of these were Indian trails better known to the Indians than to the white men and, for that reason, dangerous to follow. Boats could be used on some of the streams that were not too shallow and that were not blocked by fallen trees. But where the streams were narrow and forests grew close to their banks there were hiding places for unfriendly Indians.

THE NEW WORLD AND THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT 

A. 

of the river were compelled to carry their goods and their boats. Those who traveled on horseback or drove the horses or oxen that hauled the heavy carts of those early days found no roads for their teams or carts or cattle. 

Fortunately, the English, after capturing the Dutch settlements and taking control of the country, had

The forested ridges of the Appalachian Highlands. You can see how made friends with the Indifficult it would be to break a trail through this country. 

Along the streams and in the valleys and hollows of the forests there were swamps to be avoided. Valley slopes, hillsides, and mountains were covered with a dense growth of trees. You would find such a country difficult to cross today (A). It must have been even more difficult for the early pioneers who were trying to make their way toward the west.

For a time they were stopped by the Appalachian Mountains, but later they found their way across these into the more level lands beyond. 

You will remember that the English captured the Dutch settlements along the Hudson and Mohawk rivers. By following these rivers they were able to reach the plains that border the Great 

Lakes. It was the easiest of all the routes from the Atlantic Ocean to the interior of the continent between the St. Lawrence

Valley and the southern end of the Appalachians.

But even this route was a difficult one to travel in those early days. There were falls in the Mohawk, and around these falls those who traveled west by way 

dians the fierce and warlike Iroquois. Because of the friendship of the Indians through whose country they were passing, colonists from New England and New York were later able to follow the valley of the Mohawk and the Lake Plains to the west. The map on page 87 shows you that this route led them to the eastern end of Lake Erie. 

From this point boats might be used or the plains along the lakes might be followed. Beyond the Appalachians and along the Great Lakes the English were entering lands that had long been claimed by the French. This movement of English settlers into territory claimed by France was sure to lead to trouble.

While the English were moving into the lands that the French had claimed, the French were pushing south from the 

St. Lawrence and east from the Mississippi. They built a fort in western 

Pennsylvania, where the Allegheny and 

Monongahela rivers join to form the

Ohio. They also settled at other points in the valley of the Allegheny. In doing this they were pushing into parts of the country that the English colonies had long thought of as their own.

A. At the close of the French and Indian War Great Britain laid claim to nearly all the territory east of the 

Mississippi River. — - The French and Indian War. A war, known as the Seven Years' War, broke out in Europe, and England and many European countries took part in it. 

England and France were on opposite sides. The English and French colo nists in North America also went to war. 

This American war became known as the French and Indian War, because the French stirred up many of the Indians to take part in it against the English. At first the French were successful, but in the end the English won. When the final treaty of peace was signed, the English took control of the French possessions along the St. Lawrence River and nearly all of the territory that France had claimed east of the Mississippi. Study carefully the maps on this page. 

Settlements beyond the Appalachians.

The map on page 87 shows you that, although the Ohio River has a winding course, it flows in general toward the southwest to join the Mississippi. In the first part of its course the Ohio flows through high and hilly country. Farther west the bordering lands become rolling plains. At points where settlers moving toward the west reached the Ohio, many of them built large flatboats on which they loaded their families, their household goods, and their farm animals. In these flatboats they floated down the river until they found some good place to settle. 

Some made their way up the streams that flowed into the Ohio, and on the banks of these streams built new homes in the wilderness.

THE NEW WORLD AND THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT 

The troubles for those from the eastern colonies, who sought new homes west of the Appalachians, were not yet over. It was soon learned that the British government wished to stop any further settlement in this country. Attempts were made also to have those who had already settled there move away. The Indians had been promised that these lands would be held as hunting grounds for them.

But some of the eastern colonies claimed that much of this land had been given to them by English rulers many years before, and that the British government had no right to make such a promise. New settlers, therefore, kept on moving across the mountains, and especially into the country that could be reached by way of the Ohio 

River and its branches, and by the Great Lakes. The pioneers who took part in this movement were ready to face any danger that might arise.

2. The English colonists began to move westward from the Atlantic into the interior of our continent. 

3. The easiest route to follow was along the Mohawk 

River and the plains that bordered the Great Lakes.

In all the earlier and in some of the later movements of people across the vast central plains of our continent, they went by wagon, on horseback, and afoot. They traveled hundreds of miles following the banks of streams, or faint trails, or across the open country where there were no trails of any kind to guide them. 

They waded or swam the smaller streams and built boats or rafts to cross the larger ones. It often took them several weeks to cover highest peaks in the West. the distance that we are A. Mt. West of the Louisiana Territory, and north of the Spanish claims, was a large area that extended to the Pacific, which came to be known as the "Oregon Country" (map, page 25). This Oregon Country was claimed by both Great Britain and the United States, and you will be interested in the story of its exploration and settlement.

FROM  TO THE 

PACIFIC 

Most of the western half of our country is a region of mountains (A) and plateaus. 

On the east of the Rocky Mountains the border of this area is a region of high plains, to which the name Great Plains has been given. On the west it is bordered by the Pacific Ocean. Along the

Pacific this mountain and plateau area is made up of a narrow coastal plain, coast ranges, and interior valleys between the 

Coast Ranges and the Sierra Nevada and

Cascade Mountains farther east (map, The interior valleys between page 92). the Coast Ranges and the Sierra Nevada and Cascade Mountains are long and narrow. They are divided into different parts and are very fertile and productive.

FROM  TO THE PACIFIC 

27 A. 

Official Photograph, 116th Photo Section, 41st Div., Air Service, Spokane 

- The Grand Canyon of the Snake. For many miles of its course, the Snake River forms the boundary between 

Oregon and Idaho. Find it on the map, page 86.

Taken together they make up the largest and best lowland area in the western half of the United States, and are drained by large and important streams. There are very much larger rivers that drain the western half of our country. These include such streams as the Columbia, the Snake (A), the Missouri, the Rio 

Grande, the Colorado, and others that have cut deep narrow valleys along parts of their courses. These deep and narrow valleys are generally called gorges or canyons. 

Exploring the West. - Wild, rugged, or barren as much of this country still seems to be (28 A), we know that since the white man first entered this region there have been great changes. The best routes across these lands between the east and west and the north and south have been located. The passes across the mountains have been found. Transcontinental lines of railroads and branch lines have been built. In addition, national highways for automobiles and scenic and other highways have been constructed. Mule paths and trails now lead into the mountains to high pastures, to mining centers, to camps, and to hotels, and to natural wonders and scenes of great grandeur. Streams have been bridged, waterfalls have been harnessed to produce power, and in many places water from streams is used to irrigate fields that would otherwise be barren and unproductive. At favorable points, in irrigated areas, cities and villages have grown up and there are farm homes. 

How different are these conditions from those that were found there when the first white settlers were pushing their way into these western lands! 

Before the coming of the white man, there were Indians in different parts of this western country, but their total number was small. They were for the

A. The Sawtooth Mountains, in Idaho. In the center foreground there is a mountain lake. It was very difficult to explore country as rugged as this, and to blaze trails and find passes. most part hunters and fishermen. It was only in the southern part that some of the Indians led a settled life. It was in this part of our country also that the Spanish had established missions and attempted to train the Indians in agriculture and the raising of cattle. The Spanish missionaries had brought with them seeds for planting and such domestic animals as cattle, sheep, and horses. 

Along streams, in woodlands, in mountain valleys, and in some small but wellwatered plains there were wild animals that the Indians hunted for meat or for their furs. Among these wild animals there were thousands and thousands of buffaloes, upon which many of the Indians depended for food and whose skins they used for clothing, tents, and in other ways.

It was the fur trade that first led trappers and traders to make their way into this western mountain land. 

Explored from Many Directions. 

From what you have already learned, you no doubt expect that this western half of the United States, about which you have been studying, was explored and settled from its landward side. On the south settlers came in from Mexico. On the east and north low passes or notches in the mountain walls made it possible for the trappers and traders from the 

United States and Canada to make their way into the mountains. But before learn about the exploration of western 

United States from the landward side, there is an interesting story to be told of explorers who visited the Pacific coast of 

North America. you

EXPLORATIONS ALONG THE PACIFIC COAST

EARLY EXPLORATIONS ALONG THE PACIFIC COAST 

In this story we must go back to 

Magellan. You will remember that he was the first to sail from the Atlantic into the Pacific, through the stormy strait which has been given his name.

The route around the southern point of South 

America was discovered later. But the route through the strait, although a very dangerous one, is shorter and was much used. It was this route that was most often used for a long, long time.

Why the Pacific Coast Was Explored. 

There were a number of reasons for these explorations. (1) There was the wish to discover new lands that could be claimed by the discoverers for their own country. (2) There was the hope that a sea route, or strait, across North America might be found from the Pacific side.

Stories had long been told among the seamen of different countries about such a strait. It was said that one had been found at a much earlier date and then forgotten. (3) There was a story about a great river — "the River of the West" that flowed out of the heart of the continent and poured its waters into the Pacific. (4) There were fur-bearing animals in the forests and in the coastal waters. Furs would bring high prices, both in the markets of some of the European countries and in China, making it possible to build up a profitable trade. 

Treasure Found by the Spaniards. 

Magellan found his way into the Pacific in 1520, but he did not explore the western shores of the New World. It was not long after this that Spanish conquerors and the soldiers under their command learned of the mines of gold and silver that had long been worked by the natives of Mexico, Central America, and northwest South America. They learned, also, that the native peoples of these countries were skillful metal workers, and that they had made many ornaments and vessels of gold and silver. 

Spanish armies under different leaders conquered the native peoples, took the gold and silver vessels and ornaments that they found, and forced the natives to work in the mines as slaves. 

The treasure found in northwestern

South America was taken to the coast and loaded into ships that carried it to the Isthmus of Panama. There it was unloaded, sent across the isthmus, and again put into ships to be sent to Spain. 

Drake Captures Spanish Treasure 

Ships. Other nations in Europe saw

Spain becoming richer and more powerful with the help of the treasure taken from the natives of the New World. The sea captains of some of these other countries reasoned that what the Spanish had taken from others by force they might take from 

Spain by force. Sea raiders from England and France, especially, began to capture the Spanish treasure ships. 

One of the boldest and most daring of the English sea raiders of this time was 

Francis Drake. He learned about the shiploads of treasure that were being sent from Spanish settlements on the Pacific coast of South America to Panama. In 

1577 he sailed from England, passed through the Strait of Magellan, and followed the western coast of the New World to the north. He raided the settlements along the coast and captured a number of treasure ships. Finally, satisfied with what he had taken, he sailed northward along the Pacific shores of North America.

great Spanish fleet known as the "Armada." By this fleet the Spanish king had hoped to break the growing sea power of England. But the English were victorious and the sea power of England increased while that of 

Spain was almost destroyed.

The world's seas became free to other nations.

Later Spanish Explora tions. Long before Drake had visited our Pacific shores, Spain had laid claim to them. But the scattering of the Armada had been so great a blow that Spanish exploration of our western coasts did not begin again for nearly 200 years. Then, late in the eighteenth century, Spain began once more to take an interest in western 

North America. A Spanish captain sailed north along the Pacific coast of North

Later in July, 1579, Drake turned toward home. He crossed the Pacific and 

Indian oceans, making a number of stops on the way. He reached England again late in September, 1580. Queen Elizabeth, who was then the ruler of England, made Drake a knight, and he is known in history as Sir Francis Drake. 

The Destruction of the Spanish 

Armada. Eight years later Drake helped to scatter and partly destroy the 

The next year another Spanish expedition again explored the coasts of Washington, where a cross was put up and the land once again claimed for Spain. 

You will remember that before the time of these two expeditions England had captured a large part of the French possessions in North America. Shortly afterward the Revolutionary War began, as a result of which the English colonies on the 

Atlantic coast won their independence.

English and Russian Explorers.

Spain did not establish colonies in northwestern North America, or develop trade. 

Other countries were more active. 

1778 Captain James Cook, one of the most famous of English explorers, made a voyage to these coasts in search of the long-looked-for passage into the Atlantic. 

His route was by way of the Indian and 

Pacific oceans. On his way he visited again Australia, the neighboring islands of

New Zealand, and other islands which he had seen on an earlier voyage. In crossing the Pacific he rediscovered the Hawaiian Islands, which he called the Sandwich 

Islands. These islands had been known to Spain for some time, but they had been largely forgotten by others. 

On the coast of North America he sailed as far north as the Bering Strait and beyond. In Alaska he met Russian fur trappers and traders, who had crossed from Asia to America. This had happened after Bering, a Danish sea captain sailing for the Russian czar, had discovered the strait now called by his name. 

Cook named many points along the coast, and crossed over to Asia. He afterward returned to the Hawaiian Islands, where he was killed. this northwestern coast of North America.

The Russians, also, were active in the fur trade, and claimed as their own the part of the continent that we now call Alaska. 

In 1792 George Vancouver, in command of an English ship, and Robert Gray of 

Boston, in command of an American ship, met off the coast of the present state of 

Washington. Vancouver explored and named Puget Sound, and gave to more than 70 other geographical features the names that they still have. On the map on page 6 find the island that was named after him. 

Robert Gray was the first to discover the River of the West, for which so many had been looking. He named it the Columbia, after his ship, and that is the name by which it is known today. Before this discovery this long-sought but unknown river had been called the Oregon a name that has been given to one of the three great Pacific coast states of our country. 

Because of Gray's discovery of the Columbia, the United States was later to claim a large area that was long known as the 

Oregon Country.

Alexander Mackenzie.— 

One of the men of the 

North-West Company was 

Alexander Mackenzie. fore becoming a partner in the North-West Company he had had some experience in the fur trade. When he was only a little more than thirty years of age he had charge of one of the posts of his company on the south side of Lake Athabaska, west of Hudson Bay (map A). 

He wished to make his station the trade center for much of western Canada. 

With this wish in mind, he set out to learn about the network of waterways of which Lake Athabaska is a part. This led him to follow the stream that connects Lake Athabaska with 

Great Slave Lake. From 

Great Slave Lake he followed the river that flowed out of it toward the northwest and on to the Arctic Ocean. To this stream his name has been given, and you will find it plainly shown on the map on page 6. Mackenzie followed the courses of other streams, also, and in 1793 he crossed the Rocky Mountains and reached the Pacific Ocean. 1 NORTH

THE WEST EXPLORED AND SET- TLED FROM ITS LANDWARD SIDE 

Let us turn now to the story of the exploration of the western half of the 

United States from its landward side.

The North-West Company. After 

France lost the provinces that later united to form Canada, a new English fur-trading company was formed to carry on a fur trade in opposition to the older Hudson's

Bay Company. This new company took the name of the North-West Company.

The men of the new company were very active and carried on explorations, built forts or trading stations, and pushed their search for furs into unknown parts of the continent.

WIS. GOO 

David Thompson. - In 1797 David

Thompson, who was anxious to explore and map the northwestern part of North America, became a member of the North- West Company. He was sent out to make a map of the fur country and to mark the location of all fur stations. In carrying on his work, Thompson roamed far and wide from Hudson Bay to the

THE WEST EXPLORED FROM ITS LANDWARD SIDE 33 

as a whole, it was a long and dangerous trip, and both of the explorers needed all their strength and courage to carry it through.

Missouri. He crossed the Rocky Moun- | shown on the map on page 34. Taken tains and explored the Columbia and its tributaries, where he built forts and traded with the Indians. Thompson's explorations carried him into those parts of British Columbia and the country now included in the states of Montana, Idaho, and Washington that are drained by the 

Columbia and its tributaries.

Through the work of Mackenzie, Thompson, and many others, northwestern North America, especially large parts of the Oregon Country, became better and better known. More and more firmly this company of British fur traders were establishing their claim to the lands north of the Columbia. What, if anything, were the people of the United States doing to add any part of this region to our country?

The Lewis and Clark Expedition. 

One of the men who was greatly interested in saving the Oregon Country for the 

United States was Thomas Jefferson, who was the third President of our country.

It was during his first term as President that Louisiana was purchased from 

France. Not long after that purchase was made, Jefferson sent two young 

Americans Meriwether Lewis and 

William Clark on a trip that took them across the Oregon Country to the mouth of the Columbia River. 

Jefferson wanted this trip made so that he might learn more about the country that they were to cross. But especially, he wanted it made so as to show the British that the United States had not given up its claims to the drainage basin of the river that Gray had discovered. The route from a point on the Mississippi near St. Louis to the mouth of the 

Columbia, and the return routes, are

The Westward Trip. Lewis 

Lewis and

Clark had with them a number of men to help with the boats. They also had as a guide a Frenchman who knew a part of the country through which they were to pass. With this Frenchman, whose name was Charboneau, was his young Indian wife, Sacajawea the "Bird-Woman." 

The Indian woman was a Shoshone.

She had been captured when she was a little girl by other Indians and sold to

Charboneau as a slave, and he had later married her.

Without the help of Charboneau and 

Sacajawea, the trip of Lewis and Clark from the Mississippi to the Pacific would have been almost impossible. Even with their help there were many dangers and difficulties to be met and many obstacles to be overcome. The first part of the trip up the Missouri was fairly easy.

The boatmen were skillful, the river current was not strong, and the expedition moved forward quite rapidly. Later, however, sand bars, shallow water, fallen trees, rapids, and falls frequently blocked the way. At times one or more of the boats would run aground or be overturned, or it would be necessary to carry goods and boats around some obstruction. 

When the explorers reached the three rivers that joined to form the Missouri, they were in the country of the Bird- 

Woman's people. She learned that her brother was a chief of the Shoshones.

Through his help Lewis and Clark and their companions were able to make their

The routes of Lewis and Clark and the Oregon Trail. way overland to a stream that finally led them to the Snake River, a tributary of the Columbia. In this overland trip they crossed a very rough part of the country now included in the states of 

Montana and Idaho.

As soon as they reached the Snake River, whose waters find their way to the Columbia and the Pacific Ocean, they built canoes, and once more traveled by water. Here again it was at times necessary to carry their canoes around falls or rapids, but at last they reached the mouth of the Columbia and the ocean. 

Their long westward journey was ended. 

You may be glad to know that Lewis and Clark kept a record of their trip which you will enjoy reading. Clark was a poor speller, and he followed no rules in the use of capital letters, but you will find what he wrote most interesting.

The Return of Lewis and Clark and the 

Growth of the Fur Trade. On their return trip Lewis and Clark did not follow exactly the route they had taken in going west. At one point they separated and took different routes, so that they could see more of the country. But they came together again at a point on the Missouri River and went on to 

St. Louis.

After the return of Lewis and Clark to 

St. Louis, fur traders from that city made their way into the western country. 

There they traded with the Indians who were friendly, and there they had trouble with the traders and trappers of the North-West Company until the boundary dispute between Great Britain and the United States was settled. All of this region finally became a part of the United States.

THE WEST EXPLORED FROM ITS LANDWARD SIDE 35 

John Jacob Astor, a Trader in Furs. 

John Jacob Astor of New York made a great fortune out of trade in furs. He tried to build a chain of fur-trading stations across the plains of central 

United States, in the western mountains, and along the Columbia River. He sent men to the Columbia River country to build a trading station where the city of Astoria, in Oregon, now is. But the men of the North-West Company were working against him, and in the end 

Astor's men gave up the attempt to carry on the trade along the Columbia.

The stations that had been built were sold to the North-West Company.

In The War of 1812 and Afterward. 

1812 Great Britain and the United States were again at war. When peace came, there was a question as to where the boundary between the western part of the United States and British America was to be drawn. This was settled in 

1812. But the dividing of the Oregon 

Country came much later. 

The Rule of the Father of Oregon and the Coming of Settlers to the Oregon

Country. - The Hudson's Bay Company had taken over the North-West Company and sent Dr. John McLoughlin to the 

Columbia station. There he ruled for 

22 years, and there he earned the name "Father of Oregon." For a period of about thirty years after the War of 1812, the history of the Oregon Country centered around the trade in furs by the 

British and the coming of settlers from the east over the Oregon Trail. With the passing years, more and more settlers crossed the mountains, and at last the

British government and that of the 

United States agreed upon a boundary the one we now use.

The Missionaries. -One chapter in the story of the settlement of the Oregon 

Country must not be forgotten. As early as 1834, Jason and Daniel Lee entered the Oregon Country and founded missions. Two years later 20 more missionaries came to work among the Indians and the trappers and traders. In 1836 

Dr. and Mrs. Marcus Whitman established a mission in the southeastern part of the present state of Washington. As a result of the work of the Whitmans, a school was started and a considerable amount of land was planted to crops. 

Catholic fathers also went into this northwestern country to preach to the

Indians and to be of help to others as they were able.

1. The dividing of the Oregon Country between Great Britain and the United States.

THE NEW WORLD AND THE WESTWARD MOVEMENT OVER THE OREGON TRAIL 

The Oregon Trail, along which so many thousands made their way into the Oregon Country and California at the time the West was being settled, is shown on the map on page 34. The trail followed in part the route of Lewis and 

Clark. But most of the emigrants traveled overland for the entire journey, while Lewis and Clark used boats and canoes for a large part of their journey. 

The Route. The map shows you that the Oregon Trail started from St. 

Louis, on the west bank of the Mississippi 

River, and followed the Missouri River for a considerable distance. Many of the pioneers who took this trail into the 

Oregon Country bought their supplies at St. Louis. But the real eastern end of the trail was at the small settlement of 

Independence in Missouri. From that point it struck off across the plains to the Platte River. It then followed along the Platte to a point near the present city of Casper in Wyoming, where it turned southwest into the region around 

Great Salt Lake. There the trail that led to California broke off from the main trail which led to the Oregon Country. 

From the region around Great Salt 

Lake the main trail turned toward the northwest into Idaho and the valley of the Snake River. This stream was reached where the city of Pocatello has grown up. From Pocatello the trail followed the Snake River for some distance, and then, leaving the river, it turned toward the northwest through 

Boise and crossed the Snake River again into the present state of Oregon. It crossed the northeastern part of that state to the Columbia River, which it followed to the Pacific.

The story of the trials and the sufferings of the thousands who followed this trail to the West is one of the great stories of our history. Hundreds and hundreds died on the way, but many thousands got safely through. A. 

OP. F. Collier and Son

Ox teams making their way westward. They were escorted by outriders for protection against Indian attacks.

OVER THE OREGON TRAIL 

37 

From St. Louis to the 

Great Plains. The first part of the route across the 

Great Central Plains of our country was the easiest part of the trip. Long lines of covered wagons carried the household goods of the emigrants (36 A). In these wagons the youngest children and some of the women were made as comfortable as possible. Many of the teams that hauled the wagons were driven by women, while the men and boys rode horseback alongside. In the Indian country scouts were sent on ahead to give warning of any danger. At night or whenever there was an

Plains there is not much rain and because they are so dry they were very early called the "Great American Desert."

Indian attack the wagons reproducing a scene from the early days. Notice that the wagons are drawn were drawn up in a circle and the horses cared for so they could not be | run off by the Indians (A). At the time of an Indian attack, the men of the party fought from behind their covered wagons. 

When everything was peaceful at night, campfires would be lighted, over which the evening meal would be prepared.

Afterward there was usually some music and dancing, as in almost every party there was some one who could play the "fiddle" and many who enjoyed singing.

When it was time to sleep, the guards for the night took their places and the others rolled up in their blankets. 

The Great Plains. The Central 

Plains increase slowly in height toward the west. The plains near the mountains are very much higher than farther east, and have been given the name of Great 

Plains (map, page 80). Over the Great 

Across these high, dry plains the Oregon

Trail wound its way. This part of the trail, as far as and through the South 

Pass where the trail crosses the Rocky 

Mountains, was carefully gone over and described by the American soldier and explorer John C. Frémont. His notes on this part of the route were of the greatest value to the emigrants who followed it. A year later, Frémont with the help of Kit Carson, a famous guide, marked out the route of the Oregon Trail to the mouth of the Columbia River. Forts where the pioneers could find rest, safety, and supplies were built at Bridger, Fort 

Hall, Boise, and at other points a few days' journey from each other.

The Trail beyond the Rockies.

- The -The trail located by Frémont and Carson, west of the Rockies, was difficult and dangerous. Those who started late or who for any reason were delayed might be compelled to travel this part of the route late in the year when the mountain passes were filled with snow. 

It was on this part of the trail that so many died from cold and hunger as well as from attacks by the Indians. Frémont found a way across the present states of 

Utah and Nevada. In midwinter he made his way in safety across the high, snow-covered, wind-swept heights of the 

Sierra Nevada. 

Explorations by Pike and Bonneville.

Two other Americans who added much to our geographical knowledge of the 

West were Zebulon Pike and Benjamin 

L. E. Bonneville. Pike explored the country west and southwest of St. 

Louis, followed the Arkansas River west through the Royal Gorge (96 A), and saw the mountain peak to which his name has been given (A). Later explorations took him to the Rio Grande. 

Bonneville, who was born in France, came to our country when only six years old. He was graduated from the United States Military 

Academy at West Point on the Hudson River, and he rose to the rank of captain.

While on a leave of absence from the army, he spent more than three years exploring the West.

A religious group, the Mormons, who crossed the 

 plains and mountains from 

Illinois to Great Salt Lake, founded settlements there and have since become very prosperous. become very prosperous. In the early days they were of much help to the pioneers who turned from the main line of the Oregon Trail to cross the 

Great Basin and Sierra Nevada into the 

Valley of California (map, page 80).

SETTLEMENT OF THE SOUTHWEST 

39 

EXPLORATION AND SETTLEMENT OF THE SOUTHWEST 

You have already learned that the early explorers of what is now southwestern

United States were Spanish people who had settled in Mexico. Some of the first of the expeditions passed through a region where the Indians had built their homes, or pueblos, on the slopes or tops of high masses of rock. These rock masses were called mesas, and rose steeply from the surrounding plains. Many of the pueblos were more than one story high, and most of them could be reached only by ladders or by steps cut into the rocky sides of the mesas. Because of their location, they could easily be defended from the attacks of other Indians, and some of them were successfully defended for a time against attacks by Spanish soldiers.

Seen from a distance the pueblos of some of the mesas looked like great castles. When the explorers, who first saw them but did not reach them, returned to Mexico, they told wonderful stories about the castles and great cities that they had seen. The men of later expeditions were greatly disappointed by what they found when they were able to visit these Indian homes. 

A Search for Precious Metals. The leaders of most of the early expeditions into southwestern United States, which then belonged to Spain, were searching for mines of gold and silver. They hoped, also, to find ornaments and vessels made of these precious metals, such as had been found in Mexico, Central 

America, and parts of South America. 

Some of these expeditions went east and then north as far as the present state of Kansas. Others went north across the higher plains, or plateaus, as far as 

Utah. Still others went west through the present state of Arizona into southern California. 66 

The Discovery of the Grand Canyon. 

A Spanish writer, telling the story of an expedition that went in search of a great river in the northwestern part of the present state of Arizona, had this to say: they came to the banks of a river which were so high that from the edge of one bank to the other appeared to be three or four leagues (from 8 to 10 miles) in the air. The country was elevated and full of low twisted pines, very cold, and lying open toward the north. They spent three days on this bank looking for a passage down to the river, which looked from above as if the water was six feet across, although the Indians said it was half a league wide (about 1 miles)."

This great gorge, which was first seen by white men at this time (1540), we now know as the Grand Canyon of the Colorado. It is one of the most wonderful canyons of the world. You will later learn about the way in which it was formed and how it looks today.

The Early Expeditions into New 

Mexico. At first the name given to the new lands into which these expeditions were sent was "New Mexico." This area was later divided into several states, of which the present state of New Mexico is one. 

Most of the military expeditions sent into this old region of New Mexico failed to find the gold for which they were looking. Worse than this, each of these expeditions had trouble with the Indians, many of whom they killed. Because of the cruelty with which they were treated,

A. Spanish missions in the early days. even the Indians who were friendly at first finally became bitter enemies. As a result, when rocks with gold in them were at last discovered, the Indians refused to work in the mines. So, even after gold had been found, it was many years before the mines were producing gold enough to make them pay.

Missionaries and Missions. - After the failure of the military expeditions, it was decided that any other expeditions were to be led by missionaries. These priests were sent out to convert the Indians to Christianity. They were told to build missions wherever they thought they should do so. They were told, also, to gather the Indians into villages around these missions and to help them to live a more settled life.

Many of the mission settlements were successful. The priests taught the Indians to plant and harvest crops. In re- gions of little rainfall, water was brought to the fields by ditches from distant streams. Among the crops raised were corn, wheat, many vegetables, cotton, and grapes for wine. There was wool from the sheep that the priests had brought with them, and both wool and LA. (New Orleans)

GULF OF MEXICO 

cotton were made into cloth. 

Other domestic animals that were raised included cattle, goats, horses, mules, and fowls. The skins of the domestic animals, as well as those of buffalo, deer, and rabbits, were made into garments and used for saddles or in other ways.

Spanish Settlement In creases. The Spaniards in 

Mexico heard stories about the pleasant and productive lands near the missions. Many of them came to settle in these new lands, and population increased slowly but steadily. 

Missions were established in Lower

California, and later in our present state of California, then called Alta California.

Military colonies and missions were set up at San Diego and Monterey. Colonists went forward by both land and sea. 

One of the land expeditions lost its way in crossing the mountains but finally reached the coast far enough north to discover San Francisco Bay. In 1821

Mexico became an independent country.
