THE GREAT GOLD RUSH 

41 

A. The Santa Fé Trail, west of Dodge City, Kansas. 

THE GREAT GOLD RUSH 

Some settlements by Americans had been made in the region that is now 

California even before gold was discovered in 1848. But it was after this discovery that California began to attract large numbers of people. 

Routes to the Gold Fields. Thousands started for the gold fields to seek their fortunes. Some of those from the eastern and central parts of our country followed "overland trails" across the plains and mountains. Others took ships to the Isthmus of Panama, crossed that isthmus, and took ships again for 

California. Still others took the long sea journey around the southern end of

South America. Many of those who started out with high hopes lost their lives on the way. Some of those who followed the overland trails were killed by the Indians or died from thirst, starvation, or disease. It is said that cholera alone caused the death of more than 5000 of these gold seekers. Some of the ships that followed the long sea route around Cape Horn were never never heard of again. 

The Easiest Route. After a time the route across the Isthmus of Panama became the most popular. It was not easy, but it was easier than the route across country and shorter than the one around the Horn. Sometimes there were long delays. This was because there were more ships on the Atlantic by which people could reach the isthmus than there were on the Pacific side to carry them on to San Francisco. 

Where Gold Was Sought.-This is not the place to tell the story of the many mining camps that were established, or to draw a picture of the rough life of these early seekers after gold. It is enough to say that in their search for the precious metal the gold hunters explored much of the mountainous area of the 

West. Their trails and camps extended northward into British Columbia and southward into our present states of 

Idaho, Montana, and Wyoming.

Other Occupations. Many of those who went west to "seek their fortunes" were not successful. They became discouraged and some of them turned to trading or farming, and others returned to their eastern homes. The Mormons,

The Days of the Cattle Kings and 

Cowboys. -Farther south in this same 

Great Plains area, cattle and horses that had been brought by Spanish explorers and settlers had escaped and found the grasslands north of the Rio Grande.

They also increased in numbers. The 

Indians had long known how to catch, tame, and ride the horses. The white men who made their way into this country from Kentucky and Tennessee also knew how to catch and ride wild horses.

There were so many of the long-legged, long-horned cattle, that they had little if any value. But some of the early settlers claimed great areas of land and many thousands of cattle (A). Each of these "cattle kings" hired "cowboys" to look after their cattle which grazed with those of other owners over the unfenced pastures. When, at an earlier date, the well-watered lowlands of the Mississippi Valley were settled, the great herds of buffalo had moved west into the Great Plains. How Mail and Goods  

Were Carried in the Early A.- Indians holding up a stagecoach. Although this is a modern picture 

Days.- Wherever people go of early days in the West, it shows a typical stagecoach that carried baggage, they like to hear from those whom they have left behind, and they like to send back messages to their old homes. 

After they have founded new homes in new lands and have some surplus for sale, they like to have a route over which such a surplus can be sent to market.

It was so with the pioneers who went west to make their homes in the lands beyond the Rockies. They wanted some way of receiving and sending both messages and goods.

In the older settled lands of eastern United States, these same needs had been met in many different ways when the settlements were very young. Later, but long before the western part of our country was settled, new and better ways were found. Roads were improved and stagecoaches were carrying passengers, mail, and goods between neighboring towns and cities. Canals that had been built furnished another and cheaper way of transporting goods. Railroads came later, but many of these had been built before 1860, and on these mail, express, passengers, and mail. and freight, as well as passengers, were being carried as they were also on steamboats that had come into use. Finally, the invention of the telegraph made possible the almost instantaneous sending of messages between points connected by the telegraph wires.

In the West, after permanent settlements had been established, stagecoaches soon came into use. The roads over which they traveled were always rough and often dangerous. In addition to the usual dangers and discomforts of bad roads, men and animals had to face the cold and snow of the mountains and the lack of water in the deserts that they crossed. One other danger, also, they were compelled to face. Robbers or highwaymen might hold up the stage and rob the passengers, express packages, and mail bags or pouches (A). For messages, especially, a much more rapid method of sending them was needed, for the stagecoaches were slow at their very best.

Such a method was soon found.

GULF OF 

MEXICO - Routes of the Pony Express and early western railroads. 

The Pony Express. - In 1860 a rapid mail service was established between St. 

Joseph, in the state of Missouri, and 

Sacramento, in the state of California (map, pages 86-87). This was known as the Pony Express (45 A), although the swift horses that were used in this service could not properly be called ponies. 

It was on April 3, at four o'clock in the afternoon, that the first Pony Express rider started from St. Joseph. On the same day, another rider left Sacramento.

Each of them had mail pouches slung across the saddle. The route that was to be followed had already been carefully laid out (A). At various points along this route there were stations where horses were kept. At first these stations called relay stations were about twenty-five miles apart, but later in the rougher and more difficult parts of the route the stations were only ten to twelve miles apart. 

From St. Joseph the first relay station was twentyfive miles to the west, and that distance was covered as rapidly as the pony could make it. Shortly before the rider was due, another pony was saddled and waiting. 

As the rider came into the station he would change from the tired to the fresh pony, and taking his mail pouches with him, be off for the next station with the shortest possible stop. 

Each rider used three ponies and rode at first about seventy-five miles in the more open country.

Later, when the distances between some stations were made less, the riders over that part of the route did not have so far to go. At the end of his run each Pony Express rider would find another rider already in the saddle, ready to grab the mail pouches and be off for the part of the trip that was his. It was often true that the new rider was away before the man who had just arrived had time to get out of his saddle.

In this way the mail was hurried forward without stop, except when Indians, robbers, or accident prevented. When the mail reached Sacramento, that part which was to go to San Francisco was sent forward by a fast steamship. The first trip from St. Joseph to San Francisco took about eleven days, and one fast trip was made in seven days and seventeen hours. Today, as in the days of the Pony 

Express, the motto of those who carry the mail is: "The mail must go through."

COMMUNICATION 

45 A. 

A Pony Express rider crossing the Great Plains. In the distance you can see hostile Indians trying to overtake him.

The End of the Pony Express. — In this way, for nearly two years, mail was carried across the plains, mountains, and plateaus of western United States. At first a rider started from St. Joseph daily when the train from the East brought in the mail that was to be sent on. Later only two trips a week were made. But once the mail was placed in the hands of a Pony Express rider nothing except accident was allowed to delay it. It went forward in winter as in summer, in bad weather as well as in good weather. 

In 1861 a telegraph line that connected eastern lines with San Francisco was completed. Those who needed to get messages through quickly made use of the telegraph. Letters were 

Letters were sent by stagecoach, and later, after the transcontinental railroads were built, by train. 

With the building of the telegraph there was no longer any need for the Pony 

Express and this service was soon afterwards given up.

(Lippincott) also has a good account of methods of travel before the building of railroads in the West.

THE COMING OF FOREIGN PEOPLES 

The people who took part in the early western movement in the United States were mainly from older settlements in the eastern part of our country. Others came from Great Britain, and some from 

Germany and France. When people in southeastern United States began to grow cotton on a great scale, they brought many Negroes into the country to work in the cotton fields.

 HELEN G. PRATT, Assisted by The Elementary Curriculum Committee

HOW OUR ISLANDS WERE BUILT 

Our islands have been built almost entirely by volcanoes. The island of 

Hawaii is still being built. Lava still flows within the great craters of Kilauea and Mokuaweoweo (50 A), which is at the summit of Mauna Loa. From openings farther down the slope of Mauna Loa, lava sometimes flows slowly toward the sea. It cools and hardens and forms black masses of new rock across the land. 

Older flows are not so black. Soil has begun to form on them and a few trees are beginning to grow here and there.

Still older flows are covered with thin soil and trees, but the rock is near the surface. After a very long time, the rock is broken down or eroded, and deep soils are formed. 

Copyright, 1934, by American Book Company. E. P. I 47 

The Lava Flows. This is the way all our islands were built. The lava first flowed out deep down on the bottom of the ocean. The rock piled slowly upward until it began to appear above the sea. It kept on piling up higher and higher. The volcanoes of Hawaii have built two very high mountains. From the bottom of the sea to their tops, Mauna Loa and Mauna Kea are two of the highest mountains in the world.

We can see less than half of them, for the rest lies under the ocean. 

Millions of lava flows were piled up to form our islands. For many, many years they were bare rock, like the new lava flows of Hawaii. The wind and the rain began to break up the rock, and soil was formed. Birds may have brought the first seeds which took root and grew.

As plant life increased, the roots split | High fertile volcanic islands have many different kinds of plants which cannot grow in the poor soil of low, hot coral islands. the rocks still further. Life slowly grew on the islands. This went on for many hundreds of years. 

The oldest islands are Niihau and 

Kauai. Other very old parts are the 

Waianae region on Oahu, West Maui, and Kohala on Hawaii. The youngest parts are Haleakala on Maui (56 A), and all of Hawaii except Kohala. Older parts have deep valleys cut by streams and rain and wind during hundreds of years. 

The deepest, most famous valleys are found in the parts of our islands that are oldest.

The Coral Reefs. Coral reefs are found near parts of the islands. They are largely built by the skeletons of tiny animals which can live only in fairly shallow, warm salt water. Coral reefs protect many of the beaches and form harbors. Surf riding in canoes or on boards is made possible where the surf breaks on these reefs. Coral rock found near the coast shows that the sea once covered this land region. 

High Volcanic Islands. Since coral animals cannot live and work out of warm water, islands which they build up are always low and flat and found in warm regions. High volcanic islands are much pleasanter, for the ocean cannot sweep over them in a storm. The mountains stand in the path of rainbearing clouds and make them drop their moisture. The rain forms streams. It waters the land so that trees and plants grow. The higher we go up the slopes of the mountains, the cooler it is. More kinds of things can grow since the islands have both warm and cool regions. Soils formed on volcanic islands are more fertile than soils made out of coral rock. 

People live more comfortably when many different kinds of plants can be grown. People are happier in beautiful surroundings. Volcanic islands, with their high mountains, their deep valleys, their streams, and their forests, are very beautiful.

The people who discovered and settled the islands came from islands farther south. No one knows exactly when this happened. We know more about the later pioneers who began to come about nine hundred years ago, perhaps from

Tahiti (map, pages 318-319). They traveled back and forth over the ocean, and told about their adventures in songs and in chants which they taught to their 155°

A. 

Lava still flows within the great crater of Mokuaweoweo. children. In this way the story has come down to us. These settlers brought many food plants. They brought some small animals: the chicken, the pig, and the dog. 

The Polynesian Pioneers. These pioneers were Polynesians and later came to be known in our islands as 

Hawaiians. They were great adventurers. 

They traveled in huge canoes fastened together by a sort of deck, so that the whole thing looked like a small ship.

They spent months preparing for the voyage and waiting for favorable weather. 

They put in stores of yams, dried taro, dried sweet potato, and dried bananas.

They prepared hala or pandanus food and dried fish. Drinking water stored in gourds and in bamboo joints. 

Stones, earth, and firewood were put on board for the imu to be used for cooking was on the voyage. The canoes were most carefully made ready for the journey. 

They were equipped with paddles, with sails made of mats of lauhala, with bailers, and with stone anchors. Plants and seeds were put on board. When everything was ready, the men of the crew, the leaders of the expedition, the women and children who were going, and the animals to be taken went on board. 

Then the little ships set sail on the voyage to distant Hawaii. 

The leaders were skilled sailors. They knew where they were going and how to steer their way by the stars. They watched the winds, the currents, and the skies.

The Polynesians discovered and settled the great islands of the eastern Pacific. 

They went out to find new islands and returned to tell of their discoveries.

HOW THE HAWAIIANS LIVED 

51 

HOW THE HAWAIIANS 

LIVED 

More than six hundred years ago, the Hawaiians stopped making making voyages between Hawaii and the islands to the south. They had everything they needed which could be obtained from the south. For five hundred years the Hawaiian people lived alone in these islands. The people in the south made no more voyages to the north. The people in the rest of the world had never heard of Hawaii. one came from the outside world to return and tell of the islands. During all this long period, the people of

Hawaii lived on what they could raise here, on what grew in the mountains, and on what lived in the sea. All their food and clothing, all their tools and utensils, all their materials, canoes, houses, and house furnishings everything they used -came from the land and the ocean about them.

Photo by 11th Photo Section, A. C., Wheeler Field, T. H. 

A. The pit of Halemaumau in Kilauea.

They spread the food plants- the coconut, the sugar cane, the banana, the breadfruit, the sweet potato, and the yam over this whole region. They were the first Pacific explorers.

1. What exploring expeditions do you hear of today? What preparations are made for these expeditions? Compare these with the preparations made by the Polynesian explorers.

Geography, Book I, pages 22-25; Our Past in Western Europe, pages 251-263; and Introduction to World Geography, pages 27-29. On the map on page 30 in this book you will see the routes followed by early European explorers in the Pacific Ocean. 

No 

Stone Tools. - Hawaii has no metals, so nearly every tool was made of stone. 

Perhaps you have seen the stone adzes, stone chisels, and stone poi pounders made in those days. With these tools, the Hawaiians cut down the great forest trees used for canoes and hollowed out the hard trunks. With these tools, they did all their wood carving. Since there were no metals, there were no nails. So the framework of the house was tied together with olona. The Polynesians, who were sailors, knew how to tie things together. HAW -4

A. 

Waimea Canyon on Kauai. This is one of the famous deep valleys of the islands. 

Hawaiian Kapas. - Hawaii had no fiber plants, such as cotton or flax, from which cloth could be woven. There were no large animals whose skins could be used for clothing. The inside bark of the wauke was made into the kapa, which was used for clothing and bed coverings. Hawaiian kapas are the most beautiful ones to be found in all Polynesia. Floor mats and canoe sails were made of lauhala. 

Early Hawaiian Food. There were only a few small animals, not nearly enough to supply meat for all the people. 

The Hawaiians became expert fishermen, obtaining most of their meat from the sea. The olona plant, which grew in the uplands, supplied a fiber which was woven into fish nets and fish lines. Fish hooks were made of bone. Fish ponds with walls of stone were built in places along the shores. The people fished in the ponds, near the shore, and out in the deep sea. 

As there was no material to make pots and kettles which could be used over a fire, the food was cooked in underground ovens or imus. There were no good

HOW THE HAWAIIANS LIVED 

53 clays from which dishes and pottery could be made, so bowls and platters were carved from wood. Hawaiian bowls are noted for their graceful lines and fine workmanship.

Wet-land taro, one of the chief food plants, is grown in slowly moving fresh water. Very skillful irrigation was arranged, and the great taro fields were cultivated in terraces through which the water was run. The only tool used was the oo, a sharp-pointed stick. Since no artificial fertilizer could be obtained, different kinds of plants were used to fertilize the soils. Hau branches were one of the chief things used for this purpose. Sweet 

Sweet potatoes, yams, and dry-land taro were raised. Bananas and sugar cane grew without much cultivation. The Hawaiians chewed the sugarcane stalks just as children do today. 

The Hawaiians did not discover the secret of making sugar from the cane. 

As we know, the juice from the sugar cane must be boiled for a very long time to make sugar. The Hawaiians obtained all their drinking water from streams and springs on the islands.

Though there were not many different kinds of foods in old Hawaii, we know that the foods which were here made up a fine diet. The people were tall and strong and had splendid teeth.

How the Early Hawaiians Lived. 

Most of the things which the people needed were found at the shore, along the streams, and in the lower parts of the valleys, so the settlements were usually found in these regions. There were no cities. People built their homes where it was most convenient for them to live. 

Because the people produced everything they used, there was no buying and selling. Some men went high up on the slopes of Mauna Kea and found there an especially good rock for adzes. They sometimes exchanged these adzes for things they needed. Particularly fine mats were made from a sedge growing only on Niihau, and the people who made these mats sometimes exchanged them for other articles. There was no money, for it was not needed. Taxes were paid to the chiefs in labor done on the land of the chiefs, or in things, such as food, mats, feathers, and kapa.

All the work done by the people was difficult. Try to make a lauhala mat or a piece of kapa. Try to tie the framework of even a toy house together, or to shape wood with stone tools. You will find that it is not easy. Children and young people in old Hawaii had to be taught many things so that they would be able to provide food, clothing, shelter, and tools for their own families. Children helped with the work the older people were doing and learned many things that way. Some of them had special training, so that they could teach other young people. Some of them learned all about the uses of the different plants. Some of them learned the songs and chants which told the story of the people. Everything found out by their fathers and grandfathers had to be learned so that it could be passed on, for there were no books. 

One very interesting thing about life in our islands in those early days is that the Hawaiians found ways of doing things so that they could get along comfortably without anything from the outside world. 

Nearly as many people lived here then as today, and they got along with the things which could be found or raised here.

THE DISCOVERY OF HAWAII BY THE OUTSIDE WORLD 

In 1778, Captain Cook, an English explorer, happened to find Hawaii. 

He discovered Kauai first and sailed from there to the other islands of the group. Other explorers and traders began to come to Hawaii. Captain Vancouver was one of the most important of these, for he brought the first cattle, sheep, and orange trees to the islands.

A Book to Read. Read Stories of Hawaii, Captain Cleveland brought the first pages 47-109. horses. 

Williams Studio

A. 

Honolulu harbor, about 1870.

THE DISCOVERY OF HAWAII BY THE OUTSIDE WORLD 55 Trading with the Outside World Is 

Begun. These explorers and traders came in little sailing ships which would look very small and strange to us today. 

The Hawaiians had never seen such ships. 

They had never seen iron tools. They had never seen guns. They were very much interested in these new things, particularly in iron. They saw that iron tools were easier to use than stone tools.

They wished to have some of these useful articles.

In those days, a sailing voyage from 

Europe to the Pacific Ocean took months.

People had not discovered how to can food. For these long voyages, meat packed in salt to preserve it was the chief food. Fresh water was carried in barrels, and the supply often ran short. 

Travelers in those days were very glad to come to a place where they could get fresh food and water.

The ships that came to Hawaii wanted food and water. The Hawaiians wanted some of the new things from the outside world. 

So trading began.

The Hawaiian people had never used money. They had no written language and no way of keeping accounts. They had not needed any of these things when they lived alone on the islands. Now they began to feel that they needed to learn new things.

THE FIRST SETTLERS FROM 

AMERICA

After trading began, some traders made their homes in Hawaii. But in 1820, the first people who planned to settle in 

Hawaii, to learn the language, and to teach the people came to the islands. 

They were missionaries from New England. They came from the port of Boston. If you look at the map, pages 8687, you will see that the whole of the 

United States stretches between Boston and the Pacific Ocean. At that time, 1820, some of this great territory did not even belong to the United States. Indians lived on the plains, and great forests covered much of the country. 

There were no roads across the region. 

So the missionaries could not come across the continent and sail from the western coast. They had to take a ship from 

Boston and sail south around South

America (map, pages 318-319). Their ship could not come across the narrow isthmus at Panama, because there was no canal there then. It took a little sailing ship five or six months to reach 

Hawaii from Boston.

What the Missionaries 

Missionaries Found.

When the missionaries finally reached 

Hawaii, they were surprised to find so beautiful a place. The long voyage on the little ship had not been easy. But this new land was attractive, and the 

Hawaiians met them with kindness.

Many things in the islands were strange to the people from New England. The clothing, the language, the houses, and the trees and plants were different from anything they had known. The food of the people was strange to them. A story is told of one of the missionaries who

Photo by 11th Photo Section, A. C., Wheeler Field, T. H. 

A. In the crater of Haleakala, on Maui. heard that he would have to eat bananas in Hawaii. He had never eaten bananas, for in those days bananas were unknown in New England. They grow only in warm countries, and they ripen quickly after they are picked. No ship traveled fast enough to bring bananas to the north. So this missionary bought his first bananas when his ship stopped at a 

South American port. Every day he ate a banana from the bunch, trying to get used to this new food.

The missionaries had to learn to eat strange food, and to live in strange houses. They had to wait a year to get an answer to letters. They did not know what was happening in the world outside, for there were no cables and no wireless then. They got news perhaps twice a year when a ship came in with mail. 

Every year all the missionaries came from the different places on the islands to a meeting in Honolulu. The journey from the other islands often took several days. It was even hard to travel to 

Honolulu from places on Oahu. In those days, there were well-marked trails, but no roads. The Hawaiians had never needed roads. They had no pack animals, and they had no particular need to travel from place to place. In order to reach 

Honolulu, a missionary living at Waialua on Oahu had to travel over trails through the gulches to Ewa. There he took a canoe to Honolulu. The journey might take three days. Many of the missionaries saw their friends only once a year, at the meeting.

The missionaries had to endure many things which were not easy. But they

THE FIRST SETTLERS FROM AMERICA 

57 never had to suffer hunger or cold or danger which early settlers of other places generally had to meet. The Pilgrim settlers of New England nearly starved to death. The early settlers everywhere in the United States were in constant danger of Indian attack. Hawaii, with its pleasant climate and its kindly people, has never offered real hardship or danger to any newcomer.

The missionaries learned the Hawaiian language. They selected an alphabet, and printed books in Hawaiian. Then they began to teach the people to read. 

Schools were started. The first great school was started at Lahainaluna, on 

Maui, over one hundred years ago.

LIFE IN THE ISLANDS BEGINS TO CHANGE 

The missionaries sent back to New England for many things. They sent for seeds and plants. They sent for cloth to make clothing. They sent for lumber to build the kind of house they had known. in New England. The boards for the first wooden house in the islands were brought around Cape Horn in a sailing vessel. This house still stands in Honolulu, on King Street, next to Kawaiahao church. Nails, tools, and household furnishings were brought to the islands. 

The Whaling Ships. These were the days of the whalers. Whale oil was burned in the lamps used at this time. 

Kerosene was unknown, and gas and electric lighting had not been invented. 

Hundreds of ships went out of New

England ports, to travel down through the Atlantic around South America and north to the whaling grounds. The whaling ships came to Hawaii to spend the winter. Sometimes two hundred of these ships were anchored here at one time. Lahaina, with its large roadstead protected by Maui and Lanai, was one of the favorite stopping places for these fleets. The ships often needed new ropes, new sails, flour, salt, and other supplies.

The Growth of Trade. Because of the demand for many new things, traders came to the islands to open stores. Business people came from England, other European countries, and the United States. 

The newcomers in Hawaii saw sugar cane growing everywhere in little patches. 

They knew that more of it could be raised and sold. Sugar cane grows only in warm countries. The United States has never been able to produce enough sugar for its people. The newcomers in 

Hawaii knew that sugar produced here could easily be sold in the United States. 

They began to plant great fields of sugar cane.

The Hawaiian people needed money to buy the many new things which were being brought to the islands. Some of them earned money by going as sailors on the ships stopping here. Some of them sold or rented their lands to the people who wished to raise sugar cane. 

Some of them made the salt needed by the fleets. They no longer supplied themselves with everything they needed, as they had done earlier.

For all these different reasons, life in the islands changed very rapidly. The people grew more and more dependent on other countries, particularly the 

United States.

NEWCOMERS FROM ASIA AND EUROPE 

The sugar plantations increased in number and in size. Plantation workers were needed and men went from Hawaii to different countries to persuade workmen to come here. Workers came from the southern part of China, near Canton (map, pages 318-319). These men did not know what the islands were like.

They did not know the languages spoken in Hawaii. They came to a strange, unknown country to earn money because they found it hard to support their families in the crowded lands of southern

NEWCOMERS FROM ASIA AND EUROPE 

59 A. 

Courtesy Fleet Air Base, Pearl Harbor, T. H. 

One of the improved highways of the islands. Notice how the road winds and curves. Why? 

China. The first Polynesians who took the long voyage to Hawaii probably came because their own islands were becoming crowded and they needed another place to live. The first Chinese came for much the same reason, except that they did not plan to remain in Hawaii. 

Many of them returned later to China. 

Others stayed in Hawaii. They learned the Hawaiian language, the most common language in the islands at that time. 

Some of them married Hawaiian women.

They worked hard and provided well for their families. 

The Reciprocity Treaty with the United

States. At this time, Hawaii was still an independent country. More and more people from the United States were coming to the islands to engage in business. Partly through their influence, Hawaii was able to make a treaty with the United States known as a Reciprocity Treaty. That is, it was arranged that things sent from Hawaii to the mainland should not be taxed on entering the United States, and things coming to the islands from the United States should not be taxed on entering Hawaii. 

In addition, Hawaii gave Pearl Harbor to the United States to use as a naval station. Before that time the American battleships had no coaling base in the Pacific. 

This new trade arrangement made the sugar industry more profitable, for sugar could enter the United States free from tax. More fields were planted in cane. 

More workers were needed.

More People Come to the Hawaiian 

Islands. Portuguese people from the

Canary Islands and the Madeira Islands came to Hawaii. The voyage was long, through the Atlantic Ocean, around South

America, and on to Hawaii (map, pages 

318-319). At that time, some of these voyages took three months. The Portuguese came with their wives and children, planning to live in Hawaii.

When more and more people came to the islands, more stores were needed.

More houses had to be built. As trade increased, more roads had to be made. 

Rice raising began, because so many of the newcomers were rice-eating people.

Many of the people began to work at these different things, and still more and still more workers were needed for the plantations. 

People from Japan came to Hawaii.

Many of the Japanese sent back to Japan 

Hawaii Today. - Since about 1931, no new workers have come to the islands. 

There are enough people here to do the work of the land. The thing to do today is to divide the work so that every one already here has a chance to earn a living. 

The great industries are interested in making plantation life attractive to the people born in the islands.

In Hawaii today there are many people whose ancestors came long ago from the islands to the south. There are people whose fathers or grandfathers came from 

Europe, or from the United States, or from China, from Japan, from Korea, or from the Philippine Islands. All

SOME THINGS GIVEN TO HAWAII BY ITS PEOPLE 

The newcomers from many different countries spoke many different languages. 

English had been taught in some schools ever since the missionaries arrived. More and more people in the islands were speaking English. It was decided that English should be the language taught in all the schools to all the children.

The English language has made the people of Hawaii a part of the Englishspeaking peoples. It has made it possible for any one educated in the islands to read books printed in English, to travel to English-speaking countries, and to feel at home there. 

Hawaii and the United States. 

Hawaii's connection with the United

States has helped to make the many different peoples of the islands into one people. In 1898, the independent country of Hawaii became a part of the United States. In 1900, it was made a 

The English language, the public schools, and United States citizenship peoples of Hawaii into one people. are important in making the different 

The Hawaiian Flag. The Hawaiian flag is often flown on holidays. It is always flown from Iolani Palace in Honolulu when the legislature is meeting.

The design of the Hawaiian flag combines the British Union Jack and the American flag. The eight stripes stand for the eight islands. This flag is another evidence of the influence of all Englishspeaking people on Hawaii.

What Our People Have Given Hawaii. 

Other people of Hawaii have given things which we all like and use. Many 

Hawaiian words have been added to the 

English language spoken here. You will find some of them in English dictionaries today. One of the Hawaiian words which people here use constantly, and which people elsewhere are coming to know, is aloha. There is no English word to express all the ideas which aloha tells us. 

It expresses the welcome which Hawaii has always given to strangers. The feeling of aloha helps to make the peoples of Hawaii into one people. The 

Hawaiian custom of giving leis to express aloha is followed by everyone here.

People all over the world know about the leis of Hawaii. 

In our homes, we have Hawaiian things perhaps lauhala mats, or a calabash.

SUGAR CANE IN HAWAII 

You have learned how our islands were built and how the life of the people is influenced by the kind of islands we have. You have found out how the 

Pacific was first explored and how our islands were settled by Polynesian people. You have learned something about the life in old Hawaii which was un High-school pupils drawing in one of the Chinese Rooms of the Hono- known to the rest of the world. You have read about how these lovely islands were discovered by the outside world, and how traders and settlers came here.

With better communication across the 

United States, and the coming of new people to Hawaii, the island life was changed. The island people were changed. You will now find out something about the life of the people today. 

We have Chinese things- perhaps some dishes, silk, a screen, a teak table, or a lacquer box. We have Japanese things perhaps a kimono, some sandals, a blue and white tablecloth, a bamboo stool, or a cloisonné vase. Perhaps we have a beautiful Korean chest. We may have 

Filipino things embroidered pina cloth, or some slippers. 

If we go to the Honolulu Academy of 

Arts (A), we see many of the most beautiful things each of these countries produces. In Hawaii, we have a chance to know and enjoy beautiful useful things brought in from the different countries from which our people have come. 

The people who make up the family of Hawaii have all given something to us which every one likes and uses.

The raising of sugar cane is now the greatest industry of the islands. It influences the life of more people than we may think. Plantation workers are not the only people influenced by the sugar industry. You will find out some of the ways in which this industry affects the lives of most of us. 

Water. - Great sugar plantations are found on each of the four large islands.

All together, these plantations cover about 240,000 acres. Nearly half of the land used for sugar cane is on the island of Hawaii. The other half is almost evenly divided among the three other large islands. There are no sugar-cane fields on the four smaller islands. The chief reason is the lack of water there.

SUGAR CANE IN HAWAII 

63 

Courtesy Fleet Air Base, Pearl Harbor, T. H. 

A. 

- Sugar-cane fields. Point out the reservoirs and the irrigation ditches.

Growing cane needs a great quantity | collected below it flows out, or is pumped of water (A). Unless the plantation is out, to higher levels. in a rainy region, the fields must be irrigated. The artesian wells of Oahu, Kauai, and Maui supply most of the water used on the sugar-cane plantations of these islands. The island of Hawaii has no artesian wells. Nearly all its plantations lie in very rainy districts where irrigation is not necessary.

Artesian water was found in 1879, when the first well was sunk. Rains falling in the uplands soak into the soil. 

The water moves slowly downward through the soil and porous rocks. It comes to a region in which there are layers of non-porous rocks. These layers of non-porous rocks, which in our islands are coral or limestone rock, hold the water. When a well is sunk through the non-porous layer on top, the water The greater part of the water used by the city of Honolulu is artesian water. 

Much water is drawn out every day by the city, and by the plantations of Oahu. 

Since the source of artesian water is the rain, the artesian level is lowered when there is a long dry season. If the water falls too low, the ocean just outside presses in. The well becomes salty and is spoiled. Engineers continually measure the artesian waters. They warn the people when the level is falling, so that they will be careful not to waste water. 

In some parts of the islands, all the water used in homes comes from rain water collected in tanks. In other parts, it comes from mountain streams.

Some of the water used in the cane fields is obtained from tunneling into

Hawatt Tourist Bureau 

Cutting cane on a sugar plantation. the mountains. Porous volcanic rocks hold water in somewhat the same way as a sponge holds it. When the mountains are pierced by tunnels, the water seeps through the rocks from the higher slopes on which the rain falls. The water is collected and piped into reservoirs and irrigation ditches. Sometimes pockets of water are found in the mountains. 

They are old lava tubes or small, cavelike places in which the water continually collects and from which it is piped out into the fields.

The original source of all the fresh water in the islands is the rain. When rain falls to the ground, particularly in heavy storms, it runs swiftly down the slopes of the land and off into streams and into the sea, unless something holds it. Forest trees, grass, and undergrowth help to hold the water. Their roots hold the soil together. Water runs quickly down bare slopes, but it sinks into the ground where there are forests. Many of the forests of old Hawaii have been destroyed. After cattle were introduced into the islands, they were allowed to enter the forest regions. They destroyed many of the trees. Insect and plant pests have helped to kill the trees. Because of the destruction of the forests, much water is lost. On every island today there are forest reservations in which trees are being planted again. 

On all the islands, there are sixty-two of these reservations. They cover about 

1,000,000 acres, or about one fourth of all the land. Men are employed to plant trees, to watch the reservations, and to care for the growing trees.

Experiment Station. One of Hawaii's plantations holds the world's record for sugar production per acre. The average sugar production per acre throughout the islands is higher than that of Java or Cuba or any other sugar-cane region.

When the sugar cane has been cut and sent to the mill, the chief machine work begins. Great rollers crush the juice from the heavy stalks. From the time the stalks go under the rollers until the sugar comes out into bags, almost all the mill work is done by machinery.

11. Sugar cane needs the largest amount of water used in the islands. But other plants are irrigated. Name some of these. 

Field and Mill. - Sugar cane in Hawaii is grown without the use of much machinery. Great steam plows are used to break up the land and to prepare it for planting. But, from the planting to the harvesting, most of the work must be done by hand. If you look at growing cane, you will understand one reason for this. Hawaii's sugar cane is usually allowed to grow for eighteen months. 

No machine has been developed in 

Hawaii which can move through this dense mass to cut it when it is ready to harvest. Another reason why machinery is not much used in field work is that numbers of irrigation ditches cross the fields and make the use of machines difficult. Many field workers are needed to plant, cultivate, fertilize, and harvest sugar cane. were invented in the islands, and many. of them were built here. Sugar machinery for many different sugar-cane regions has been made in Honolulu. Machinery made here has been sent to the Philippine 

Islands, Java, and even Cuba. 

The sugar we commonly use in our homes is white. It is refined sugar. 

Most of Hawaii's sugar is sent to California to be refined. It is coarse and dark brown when it leaves the islands.

After the mill has finished its work, the bags of raw sugar are ready to be sent to the dock to be shipped. 

Railroads carry some of this sugar. These are not plantation roads, but are what we call public carriers. There are five such railroad lines on the islands. They carry passengers as well as freight. 

Some of the sugar is sent to the dock in trucks. Freight boats call at the different islands and pick up the freight for the mainland. 

Most of it is sugar.

TRANSPORTATION 

Much of our sugar cane reaches the mill in cars run on plantation railroads. 

Thirty-two of Hawaii's plantations have their own railway systems, with locomotives, thousands of cane cars, and over 900 miles of track. In addition, portable tracks are carried around and put down where they are needed. Five of the plantations have their own railway shops, where they build cars and do repair work. Some of the plantations which have a great deal of water flume their cane to the mill. Some of the flumes are carried around and set up where they are needed. Other flumes, particularly those built over large gulches, are permanent. Three plantations on the island of Hawaii, where the land is very steep, send the cane down to the began to want things from the outside world, more ships came to bring these things. When Hawaii began to send out freight cargoes, more ships were needed. Mails thus became more frequent. This made it easier to carry on business with the mainland of the United States. Most of the ships coming to Hawaii carry passengers as well as freight, but very few ships are run for passengers alone. 

The larger profit for the steamship companies lies in the freight and in the mail carried. 

The hundreds of thousands of tons of sugar which leave Hawaii every year for the coast are one of the reasons why so many ships come to the islands. 

When the freight shipments were small, little boats went around the islands, stopping at steamer landings and taking on the freight brought out in lighters. They brought the freight to Honolulu to load

TRANSPORTATION 

67 77777 A. 

Photo by 11th Photo Section, Air Service, U. S. A. 

Ahukini Landing. The railroads run down to the pier. Do you see the machinery used for loading? it on ships for the coast. When the freight shipments grew larger, this way of handling the freight over and over was not economical. Larger freighters were used, but these larger ships could not stop at the small landings. They required a dock to which they could tie. 

Docks are equipped with machinery for loading and unloading the cargo. Railroad lines and trucks take the freight directly to and from the docks. Docks have been built at Hilo, Kahului, and 

Nawiliwili. The harbor of Honolulu has twenty-six docks. 

Harbors and seacoasts need protection and care. Channels have been marked and dredged. Breakwaters have been built. Lighthouses have been constructed to guide ships at sea. Docks are lighted and policed to guard against accident and damage. 

Over $100,000,000 worth of freight was sent out or exported from the Hawaiian ports in 1931. Over three quarters of the island exports were sugar. In the same year, more than $86,000,000 worth of freight was brought in, or imported. 

More than 1200 ships called at the ports of the islands in 1931. Nearly three fourths of these came into the harbor of Honolulu.

When a few ports began to carry the trade of the islands, more good roads were needed to get the goods to the water front. Improved roads were constructed on all the islands. 

Much of our comfort of travel on land or sea today is due to the fact that large quantities of freight, of which sugar makes up the greatest amount, must be transported on the islands, and to other parts of the world.

PINEAPPLES IN HAWAII 

The second greatest industry influencing the lives of the people in Hawaii is the raising of pineapples. This work has been highly developed, especially through the work of the Pineapple Pro ducers' Experiment Station. Pineapples are grown on all the islands except 

Niihau and Kahoolawe. About one third as much land is given to pineapples as to sugar cane. 

How Pineapples Are Raised. All of the pineapple lands are not under cultivation at the same time. Fields are allowed to rest for a period of time. 

Pineapples need well-drained land (69 A).

They do not have to be irrigated, for they do not require much water. 

The use of mulch paper to keep down weeds, and hold in moisture and warmth, developed in Hawaii. It was first used on a sugar plantation, and the paper used A. 

Planting pineapples. Mulch paper is used on this plantation.

PINEAPPLES IN HAWAII 

69 

Photo by 11th Photo Section, A. C., Luke Field, T. H. 

A. - A pineapple plantation.

The was made out of bagasse. Sugar plantations did not adopt the practice widely. 

They preferred to use the bagasse for fuel. The pineapple industry began to experiment with mulch paper. It was found that the production was greatly increased. The pineapple industry (68A) spends much money for mulch paper, but it finds the results worth the cost. paper used now in the fields is Americanmade. Mulch paper is also used on some vegetable farms in California. Hawaii is responsible for developing this method. 

Pineapples are not a staple food, as sugar is, so the demand for pineapples is not so steady as the demand for sugar. 

It is necessary to advertise them widely, so that people will buy them. 

The pineapples of Hawaii have been much advertised and they are well known. 

Canneries. -Even in these days of rapid transportation, the shipping of fresh pineapples is not entirely successful. The pineapple which ripens in the field tastes much better than the pineapple which is picked green and shipped to market. Several thousand cases of fresh green pineapples are shipped to California every year to be sold. Many cases of nearly ripe pineapples are taken on board passenger ships stopping here, to be served during the voyage. But almost all the pineapples are canned before they are shipped.

Machinery has been developed to handle most of the processes in canning

39 

A. A pineapple canning factory. Huge machines do most of the work. the fruit. This makes the labor cost of pineapple canning less than the labor cost in the canning of berries, plums, peaches, and other fruits which are prepared for canning by hand.

Hawaii's canneries run during the summer. Older boys and girls work girls work there in the school vacation, for in the canning season extra workers are needed.

COFFEE IN HAWAII 

In Hawaii, sugar and pineapples are raised on large plantations, but coffee is raised by farmers owning or leasing their lands. Coffee is the most important crop raised for export which is produced by independent owners. It is an industry in which all members of the family may help. 

Our Annual Crop. About 5500 acres of land are used for coffee trees. About 

9,000,000 pounds were produced in 1933. 

Forty-six per cent of this crop, valued at over $500,000, was sent out of Hawaii to be sold. The Hawaiian coffee crop is small compared with the world's supply. 

It is about one per cent of the total produced in the world. Most of the world's coffee comes from Brazil, in South America. from

Beginners, Book I, pages 179-183; The Foods 

We Eat, pages 162-167; How We Are Fed, pages 103-108; Living Geography, Book I, pages 206-208; The Americas, pages 265–266.

FOOD RAISED FOR HAWAII'S MARKETS 

The people of Hawaii do other things besides raise, prepare, and ship the three great commercial crops sugar, pineapples, and coffee. They provide themselves with some of the food eaten in the islands.

FOOD RAISED FOR HAWAII'S MARKETS 

71 

Taro. Many 

people like taro.

Sometimes they boil it and serve it instead of potatoes or rice, or make the boiled taro into cakes. Most of the taro of Hawaii is made into poi, which is still a favorite food of many people. Almost all the poi is made in factories today. 

The bags or barrels of cooked and pounded taro are sent to the market.

People buy this and mix it with water at home.

Taro is one of the best starchy foods known, because it is alkaline. Many starches, particularly rice, are known as acid starches, and are less desirable staple foods for that reason. Taro, however, is more expensive than rice. 

All the taro eaten here is raised here.

Taro cannot be shipped great distances. 

If taro is packed too closely, it spoils. If it is left in the sun, it dries.

It is carefully bunched and especially packed for market, and it must reach its market quickly. About 400 acres of land were used for taro raising in 1931. 

Most of the taro fields are in valleys, in some of the most beautiful surroundings to be found in the islands. 

Wet-land taro fields are terraced, and the water is allowed to run from one field to the next, in much the same way as the Hawaiians in the old days arranged their taro patches. Some dry-land taro is raised. 

Vegetables. Only about one fourth of the vegetables eaten in Hawaii are raised in the islands. The rest are brought in, largely from California. In 

1933, the value of the imported vegetables was over $1,400,000.

Though the cane fields and the pineapple fields take up much of the fertile lands of the islands, there is plenty of A. 

- A coffee tree in bloom. The flowers are much like orange blossoms. land left which could be used for raising vegetables. School children are encouraged to raise home and school gardens, and to become interested in raising vegetables. The University of Hawaii Extension Service and the Hawaii Exper iment Station offer help and advice to people engaged in this work, to other small farmers, and to people raising pigs and poultry. 

Rice. Thirty years ago, about ten times as much land was used for rice as is now used. Now we have about 1200

acres planted in rice. If more rice were produced here, it could easily be sold in the islands. 

Most of the imported rice comes from

California, where machinery is used in the large rice fields. The rest comes from Japan and China, where the cost of labor is less than it is here. 

Bananas. - Bananas grow easily and require little work in cultivation. Many new kinds of bananas have been introduced. About half of the bananas raised here are sold in our own markets. The rest are shipped away. About 6,000,000 pounds, valued at over $90,000, were shipped out in a recent year.

OTHER FOODS PRODUCED IN HAWAII 

Milk and Dairy Products. Most of the people living in the islands have not been used to drinking milk. Children are being educated in the schools to drink more milk, so that the use of this excellent food is increasing.

There are about 150 dairies on the different islands. Over half of these are near Honolulu, where the cattle are not put out to pasture as they are in regions with more land. Instead, fields are planted to hay and grain which are cut and fed to the cattle. Some dairymen go into the hills and gather grass in trucks. Much of the cattle food is brought in from the United States. 

Very little butter and cheese are made, because the milk is too expensive. Butter, cheese, and canned and dried milk are brought in from the United States and from New Zealand. 

Meat and Eggs. - Over 

Over 1,000,000 acres of land are given to ranches where cattle are raised. The land used is too rocky or too dry for cultivation. Over half the area used for cattle ranches is on the island of Hawaii. Hogs are raised on small farms. The meat is sent

OTHER FOODS PRODUCED IN HAWAII 

73 X347 X118 X3GS X140 

A. Sampans in port. to the markets to be sold, but the greater part of the meat we use is imported. It comes from the United States and from 

New Zealand. 

The raising of poultry, including chickens, ducks, turkeys, and pigeons, is increasing, especially in districts near 

Honolulu. Over half the poultry and eggs used in the islands are imported. 

Eggs and fowls are brought in from 

Australia, New Zealand, China, Japan, and the United States.

It takes many acres of land to raise cattle, but hogs and poultry can be raised by young people on very little land. If more of these were raised in 

Hawaii, they would find a ready market. 

Fish. Fish is a favorite food of most of the people living in Hawaii. The 

greater part of the fish in the markets is brought in by fishermen who go out deepsea fishing in sampans (A). The boats are supplied with ice for preserving the fish, with oil for fuel, and with food for the men, for a fishing trip may last several days. Some fish are caught offshore with nets.

Tuna is canned and sent to the mainland, but not enough tuna is caught to keep the cannery busy all the time.

VISITORS TO THE ISLANDS 

Hawaii has such a pleasant climate, and is so beautiful a place, that many visitors come here. They come from many colder countries to enjoy the warm weather, the swimming, and the outdoor life of Hawaii. These visitors are called tourists." So many of them come at times that large hotels, such as the 

Moana and the Royal Hawaiian (A), have been built for them. The tourist trade is one of the important sources of income in the islands, for these travelers

THE CITY OF HONOLULU 

Our islands have one important city,

Honolulu (A). Its population, compared with the population of large cities elsewhere, is small. It is the capital of the 

Territory, and the center of business and government. 

Honolulu is the chief shipping center for the islands. Mail, freight, and passengers from Asia, Australia, New Zealand, the United States, Canada, and 

Europe enter and leave Honolulu. In 

Honolulu are the chief banks, the largest stores, bakeries, canneries, and hotels.

A. 

Fleet Air Base, Pearl Harbor, T. H. 

A part of the Civic Center of Honolulu.

In this group are Iolani Palace and grounds, the Federal Building, the 

Judiciary Building, and the Territorial Office Building. 

The University of Hawaii, the Governor's headquarters in Iolani Palace, the Bishop 

Museum with its great collection of 

Polynesian things, and the Academy of Arts with its beautiful collection of art objects from China, Japan, Korea, the Philippines, and Europe are in Honolulu. The largest newspapers are published there. The Hawaiian Sugar Planters' Experiment Station, the Hawaii 

Experiment Station, and the Pineapple 

Producers' Experiment Station are also in this city. It is a great center of business activity. 

Honolulu does for the islands what cities elsewhere do for people. But it does fewer things, for our islands do not have the great manufacturing industries, such as textile and iron and steel, that are found on the mainland. Cities have grown up all over the world largely because of the growth of manufacturing. 

Hawaii does little manufacturing, and it will very probably never do much. 

Manufacturing uses coal, iron, and raw materials such as cotton, wool, and lumber, all of which would have to be imported at considerable expense.

77 

A. - A plantation village. The large buildings are the mill. The smaller buildings, set among trees, are the homes of the workers.

Hawaii is an agricultural country. Its life comes from the land and not from manufacturing. Most of our people live in villages (A) and small towns. The greater part of the business of our one city centers around shipping.

SUMMARY 

Hawaii is an agricultural country. Its people work on the farms and plantations. They work in the shipping and in the marketing of the things raised.

Hawaii depends chiefly on the United 

States for its market. 

Hawaii brings in from outside regions, chiefly from the United States, nearly everything used every day by all the people, including a large amount of the and much of its clothing. It imports food eaten. It imports all of its cloth nearly all its building materials and furniture, its automobiles, gasoline, oil, and tires. It brings in all household equipment, such as tools, plumbing fixtures, porcelain, and metal ware. produces some food. It pays for nearly everything through the production of It

A. A part of the beautiful shore line of Hawaii. sugar, pineapples, and coffee. Hawaii | speaking from the capitol at Washington, could produce more food and sell it here. 

In the years since Captain Cook discovered the islands, the life of the people has entirely changed. 

Changes are still going on. In the last few years, air service between the islands has been established. The air trip between the islands is measured in minutes. The army and navy have fleets of airplanes. From the air, pictures are taken which show the country as no other pictures can show it. A few years ago, pictures like many you will notice in this book could not have been obtained. Airplanes today mean fast travel, swift mail service, and protection.

Radio has changed life in the islands in the past few years. People can now hear the President of the United States grand opera from New York, and famous symphony orchestras from all parts of the United States. There is telephone service between the islands, with the mainland, and with Europe.

When Hawaii became a Territory of the United States, it formed the most western portion of the country. Arrangements were made to protect this western outpost. So we find army posts here, such as Schofield Barracks, Fort Ruger, Fort Shafter, Fort Kamehameha, and 

Fort De Russy. There is a naval base at Pearl Harbor, with a dry dock, shops, and stores of things needed by ships. 

The influences which make our island home what it is today may be summed up in these words: (1) farming, the chief source of income; (2) dependence on

SUMMARY 

79 regions outside the islands for materials, for a market, and for protection; (3) a friendly and hospitable people; and (4) beauty. Perhaps no country in the world is more beautiful than our islands. 

You will see interesting sights when you travel, but many people think you cannot find a more beautiful place.

NATURAL REGIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

Do you remember the lowlands and highlands of North America? Turn to page 11 and read again what is said there about them. Notice that the United

States stretches east and west across the continent. See the maps on pages 6 and 

10. Notice also that the great highland area in the western part of the continent has a northwest-southeast direction. The smaller eastern highland has a northeastsouthwest direction, as does the lowland between it and the Atlantic Ocean.

The central lowland of the continent, with a varying width, stretches north and south between the Gulf of Mexico and the Arctic Ocean.

There are parts of all these great physical regions of the continent in the United States. The map on pages 82-83 shows them on a larger scale. This is a physical map, like that on page 10, which uses colors to show the height of the lands above sea level. The black and white map on page 80 shows clearly the natural regions into which the country is divided. 

Read the names of the natural regions on that map. 

Atlantic Coastal Plain. - Find the lowland bordering the Atlantic Ocean known as the Atlantic Coastal Plain. It stretches along the coast from New York to the southern end of Florida and includes at the north Staten Island, Long

Island, and Cape Cod. At the south it joins the lowland region north of the Gulf of Mexico called the Gulf Coastal Plain. 

The Atlantic Coastal Plain was at one time a part of the sea bottom. The rocks under its surface are soft and in them there are shells like those found on the seashore. No part of the plain is very much above sea level. The land slopes gently toward the ocean. The water in the rivers that cross it moves so slowly toward the ocean that these rivers are called sluggish. Railroads built on the plain run for long distances in straight lines or with very gentle curves. 

Piedmont Plateau. West of the 

Atlantic Coastal Plain the land is higher and the rocks are older and harder. This region is called the Piedmont Plateau.

The boundary between the coastal plain and the Piedmont Plateau is the Fall 

Line. Where rivers cross the Fall Line there are usually rapids or waterfalls which furnish power, and at many such points cities have grown up. 

Find the Piedmont Plateau on the map, page 80. The word piedmont means "foot of the mountain." The plateau is given this name because it is the foothill region of the Appalachian 

Mountains. Continuous with the Piedmont Plateau there is a lower region to the north and east of New York City.

In many places it is rough and rocky. 

The hills were once much higher but they have been worn away. 

Appalachian Mountains. West of the 

Piedmont Plateau are the Appalachian

Mountains and Valleys. They extend from central Alabama (map, page 87) northeast until they cross the northern boundary of New Hampshire and Vermont.

The rock layers out of which these mountains are formed were once horizontal. 

Long ages ago they were crumpled into ridges and valleys. Out of these the present mountain ranges and valleys were carved by the work of rain and rivers, sunshine and frost, heat and cold. 81

NATURAL REGIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

85 The Appalachian Mountains may once | and winter temperatures of the bordering have been much higher. Now they vary lands more moderate. In what regions in height from less than 2000 feet to a would you expect these differences in little over a mile. Only a few of the temperature to increase the length of the peaks are rocky. Their steeper slopes are growing season? (Map 84 A.) covered with forests. There are heavy winter snows in the northern part and in the higher regions, but these all melt in the summer, so there are no glaciers. 

Different parts of this mountain region have been given special names in several of the states. One very wide and beautiful valley is the Great Appalachian Valley. It extends from Alabama through a number of states to the northeast. 

Appalachian Plateau. -West of the 

Appalachian Mountains is a highland region called the Appalachian Plateau.

It is nearly as high as the mountains, but the rock layers are horizontal instead of being folded. At one time its surface was nearly level, but many streams have cut through it, so it is now a region of hills with deep valleys between them. 

By climbing to the top of one of the hills, and imagining all of the valleys filled again, you would get a good idea of its former surface. In general the northern part of the Appalachian Plateau is known as the Allegheny Plateau, and the southern part is called the Cumberland Plateau. 

Lake Plains. On the northwest the highlands decrease in elevation and slope down to the plains of the Great Lakes.

Notice the extent of the Lake Plains as shown on the map, page 80. 

Through four of the five Great Lakes, trace the boundary line between the 

United States and Canada. Which one of the five lakes is entirely in the United 

States? You have already learned how large bodies of water make the summer 

Central Regions. West from the

Great Lakes region and the southern part of the Appalachian Plateau are wide lowlands on both sides of the Mississippi

River. They are gently rolling and nowhere more than a few hundred feet above sea level. On the south they join the plains that stretch from Florida to 

Texas north of the Gulf of Mexico. 

What are these two lowland regions called? (Map, page 80.) 

Within the great lowland area of central United States there is a small highland region called the Interior Highlands.

It is made up of a mountain region and a plateau region, much like the Appalachian 

Highlands. Find it on the map. 

Western Highlands. Several of the natural regions of our country are included wholly or mostly in the Western Highlands. 

Just west of the Central Plains is the natural region known as the Great Plains. 

Find it on the maps, pages 80 and 82.

Its elevation increases gradually from east to west. The western part of the

Great Plains is a region of scanty rainfall whose grasslands furnish food for grazing cattle and sheep, and where few crops can be raised without irrigation. 

The Rocky Mountains are high and rugged, and some of the higher slopes and peaks are snow-covered throughout the year. Some other slopes and peaks, too, are so high that trees will not grow on them. The belt along the slopes where the trees thin out until there are no more is known as the timber line.

A.Canyon in the Rocky Mountains, Colorado. 

Rain falls on the Rocky Mountains in summer, and snow in winter, especially on the western slopes. Much of the winter snow melts in the summer time, and many great rivers flow out of these mountains (A). Most of them flow toward the east across the Great Plains. 

Find some of them on the map, page 82.

On the western side of the great highland about which you have been reading are other great mountains. To the north the mountains are called the Cascade Range. West of the Great Basin the mountains are called the Sierra Nevada.

Several of the high peaks in these two mountain ranges have small glaciers. 

How were they formed? (Pages 12-13.) 

West of the Rocky Mountains are vast plateaus that vary in height from 2000 to 10,000 feet above sea level. Find the two regions called the Columbia Plateau and the Colorado Plateau. You will 

Close to the Pacific Ocean are some rugged Coast Ranges. They are not so high as the Cascades or Sierra Nevada, from which they are separated by wide valleys.

West of the Colorado Plateau is a high range called the Wasatch Mountains. Find it on the map, page 82. 

Find it on the map, page 82.

Beyond the western base of the Wasatch range is the Great Basin. It is called the Great Basin because it is bordered on the east and on the west by high mountains, and because its rivers do not reach the sea.

Some of the streams flow into lakes that have no outlets. Evaporation from them takes away as much water as the streams bring in. The streams are small, because there is not much rain in the Great Basin. See map 84 B. to find the names of the states in each group. they lie. Many facts and relations that you study in geography are based upon the census.

Map Study. -1. The Western States of our country are eleven in number. Eight of them are called the Plateau States because they lie wholly or in large part within the greatest highland region of the United States. 

Make a list of the Plateau States. Find on the map the capital of each state and write its name alongside its state. 

2. Turn to the map on page 80. The eastern portions of four of the Plateau States are in the Great Plains; name them. Which states share in the Rocky Mountains?

Which two states are largely in the Great 

Basin? Which states share in the Colorado 

Plateau? Which states share in the Columbia Plateau? 

3. Notice that nearly all the states in this group have straight-line boundaries. 

Where do you find some natural features, such as rivers or mountain ranges, forming parts of these state boundaries? Can you think of any reason why so many more straight-line boundaries for states are found · in the western than in the eastern half of our country? 

4. All the rivers of eastern New Mexico, Colorado, Wyoming, and Montana drain to the Gulf of Mexico and, finally, the Atlantic Ocean. Rivers that rise in the western parts of these four states flow toward the Pacific 

Ocean. If you will place your finger near the southwestern corner of New Mexico and move up the map, being careful not to cross the course of any stream, your finger will trace a very crooked line which marks the continental divide. All the water to one side of it flows to the Atlantic Ocean; all on the other side flows to the Pacific Ocean. 

Name the large rivers in the Plateau States. 

5. Several of the best-known of our national parks are in the Plateau States. Find one in Arizona, one mostly in Wyoming, and one in Montana. 

6. In Utah is a large lake that has no outlet to the ocean. 

Find this lake and its name. 

What do you know about this lake?

The Western States lie in a vast region of mountains, plateaus, and valleys.

Most of this part of our country is usually divided between two state groups (map, page 88). The larger group is called the 

Plateau States (page 90), and the other, including three states, is called the Pacific 

States (page 92). Look at these two groups carefully on the maps, so that you know where they are.

Taken as a whole, the Western States are divided into a number of natural regions (map, page 80). One of these, the 

Great Plains, is shared with other states to the east, which you will study later. 

West of the Great Plains, towering above them like a great wall, are the Rocky 

Mountains and the Mexican Plateau.

Beyond the Rockies are the Great Basin, the Columbia and Colorado plateaus, and the Arizona Highlands and Sonora Desert. 

Next beyond the plateau and basin regions is the lofty region made up of the Cascade Range and the Sierra Nevada.

Between these and the Coast Ranges that front the Pacific Ocean are the 

Willamette-Puget Sound Valley and the 

Valley of California. All of these natural regions are shown on the map, page 80. 

CLIMATE AND SCENERY 

The climate of the Western Highlands shows a wide variation between its northern and its southern parts. In Montana the temperature averages less than 70° in 

July and 20° in January. In southwestern Arizona it averages 90° in July and 50° in January. During severe winters it may be impossible to pasture flocks and herds on the plains of Montana and Wyoming. In the pasture lands of New

CLIMATE AND SCENERY

Asahel Curtis

A. 

Sagebrush in Washington. This desert is a waste land only because water is lacking. The soil is rich in plant food and the climate is suitable for farming. Where the land is irrigated with water brought from the mountains, the fields are green. can live out of doors at all seasons. 

Southwestern Arizona is low and is one of the warmest parts of the United States. 

In Montana, Utah, and Colorado there is snow all the year on some of the higher mountains (89A). Most of the plateau surface is so high that the air is always cool at night. It is comfortable in the shade, even at midday. 

How the East and the West Differ in

Climate and Scenery. For differences in rainfall between the East and the West use the rainfall map on page 84. In our 

In our western region, where there is enough moisture, there are forests on the western slopes of the mountains except on the highest slopes and on the summits.

The eastern and western parts of the United States are very different in several other ways. In the East the fields are generally green and most of the mountains are low enough so that they are covered with forest. In the drier parts of the 

West (A) there are scattered grasses and shrubs and the landscape is gray, except where there are trees bordering the streams and where irrigation has covered the fields with green. In other parts the land is high and rugged. 

In the East the rain storms are seldom very severe. In the drier parts of the 

West there may be no rain for months, but there are sometimes violent storms called cloudbursts. Then torrents of water rush along the beds of streams while the storm lasts, only to leave them dry again until another storm comes.

In the East the air is often humid, or full of moisture. That is why so many summer days are said to be sultry. In the West there is usually much less moisture in the air. When this is true the air

Asahel Curtis

A.Mt. St. Helens, one of the highest peaks in the Western Highlands. The lava streams which flowed from this volcano during its last eruption cooled before the trees on the slopes were entirely destroyed. The cone is covered with coarse pumice, light enough to float in water. is clear and bracing. In the Plateau

States the general elevation of the land is about a mile above sea level. The air is so thin, or rare, that people breathe more rapidly and deeply. Because of this rareness of the air and its dryness, the region has become a noted health resort. Those who come from the dense air of the lowlands are sometimes made very uncomfortable by the rare air of these higher lands. They are unable to run or to work as they have usually done, until they get used to it. The clearness of the air makes the sun and the stars seem to rise and set very suddenly. At night the stars are more brilliant, and more stars can be seen, than in the East.

There is a difference also in the natural scenery of the West and the East. The western mountains are higher and more majestic (A). Mt. Mitchell, the highest of the Eastern mountain peaks, is only 

6711 feet high. There are many peaks in the Western Highlands more than 14,000 feet high, while Mt. Whitney in California, the highest peak in the United States, is 

14,500 feet high.

In some places in the West the waterfalls and rapids of mountain streams have been used to generate electricity (95B). 

The electricity furnishes power and light in mines and villages and for the homes of farmers. But only a small fraction of the water power of these streams has yet been put to work.

A long time ago many of the mountain peaks were active volcanoes, from which at times came melted rock or lava and

CLIMATE AND SCENERY 

95 fine dust, steam, and gases that looked like smoke. Lassen Peak in northern

California is the only volcano in the United States that is now active (A). Other old volcanic peaks such as Mt. Shasta in 

California, Mt. Hood in Oregon, and

Mt. Rainier in Washington (map, page 

92) now have snow fields that last throughout the year. From the snow fields of Mt. Shasta small glaciers form and move slowly down its sides until they melt. 

On the desert plains the winds blow with great force, for there are few barriers to hinder their movement. There is little cover of grass and there are few roots to bind the earth and sand. Enormous quantities of dust are sometimes blown into the air. Such storms may be somewhat like the blinding snows of a winter blizzard. In some places the winds pile the sand into hills, forming sand dunes.
