THE ROCKY MOUNTAINS 

The early explorers and pioneers who made their way westward across the 

Great Plains found themselves facing snow-clad mountains. From a distance they saw no way of crossing them. It almost seemed as if they had come in their covered wagons to the "end of the trail." Later they were to find the actual end of the trail on the shores of the Pacific Ocean nearly a thousand miles farther west. 

The most promising routes across the mountain wall before them seemed to be the valleys of the rivers. But these often led into steep-walled canyons where roaring waters filled their beds from wall to wall. Later some of the pioneers climbed up the slope to a notch or pass. 

To reach even the lowest of the passes was a long, hard climb, with a steep descent on the western side. 

Today some railroads make use of canyons that the rivers have cut through the mountains from the plateaus farther west. But this was not possible until railroad men had made places for the tracks and for necessary bridges. The The 

Royal Gorge route through the canyon of the Arkansas River is a wonderful engineering work (A). 

Farms and Pastures in the Rockies.

Hidden in the mountains are many valleys. Some of them receive enough moisture during the year and are warm enough in summer for the growing of crops. Others get little or no moisture because they are shut in by mountains, but there are mountain streams whose water can be used for irrigation. Still others are too cold to permit the growing of any crop except grass.

A. Royal Gorge, on the Arkansas River, in Colorado.

Among the forests that cover the lower mountain slopes there are pasture lands where cattle or sheep can graze during the summer. On higher slopes, even above the timber line, there are pasture lands for sheep that can be used from the time the snow melts in the spring until the fall. the fall. A sheep herder and a camp tender, with a number of sheep dogs, are usually in charge of two or three thousand sheep. Each year the herder and his camp tender live alone for months at a time with a covered wagon for a home.

Much of the pasture land in the mountains forms a part of some national forest reserve (109 A). Before sheep or cattle can be grazed on these lands the owner must get permission from the officer in charge, and must pay a small sum for each animal. 

Mines and Mining. - The mineral resources of the Rocky Mountains are

THE ROCKY MOUNTAINS 

97 A. 

State of Calif. Div. of Mines - Hydraulic mining of gold. rich and varied. They include iron, copper, lead, zinc, silver, and gold. All of these metals are usually found in ores, mixed or combined with other minerals. 

In addition to the metals named above, coal and petroleum are also found. The chief regions where each of these minerals is produced are shown on the map, page 141. You will notice that the same minerals are found in many other parts of the Western Highlands. 

Precious Metals. - Gold and silver are called precious metals because small amounts of them have a high value.

They are used in making coins, ornaments, and many artistic as well as useful things. These metals are most commonly found in mountain regions. The reason for this is that the rocks in such regions have been folded, cracked, or cracked, or crushed, and sometimes heated. In some 

In some places the metals have been dissolved by water in the rocks and deposited in pockets or cracks with other substances. 

When these pockets and cracks are filled in this way they are called mineral veins.

During the long, long time that has passed since the mountains were formed their tops have been worn away by glaciers, frosts, and streams. Rocks containing gold have been worn away with the other rocks. Many particles of gold were mixed with the sand and gravel carried down the mountains and deposited by the streams. This kind of gold-bearing earth is called a placer. 

Mining Methods. In getting the gold from placers, the miners put the sand and gravel in a pan with water and shake the pan. The gold particles are heavier than the sand and sink to the bottom. When the other material is washed away the gold can be gathered from the bottom of the pan. Large pieces, called nuggets, are sometimes found.

In order to get the gold more rapidly, a powerful stream of water is turned upon a bank of gold-bearing gravel, through a large hose (A). The gravel may be washed through a trough or sluice having crossbars on the bottom. The gold, being heavy, sinks behind the crossbars and is gathered. This process is called hydraulic mining. 

When metal-bearing rock is found in veins, it is dug out. The mines may be at a depth of hundreds or thousands of feet beneath the surface. Shafts are sunk and tunnels, called drifts, are dug from the shaft to reach the vein. Sometimes the drifts are dug into a mountain side if the vein can be reached in this way. 

The vein may be several inches or several feet thick, and this is followed and blasted out. In many of the great mines there is a network of shafts and tunnels, with every help for drilling, blasting, and hoisting the ore. Pumping is often necessary to keep the mines from being flooded with ground water. The roofs of large openings must be supported by timbers or concrete posts. The drills may be driven and the mines lighted by electricity.

Some ores are put through a blast furnace or smelter, as in the case of iron, to separate the metal from the other materials in the ore. You will read about iron smelting later in your study. There are smelters at Pueblo and Denver in 

Colorado; at Butte, Great Falls, and 

Anaconda in Montana; near Salt Lake 

City in Utah, and at many other places.

Some ore is shipped as far east as Omaha. 

Many of the richest mineral regions are on lands owned by the government. 

A man who searches for ores is called a prospector. When he finds good ore he files a claim on the land and is then given the right to work his claim. 

Rich finds of the precious metals often cause great excitement. Men rush in, it may be by thousands, hoping to gain sudden wealth. Tents are set up, rude cabins are built, mining machinery is brought in, and stores are quickly fitted out with all kinds of supplies. If the region remains productive for a number of years, better buildings are put up and streets are laid out and improved. If the supply of ore is very large, or if the place becomes a center of trade for surrounding regions, a permanent city is established. If the ore is all removed and the place has no other advantages, it may be abandoned after a few years. 

The Great Metal-Mining States. — All the states of the Plateau group produce gold and silver. Utah and Montana lead in silver mining, and rank next after

Arizona in the mining of copper. Butte, in western Montana, is the center of a mining region that has produced much copper for many years. 

Great quantities of silver and lead are produced in the Coeur d'Alene district in northern Idaho. Much of the trade of this region goes to the important city of Spokane in Washington.

There are many mining regions in various parts of the Rocky Mountains of Colorado. From these much gold, silver, zinc, and lead, and some tungsten, radium, and other metals are obtained. 

Lumbering. -There are very large evergreen forests in the Rockies, especially in Idaho and Montana. They are chiefly on the western slopes of the mountains and in the north where the rainfall is heaviest. Lumbering is carried on (99 A), but this is not one of the great lumbering regions of our country. One reason is the difficulty of getting the lumber to market. Logs cut on the mountain slopes must be lowered by cables or allowed to slide down the mountains in wooden troughs or chutes. The sawmills are usually in the valleys.

Around Grand Junction you will find great orchards and large gardens where the land has been irrigated.

THE GREAT BASIN 

Read again what is said on page 88 about the Great Basin. Why is this part of the plateau country called the Great Basin? Study this region on the maps, pages 80 and 90. Notice that it has, except at the south, a surface several thousand feet above sea level, and that it is bordered, east and west, by very high mountains. 

Great Salt Lake. Streams from the

Wasatch Mountains dissolve salt and other materials from the rocks and flow into lakes and mud flats whose waters do not reach the sea. The largest lake of this region is Great Salt Lake. If there were enough rain, the level of this lake would rise until it overflowed at some point and its waters would then reach the ocean. But now the water evaporates from the surface of the lake, and is carried away in the air in the form of invisible vapor, about as fast as water flows into it. The salts are left behind, and the lake water has become a brine so dense that bathers float in it without any effort. 

Where the Mormons Settled. - There are many short ranges of steep mountains running north and south through the 

Great Basin. Waste from these mountains has spread over the land below them, and sagebrush and other desert plants are almost the only natural vegetation. There are not many people in the Great Basin, except near the Wasatch

Mountains. They are there because the rainfall on the western slopes of the Wasatch is heavy enough to furnish water for irrigation of the farm lands near by. 

In 1847 a company of Mormons came from Illinois, across the Great Plains, through the Rocky Mountains, over the 

Colorado Plateau and through the passes of the Wasatch range. Between the

Wasatch Mountains and Great Salt Lake they found flat land, with a soil so good that it needed only water to make it fertile. They built ditches to irrigate the fields and made their new home into a great garden. 

Their leader told and plant alfalfa." As you know, much alfalfa is grown on irrigated lands. It makes a fine hay or forage crop and gathers nitrogen from the air. The nitrogen makes the soil richer. The roots of this plant go deep into the earth. 

In irrigated regions it grows rapidly and can be cut several times a year. The alfalfa pastures are rich, and grazing is carried on in parts of the Great Basin. them to "plow deep

After 1848 the main route to California crossed this Mormon territory, and the population there steadily increased. 

When, later, a railroad was built through this region it brought more people. The territory of Utah was admitted as a state in 1896. It was found that this is also a region of great mineral wealth, for there are vast deposits of copper, lead, silver, gold, and zinc. The ores of these metals are usually found combined, and it is only in recent times that it has become possible to separate them profitably.

THE COLUMBIA PLATEAU 

The plateau region in southeastern 

Washington, eastern Oregon, and in much of Idaho is called the Columbia Plateau.

It is named after the Columbia River, the chief stream in the northwestern part of our country. The Columbia and its principal tributary, the Snake River, flow across the plateau in deep canyons. 

Those of the Snake River vary in depth from 400 to 6000 feet, and those of the 

Columbia River from 100 to 1000 feet.

Turn to the maps on pages 80, 90, and 

92 and see just where this plateau is before you read about it and about the industries carried on within its borders. 

This 

How the Plateau Was Formed. great plateau was formed largely from flows of lava over a very long period of time. There was so much of this lava that it filled in valleys and flowed over

the tops of some of the mountains that were there before these flows began. In the Snake River region the lava was very liquid and flowed so easily that it covered large areas before it cooled. It came from many volcanoes that were close together over most of the area. In 

Washington and Oregon the lava and other volcanic material came to the surface through long cracks or fissures in the earth. 

Climate. Because the plateau is shut in so completely by mountains its average temperature is higher than it would otherwise be. The rainfall is largely shut out by the Cascade Range on the west of the plateau region because the chief winds are the westerlies. They leave their moisture as either rain or snow on the western slopes of the mountains. The rainfall of the Columbia Plateau varies from 8 inches a year in eastern Oregon and on the plains along the lower Snake, to 25 inches in higher areas. There is enough rainfall in southeastern Washington to permit dry farming.

Throughout most of this region the winters are very cold, and there is heavy snowfall in some of the surrounding mountains. This snow melts slowly in the spring and helps to keep up the flow of the rivers.

Soil and Crops. - The topmost layers of lava, especially in Washington and

Oregon, have decayed to form a rich and fertile soil. In some places it has a thickness of over 60 feet. Other soils are the fine rock waste that was deposited over the bottoms of old lakes once found in this region. There are also soils made up of fine wind-blown rock materials that have been left by the winds that have swept across this region. 

The natural vegetation is sagebrush and bunch grass, but wheat is grown by dry farming in eastern Washington and 

Oregon. This is one of the great wheatgrowing regions of our country. Can you name any others? As in other dryfarming regions, there is of course some rainfall. It comes usually in winter or spring and is enough to grow the crop if the seed is planted at the right time. 

When the wheat is ready for the harvest the days are usually bright and sunny, and the grain ripens before it is cut. 

Combined harvesters and threshing machines are used (103 A). The harvester knives (on a cut bar) move back and forth very quickly and cut off the heads of wheat from the standing stalks.

These are carried to the thresher and the grain is threshed at once and bagged or run off into an open wagon that moves along beside the thresher. There is so little rainfall at this time of the year that the bags of wheat may be piled in the fields and left until the farmer is ready to ship or store them.

In the plateau section of central Washington and Oregon there are several valleys where irrigation has made it possible to raise all the fruits and vegetables of temperate latitudes. One of the best of these fruit-growing regions is the Yakima Valley in Washington, famous for its wonderful orchards and hop yards. Other equally famous orchards are in a valley in northern Oregon near Mt. Hood.

In Idaho dry farming is carried on along the borders of the Snake River plains. There are irrigated regions in many places where the water from smaller streams can be used. Cattle and sheep are raised and mountain pastures are used in the summer.

THE COLUMBIA PLATEAU 

103 A. 

Ewing Galloway 

Combined harvester and thresher in eastern Washington. The threshed wheat is run into bags; the straw and chaff are left on the ground. The machine shown here is a special kind for use on hilly land. 

Cities. - Spokane in eastern Wash ington is the only large city of the Columbia Plateau. Several railroads that cross the continent gather here as if in a knot. 

Railroads run south on the Columbia 

Plateau, north into Canada, and east and west to connect the city with other parts of our country. Spokane is a great trading center, collecting the products of the surrounding region to be manufactured or shipped out, and distributing such articles as the people of the region need. Its manufactures include meat packing, flour, and lumber, and products made from lumber.

Walla Walla and Yakima in Washing ton, and Boise, the capital of Idaho, are also important trading centers of the plateau.

THE COLORADO PLATEAU 

The Colorado Plateau is one of the wonderlands of our country. Before beginning its study turn to the map on page 80 and be sure that you know just where it is. High, rocky, and for the most part arid, it is a region of painted splendor. 

Some parts of it are so desolate that you can think of them only as deserts. Many of the rocks are gorgeously colored. You almost believe that some mad painter, with a giant paint brush, has splashed them with all the colors of the rainbow. 

Industries. — Within the Colorado

Plateau there are irrigated regions and places where dry farming is carried on. 

There are sections where the sagebrush and bunch grass furnish natural pastures for sheep and cattle. There is a small forest area and there are oil wells and deposits of coal, copper, lead, and zinc.

The map 125 A shows the irrigated lands in this region. Some of the irrigation projects include the making of large reservoirs or lakes that add to the beauty as well as to the usefulness of a region. 

Some are the result of great feats of engineering; for example, the digging of a tunnel through a mountain from the canyon of the Gunnison River in western Colorado to water the flat lands bordering its tributary the Uncompahgre. In the one valley there was water but little land that could be irrigated. In the other there were broad fields but very little water. The irrigated lands along the Rio Grande border that stream from 

Colorado into Texas.

Population. -The population of the 

Colorado Plateau region is not large. 

There are settlements where mining is carried on, there are cattle and sheep ranches, and some irrigated districts. 

A few hotels provide food and shelter for visitors who come to see the wonders of the plateau, but as a whole this region has only a sparse population. 

Some Indians are still to be found in this region. They usually farm small patches of land, look after their sheep or cattle, and work at such native industries as rug and basket weaving and making pottery. Many years ago the Indians who lived in this region were frequently at war with other tribes. To protect themselves from their enemies they built their homes on shelves of rock high up on the steep cliffs that border the canyons. 

The ruins of their villages may still be seen. Other villages were built on the flat tops of great masses of rocks to which the early Spanish explorers gave the name of mesa, meaning "table." Their crops were grown on the surrounding plains. 

The methods that they followed were in many ways much like those used in irrigated regions or in dry farming today. 

The Grand Canyon. - The Colorado 

River rises in the lofty Rocky Mountains of northern Colorado and flows southwest across Utah. On its way it passes through deep gorges that it has cut into the rocks of the plateau. In Utah it is joined by the Green River, a large tributary which rises in Wyoming. When the river enters Arizona, it bends to the westward and flows in a canyon which in some parts is more than a mile deep. This is the Grand Canyon, the most wonderful gorge in the world (105 A). Its width varies from 5 to 15 miles and it has a length of nearly 280 miles, of which 105 miles are within the boundaries of the Grand Canyon National Park. At the bottom of the canyon the river flows

THE COLORADO PLATEAU 

105 A. A small part of the Grand Canyon of the Colorado River, in Arizona. You are looking into the inner canyon, with its steep walls. The broad, swiftly flowing river reaches from wall to wall. Most of the rocks are bare, and many of them are brightly colored. as a swift, muddy torrent, with many dangerous rapids. The walls rise in places almost straight up from the water for half a mile or more before the canyon begins to widen out.

In ages past, probably a million or more years ago, the region you are now studying was below the level of the sea. The water that covered it may have been a huge lake or it may have been a bay or an arm of the ocean. From ancient mountains the rivers of that long ago carried rock waste into this lake or sea as the Mississippi is now carrying it to the Gulf. The mud and sand were spread over the bottom of this body of water. 

Through the years and the ages it accumulated, growing thicker and thicker and slowly changing to rock. 

At last the long period of accumulation ended. Slowly the land began to rise.

Rivers were formed and found their way to the ocean. How many there were no one knows, but the Colorado was one, and with its tributaries it has done most of the work of cutting the famous canyons of this region. As the land rose the rivers cut through the rock layers deeper and deeper like a great saw eating its way through a log that is pushed against it.

The tools with which each river worked | light or shadow they make a wonderland of great beauty. were the sand and gravel from its tributaries and the rocks from its bed and the canyon walls. The Grand Canyon was formed by the slow wearing of waterborne sand and stones on rock, just as today the river is still cutting away at its bed and widening its walls and valley. 

It is still carrying seaward, as it has for untold ages in the past, rock waste to enlarge the great plain that it has formed at its mouth by filling in the sea. 

It is well to remember that what rivers and winds, rains and frosts, and snow and moving ice did in the ages past they are still doing today. It is also just as true that what we see them doing today they did in the past. Always they have worked slowly, as it seems to us, but through the long ages they accomplish mighty tasks. The waters of a melting ice sheet carve a valley and in this way provide a trade route. The falling waters of Niagara carve a great gorge and furnish water power for electric generators. The waste carried by a Mississippi fills a great bay with fertile soil on which millions of people earn their living. 

A river, coming from the mountains, flows across rising land, but holds its course and cuts a canyon that is one of the wonders of the world.

There are many side canyons that join the Grand Canyon of the Colorado River.

In the Grand Canyon the layers of rocks have been cut into wonderful shapes. 

There are rock masses that, seen at a distance or in the evening shadows, look like the towers and temples of a city of Asia, or a great European cathedral. The layers of rock are of many different colors. 

There are reds, grays, yellows, and browns in shades without number. Seen in sun- 

A railroad and an excellent highway reach the south rim of the canyon, where there are hotels to care for visitors.

Those who wish to go down to the level of the river can use a trail down the side of the canyon. The trip down and up this " Bright Angel Trail" is usually made on the backs of donkeys or horses, as the way is steep. Every year thousands of travelers from all parts of the earth visit the Grand Canyon.

The Lower Colorado Region. 

national government is building the 

Boulder Dam (or Hoover Dam) at the lower end of the Grand Canyon. It will provide power, furnish water for irrigation, and prevent floods in the plain below. The lower part of the Colorado 

River flows through a desert plain in which the heat is often very great. This plain is made of the earth and rock waste brought by the river from the canyon and from the highlands far to the north, and spread out in old deltas. 

Find Phoenix (map, page 90) in southwestern Arizona. It is the capital of the state and a trading center in the midst of a rich agricultural region and not far from many mines and smelters. To the northeast of Phoenix a highland area, known as the Arizona Highlands, crosses the state from northwest to southeast and extends into Mexico. In these highlands, on the Salt River, a tributary of the Gila River, the Roosevelt Dam was built. The water from this reservoir has been used to generate electricity for light and power and to irrigate thousands of acres of former desert (map, page 125).

Africa by the United States Department of Agriculture. Other tropical fruits, vegetables, and alfalfa and other forage crops are raised there also.

Arizona leads all the states in our country in the production of copper. The copper ores are found not only in the Arizona Highlands but also in the region to the southwest of the highlands and east in New Mexico. Prosperous villages have grown up around the mines and smelters.

NATIONAL PARKS AND NATIONAL FORESTS 

Before leaving the Plateau States to take up the study of the Pacific States, you should know something more about the national parks and forests within their boundaries.

The Yellowstone National Park. - In the high mountain region where the three states, Wyoming, Montana, and Idaho, come together, is the Yellowstone National Park. Most of it is in the state of Wyoming. The continental divide crosses the park, so that a part of it lies in the basin of the Missouri River and another part in the basin of the Snake River. 

The canyon and falls of the Yellowstone River, and hot springs and geysers to the number of a thousand or more, furnished the chief reasons for laying out a national park here. Hot springs are found also in some other parts of the United States. Their waters have been heated by hot rocks not far below the surface of the earth.

The Yellowstone Park is one of the few places in the world where there are geysers, or springs that spout hot water.

(6 

A. The geyser Old Faithful in eruption. 

Old Faithful" is so called because once in a little over an hour, with great regularity, a column of steaming water rises from it 120 or 150 feet into the air (A).

Other geysers spout every few hours, or days, or irregularly, but the cause is the same in all. At some distance below the surface of the earth the water in the geyser becomes so hot that some of it is changed to steam. When the pressure of the steam becomes too great, it forces its way out through the water above, pushing some of the water out with it. 

The column of steam and water rushes out of the opening and rises high into the air just as steam comes out of the spout of a teakettle. When the steam has escaped, water collects again and is heated until the spouting is repeated. 

Hot water dissolves many things out of the rocks. When it reaches the surface, the water becomes cooler and drops much of the dissolved material at the mouth of the spring. In this way terraces of wonderful colors and forms have been slowly built up about some of the hot springs in the park. 

Hunting in the park is forbidden, and the elk, bear, and other animals there lose their fear of man and become less wild. 

There are excellent roadways and hotels in the park. The clear air, and the geysers, springs, lakes, rivers, waterfalls, and canyons attract thousands of visitors every year. — - 

Glacier National Park. In 1910 the government set aside an area of about 

1500 square miles in northwestern Montana as a national park. It is a region of great natural beauty, with towering peaks, lovely valleys, and gem-like mountain lakes. As there are many glaciers within its borders, it has been named the 

Glacier National Park. 

— - 

National Forests. In the Plateau

States, and also in some other states, the government has established a number of forest reserves (109 A). They are mainly in mountain districts where there is enough moisture for trees to grow. It is expected that these forest lands will always be kept under government control. 

The preservation of the forests will prevent the washing away of the soil and the destruction of property in the valleys. 

Men trained in forestry, which is the science of caring for trees, have charge of these reserves. Under their direction are many forest rangers, who lead a lonely but a very busy life, constantly moving about on horseback or on foot. Among their many duties they look out for forest fires, put them out when started, and prevent the stealing of timber.

A. National forests and national parks. 

The reservation of these lands by the government does not mean that they cannot be used. As you have learned (page 96), cattle, horses, and sheep may be pastured here, under government regulation. Tourists and hunters are free to. use wood. Permits are given also to cut timber, but only the full-grown trees.

The Sierra Nevada. - California is the largest state in the Pacific group and has the highest mountains. These mountains, the Sierra Nevada, extend nearly north and south through eastern California. The name means snowy mountain range." Much of the Sierra has an altitude of 13,000 feet, or almost two and one half miles. Find Mt. Whitney on the map. What do 

What do you know about this and other mountains in the Pacific States? (pages 93-95.) The range has a gentle 66 big tree."

National Park Service slope to the west, but is steep on the east, where it overlooks the Great Basin in Nevada. The lower western slopes of these mountains are covered by forests or have been cleared to form fertile fields.

The upper slopes are above the timber line, and consist of bare rocks and rock waste. You will remember that the timber line is the belt above which trees cannot grow because of cold.

The streams that flowed down these long slopes cut into the rocks, sawed across veins of gold, and carried the dust and nuggets of the gold to the valley. 

It was the finding of this gold that started the gold rush of 1849.

In California there are several kinds of trees unknown in other parts of the world. 

They grow to heights of 200 or 300 feet.

Many have diameters of ten, twenty, or even thirty feet. Among the greatest are the "big trees" (A) that grow on the

slopes of the Sierra Nevada and Coast

Ranges. Both the nation and the state have set apart reserves containing the big trees in order to save them from the lumberman and from destruction by fire. 

Some of these trees are believed to be at least four thousand years old. In the 

Coast Ranges of northwestern California there are extensive forests of the very large trees called redwoods. Picture to yourself a tree so big that if it were hollow at the base you could hold a goodsized class in it!

The Yosemite Valley is a deep canyon in the Sierra Nevada. The Merced River and some of its branches enter the canyon by falls from 300 to 1600 feet high (A).

The Yosemite Valley is as well known, though not so large, as the Grand Canyon of the Colorado. It has been set apart as a national park. 

Cascade Range. The lofty mountain range of eastern California continues northward through central Oregon and Washington, but in those states it is called the Cascade Range. The Cascade 

Range is not so high as the Sierra Nevada, but is broader and is nearly all covered with great forests. Above it rise several volcanic cones, to the region of glaciers and perpetual snow. Seen in the sunlight they are like bright jewels rising above the setting of the dark-green forest. 

Among these peaks are Mt. Hood in 

Oregon, and Mt. Rainier and Mt. Baker in Washington. Mt. Rainier is 14,363 feet in altitude, and is one of the noblest mountain peaks in the United States.

In the heart of a low, broad, volcanic mountain in the Cascade Range is a pit six miles across and 4000 feet deep, half filled with water, called Crater Lake. 

The basin was formed by the sinking of

the central part of the mountain. This place, like the Yosemite Valley, has been reserved by the government as a national park. The banks of the lake are 1500 feet high. 

Coast Ranges. Like the Sierra 

Nevada and Cascade Range, the Coast

Ranges extend north and south through the three states. The northern Coast 

Range in Washington, between Puget

Sound and the ocean, is known as the 

Olympic Mountains. The Coast Ranges are rugged in many places, but are not nearly so high as the Sierra Nevada.

These mountains are almost on the very edge of the continent. Between them and the sea there is no such coastal plain as you find in the eastern part of our country (map, page 80). WP-7

San Francisco Bay. Oregon has one in the lower part of the Columbia River. 

Washington has one in the Strait of 

Juan de Fuca and Puget Sound.

The Valley of California. - - West of the Sierra Nevada and Cascade Range is a series of wide, open valleys, with a fertile soil and a large population. 

One of these is the great valley of central 

California, shut in by the Sierra Nevada on the east and the Coast Ranges on the west. It is four hundred miles long and fifty miles wide. The southern part is drained by the San Joaquin River, which flows northwest. The northern part is drained by the Sacramento River, which flows southward. Both rivers receive many tributaries, and they unite before entering San Francisco Bay. The bay connects with the ocean through the 

Golden Gate. The eastern tributaries of both rivers are fed by the rains and melting snows of the Sierra Nevada.

Many of them are small during the dry season. The western tributaries, coming from the eastern slope of the Coast Ranges, are much less important. This slope gets but little rain. For this reason it is scantily covered with forest. There is little or no flow of water in the streams from this region during the dry season. 

The Willamette-Puget Sound Valley.- 

West of the Cascade Range in Oregon is the fertile Willamette Valley, a region about the size of Massachusetts. Through this valley the Willamette River flows northward and joins the Columbia River.

On the Willamette, a short distance from its mouth, is the city of Portland. West of the Cascade Range in Washington is a broad, fertile valley whose northern part is deeply indented by Puget Sound.

Climate. The climate of the Pacific 

States shows sharp contrasts. The climate of western Washington is temperate, while that of Death Valley and some other parts of southern California is very hot. In each state the temperature of the great valleys is moderate, while the high mountains have frost, snow, and ice. 

The lowlands near the Pacific Ocean have a mild climate, being cooled in summer and warmed in winter by the prevailing westerly winds blowing from the sea.

There are also great contrasts in rainfall. Here are the wettest, and some of the driest, lands in the United States. 

Except in high mountains, the winter season is wet rather than snowy. The summers are dry so dry on some of the lowlands that bags of wheat and trays of fruit can be left out of doors for weeks without injury from moisture.

The average annual rainfall in western 

Washington is about 65 inches. In some parts of the mountains it is even more than this, and is the heaviest in the 

United States. Eastern Washington, on the other hand, is east of the mountains and has but about 20 inches of rainfall.

A. 

- Groves of orange trees in southern California. Notice how very large these groves are. The San Bernardino 

Mountains are shown in the background. 

Oregon shows a like difference between its western and its eastern parts. Northern California, and the high mountains toward the south, have plentiful rains, while the southern parts of the great valley are so dry as to need irrigation. 

Parts of southeastern California are among the driest of deserts. The range of annual rainfall in this state is from 3 inches in some southern parts to 80 inches on the lofty mountains of the northwest (map, page 84). 

Agriculture. -The leading industry of the Pacific States is agriculture. In the 

Puget Sound and Willamette valleys and in the northern part of the Valley of California, rainfall is sufficient for crops. Even where the rainfall is scanty, as is the case in much of eastern Washington, eastern Oregon, and the southern and south-central parts of California, either dry farming is possible or irrigation works may bring the water of the mountain streams to the fertile lands of the valleys.

In the early days of farming in these states, wheat was a leading crop, and it is still an important crop in eastern Washington and Oregon (page 102). In California the large wheat farms of the early years have disappeared. They have been divided into smaller farms which are more carefully cultivated and on which products in great variety are now raised. The 

Willamette Valley also is a most prosperous agricultural region. In many parts of the Valley of California the land has high value. There the farms are small, and fruits and garden crops are raised for the market. Some agriculture also is carried on in the valleys of the west slope of the Coast Ranges.

The recent agricultural development of California has been in the raising of fruits, vegetables, and nuts. It is now the greatest fruit state in our country. 

It is best known for fruits of warm climates, such as oranges (A) and lemons. 

However, three fourths of all its fruit crop is in fruits of temperate latitudes,

such as apples, peaches, apricots, and pears. California leads all other states in raising grapes, from which grape juice, wines, and raisins are made. In recent years California has become more important than Spain as a raisin-producing region. Many of the vineyards are in the valley of the San Joaquin River, in the southern part of the Sacramento River

Valley, and in the district north of 

San Francisco Bay. The region around 

Fresno is one of the great raisin districts.

The orange groves are chiefly in the southern half of California. Many of the mountain streams, when they reach the plains, are turned into irrigation canals and distributed over the fields. Here in a region once given over to herds of cattle and great wheat farms, there are now flourishing cities and villages, and a large and prosperous population. In addition to the products already named, sugar beets, sweet potatoes, almonds, walnuts, melons, rice, cotton, olives, and figs are raised in large quantities. 

In the dry plains and mountain pasture lands of California there are many stock ranches, and cattle and sheep raising are important industries. On many of the farms of the Valley of California dairy cattle are kept and the milk, butter, and cheese find a ready market. Poultry raising is increasing, especially on the farms in the great valley and in the neighborhood of the larger cities. 

Forests and Lumbering. - One of the largest and most important of the lumbering regions of the United States is in the three states of the Pacific coast. The great forests there are in part due to the heavy rainfall (map, page 84). Both 

Oregon and Washington have a very large amount of timber still standing, and Washington cuts more lumber than any other state of the Union. The manufacture of lumber products is carried on at 

Seattle, Tacoma, Everett, Bellingham, and other Puget Sound ports, and at Portland in Oregon. From these places some of the lumber is sent to such distant points as Alaska, Hawaii, Japan, China, Australia, and South America. There is a large demand for lumber also in the growing cities and the many mines of the 

Pacific States.

Pacific States. As the lumbermen do not depend upon snow on which to sled the logs, the work can be carried on at all seasons. To prevent careless lumbering and destructive fires, the national government has set apart vast tracts, as national forests, in the mountains of the three states (map, page 109).

Fisheries. Salmon live in salt water, but every year, at the spawning or breeding season, the full-grown salmon swim up the rivers into fresh waters. 

They may be seen hundreds of miles from the ocean as they swim up the head-

unimportant, and the fishing industry has taken its place. Canning is also done at Bellingham and elsewhere on Puget Sound. This industry is important also along the shores of western Canada and southern Alaska.

The state governments restrict fishing so that many fish can escape the nets and other fishing apparatus, and make their way up to the spawning grounds. If it were not for these government restrictions, a valuable industry would be destroyed in a few years. 

Fish hatcheries have been established to to breed young salmon.

Ewing Galloway waters and branches of the Columbia 

River, and of the Fraser River, in western

Canada. In the mountain streams the young salmon are hatched, and swim down to the ocean to make their growth. 

After about four years they are fully grown; then they move up the rivers to spawn, and vast numbers are caught on the way. 

When Lewis and Clark and other explorers came to the region, they found the Indians living mainly on these fish. For many years the white man has made a business of catching and canning them (A). Cans of salmon from the Columbia River or from Puget

Sound are seen in almost every provision store. If properly put up in air-tight cans, the fish can be kept a long time. 

Fresh salmon also are shipped long distances in refrigerator cars.

One of the canning centers is Astoria, a small city in Oregon at the mouth of the Columbia River. This city was named for John Jacob Astor, who made the place a station for the fur trade of the Northwest. The fur trade has become 

Tuna, halibut, crabs, and oysters are taken along the Pacific coast. 

Mining. Much that is said about mining in the Plateau States is true for the Pacific States also. The discovery of gold in California in 1848 caused mining to become very important in the West. 

Ever since then California has been a great producer of gold, and it now ranks first among the states. There is not so much placer mining as formerly, but the larger veins in the mountains are worked, and the gold is separated by crushing and other processes. California produces also much of the liquid metal called quicksilver or mercury. All the Pacific 

States are producers of gold, silver, lead, and copper. 

One of the largest smelters is at Tacoma, Washington, where silver, gold, lead, and copper ores are treated. A part of the ore smelted in this and other Puget 

Sound smelters comes from Alaska, Asia, and South America. There are smelters also in eastern Washington and in California.

There are rich vegetable gardens where the Sacramento and San Joaquin rivers come together, and there are rice fields and fruit orchards farther north in the

The harbor of San Francisco is one of the best in North America, and ships go from it to ports of all nations which border on the Pacific and to many on the Atlantic Ocean.

All about the bay are cities and large towns which are suburbs of San Francisco. The largest is Oakland, directly across the bay, where some railroads from the east come to an end. Berkeley is the seat of the University of California.

Los Angeles and Southern California. 

There is great variety in the scenery of southern California. Go 200 miles north of Los Angeles and you come to Mt. Whitney, the highest point in the United 

States. Go down the western slope of this mountain region and you are under the largest trees in the world. Go down the eastern slope, and you come to Death 

Valley, below sea level, the hottest and most barren desert in North America. 

Go east from Los Angeles a hundred miles, and there is another range, the San 

Bernardino Mountains. You will pass through many villages and will see many farms, gardens, and groves of orange and lemon trees (113A). The rain and melting snows of the San Bernardino Mountains make all these possible. A fertile soil, a climate free from frost most of the year, and water for irrigation - these are the great advantages of southern California. 

These facts will help you to understand why Los Angeles has become the largest city west of the Mississippi River. Many people come to Los Angeles from colder parts of our country to spend the winter or to make permanent homes. Tall derricks show that this region has oil in the rocks below its lowlands. The city has also large manufacturing industries. 

As Los Angeles grew and needed more water for city use, engineers went 250 miles into the Sierra Nevada for it. It is carried to the city through tunnels, steel pipes, and canals over mountains and across a desert. ocean. 

Los Angeles was 25 miles from the

The people of the city wanted a harbor and so they added a strip of land, which they called "the Shoe String," that runs down to the seashore. They built a breakwater to give safe anchorage to ships, and made Los Angeles one of the leading ports in the United States (A).

Many people know Los Angeles best for that part of it called Hollywood, which is the center of the moving-picture industry. Other well-known residential cities and resorts near Los Angeles are 

Pasadena, Long Beach, and Santa Barbara. are 

On the coast in the southwest corner of the United States is San Diego, on a fine harbor. It is a center for fishing and canning, but better known as a winter resort. Cross the mountains east of 

San Diego and you come into the warm

Imperial Valley, a part of which is below sea level. There flood waters from the 

Colorado River have made the Salton 

Sea. Today waters from that river help the farmers of the Imperial Valley to raise crops of winter vegetables, oranges, olives, dates, and cotton with long fibers.

Portland and the Willamette Valley. 

If you should sail up the Columbia River and turn southward up the Willamette 

River, you would come to Portland, the chief city of Oregon (A). It is about 100 miles from the ocean, and ranks with 

Los Angeles, San Francisco, and Seattle as one of the great cities of the Pacific coast. The water is deep enough for ocean ships. This makes Portland both a seaport and a river port. It is the outlet of the rich farm and fruit country of the Willamette Valley, and is the chief market and manufacturing center of the state of Oregon. Live stock, poultry, fruit, potatoes, flour, lumber, and wool are among the principal commodities. 

Ships connect Portland with Alaska, with the countries of Asia, and with other lands bordering the Pacific Ocean. 

Courtesy Portland Chamber of Commerce 

A. Part of the city of Portland, Oregon, with Mount 

Hood, fifty miles away, towering in the background.

The Cities of Washington. Go north from Portland and cross the Columbia 

River. You will still be in a broad lowland of fields and great forests. Two or three hours after crossing the Columbia you will reach a seaport, Olympia, at the head of Puget Sound. Follow the east shore and you will find a chain of cities: 

Tacoma, Seattle, Everett, and Bellingham. From Tacoma, Seattle, or Everett you could take a train across the Cascade Range through a tunnel almost eight miles long, the longest railroad tunnel in the United States. 

At Tacoma much smelting is done (page 115) and there are also sawmills where huge logs from the forests of the 

Northwest are made into lumber.

Seattle is the center of trade with Alaska. This is mainly a summer trade, but all the year ships go between this port and the Orient. Seattle is a city built on hills. Some hills that were too steep for building were washed down by streams of water driven against them. with great force. Among the industries of Seattle is shipbuilding. 

Manufacturing and Commercial Advantages. Manufacturing is developing rapidly in the Pacific States. Already it stands next to agriculture in importance. To a large extent the other industries already studied furnish the raw materials for the leading manufactures, which include: (1) the cutting of timber and its manufacture into boards, shingles, boxes, and furniture; (2) the manufacture of flour; (3) slaughtering and meat packing; (4) the canning of fish, fruit, and vegetables (A); (5) the refining of petroleum; (6) the manufacture of butter, cheese, and condensed milk; and (7) the smelting of ores. A. 

Sorting asparagus in a California canning factory.

The stalks are being graded for size and color. 

The manufacture of lumber and lumber products is highest in value in all three states. Flour is milled in Portland, Seattle, and Tacoma, from which points it can easily be shipped to Pacific coast, South American, or Asiatic markets.

Some flour is made at Spokane, in the wheat-growing region of eastern Washington. The falls in the river there can furnish an enormous amount of power. 

The manufactures of the Pacific States are helped by railroads and water routes.

Railroads have been built through the great farming and mining regions, and they bring the products of the fields and mines to the cities. Every year the transcontinental railroads carry many trainloads of fresh fruit across the mountains to eastern markets. The fine harbors of the greater seaports are easily reached from the ocean, and to them come ships from China, Japan, and many other distant lands.

The Panama Canal has increased the water traffic with New York and other

Atlantic ports, and also with European countries.

Interesting Facts. - Tell one interesting fact about each of the following cities: Portland, Seattle, Tacoma. of buffaloes. There were so many buffaloes that much later, when railroads had been built across the plains, trains sometimes had to stop and wait when a herd was crossing the track.

The hides of buffaloes were cured and made into robes that people could wrap up in so as to keep warm during a winter ride. Buffaloes were hunted until almost all of them were killed. Finally our government and that of Canada set aside

A. 

Cattle on the Great Plains, in northern Montana. certain areas as reservations where small herds are kept. If the buffaloes had not been protected in this way there would be none living now. 

Grazing on the Great Plains. Upon what did these vast herds of buffaloes on the Great Plains live? They ate the native bunch grass. This grass grows green and fresh in the time of the spring rains, and is dried up and cured like hay by the summer sun. The buffalo therefore found food on the open plains in winter as well as in summer. The first settlers in this region naturally turned to grazing and farming. The grazing industry was successful and grew rapidly. 

Farming was less successful and many of the early farms were soon abandoned. 

Farming methods, however, were SO changed and improved in later years that there are now many fine farms in the 

Great Plains.

In the early years the cattle were allowed to roam the plains in the care of cowboys. They were left out of doors the year round, even in the cold northern part of our country. They fed on the | native bunch grass as the buffaloes had before them. Better methods are now in use in the north, where fierce winter snowstorms called blizzards sometimes cover the ground with snow. In the old days when this happened the cattle often died from cold and hunger. Now they are sheltered and fed during the winter. 

Their increase in value more than pays for the cost of the shelter and food provided, and for the necessary extra labor. 

The Round-Up. - Pasture lands in the Great Plains are now being fenced in, and the unfenced ranges are rapidly disappearing. Where they still remain the cattle are brought together in a round-up twice a year. The cowboys of various ranches work together. They spread out in lines or parties and drive the cattle scattered over a large area into one small area. Day after day they take a different section of the country. When the cattle have been rounded up, they are found to belong to many different owners. Each grown animal has its owner's brand. 

Most of the young calves will follow their mothers, and thus it is easy to pick them

EACH DOT REPRESENTS 

5,000 HEAD 

A. Sheep in the United States. out also. The other calves are divided among the owners according to the number of cattle each one owns. Each calf is then branded by a hot iron with its owner's mark.

In the autumn round-up the animals that are ready for market, or to be shipped to the prairie states for "finishing off," are separated from the herd by the cowboys. The rest of the herd is allowed to wander back on the range. 

Ranchmen. Much of the old range land has now been fenced in as large ranches. The ranchman's house usually is built by a stream or spring, and near it are smaller houses for the helpers, corrals and barns, a garage, a blacksmith shop, and a pasture for horses. The helpers may still, as in the old days, be called cowboys, but their work is very different. Instead of herding the cattle and protecting them from cattle thieves, they help the ranch owner in keeping the fences in repair and in watering the cattle. 

In the more northern states where winter shelter is necessary they help in cutting hay and getting it into the barns. A single ranch may include hundreds of 

B. Shearing sheep. acres or even many square miles. One or two acres of corn or grass land in 

Iowa will keep a beef animal. To keep one in the dry plains where the wild bunch grass furnishes most of the pasture, from fifteen to twenty-five acres are necessary for each animal. 

Ranchmen today use every effort to raise the kind of beef animal that can be sold for most in the market. The longhorned scrawny animals of the early open-range days are no longer seen. In their place you find heavy-bodied, shortlegged beef animals of the finest grade. 

They are no longer driven hundreds of miles to market as they once were. Instead they are sent by fast freight trains and are carefully fed and watered and given time to rest on the way. 

Sheep on the Great Plains. In the days of the open range, sheep as well as cattle were pastured on the Great Plains, but the sheep herders and cattle men had many bitter quarrels. There were several reasons for this: (1) The cattle men were there first and did not want any sheep to come into their pastures. (2) Sheep eat the grass much closer than

cattle do, sometimes even pulling up the roots to eat them. (3) Sheep leave an odor on grass over which they pasture.

This odor is at times offensive to cattle, and they are said to avoid pastures where sheep have been recently grazing. Now that much of the pasture land is fenced, cattle and sheep can easily be kept in different pastures. On the map, notice particularly the sheep-raising region of 

Texas. More than half the goats raised in our country are found in this region.

Rainfall on the Great Plains and 

Farther West. - Over most of the Great

Plains the rainfall is less than 20 inches a year (84 B). You remember that the same thing is true for much of our country that lies west of the Great Plains. The map shows two regions of heavy rainfall running north and south in Washington, Oregon, and California. It also shows several areas of rainfall of from 20 to 

40 inches in the region of the Rocky Mountains. 

The amount and seasonal distribution of rainfall in any region has an important effect on agriculture. Before taking up the study of farming on the Great Plains it will be well to stop for a review of the rainfall in the western part of our country. 

Over most of the eastern and central part of the United States the rain-bearing winds are from the Atlantic Ocean and the Gulf of Mexico. In the western part the rain-bearing winds are chiefly from the Pacific Ocean. They are called westerly winds and come for the most part from the southwest. 

Blowing from the Pacific toward the western coast they bring much moisture to the land. It is in the form of invisible water vapor that the wind picked up from the surface of the sea as it blew across it. 

A warm wind can contain more of these invisible water particles than a cooler wind. If, therefore, a wind from the

Pacific Ocean has all of the moisture it can contain, it must drop some of this moisture when it cools. In crossing mountains the winds are forced to greater elevations, and are cooled as they rise. 

Rain is therefore left on the western slopes of the Coast Ranges, the Sierra Nevada and the Cascade Range, the ranges of the plateaus, and those of the Rocky 

Mountains (pages 80, 82, 84 B).

After crossing any one of these ranges the wind moves downward over its eastern slope. As it does so its temperature is raised. It can pick up more moisture as invisible vapor. There will be very little rainfall. For this reason, even when the western mountain slopes have heavy rains or snows, the eastern slopes, the interior valleys, and the plateaus between ranges are much drier. 

From what has been said you will understand that the westerly winds that blow down the eastern slope of the Rockies and across the Great Plains leave but little rain. Not only is there but little rainfall in the Great Plains region, but violent storms sometimes sweep across

A. 

- Irrigation ditches. The field shown in this picture slopes gently from the foreground to the background. the country. These are twisting storms that follow a narrow path and cause a great deal of damage. They are called tornadoes. Once in a while cold northern winds called northers sweep south even as far as Texas, causing much damage and distress. the rain that does fall comes down in fierce storms and runs quickly away or soaks into the porous soils of the plains, beyond the reach of roots. 

Farming on the Great Plains. In the eastern part of the United States and on the prairies, the farmer depends on the rains to water his crops and to supply the wells and springs. When no rain falls for a time, he may wish that he could water his potatoes or his corn, but it very rarely happens that any of his crops fail for lack of rain.

In the western part of the Great Plains, and in many parts of the Plateau and Pacific States, the annual rainfall is small, as you have just learned. Frequently 

Irrigation. There are parts of the 

Great Plains where water from mountain streams and from deep wells is used to water the fields for crops. As you know, this is called irrigation. Farmers have used irrigation since ancient times in the dry regions of Egypt and in many other countries where the rainfall is scanty. 

The Indians of our Southwest used irrigation, in a small way, long before the 

Spanish explorers came to that region.

The large rivers of the Great Plains come from beyond the crest of the Rocky

Mountains and are fed by the rains and snows at their sources. They furnish much water to the plains. The waters

of the South Platte and 

Arkansas rivers in Colorado (map, page 90) have long been used for irrigation. Find 

Denver in Colorado, east of the Rockies. 

Large ditches near this city take water out of the South Platte River. 

Each ditch is tapped at many points and the water carried in smaller ditches to the fields below it. If necessary, the fields are leveled and still smaller channels and furrows lead the water to the seeds and roots (124 A). The Reclamation Service. In 1902 

the government of the United States organized the Reclamation Service. It was to reclaim, or make useful for agriculture, more of the dry or arid lands. Under this plan the government finds a good location, or site, on a large river and builds a great dam. A site is a good one (1) if the valley is narrow with a bottom solid enough to carry the weight of the dam; (2) if the subsoil or rock is not so porous as to let the water seep through under the dam; (3) if the land back of the dam is so shaped that a large lake or reservoir can be formed; and (4) if the lands below the dam have such gentle slopes that the water can spread over a large area. The map on this page shows many of the 

the national projects of the Great Plains. 

Life in Irrigated Regions. As a usual thing farmers who make use of irrigation do not have large farms. All of a man's time is taken up in work on a few acres. 

He finds that it pays him to cultivate his land with great care, because irrigated farms can be made to yield large crops. 

Where many small farms get their water from the same canal the farmers live as near neighbors, almost as in a village. Schools, churches, and stores can be built near all the people. Life in in irrigated regions has many comforts and advantages that cannot be had by those who are scattered on the large farms of the prairies and on ranches.

Among the crops grown on irrigated lands are wheat and other grains, alfalfa,

A.-Kafir corn, one of the forage crops grown in regions of light rainfall and in irrigated districts of Texas, Oklahoma, Kansas, and other Western States. sugar beets, and kafir corn (A); and especially cantaloupes in Colorado and onions in western Texas. 

gotten, and much of the land was used only for grazing cattle and sheep. Later, methods of farming were so improved that large areas are now kept under cultivation where the annual rainfall is less than 20 inches. This is called dry farming, although, of course, no crop can be grown without any water. Dry farming means farming where there is less moisture than is needed in ordinary farming. 

In dry farming there are four important things to do: (1) The ground is plowed before the rainy season so that as much rain as possible will soak into the soil, and as little rain as possible will flow off the surface. (2) The surface or top layer of the soil is cultivated frequently. 

This is to prevent the loss of much moisture from the soil by evaporation. (3) Such crops are raised as need the least moisture for their successful growth.

Dry Farming. In some parts of the

Great Plains and in other parts of the West where there was not enough rain, farmers at first tried to raise crops in the usual way. Too often, however, their crops failed, and the farms were given up. 

Villages that had grown up in such regions were abandoned and almost forseed that was brought here from dry countries. (4) Sometimes it is best to grow only one crop in two years, but to keep the soil cultivated so as to prevent much evaporation. Thus the farmer has what is left of two years' rainfall for the use of the plants in one year. 

The Bad Lands. In parts of western 

South Dakota and North Dakota, and of eastern Montana, the heavy rains, which sometimes come even in dry regions, have washed away much of the soil. Hills and gorges with sides and walls partly of bare rock have been left. These regions, called Bad Lands, bear no crops, although there is some pasturage. They are so rough that it is difficult to travel through them.

The Black Hills. The Black Hills are a group of rugged mountains in the

A. A view in the Bad Lands. Do you see why no crops can be raised in such a region? western part of South Dakota. Part of the region is covered with a forest which has been made a forest reserve by the government of the United States. Gold and other minerals are mined, but the region as a whole is thinly settled. 

Other Minerals of the Great Plains. 

Both oil and coal are found in the Great 

Plains (map, page 141). The chief oil fields are in Wyoming in a district north of Casper. The largest of these fields has been named the Teapot Dome district.

Other deposits are found along a small river called Salt Creek. Oil refineries have been built at Casper in Wyoming and at Great Falls, Montana (map, page 

90). A pipe line from the Teapot Dome and Salt Creek districts has been built across Nebraska and Kansas to an oil-distributing center in Missouri. This line is about 700 miles in length. Find it on the map, page 143. 

There are soft coal deposits of great extent and value in the Great Plains.

On account of the difficulty of getting this coal to market at low prices the mines have not developed rapidly. A coal from which coke can be made is found not far from Pueblo, Colorado. Because much coke is used in smelting, large smelters have been built at Pueblo. Find

A. Cotton field in Oklahoma.

They include that part of our country where one crop has been so important for many years that people speak of it as the land where "Cotton is King."

THE COTTON BELT 

Map B on page 133 shows where cotton is grown in the United States. You will see that it is raised chiefly in the southeastern part of our country. It is the chief crop, or one of the chief crops, over a broad stretch of country from Virginia to Texas. It is grown in the drier parts of the Coastal Plain, and it is a great crop in the Piedmont region in North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia. The cotton-growing region includes most of 

Alabama and Mississippi, large parts of 

Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Texas, and some of Louisiana and Tennessee.

Very little cotton is grown in Florida or in the hot and swampy lowlands of other South Atlantic States. It grows well in the Mississippi lowlands, except in the swamps of Louisiana. It is not grown in the highlands of Oklahoma, western Texas, or Tennessee. 

Cotton needs a warm climate but not great heat. It needs a growing season of about 200 to 240 days. During its growing season it needs rain every few days, and it must have an annual rainfall of not less than 23 inches if the fields are not watered by irrigation. What is the annual rainfall in the different parts 132

THE COTTON BELT 

133 of the cotton-growing region? (Map B, page 84.)

While the cotton is ripening and being harvested, it needs weather that is bright and sunny. If there is too much rain at that time, the bolls decay. 

For its best growth cotton needs a fine, rich, welldrained soil that can be easily worked. Such soils are found in the coastal plains and in the Mississippi lowlands. A region of rich black B. loam in Alabama and Mississippi, called the "black belt," and another region in 

Texas, called the "black waxy prairie," are especially fine cotton soils. Texas raises more cotton than any other state. 

Cotton Growers. - Cotton is raised on large farms or plantations whose owners are called planters. Many planters rent parts of their plantation to farmers who wish to raise cotton but have no land of their own. 

Farmers who rent land may own tools and farm animals or they may not. If A. 

Cotton bolls, nearly the actual size. from inch to 14 inches long. 

The fibers are 

Each dot represents 

2.000 boles 

Cotton-producing regions of the United States. they do not, they depend on the planter for mules and tools and for whatever else is necessary. In each case the rent to be paid is agreed upon and it is usually paid in cotton when the cotton is picked. 

Some part of the rented land is generally planted to food crops for the farmer and his family and for any farm animals he may have. 

There are many farmers who own small farms on which cotton is the chief crop. 

They also grow food crops and keep some farm animals. 

How Cotton Is Grown. Cotton is grown each year from seed. In the southern part of the cotton-growing region planting begins as early as February. 

In the northern part the seeds may not be planted until early April. Why is the planting done later there? 

About three months after the seeds are planted, the plant has grown and is covered with blossoms. As the blossoms drop off, seed pods, called bolls, are formed. The fine white fiber or lint that we call cotton grows on the seeds inside these bolls. As the bolls ripen they open, and fluffy masses of white fiber burst out (A). WP- -8

PREST 

CYPRESS 

Courtesy Mobile Chamber of Commerce

A. Bargeloads of cotton brought down a river of Alabama to the port of Mobile for export. Barges of this size will each carry hundreds of bales of cotton. How much does a bale weigh? 

Every year great damage is done to the cotton crop of our country by the boll weevil. This is a beetle that punctures the flower buds and the young bolls and lays its eggs in them. The grubs that develop from the eggs cause the buds to drop from the plant and ruin the fiber in the bolls. No way of completely preventing this damage has yet been found. 

How Cotton Is Harvested. - The bolls do not all open at the same time. For this reason cotton pickers must go over the field several times. In southern 

Texas the picking may be kept up until late into December if the bolls continue to ripen. Most cotton picking is still done by hand, although some cottonpicking machines are now being used.

The cotton is then taken to a gin house, where it is run through a machine called a cotton gin, which separates the lint from the seeds. The cotton gin was invented by Eli Whitney in 1793. Before that time the lint was pulled from the seed by hand. It was a very slow and expensive way of doing this work, and the result was that no very great amount of cotton was grown. Now, by using the gin, the cost of preparing the crop for market is so much less that cotton is grown in great quantities. It is the fiber most used in the making of cloth. Our country grows more than half of all the world crop. 

More than half of the workers in this industry are Negroes. 

Marketing the Cotton. After the lint is separated from the seeds it is pressed into bales of about 500 pounds each and in this form it is sent to market. It may go to cotton mills in or near the state in which it was raised. It may go to England or some other foreign country. It may be sent north to mills in the northern part of our own country. Savannah and Charleston on the Atlantic coast, and 

Galveston, Houston, New Orleans, and

Mobile on the Gulf coast, are ports from which cotton is shipped.

THE COTTON BELT 

135 A.- Cotton mill on the Fall Line near Columbus, Georgia. Notice the great size of both buildings. This mill employs nearly 3000 people. 

Formerly cotton growers saved only enough seeds for the next year's crop and threw the rest away. Then it was found that if the seeds were hulled and crushed they could be used as a fertilizer or as a food for cattle. Today the seeds are hulled, ground, and then pressed to get the oil that they contain. The hulls and the seed cake or meal that is left are sold as cattle food in our own country, Canada, and several European countries. 

Cottonseed oil has many uses. It is made into a substance like lard much used in cooking. It is used as a substitute for olive oil, it is burned in miners' lamps, and it is used in making fine soaps. 

For a long time the southern states were growers of cotton, but had no mills for making cotton cloth. The cotton was used in the mills of New England and other northern states or sold to foreign buyers. In recent years many cotton factories have been built in the South. 

These modern mills are among the finest in our country (A). The southern mills now use more bales of cotton than are sent to the New England factories.

There are many of these great cotton factories in North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia. They have been built in the Piedmont and along the Fall Line, partly because the falls in the rivers furnish water power.

The cotton grower has a great advantage in marketing his crop because it does not spoil quickly. The grower of small fruits and vegetables in Florida and elsewhere must market his crops at once. Crops of that kind spoil quickly after they ripen and must be sold even if the selling price is less than the cost of raising the crop.

What becomes of all the cotton goods that are made in the factories of our own and other countries? In the hot parts of the world most of the clothing of millions of people is made from cotton cloth. 

Even in cooler countries such as our own, cotton cloth is used for much of the clothing worn. Clothing factories use fine cotton cloth in the manufacture of men's shirts and underclothing and for dainty dresses and trimmings. Men's overalls and working dresses for women are made of the heavier kinds of cotton goods. 

Cotton is used also for window hangings, for bed sheets, and for many other things.

A. Cultivating young cotton plants. For best results, much cultivation is needed.

In the days when our country was first settled the colonists were forced to make much of their own cloth, and their own clothing. At first there was no cotton. 

Linen and wool were the fibers chiefly used.

These were twisted into threads by using a spinning wheel, and the threads were woven into cloth on hand looms. Every housewife was kept busy making cloth and clothing to meet the household needs. 

Modern methods of manufacture have made it possible for much of this work to be done in great factories.

OTHER CROPS OF THE COTTON 

BELT 

For many years most southern farmers raised nothing to sell but cotton and a little corn. Cotton was king. Year after year the same crops were planted in the same fields (A). Little by little these plants took from the soil the plant food that they needed. It finally was necessary to return food to the soil by using fertilizers. This made it more expensive to raise the cotton, but the farmers could sell their cotton for money and they needed a money crop. For that reason they continued to raise cotton. In recent years,. however, they have also raised other important crops to sell. 

Mixed Farming. When the boll weevil began to damage the cotton crop, many of the southern farmers turned to mixed farming. In mixed farming the land is not used every year for the same crop. One crop may be planted for a year or more, and then followed by a different crop that does not use the same

What uses are now this changing or rotation of crops the soil does not wear out so quickly and it is not necessary to use so much fertilizer.

Many southern farmers who still raise cotton and corn also raise hogs and beef cattle and the crops needed for their food. 

On many dairy farms in the northern part of our country the farmers raise corn and cut up the ears and stalks for the silo. This gives the cattle a good winter food when the pastures are covered with snow. The southern farmers also raise corn for their live stock, but the growing season is longer and most of the crop is left standing until the ears have ripened.

OTHER CROPS OF THE COTTON BELT 

137 The ripe corn is fed to the poultry, beef cattle, horses, and mules, and is used to fatten hogs. Sometimes the whole ear is given to the animal. At other times the kernels of corn are taken from the ear and fed as shelled corn, or the shelled corn is ground before it is fed. 

Other crops used as food for farm animals are peanuts, cow peas, velvet beans, soy beans, and sweet potatoes. Some farmers feed both the nuts and the vines of the peanut plants to their farm animals. 

Others sell the peanuts and feed the animals the vines. On many farms the vines are cut as hay. On other farms the farmers turn their animals into the field to eat the vines or the cornstalks, after the peanuts or ears of corn have been gathered. Sweet potatoes are used chiefly as food by people, but they are also used to feed hogs that are to be fattened. 

Dairying is not one of the most important industries on southern farms. Usually a farmer will keep cows to supply his family with milk and cream, and there are dairies near southern cities. It is in the northern parts of our country that dairying is carried on most extensively. 

There you find the green pastures, clear spring water, and cool climate that are most favorable for this industry. 

Orchards. Peaches are grown in many parts of the South. They ripen early and are ready for market long before those grown in northern orchards. The best of the southern peaches are famous for their size and quality. More peaches are grown in Georgia than in any other southern state. The raising of pecans has become an important industry in the South. Groves of pecan trees are under cultivation along the Gulf coast and as far north as South Carolina.
