Ewing Galloway 

Packing peaches for shipment in central Georgia.

In the extreme southern part of Texas there is a subtropical region in which vegetables, oranges, and grapefruit are raised in large quantities for sale in northern markets. —➖ 

Special Crops. Tobacco is one of the valuable crops raised in several southern states. There is a wheat-growing region in western Texas and Oklahoma, and in some of the higher and drier parts of Texas and Oklahoma kafir corn is raised as a cattle food. The chief wheatgrowing section of the United States is in the states of the Mississippi Valley, but some wheat is raised in many other states. Sugar cane and rice are important crops in certain states drained by the Mississippi; but before studying them you should read something about the great Mississippi lowland. 

The Mississippi River. Starting at the mouth of the Mississippi River (map, page 83) follow it north to its source in 

Lake Itasca. As you go up the stream notice where it is joined by the Ohio River on the east and the Missouri River on the west. These two streams are the largest tributaries of the Mississippi. On the east the Ohio and its branches drain a large part of the Appalachian Highlands.

Floods. Over most of the region. drained by the Ohio River the average annual rainfall varies from 40 to 60 inches. 

Sometimes large parts of this drainage area will have unusually heavy or longcontinued rains. When this happens, the smaller highland streams change suddenly from pleasant brooks into rushing, roaring torrents. They pour their water into the larger branches. These increase suddenly in size, flooding the lowlands and hurrying on to the Ohio itself.

On the west the Missouri and its tribu- | flows rapidly it can carry along the rock taries drain parts of the Western High- waste that its tributaries bring to it. As lands. Through these and other tribu- the slope of its bed decreases and it moves taries the Mississippi receives also the less swiftly, it must drop a part of its drainage of much of the central lowlands. load. 

The lower Mississippi River is a very muddy stream. The "mud" in the water is really very, very fine particles of rock material or earth. Some of it comes from streams whose waters flow so swiftly that the stones in their beds are bumped or rubbed against each other.

In this way large stones are broken into smaller pieces. The rough and broken edges are worn off, and after a long, long time the stones themselves are worn away. Much of this rock material is washed into the rivers from the valley slopes that border them. This happens when there are heavy rains in the valley, or when the snow that covered the hillsides in the winter melts in the spring. 

At these times every gully in the fields and on the hillsides and every gutter by the roadside will be filled with muddy water rushing toward the nearest river.

From stream to stream this muddy water passes until at last it reaches the Mississippi, the "Father of Waters."

In thinking of the rock waste that the 

Mississippi carries, keep these facts in mind: (1) It comes from all parts of the 

Mississippi drainage basin. It is made up of particles of many kinds of rocks and should make a fine and fertile soil. (2) Streams that flow swiftly can carry a greater amount, or load, than streams that flow slowly. A stream moving swiftly among the hills can carry more than one of the same size winding a lazy way through the plains. (3) Large streams can carry a greater load than small streams. So long as the Mississippi 

The same thing may happen when heavy winter snows in the higher or more northerly parts of the Ohio drainage area melt suddenly in the spring. In either case the lower lands along the Ohio are flooded. These flood waters flow on to join the Mississippi and cause it to overflow its banks also. Heavy rains or melting snow in other parts of the Mississippi drainage area may cause floods, but it is from the Ohio that the great river receives most of its flood waters. 

Lowlands border the Mississippi from the mouth of the Ohio to the Gulf. In times of very high water hundreds of square miles may be flooded and great damage done. Buildings and live stock are swept away and crops ruined, and even some of the people may be drowned. 

The United States Weather Bureau has flood stations on the Ohio, the Mississippi, and many other rivers. Men at these stations send out flood warnings when the rivers begin to rise. This usually gives the people time to do what they can to save their property.

OTHER CROPS OF THE COTTON BELT 

139 

Courtesy Mississippi River Commission

A.-Levee on the Mississippi River, in Louisiana. Notice how low the water is. At times of high water the river will rise almost as high as the levee. The pipes crossing the levee carry water for irrigation. 

Levees. During floods the rivers are large, they flow swiftly, and they carry much rock waste. As soon as the rain stops and the snow is melted, the flood waters begin to go down. The water in the river moves more slowly. It can no longer carry its load of rock waste. The larger and heavier particles are dropped first, and then, as the water moves more and more slowly, finer particles also are dropped. 

When rivers overflow their banks more of the rock waste is dropped on the part of the flood plain that borders the river than in any other part. Because of this the banks of the river are built higher than those parts of the flood plain farther away from the stream. This means that when a stream overflows these built-up banks, the lower country beyond them is sure to be flooded. 

To keep rivers from overflowing their bordering lowlands in times of high water men sometimes build high embankments called levees (A). Many levees have been built along the banks of the lower Mississippi. It is only when there is a break in a levee, or when the water rises above it, that the land protected by levees is flooded. But these floods are the worst of all. 

The Delta Lands. When a river flows into a lake or an ocean it is lost in the larger body of quiet water. It then drops the remaining rock waste it has been carrying. This is true of the Mississippi where it enters the Gulf of Mexico. 

A long, long time ago the Gulf of 

Mexico reached far up the Mississippi

Valley. During all of the years since that time rock waste that reached the mouth of the river has been filling this ancient bay. It has now been completely filled and the land made from the rock waste is pushing forward into the Gulf.

The river deposits so much of this rock waste that it almost blocks itself, and divides into smaller streams. These smaller streams are building their banks into the Gulf. Notice them on the map (page 129). They are like the fingers of a great hand. This river-made land of the lower Mississippi is its delta. Perhaps you were in the class that last year took

an imaginary journey to the hot sunny land of Egypt in Africa. If you were, you will remember that the great river of Egypt also has a delta at its mouth. 

Can you give the name of this river?

The Mississippi delta lands are low and so far south that they have a hot climate. 

In parts of these delta lands there are swamps. In other parts there are forests. In still other parts the land is dry enough so crops can be grown. The hot climate, the fertile delta soils, and the long growing season make it possible to raise both sugar cane and rice. 

Sugar. Nearly all the sugar cane in the United States is raised in Louisiana, although a small amount is grown in Georgia and other states of the far South. 

Sugar cane needs a growing season of eight months or longer. What parts of the United States have a growing season of that length? (Map A, page 84.) The cane grows like a very large and very tall grass (A). The stems are from 10 to 16 feet high and from a half inch to over two inches thick. 

A. Young sugar cane.

When the cane is ripe, the leaves are stripped off and the cane is cut close to the ground. Then it is taken to a mill where it is crushed between rollers to squeeze out the sweet juice from which sugar is made. 

Nearly all the sugar of the world was at one time made from cane, and much of our present supply still comes from the same source. Some years ago it was found that sugar can be made from certain kinds of beets. You will learn later about the sugar-beet-growing sections of our country. You will learn also about a kind of sugar that is made from the sap of maple trees. 

Rice. Louisiana leads all the states in raising rice, as it does in growing sugar cane. Nearly half of the rice grown in our country comes from this state.

The best kinds of rice are grown on flat lands. During a part of the growing period the rice fields must be flooded.

This is usually done two or three times after the rice is planted. There is much level land in Louisiana, Texas, and Arkansas that can be flooded by pumping water into ditches that will carry it over the fields. These lands are excellent for growing rice. The soil is fine and fertile. 

The fields can be flooded when necessary, and it is easy to drain them so they will be dry during the harvesting of the crop. 

When rice is ripe, it is harvested and threshed in much the same way as other grains. The rice grain has a clinging hull or outside coat which is then removed by a machine before the grain is ready for use. In our country most people like rice that has also been polished. The polishing removes a brownish coating of great food value, and gives the rice grain the dull white look of a pearl.

MINERAL AND OTHER PRODUCTS OF THE SOUTHLAND 

On the map on pages 128-129 find the states of Louisiana, Texas, Oklahoma, and 

Arkansas. In addition to the great crops grown there, these states have also some very very valuable mineral resources about which you should learn. Among them are petroleum, sulphur, and salt. The map on this page shows where the chief deposits are located.

Petroleum and Natural Gas. - Notice on the map two of the regions producing petroleum. One of these borders the 

Gulf of Mexico in Texas and Louisiana.

The other, by far the largest and most productive, stretches from central Texas eastward into Louisiana and Arkansas, and northward through Oklahoma into 6. How delta lands have been formed at Kansas. This region is known as the the mouth of the river. mid-continent field.

A. - Oil wells in Oklahoma City, Oklahoma. The skeleton towers are called derricks. One is built over each well, for use in drilling the well. You will see from the number of derricks that a great many wells have been drilled. 

They are so close together because each land owner has the right to drill wells. In the open country the wells would be farther apart. Notice also the many round tanks for storing the oil. 

Deep in the earth, underneath the fields and the cities, are layers of rock in which the petroleum is found. This rock is usually a sandstone in which there are very small openings between the fine particles of sand. It is in these spaces and in any larger openings in the rock that petroleum and natural gas gather.

When natural gas is present, it is at the top because it is lighter than the oil. 

Petroleum is a thick, heavy oil of a very dark brown color. To get it holes are | drilled down to the rock containing the oil, and pipes are pushed into the holes. 

Through these the oil may be pumped in much the same way that water is pumped from deep wells (A). Many oil wells do not need to be pumped at first. In those wells as soon as the drilling is finished the gas and oil come out with a rush that carries them many feet into the air. Such a well is a gusher," and sometimes a great deal of oil is lost before the well is capped and brought under control. (6 

B.-Tank cars in Oklahoma, used for shipping oil.

Both natural gas and petroleum are very valuable. The gas is sent from the wells through pipes buried in the ground to homes and business places in many parts of our country where it is used for heating and lighting. The petroleum is sent to refineries where it is purified and made into many products, including fuel oil, gasoline, kerosene, and oils and greases for all kinds of machinery. 

Some of the crude oil is shipped by rail to refineries or to seaports in great tank cars (142 B). At one time this was the usual way of shipping the oil. Now there are many pipes reaching over our country from the wells to refining centers, markets, and ports on the Gulf of Mexico, the 

Great Lakes, and the Atlantic Ocean (A).

The oil is pumped through these pipes from one pumping station to another until it reaches the refinery or port. 

These pipes, like the pipes carrying natural gas, are usually buried a few feet in the ground so they will be out of the way (B). They cross plains for long distances in almost straight lines. They cross hills and wind their way along valleys. Wherever they go, the pipes are Casper Kansas Toledo Chicago

Courtesy Natural Gas Pipelines of America 

B.-Laying a pipe line. put together so well that there is rarely any loss of oil through leaks or breaks. 

Refining and Distributing. The refining is done by heating the oil in tanks or stills so as to separate it into its different products. From the refineries the petroleum products go by train to large wholesale centers for distribution to retailers. Such fluid products as fuel oil, road oil, kerosene, and gasoline go to wholesalers in tank cars, but some of the gasoline is pumped through pipe lines. Boston

They are distributed to retailers or consumers by motor tank trucks that can follow the roads and stop at any warehouse, store, or filling station. Machine oils and greases, paraffin, and medical preparations are put up in packages for sale. 

From a number of ports on the Gulf and Atlantic coasts different fluid products, but chiefly gasoline and kerosene, are sent to foreign lands in tank ships.

One of the oil refineries at Fort Worth, Texas. Notice the smokestacks; also, in the foreground, the large storage tanks with pipes for filling motor trucks. 

Growth of Population in the Petroleum 

Fields. The discovery of oil usually means wealth for those who own the land and for many of the producers. It also means growth and prosperity for the villages and cities in an oil district.

They are no longer mere trading centers for the farming region around them. As workmen connected with the oil industry move in, it becomes necessary to meet their needs for food, clothing, and homes. 

More houses are built. More and larger stores are needed. Population increases as the oil and other industries prosper.

Among the oil-refining cities in the 

South Central States are Beaumont and 

Port Arthur. These cities have also become ports from which much oil is shipped. Tulsa in Oklahoma is the chief oil center in the mid-continent field. 

Other centers of the oil industry include Muskogee and Oklahoma City in Oklahoma, Fort Worth in Texas (A), and 

Shreveport and Baton Rouge in Louisiana. Find all these cities on the map, pages 128-129.

Oklahoma City owes its growth and importance chiefly to the prosperity of the state of Oklahoma, which is largely due to the oil industry. This city is the capital of the state and its chief railroad and trading center. Sulphur. 

Along the Gulf coast of

Texas there is a region of lowland where there are beds of sulphur at depths of 

500 to 1500 feet. Sulphur is a yellow rocklike mineral for which there are many uses. Among these are its use in the manufacture of wood pulp, in making rubber goods, as a bleach for whitening cloth and other materials, in the manufacture of some medicines, and in making sprays to kill insects on trees and plants.

Most of the sulphur that the world now uses comes from Texas. In mining it wells are drilled and pipes are sunk to the sulphur beds. Four pipes are put down in a well, each one of the smaller pipes being inside the one next in size. The largest pipe is usually 10 inches in diameter. This rests on top of the sulphur deposit. The first inside pipe is 6 inches in diameter; it is used for forcing hot water into the sulphur deposit. The next pipe, of 3 inches, is to carry melted sulphur up, and the smallest pipe, 1 inch, is to carry compressed air down.

Water, hot enough to melt the sulphur, is pumped down into the sulphur bed. 

The melted sulphur is forced up to the surface by means of compressed air. 

There it is run off into large wooden tanks to cool and harden. A great block of almost pure sulphur is built up inside the

A.-Loading sulphur into railroad cars, by machinery, in 

Texas. The great block of sulphur is about 40 feet high. 

tank. This block of sulphur can then be broken into small pieces for shipment (A). 

Salt. There are vast deposits of salt underneath the surface of Louisiana and Texas. No one knows just how large these deposits are, but it is known that they are of great size. They have been found below the sulphur beds and elsewhere. 

This rock salt is very pure and white. 

It is taken out in blocks that are brought to the surface and crushed. Whenever it becomes necessary, wooden supports are put in the mines, or pillars of salt are left to hold up the roof (B). The white ceilings, walls, and floors of the great rooms that are left when the salt has been partly removed are very beautiful to see. 

Salt is prepared in different forms for different uses. You all know about the fine white salt that we use to season our food. Salt is used also in packing meat and fish to keep them from spoiling, and in curing hides. It is used in the manufacture of washing soda, bleaching powders, and many chemical preparations.

It is used in the glass and soap industries and in making glaze and enamels. B. 

Salt mine in Louisiana. The man you see is about six feet tall. How tall are the pillars?

Other Minerals of the Region. 

number of other minerals are found in the 

South Atlantic and South Central States.

The more important ones will be taken up for study when you come to the natural regions in which they are found.

Among them are coal, iron ore, and limestone in West Virginia, Kentucky, Tennessee, and Alabama.

In Georgia and Tennessee there are quarries (C) from which beautiful marble is taken. The blocks of marble are shaped and polished and sent to different parts of our country to be used in fine buildings. 

C. Marble quarry near Knoxville, Tennessee. At the right a large block of marble is being lifted out.

A. 

U. S. Bureau of Mines

A bauxite deposit in Arkansas. How large are the cars in the train shown here?

Bauxite, the clay mineral from which aluminum is made, is found in central 

Arkansas and in Tennessee. Arkansas has in some years produced half of the world's supply of bauxite (A). A loose and crumbly rock material called phosphate rock is found in Tennessee and in 

Florida. The Florida deposits are the largest in the world. The rock is dug with a steam shovel, after the surface soil has been removed. It is then washed and ground. It is used alone, or with nitrate and potash, as a fertilizer. 

The Forests of the Southland. The southern pine grows on the coastal plains all the way from North Carolina to Texas.

It furnishes an excellent building lumber much used for interior woodwork and for other purposes.

Many of the pine trees are tapped to get their sap. From this sap turpentine, pitch, tar, and rosin are made. 

These are called naval stores. Years ago, when nearly all ships were made of wood, the pitch and tar were used to make them water-tight. Since that time they have been used for many other purposes, but the name naval stores is still used.

The cypress is another southern softwood tree. It grows chiefly in swampy regions (B). Its lumber has a straight grain and is very easily worked. It is used in the construction of wooden or frame buildings for shingles and outside walls, and for doors, window frames, and other purposes on the inside of buildings. Cypress will not quickly decay in contact with the earth, and is therefore used for fence posts. Some of the finer wood is used for musical instruments and chests.

In addition to the pine and cypress forests of the South there are large areas where most of the trees are hardwoods. 

These areas include the southern Appalachians; northern Georgia, Alabama, and Mississippi; most of Kentucky and 

Tennessee; and parts of Oklahoma and 

Texas. These regions are higher than the Atlantic and Gulf lowlands and have a cooler climate and better drainage.

In the pine forests railroads are built into the region where the trees are to be cut down. Machines pile the logs on flat cars, which are then hauled to the larger mills. Sometimes portable mills are used; that is, mills that can be moved. B.

- Swamp cypress, in Mississippi. Notice the enlarged lower part of the trunk.

WHOR A. 

Courtesy Memphis Chamber of Commerce

Part of the waterfront, Memphis. Several boats can be seen near the shore. The land here is much higher than the water.

In the cypress swamps many of the largest and best trees grow where there are no railroads to handle them. Cypress logs are therefore bound together and floated to some place where there is a mill or a railroad leading to a mill. 

Memphis in Tennessee is on a high bluff overlooking the Mississippi River. Across the river is Arkansas, and just to the south is Mississippi. These states have extensive forests. Ships can reach Memphis from the Gulf, and the city has many railroads. It has become one of the chief lumber markets of our country, especially for hardwoods. Its factories make many articles of wood, including such products as doors, window sashes and frames, window blinds, floor boards, and stairs.

The manufacture of articles made from wood is carried on in so many of the cities and villages in the South that they cannot all be named here. Shreveport in Louisiana and Fort Smith in Arkansas are lumber-manufacturing centers.

Lumber is shipped from many ports on the Gulf and Atlantic coasts. Among the more important are Mobile, Alabama; 

Savannah, Georgia; Jacksonville and 

Tampa, Florida; Charleston, South 

Carolina; and Wilmington, North Carolina. Much lumber is sent to market by railroad. 

Furs in Louisiana. 

One other industry in the South must be mentioned because it is so unexpected. When you think of furs it is natural to think of the cold countries, for many fur-bearing animals live only in such countries. But some fur animals, chiefly muskrats, live along the marshlands and in the network of waters through the delta lowlands of 

Louisiana. Besides muskrats there are minks, skunks, otters, foxes, raccoons, and opossums. Thousands of men make their living by trapping. The furs taken each year are worth millions of dollars. — -

CITIES OF THE SOUTH CENTRAL STATES 

The rapid growth of industry in the South has increased the importance of a large number of cities in the South Central States. Among the factors that have helped in this increase are the shipping of cotton, the development of the oil areas of Texas and adjoining states, the development of water power, and the increase in manufacturing. 

New Orleans, the Gateway to the 

South. New Orleans, the largest city of the South Central States, is a most interesting city. It is on the Mississippi 

River about 100 miles from its mouth. 

It is often called the "Crescent City" because its southern part lies in a bend of the river (A). There is one part of the old city where descendants of the early 

Spanish and French settlers still live.

There the houses, the narrow streets, the languages that you hear spoken, and the quaint customs make you feel almost as if you were in a foreign city. 

New Orleans has more than fifty miles of water front for shipping. Most of the wharves are on the river, but the city also fronts on Lake Pontchartrain. A canal and an inner harbor join the Mississippi and Lake Pontchartrain. This inner harbor is large enough for ocean-going vessels. The city stands on low ground, while levees more than twenty feet high keep back the flood waters of the river.

CITIES OF THE SOUTH CENTRAL STATES 

149 YO A. Unloading bananas. The belt conveyer is lifting the bananas from the ship's hold. Many bananas are brought into the country through New Orleans. 

New Orleans carries on a river trade with cities on the Mississippi as far north as St. Paul, with cities on the Ohio as far as Pittsburgh, and up the Missouri as far as Kansas City (map, page 87). The rivers are not much used by passenger steamers now. Railroads, motor busses, and airplanes give much faster service.

Some kinds of heavy freight are sent by the river route because they can be carried much more cheaply by boat. Coal barges from the Pittsburgh district are tied together and pushed by a "towboat "all the way to New Orleans. Cargoes of wheat, cotton, lumber, and tobacco form part of the freight carried by these river steamboats (B).

Since the great ship canal was cut across the Isthmus of Panama a ship can go from New Orleans to ports on the Pacific Ocean as easily as it can to ports on the Atlantic. Because of this,

New Orleans has become an even more important shipping center for the products of the Gulf lowlands and for those of the Mississippi Valley.

NCS- 

B. Towboat with freight barges on the Mississippi 

River, near Memphis, Tennessee. A railroad train to carry this amount of freight would be about a mile long. 

From New Orleans railroads lead north over the plains to great cities and railroad centers. Other railroads from New Orleans run eastward to the Atlantic and westward to the Pacific coast. Because of the river and ocean trade and the railroads, New Orleans has become the southern gateway of the United States, as New York is the eastern gateway.

Other Cities of Louisiana. -About 100 

miles up the Mississippi from New Orleans is Baton Rouge, the capital of Louisiana.

One of the greatest oil refineries of the world is in this city. In northwestern 

Louisiana is Shreveport, second in size among the cities of the state. It has oil refineries, lumber mills, and a large glass factory. Sand for the glass is found in

Arkansas, not far away, and close by is natural gas, the best fuel for making glass.

The Seaports of Texas. - West of 

New Orleans, in Texas, there are four cities that are important in the trade of our country. They are Galveston, Houston, Port Arthur, and Beaumont. Find them on the map.

BR Maurer

A. On the Gulf front of Galveston, Texas. You are looking down on the top of the famous 17-foot sea wall which extends along the right side of the boulevard. You can see, too, the shadow of this wall on the beach. 

Galveston is one of the leading seaports of the United States. It stands on a low island at the mouth of Galveston Bay. 

In the year 1900 a storm wave from the 

Gulf swept over the city and did terrible damage. Since that time the city has built a sea wall seventeen feet high and more than seven miles long to protect it from other storm waves in the future (A). 

You will remember that Texas raises more cotton than any other state. Much of this cotton is exported from Galveston. 

No other port in any land sends so much cotton to the mills of the world. Galveston also carries on an immense trade in other products. It ships an enormous amount of petroleum and petroleum products. It has great grain elevators and ships much wheat, because wheat from the great fields to the north can reach Galveston by rail more easily and cheaply than other ports. Several steamship lines run between Galveston and 

European ports. 

About fifty miles northwest of Galveston is the city of Houston. It is on a small river that flows into Galveston Bay.

This river has been enlarged into a ship canal wide enough and deep enough for ocean-going ships. Houston is the largest city in Texas, and since the ship canal was built it has become an important port. 

Pipe lines from the oil fields bring crude oil to its refineries. Other raw materials for its mills and factories come from Texas and near-by states. These raw materials include rice that must be hulled and polished, cotton seed from which the oil is to be pressed, and lumber for planing mills and woodworking shops. Railroads run into Houston like spokes to the hub of a wheel, and there are large car shops in the city. 

Galveston and Houston are twin ports. 

So are Port Arthur and Beaumont. The 

Gulf oil field is not far away, and these cities form a natural center for the shipment of petroleum and its products.

CITIES OF THE SOUTH CENTRAL STATES 

151 נו 

A. Muscle Shoals dam, near Florence, Alabama. Under each arch there is a gate for controlling the overflow of water. In the power house at the right are machines for generating electricity. 

Interior Cities of Texas. - Austin is the capital of Texas and the trade center for the southwestern and south-central parts of the state. It is a live-stock, wool, and cotton market. San Antonio, the oldest city in the state, is the center of a farming and live-stock region with growing manufactures and a large trade. 

Dallas is the principal shipping and financial center of Texas. It has a large trade in wheat, cotton, live stock, and meats. It manufactures leather goods, cotton gins, cottonseed oil, and agricultural tools. At Fort Worth, not far west of Dallas, there are stockyards and meatpacking establishments, car shops, and oil refineries (144 A). 

El Paso is a health resort and a center of trade with Mexico. It is on the Rio 

Grande in the western part of the state. 

One hundred miles up the Rio Grande from El Paso is a great dam built by the 

United States government. The water held behind this dam is used for watering fields down the valley, where vegetables and tropical fruits are raised. 

Cities of Alabama. Near Florence, a small city in northwestern Alabama, the United States government has built a great hydroelectric power plant (A). 

This is on the Tennessee River, at a place where the river drops rapidly enough to give much water power. 

Mobile and Birmingham are the two chief cities of Alabama. Mobile (134 A) is a seaport and a place of trade and commerce; and Birmingham is a great city of iron works.

As Alabama has a share of the lumbering industry of the South, you may be sure that lumber will find its way to the wharves of Mobile. From this port cotton and lumber are sent to our Atlantic ports and to Europe.

Birmingham is close to the Great Appalachian Valley, where, at its southern end, it opens on the coastal plain. Birmingham is one of the cities in the Appalachian

A. 

Scene in the "Blue Grass" region near Lexington, Kentucky. Many race horses, trotting horses, and other live stock are raised in this part of the state. 

Highlands that owe their growth and prosperity to the mining of iron and coal and to the manufacture of steel. As the 

Appalachian Highlands are to be studied later, these cities will also be studied at that time. Among them are cities in eastern Tennessee, in West Virginia, and farther north. 66 

Cities in the Lowlands of Tennessee and Kentucky. - In central Tennessee there is a lowland region known as the "Nashville basin." To the east, south, and west of it there are forested hill lands called the Highland Rim." Underneath the basin lands the rocks are of limestone. As limestone decays it forms very fertile soil, and so the Nashville basin is a very rich farming country. It is a region of mixed farming, producing wheat, corn, and tobacco, and raising dairy cows, beef cattle, and hogs. 

The chief city of the Nashville basin is Nashville, the capital of the state and a railroad and trading center. Among its manufactures are furniture and other lumber products, flour and other food products, clothing, and knit goods.. 

Find Memphis, Tennessee, on the map. 

What can you tell about that city? (Page 147.) 

Find Louisville, Lexington, and Frankfort on the map. These cities are in the Kentucky lowland that is bordered on the southeast by the Cumberland Plateau. This lowland has a rich soil because of the decay of the underlying limestone. It is an excellent agricultural district called the "Blue Grass" region from a kind of grass that grows in the pastures and meadows (A).

In this region you find fat cattle, sheep, and fine horses. It is also one of the great tobacco-growing regions in our country. 

Much corn is grown, and some wheat also, for the Blue Grass region is a region of mixed farming.

Louisville is the chief city of Kentucky. It was at first a very small settlement on the south bank of the Ohio River. At this point there were some rapids in the river. Boats could not pass these rapids, called the "Falls of the Ohio," and both passengers and freight were landed and transported around the falls. The navigation of the river has since been greatly improved.

A canal has been built around the falls, and the transfer of goods and passengers is no longer necessary. Louisville early became a railroad center and, because of its manufacturing industries, it is of constantly increasing importance. It is now the marketing and manufacturing center for a large tobacco region. 

Because Louisville is a great railroad center it has shops in which railroad engines and cars are repaired. 

As you would expect in a region where many beef cattle, sheep, and hogs are raised or brought in from the west to be fattened, there are meat-packing plants. There are petroleum refineries using crude oil from near-by and more distant fields.

There are woodworking plants using lumber from near-by forests as well as other timber supplies. There are factories in which a variety of agricultural tools are manufactured and there are many machine shops and foundries.

One of the wonders of central Kentucky is the Mammoth Cave, one of the largest in the world. It was formed by waters from the earth's surface that made their way to underlying limestone rock and dissolved it. There are 150 miles of underground passages. There are subterranean (under the earth) rivers, many waterfalls, and great rooms with high ceilings.

Atlantic Coastal Plain. The Atlantic 

Coastal Plain includes a small part of 

New York, nearly two thirds of New

Jersey, a narrow strip in southeastern Pennsylvania, and a large part of the 

South Atlantic group. The plain is very flat, and is bordered on the ocean side by a gently sloping beach (A). The river valleys are not deep. From the coast the plain extends westward as far as the Fall Line. This long belt of lowland is called the Tidewater Country.

The lower parts of the rivers are tidal, and many of them flow into broad bays due to the sinking of the coast. Of such bays, the Chesapeake is the greatest. 

The Susquehanna, Potomac, and James rivers flow into it. The water at the entrance of the bay, in its main channels, and in its harbors is deep enough for ocean ships. 

Northeast of Chesapeake Bay, between 

New Jersey and Delaware, is Delaware 

Bay. Ocean-going ships use it and the tidal waters of the Delaware River to reach the port of Philadelphia. From the deep water of the bay to Philadelphia a channel 32 feet deep and 600 feet wide has been dredged, at a cost of many millions of dollars. Smaller boats are able to use the river as far as Trenton, New Jersey. 

South of Chesapeake Bay, indenting the mainland of North Carolina, are two large sounds, Albemarle and Pamlico (map, page 131). They are shallow and can be safely used only by small vessels. 

They are partly shut off from the ocean by a long chain of narrow, sandy barrier beaches, or outer shore lands, built up by the waves and winds. From these beaches a number of capes extend into the ocean. Near them the water is shallow and they are dangerous to large ships.

Farther to the south the bays are smaller, but they too are due to the sinking of the land. The rivers, such as the Savannah, allow the tides to come to the inner edge of the coastal plain. 

Swamps and Their Drainage. In a country so flat and so close to sea level as the part of the coastal plain bordering the Atlantic, there will usually be many swamps. The South Atlantic States have several that are very large. One of them is the famous Dismal Swamp (A), 30 miles long and 10 miles broad, in Virginia and North Carolina. A part of it has been drained, cleared, and used for farming.

A canal through the Dismal Swamp is one of several canals that join a number of natural water bodies into an inland waterway 2500 miles in length. This waterway is used by small ships trading along the Atlantic coast, for it is protected from the storms on the Atlantic. 

Another one of the great swamps of the 

Atlantic Coastal Plain is in Florida. This state has also many lakes, and because the lakes and swamps are both caused by the same conditions they are studied together.

The rainfall map on page 84 shows you that the rainfall of Florida is very heavy. 

As most of the state is only a few feet above sea level, the rain does not run off the surface of the land quickly. As a result there are over 30,000 lakes in 

Florida, and a larger number of swamps.

The underlying rocks of much of the 

Florida peninsula are limestone. Water soaks through the soil and dissolves the limestone in some places. The surface soil sinks into these limestone pockets or sink holes. Water fills them and forms a lake. Some of the lakes are connected by underground streams that flow through 

A.- In the Dismal Swamp, Virginia. the limestone rocks. Some underground streams come to the surface as springs. 

The largest lake in Florida is Lake Okeechobee, a shallow body of water, in which there are few places with a greater depth than 15 feet. Connecting with this lake on the south and southeast is the great swamp region known as the Everglades. Over some parts of the Everglades the water is almost deep enough to be called a lake. In the shallow waters there are many islands. They are covered with a dense growth of grass, underbrush, and trees. There is much saw grass, so named because of the sawlike teeth on its edges. In this region along the border of 

Lake Okeechobee, are some of the finest cypress trees in Florida. 

Part of the Everglades has been drained by a large canal cut to the sea, and smaller canals that connect with it. The work is very expensive. Land that has been drained has been settled, and crops of fruit, vegetables, and sugar cane are being grown on it. The raising of sugar on the drained lands of the Everglades may become a very important industry.

From Florida to New York along the

Atlantic Coast. At the southern end of 

Florida and extending in a great sweeping curve south and west are the Florida 

Keys. They are islands of coral rock and of sand made from the broken rock.

Coral rock is made by the hard skeletons of tiny coral animals that live and die in warm ocean waters. Each living coral fastens itself to the rock, and when it dies, its skeleton is added to those of the corals that have lived and died before it. It took a long, long period of time for the skeletons of these coral animals to make the rocks of these islands. Part of the peninsula is made of the same kind of coral rock.

Sponge Fisheries. — Around the Flor ida Keys, at the southern end of the peninsula, and on the west coast, many men are engaged in the sponge fisheries. 

Sponges are the soft skeletons of small animals that live in warm salt water, where they fasten themselves to rocks on the bottom of the sea. In shallow water men gather them by using a pole with a hook on the end with which to tear the sponges loose from the rocks where they grow in clusters. In deeper waters men go down in diving suits and cut the sponges from the rocks. Each wears a water-tight suit and helmet, and air is pumped to him through a rubber tube. 

From what you have just read you know that coral animals and sponges only grow in warm ocean waters. You know, therefore, that the waters around Florida must be warm at all seasons of the year. To understand why this is so you should know why there is such a difference in the climate of countries that are near the poles, and that of countries that are near the Equator. 

Climate. When you think about the climate of a place you should have three things in mind: (1) the temperature, (2) the rainfall, and (3) the prevailing or usual winds.

In the northern parts of our country the winter is the cold season. It is then that the snow comes and people stay indoors more than at other seasons of the year. 

Spring follows and, especially in the lowlands, it begins to get warmer. If there is enough rain, the grass starts to grow, leaves come on the trees and shrubs, fruit trees and flowering shrubs put out their blossoms, and crops are planted. Summer is the hot season. Crops are growing and must be cultivated. This is the time for many picnics, hikes, and outdoor sports, and many kinds of outdoor work. 

In the fall it begins to get colder. The late crops are gathered. The leaves of the hardwoods change their color and are later blown from their branches. Winter comes on once more.

In the far south, and especially in southern Florida, the seasons are different. The summers are hot, but the winters are also warm. One reason for this is that Florida is much nearer the Equator than the states in the northern part of our country. There is, for example, a difference of 20 degrees of latitude between northern New York and southern Florida (map, page 87). 

At sea level, as you go north or south from the Equator, the temperature is usually about one degree less for each degree of latitude. This is not true for all places and at all times, for there are many things that may influence the temperature of a place. It is, however, usually about true and it is a fact worth remembering in your study of geography.

Why should a difference in latitude make a difference in temperature? You must have noticed, wherever you live, that the higher the sun is in the sky the warmer it usually gets. The sun is higher in the sky at noon than it is early in the morning or late in the afternoon.

Usually it is warmer near noontime than it is in the morning or evening.

The sun is higher in the sky at noon in the summer time than it is at noon in the winter time. Usually midday in summer is much warmer than midday in winter. 

So you can say that the higher the sun is in the sky the warmer it usually is. In 

Florida on any day in the year the sun is higher in the sky at noon than it is at the same time in places to the north. This is because of the difference in latitude.

On the map, just a little south of Florida, find an east-and-west line named the Tropic of Cancer. This tropic is 23 degrees north of the Equator. There is another tropic the same distance south of the Equator; it is named the Tropic of Capricorn. The part of our earth between these two tropics is called the tropical region or the Torrid Zone. The word torrid means "hot." Every place in the Torrid Zone has the sun directly overhead at noon sometime during the year. 

Every place on the Tropic of Cancer has the sun directly overhead at noon on 

June 21. North of the Tropic of Cancer and south of the Tropic of Capricorn the sun is never directly overhead. Florida is so near the Tropic of Cancer that on 

June 21 the sun is nearly overhead at noon, and on every day in the year it is high in the sky. The climate of Florida is subtropical, which means "almost tropical." 

A second reason for difference in temperature between any two places may be because one place is higher above sea level than the other. Temperature usually decreases about one degree for each 

300 feet of increase in height. Most of Florida is level and only a little above the sea. There are no mountains in that state where people can go to get cool in the summer time as they can in other parts of our country.

Florida has a more even temperature throughout the year than places that are not near the ocean or some other large body of water. This is because winds blowing over land from the bordering waters are usually much warmer than the land in winter and cooler than the land in summer. Florida is so nearly surrounded by water that winds from almost any direction will modify its temperature in these ways.

Sometimes, but not often, there are severe frosts in northern and central 

Florida, when a cold wind drives into the state from the north. During most of the winter, when high mountains and the northern parts of our country are covered with snow, southern Florida is a land of sunshine and flowers. For this reason many people who do not like the cold northern winter spend that season in Florida.

Florida is a region of heavy rainfall. 

The heaviest rainfall comes during the months from June to mid-September. 

Once in a while violent windstorms called hurricanes sweep in from the ocean and cause considerable damage. They generally come in late summer or early fall.

Some parts of the state have more than 60 inches of rainfall in a year, and every part has more than 50 inches.

more. 

Crops. Because there are not many frosts, even in winter, the growing season in Florida is much longer than it is in the northern part of our country (84 A).

In central Florida it lasts for 300 days or 

In most parts of New York state, for example, it lasts from 120 to 190 days. 

Because of the high temperature, heavy rainfall, and long growing season, the trees and some of the crops of Florida are different from those that grow in cooler or less rainy regions.

Among the forest trees the southern pine is cut for its rather coarse-grained lumber, much used for floors and interior trim. It is very different from the finegrained white pine of northern forests.

Other Florida trees are the live oak, cypress, and palmetto. Among the trees of 

Florida are some that grow in regions that are nearly frost-free. The palm 

The palm trees are beautiful and graceful. The cabbage palms are found in the central and southern parts of the state, but the splendid royal palm and the coconut palm are found only in the south. 

Florida raises oranges, lemons, limes, and grapefruit (A). These citrus fruits, as they are called, cannot be successfully grown in the northern states because a hard frost kills citrus fruit trees. Even in Florida orchards of this kind are planted only where there are few frosts. 

When frosts threaten, oil or coal fires are kept burning in the orchards until the danger is past. They can raise the temperature of the air four or five degrees.

The pineapple is another warm-country fruit that is grown in Florida. 

Truck Farming on the Atlantic Coast.

One of the large industries of the coastal region is truck farming. In most of the truck-growing regions the farmers 

A. Picking grapefruit in Florida. Notice the size of the fruit, and the size of the trees. plant their first crop after the frosts of spring and gather their last crop before the frosts of the fall. The land is under cultivation during almost all of the growing season and the truck farmers are able to raise two or more crops on the same land in one season. 

Most of the vegetables and fruits raised by truck farmers are for sale in the larger cities, especially in the northern cities.

There they bring high prices during the winter months. The first to reach these markets come from southern Florida, a subtropical region usually free from frost during the whole year. Some vegetables and fruits from Florida will be found on sale in northern cities before the end of 

February or early in March. 

Along the Atlantic coast the length of the growing season decreases toward the north. The farther north you go the later it will be in the year that the first crops ripen. Vegetables and fruits of Georgia follow those from Florida.

U. S. Department of Agriculture, Bureau of Plant Industry 

A. A well-tended strawberry field in Florida. There are several hundred plants in each row.

These are followed by the crops of South Carolina, North Carolina, and so on north to New Jersey and Long Island. Before the last of the northern crops have been gathered, the second crops from the far 

South will be coming into the market.

As a result people in the North may have some fresh vegetables and fruits at any season of the year.

This succession of crops from south to north and back to the south again includes peas, beans, celery, lettuce, cabbages, melons, tomatoes, strawberries (A), and many others. When the crops are being harvested, every available worker is kept busy, young and old alike. Many of these crops are so perishable that they must be rushed to market. Fast trains with cars kept at a proper temperature, or ships which make a quick voyage, are used. There are times when loading platforms of railroad freight houses and steamship piers are crowded with crates and baskets of vegetables waiting to be shipped.

The sandy soils of the coastal plain are easily worked, but they are not suited to all crops. 

Grass, corn, and wheat will not grow well on them. They are much better for raising vegetables. That is why this long stretch of sandy soils from Florida to Long Island has become a vast truck-raising region. Even in raising vegetables it is necessary to use fertilizers to furnish the plants a part of their food. 

Each truck farmer selects the crops that he believes will grow best on his land. In some regions most of the truck farmers raise the same crops and very few of them raise more than one or two kinds. This is because they have learned to specialize in the crops for which there will be a demand when they are ripe. Strawberries are grown all along the coast, especially from Virginia to New 

Jersey. A famous tomato-growing region extends along the shores of Chesapeake 

Bay. Melons are grown at their best in 

Delaware and southern New Jersey.

One of the best of the truck-farming regions is the peninsula between Delaware

Bay and Chesapeake Bay. The waters by which it is almost surrounded help to give it an even temperature and to keep it free from frosts until late in the fall. 

This is a region in which tomatoes, potatoes, and sweet potatoes are largely grown. The sweet potato is raised as far north as New Jersey, but it grows best in the South, where the climate is warm in and sunny, with a long growing season.

Much of the crop is used in the South, but a part is sent to northern markets.

In different parts of the truck-growing region so many vegetables are raised that they cannot all be sold as fresh vegetables in city markets. They are, instead, sent to factories to be canned. There are more canning factories in Delaware and

Maryland than elsewhere in the truckfarming regions. Baltimore, Maryland, is the chief center of this industry.

A.-Truck farm in eastern Long Island. 

Near every large city there are market gardens which supply the city people with fresh vegetables for home use. 

They are called market gardens because they are small, like gardens, and are as carefully cultivated as gardens are. For example, there are large areas on Long 

Island (A) where market gardeners raise vegetables and small fruits for the markets of New York City. They are near enough to the city so that, during the growing season, the gardeners can load trucks in the late afternoon, or at night, and make their runs during the night. In this way fresh vegetables reach the city markets so they can be sold in the morning when the people come to buy them. The truck-farming region of the island is toward the eastern end.

There the farms are larger, and most of the potatoes, lettuce, cabbages, cauliflower, and tomatoes grown are sent to market by boat or train. 

Tree Fruits. Among the tree fruits raised on the Atlantic Coastal Plain the oranges and grapefruit of Florida, and the peaches of Georgia, have already been mentioned (page 159 and page 137). 

Peaches, apples, pears, and cherries are raised farther north on the coastal plain. 

The peninsula between Delaware and

Chesapeake bays (map, page 83) is famous for the orchard fruits that grow there, and there are fine orchards of all these fruits in New Jersey.

Tobacco. Study the map on this page. 

Do you remember reading that tobacco is raised in several southern states (page 137)? One of the great tobaccoraising and tobacco-manufacturing regions is in the states of Virginia, North 

Carolina, and South Carolina. Tobacco is grown in the Piedmont as well as in the 

Atlantic Coastal Plain, and some of the large tobacco factories are in the Piedmont.

The tobacco plant is a native of the 

New World, and it was not known in 

Europe or Asia until America was discovered. The raising of tobacco as an industry began in the colonies of Virginia and Maryland.

A. Tobacco field in North Carolina.

In the early days tobacco was used in place of money in these colonies. For a long time it was one of the largest exports to Europe. The ships of those days sailed up the tidal rivers to the farmer's wharf and took his crop of tobacco to London. In time, the growing of tobacco spread into the Carolinas and across the mountains to Kentucky and 

Tennessee. Although mainly cultivated in these southern states, it also grows farther north.

Almost all the tobacco in this country is raised east of the Mississippi River.

North Carolina grows the largest crop, (A) and Kentucky ranks second. These states are followed by Tennessee and 

Virginia in the amount raised. The fine quality of much of the North Carolina tobacco gives it specially high value. 

Some tobacco is grown in western

Florida and is used in the manufacture of cigars; but most of the tobacco used in 

Florida factories comes from the island of Cuba, south of Florida. The chief 

The chief

Florida cities in which tobacco is manufactured are Tampa, on the west coast, and Key West, on one of the small islands of the Florida Keys (page 87). 

Peanuts. The chief peanut-raising region is in North Carolina and Virginia, but this crop is raised also in Alabama and in other parts of the coastal plain. 

The use of peanuts and peanut vines in feeding stock has already been mentioned (page 137). Many peanuts are roasted and eaten out of hand, plain or salted. 

Peanut butter, made from the roasted and ground nuts, is a spread for sandwiches. Peanut oil is used in the manufacture of soap and in cooking. 66 

Oyster Fisheries. One of the interesting and important industries carried on in the Chesapeake and Delaware bays is the raising of oysters, sometimes called oyster farming." The most extensive oyster beds in the United States are those of Chesapeake Bay. A few oysters are still taken from natural beds, but most of them are now raised in places that have been prepared on the bottom of shallow seas. Young 

Young "seed oysters can move about in the water. Each one soon fastens its shell to some other object, such 99

A 

as another shell or a pebble. In preparing a bed for the young seed oysters it is important that the shells or other objects to which they are to fasten should be clean. In our country old oyster shells are chiefly used. In other countries such things as tiles, bundles of sticks, stones, and branches of trees are used. 

Some of the oystermen go out in small boats and gather the oysters from the sea bed with long-handled tongs. In other cases the oysters are scooped off the bed by dredging machines that use a large bucket or a wire netting. 

U. S. Bureau of Fisheries 

See the masts and the large furled sails. 

The oysters are then washed and graded for shipment. Some of the oysters are "shucked" (that is, taken from A.-Fleet of oyster boats at Baltimore. the shell) and canned. A great part of the catch is packed in ice or in refrigerator cars to be sent to near-by markets or to cities that are distant. Baltimore,

Maryland, is the chief center of the oyster industry (A).

A. Part of Jones Beach, a state park on the Atlantic shore of Long Island, 33 miles from New York. water. The Long Island beaches, especially those that can be quickly reached from New York, are crowded with people whenever the water is warm enough for bathing. Those who like surf bathing will go where the ocean waves come in upon a sandy shore. Others will go to resorts on the more quiet waters north of the barrier beach. 

Coney Island and Rockaway Beach at the western end of the island are part of New York City (map, page 175).

They are famous for the many amusements provided for visitors. As they can be reached easily and cheaply by bus or railroad, many thousands of people visit them during the summer, especially on hot days and evenings. 

On the map (page 154), follow the shore line from New York to Delaware 

Bay. Along this shore you will notice that there are long, low, barrier beaches like those along the shore of Long Island. 

At the northern end of this line of beaches is Sandy Hook, which reaches out into the Lower Bay of New York. Along the New Jersey coast the water behind these beaches forms lagoons. Little by little the lagoons fill with mud as the tide goes in and out and the water plants grow and decay. 

See the names along the shore: Long 

Branch, Asbury Park, Ocean Grove, and 

Atlantic City. These and many smaller places (A) are pleasure resorts to which people go to enjoy the cool breezes from the sea and the pleasures of surf bathing. The greatest of these is Atlantic

City. The lagoon which was west of it has been nearly all filled, and the railroad leads to the city without crossing any large body of water. large body of water. Most of the visitors to Atlantic City and to the other pleasure resorts along the New Jersey shore are there for rest and recreation. There are more than a thousand hotels in Atlantic

City. Some of them, built where they look out over the open Atlantic, are very large and very luxurious. Others are much smaller and less expensive. 

Great piers, used for exhibits and other entertainments, run out into the ocean.

A. 

Part of the harbor at Jacksonville. See the freight trains, the ships, and the lumber on the wharf. 

There are ten miles of board walk along | the beach (155 A). It is built above the level of the sands so it can be used even when the winds drive the waves far up on the shore.

On the land side of the board walk are shops, restaurants, and the entrances to the great hotels. The board walk itself on a sunny day is filled with a happy crowd of people on a holiday. Many Many walk, but others hire wheeled chairs, each with a man to push it about. 

Atlantic City is but one hour by railroad from Philadelphia, and three hours from New York. From these two cities with their many millions of people, and from other places all over the United 

States, people come to Atlantic City and other resorts on the New Jersey shore. 

Some Famous Winter Resorts. In contrast with the summer resorts along the northern coast of the Atlantic Coastal 

Plain, Florida is a winter playground.

The larger and more famous winter resorts are on or near the coast. Winter residents of southern Florida have the choice of many fine hotels, and can enjoy many outdoor sports, including boating, ocean bathing, and deep-sea fishing.

Jacksonville, in the northeast, is on the wide, deep inlet from the sea called St. 

Johns River. It is a railroad center and the chief seaport on the Atlantic side of 

Florida (A). 

South of Jacksonville, about an hour by train, is St. Augustine. It was founded by Spaniards in 1565, and is the oldest city in the United States. It has many visitors who enjoy the narrow streets, the interesting old buildings, and the lovely flower gardens of this fine old city. 

Farther south is Palm Beach, one of the more fashionable of the Florida resorts.

It has miles of streets along which you can see the homes of people who live there only during the winter season.

Still farther south on the east coast are 

Miami and Miami Beach.

A. - Tampa, Florida. The great building surrounded by a park is a famous hotel. Other tall buildings show that 

Tampa, like many other southern cities, is increasing in industrial importance. 

Tampa (A), on the west coast, has al- | ready been mentioned (page 147). It is one of two important Florida seaports on the Gulf of Mexico, the other one being 

Pensacola. Across the bay from Tampa is St. Petersburg, a resort city to which many thousands go every winter for rest and pleasure. —- Transportation. The visitor to Florida has the choice of several ways of making the trip. From New York City and other Atlantic ports the trip may be made on fine ocean-going steamers. Those who go by automobile may travel over splendid roads from almost any part of our country. Airplanes may be used; there are excellent landing fields in several 

Florida cities. At Miami the airplanes carrying mail to South America and 

Central America take off. Fast express trains with sleeping cars, dining car, and observation car may be taken from any of the larger northern cities to all important points in Florida. 

One railroad runs the entire length of the peninsula not far from the eastern coast. At the southern end it continues along the line of the Florida Keys to Key

West, crossing the water between the islands over low bridges. 

From Key West ships and airplanes make trips to Havana in Cuba.

It is interesting to notice that in many parts of Florida the houses have been built in a Spanish style. This is partly because that style of building is suitable for hot countries. Many of them, especially in the south, have no heating stoves or furnaces, for it is seldom cold enough to make heating necessary.

Other Cities of the Coastal Plain. 

Locate, on the map, Savannah, Georgia; 

Charleston, South Carolina; and Wilmington, North Carolina. All three of these cities are ports. Each one ships much cotton and lumber, and Savannah ships naval stores. Among the industries of Wilmington are the canning of vegetables and the pressing of oil from cotton seed. In each of these cities there are beautiful old southern homes on lovely shaded streets. Many of these homes, with their wide porches, their hanging balconies, and their large rooms and great staircases, really deserve the name of mansions. 

On Hampton Roads, formed by the broad mouth of the James River where it joins Chesapeake Bay, are the cities and harbors of Norfolk, Newport News, and 

Portsmouth. Norfolk and Newport News are visited by many steamers trading along our coast, and by steamships from many foreign countries. From these WP 

-10 cities are shipped early vegetables, peanuts, tobacco, and cotton, as well as coal from the mines of West Virginia. Among the imports are nitrate from South America and potash from Europe. These are used in the factories of Portsmouth in the manufacture of fertilizers. At Portsmouth there is a very large United States navy yard. Newport News has a large shipbuilding industry (A). All three cities have good railroad connections.

mond has become an important railroad center, and is the chief trading center of a large region including parts of the On the map (page 80) trace the Fall 

Line from New York to northern Ala-Atlantic Coastal Plain and the Piedmont. bama. Notice on page 131 the large cities that are located along this line. 

Do not think of the Fall Line as a sharp line dividing two natural regions that are very different from each other. It is instead a belt of country of varying width. In crossing it there are places where you would go gradually from the flat land of the Coastal Plain into the hilly land of the Piedmont. Where the rivers have dug valleys across the Fall Line, the change from the sands and clays of the Coastal Plain to the hard rocks under the Piedmont may be very sudden.

In most of the rivers there are rapids or waterfalls at this point. The power furnished by these falls, and their situation at the head of tidewater, led to the early location of settlements along the Fall 

Line. A number of these settlements have grown into important manufacturing and trading centers.

Fall Line Cities in the South. Three cities on the Fall Line in Georgia are

Columbus, Macon, and Augusta. All of these cities have large cotton mills. 

Columbia, the capital of South Carolina, and Raleigh, the capital of North Carolina, are two other Fall Line cities engaged in manufacturing cotton. Columbia also manufactures fertilizer. 

Richmond, the capital of Virginia, is at the head of navigation on the James River. It was first settled at a time when ocean ships were small, and it carried on an important trade with Europe. The larger ships of today cannot reach the city, but some freight is still carried over the river route by small boats. Rich- 

Because of the nearness of Richmond to some of the tobacco-growing regions, the manufacture of tobacco has long been an important industry there.

Washington. -Turn to the map on page 131 and find the District of Columbia. It is between Virginia and Maryland. The boundaries of the District of 

Columbia and of the city of Washington are the same. This place was selected for the capital of our country in 1790, and became the capital ten years later. 

It is not a part of any state. It is governed under the authority of the Congress of the United States. 

Washington is so near the Fall Line that it is usually thought of as a Fall 

Line city. The Falls of the Potomac are really a short distance northwest of the city. When the city was planned and the places for the public buildings were chosen, they were some distance from one another. For that reason the city was called called "the city of magnificent distances." Since that time the spaces between these first public buildings have been filled in by other government buildings and private ones. Washington is now a city of a half million people. 

Thousands of visitors also come on business or to see the national capital and enjoy its beauties.

Among its great buildings and monuments are the Capitol and the White House. The Capitol is the large building with the great dome (169 A) where

Congress meets to make the laws for our country. The White House is the home of our President, and his offices are

A.- Airplane view of Washington. The Capitol has a large dome, and the Congressional Library a small dome. 

The large building at the right, and another like it at the left, contain the office rooms of members of Congress. in a connected building. The White 

House is a very small and modest building compared with the great rambling palaces in which the rulers of many countries of Europe live. 

Opposite the Capitol is the great 

Library of Congress. Here are millions of books, pamphlets, manuscripts, and maps. Here are kept the original copies of the Declaration of Independence and the Constitution of the United States. 

They are carefully protected, for they are two of the most famous documents in the history of our country. 

Near the Potomac River are the Washington Monument and the Lincoln 

Memorial. Across the river in Virginia is Arlington National Cemetery, where are the graves of the American Unknown 

Soldier, many other soldiers, and some of America's most distinguished men. 

Washington does not owe its growth to its location, to its trade, or to its manufacturing. It has few manufactures, and its trade meets only the daily needs of its people. It is very different in this way from other large cities. 

Washington has grown because it is the capital of a great country. Many officers of the government and many thousand workers in the different government departments make their homes in Washington. Most foreign countries have representatives living in Washington to look after the interests of their governments and their people. 

A little farther down the Potomac River is the old home of George Washington, called Mount Vernon. Thousands of people visit it every year. The house is furnished and kept up much as it was when Washington lived there.

Baltimore and Wilmington. 

One of the great Fall Line cities is Baltimore, a seaport on a tidal river near the head of Chesapeake Bay (map, page 131). It is one of the leading commercial and manufacturing cities of our country. In this city live nearly half of the people of Maryland, and it is the largest city in the South Atlantic group of states.

The harbor of Baltimore is large and the water is deep enough for ocean steamships to dock. Railroad connections reach all parts of our country. To Baltimore come articles for shipment to other ports on the Atlantic and Gulf coasts and to many foreign countries. 

Goods received by steamship are distributed over the railroads to trading centers in the different parts of the United States. 

The chief manufactures of Baltimore are clothing, metal goods, and fertilizers. 

Shipbuilding and the canning of fruit, vegetables, and oysters are also important (163 A). Where do the canning factories get their raw materials? (Pages 

160 and 163.) 

Johns Hopkins University, one of the great universities of our country, is in Baltimore.

Delaware has one large city, Wilmington (map, page 154). About half the people of the state live there. Its industries include shipbuilding and the manufacture of heavy machinery, paints, and such explosives as dynamite and gunpowder and all kinds of ammunition. 

Philadelphia. Philadelphia is on the tidal part of the Delaware River and can be reached by large ocean steamships (map, page 154). It has a population of about 2,000,000. It is larger than any other city in our country except 

New York and Chicago. 

Independence Hall is in Philadelphia.

It was in this small red brick building that the Congress of 1776 met to declare the colonies free from British rule. Here is preserved the "Liberty Bell," which rang loud and long when the Declaration of Independence was first read to the people of Philadelphia. When the war of the Revolution was over, it was in 

Philadelphia that the Constitution of the

United States was written. This city was the capital of the United States from 1790 to 1800. The University of 

Pennsylvania is in Philadelphia.

Among the many industries of Philadelphia are manufactures of wool, cotton, leather, and iron, and the refining of sugar and petroleum. The crude oil is brought by pipe lines from the oil regions (143 B). Raw sugar comes from Cuba (A). Hides and skins, from which leather is made, come from many countries. 

Wool brought from distant lands is made into carpets and rugs. Cloth, clothing, and knit goods are made.

Coal and iron come from the mountain and plateau regions not far away. 

At Philadelphia 

At Philadelphia there are shipyards, plants where steel for bridge building is prepared, and the largest plant in the world for building cars and locomotives. A. 

Courtesy Dept. of Wharves, Docks, and Ferries, Philadelphia

Raw sugar on a pier in Philadelphia, unloaded from a steamer which brought it from Cuba. The bags number many thousands; each weighs 325 pounds.

A. 

Business center of Philadelphia, looking northeast. In the distance is the Delaware River, and beyond it, at the right, part of Camden. Notice carefully the piers in the river. How many do you see? For an inside view of the upper deck of one pier, see 170 A.

The location of Philadelphia gives it many commercial advantages. As a seaport it takes part in the coastwise trade with cities of the Atlantic and Gulf ports.

It is connected by railroads with all the large eastern cities. To the west the city is connected by a great railroad system with Pittsburgh, and through Pittsburgh with all the large cities of the West and 

Southwest. Near Philadelphia, too, is a rich industrial and agricultural region for which the great city is a trading center. 

Across the river from Philadelphia is 

Camden in New Jersey. It has large shipbuilding yards, tans and manufactures leather, and makes pens, talking machines, and radios. 

Trenton. - Trenton (map, page 154) is the capital of the state of New Jersey. 

It has water power, large pottery works, and rubber factories. More pottery is made in New Jersey than in any other state in our country except Ohio. Of that made in New Jersey, Trenton makes over half. It has factories in which earthenware, white granite ware, and very fine and delicate china are made.

Much of the clay and other raw materials used in this industry are brought from other states.

NEW YORK CITY 

The first settlement on Manhattan 

Island at the mouth of the Hudson River was made by the Dutch. They called it 

New Amsterdam. Later it was taken from the Dutch by the English and was renamed New York. At that time it was only a small village at the southern end of Manhattan Island. Now it is the largest city in the world, except London in England. 

Size and Population. The city of 

New York includes five of the counties of the state, with an area of about 308 square miles. It includes Manhattan 

Island, Staten Island, the western end of

Long Island, and a part of the mainland south of Yonkers. In 1930 there were about 7,000,000 people living in the city.

Manhattan Island is more densely settled than other parts of the city. 

You may

A Visit to New York. 

already know many things about New 

York City. You may sometime visit it.

In visiting the city there are two things that you can do to help you to understand how large it is and how important it is to the people of the world. 

One thing to do is to go to the top of some very high building (A) on a clear day and study the city that lies spread out below you. The other is to take a ride around Manhattan Island in a sightseeing boat. 

Piers and Docks. Starting from the southern end of the island, most visitors take boats that begin the trip by turning east. You will find it more interesting, however, to go north first, up the Hudson 

River. Soon after starting you will see the piers that have been built out into the river from both its banks. Heavy supports or piles are driven into the river bottom. The tops of these are left standing several feet above the water so that they will not be covered even at high tide. On them a floor is laid down to make the pier, and on most piers a building has been put up a warehouse in which to store goods that have been shipped to the city.

Between the piers there is open water reaching to the river bank. These water spaces between the piers are the docks

Ships coming to New York to load or unload usually enter one of the docks and tie up alongside one of the piers. As your boat goes on up the river you will see ships in the docks, for there are always many ships in the docks of New York. 

Ships and Boats. You may be fortunate enough to see a great passenger steamer starting for Europe. The passengers will be standing on the decks waving good-by to friends who are waving to them from the pier. Slowly the steamer backs out into the stream. 

Powerful little tugboats push at its sides to keep it going right. At last it is out of the dock and away from the piers. The tugs swing it around so that it heads down the river toward the ocean. A journey across the Atlantic Ocean has begun. 

Or you may see a great steamship dock- 

A. An ocean liner arriving at its dock. See the four many tall buildings, or "skyscrapers," in the background. ing (A). Up the river it comes until it tugboats helping it into place; the ship in the next dock; is opposite its dock. Tugboats swarm about it. Great ropes are taken ashore, looped over a post, and used to help pull the ship into its dock. Passengers are on the decks. Friends on the pier wave to welcome them home. them home. At last by 

At last by pulling on the ropes, and with the help of the tugs, the steamer is worked into its dock and its passengers are landed. 

As your little boat continues on its way you see ferryboats crossing the river carrying passengers, trucks, and automobiles between New York and the cities on the New Jersey side of the river. You see barges loaded with brick, crushed stone, coal, or wheat. There may be flatboats carrying freight cars from the railroads on one side of the river to railroads on the other side. You will pass many docks where freight steamers from all parts of the world are loading or unloading their cargoes. 

Bridges and Boroughs. Toward the northern end of Manhattan Island your boat will pass under the George Washington Bridge (174 A). Shortly after that you reach the place where the Harlem 

River connects with the Hudson. It is this narrow winding body of water, really a strait, that separates Manhattan Island from the mainland part of New York state. It is crossed by many bridges. 

At its eastern end the Harlem joins the East River, which is a broader strait connecting Long Island Sound with New 

York Bay. On the map, page 175, the course of the Harlem River is shown by the boundary between Manhattan and the Bronx. 

The city of New York is made up of five counties. Counties are divisions of a state for purposes of government.

One group

On both sides of the East River, especially toward its southern end, you will pass many piers and docks, and you will see ships loading and unloading. Then soon your boat brings you again to the southern end of Manhattan Island. esting things you would see in a boat trip around Manhattan Island.

Hudson (here often called the North River) - make the harbor of New York. 

New York harbor is one of the finest harbors in the world. Channels have been dredged so that the largest ships can use it. Within the harbor ships are protected from strong winds and high waves. Ridgewood Paterson
