Metropolitan District. -Several thickly settled regions are near the city of New 

York. One of them is beyond the western boundary of New York City, in New Jersey, where there is a large group of cities and villages with a combined population of about two million. Another of these regions is north and northeast of the Bronx. It includes Yonkers, Mt. 

Vernon, New Rochelle, and a number of other places in near-by New York and 

Connecticut. A third thickly settled region near New York City is east of 

Queens in Long Island. Adding all these 

Adding all these regions together, you get an area of 2541 square miles, called the Metropolitan

District, with a population of about 11,000,000. 

Why should 11,000,000 people gather together at or near the mouth of the Hudson River? One reason is that New York

City is a great trading center. It carries on trade not only with all parts of our own country but with all parts of the world. A second reason is that New 

York is a great manufacturing city. 

Trade. The people of our country buy many things that come from other parts of the world. Our people also have or make things that the people of other countries want. countries want. Goods brought into our country are imports. Goods sent out of our country are exports. More than a third of all the imports and about a third of the exports of our country enter or leave the country through the port of New York.

It takes a great many ships to carry all of these imports and exports across the ocean. When you took the trip around 

Manhattan Island you saw ships from all parts of the world loading or unloading.

The imports that they bring will be distributed to all our larger cities, and from them to smaller villages and country stores. The exports that these same ships will take away must be gathered from all parts of our country. 

Over what routes are the imports distributed and the goods to be exported gathered together? Railroads from New

England and from the central, western, and southern parts of our country center in New York (maps, pages 254, 259). 

Freight boats sail to and from ports on the Atlantic and Gulf coasts. Even the barges on the Barge Canal have some part of this work to do. The greatest of all these trade routes is the one from the interior of our country that reaches New 

York through the valleys of the Mohawk and Hudson rivers. This is the route of the New York Central Railroad.

The New York Central passenger sta tion is at 42nd Street in midtown New 

York. Its freight lines and stations are on the west side of Manhattan Island along the Hudson. The passenger station at 42nd Street is used also by the 

New York, New Haven, and Hartford 

Railroad, one of the great railroads of

New England. The Pennsylvania Rail road enters New York by a tunnel under the Hudson River. Its station is on the western side of Manhattan between 31st 

Street and 33rd Street. This station is used also by the trains of the Lehigh Valley Railroad, and by trains of the 

Long Island Railroad which come through a tunnel under the East River. 

Several other railroads from the west and south end on the New Jersey side of the Hudson. Their passengers may reach 

New York by ferry, or by motor busses or electric trains through tunnels under the river. 

Manufactures. No other city in our country equals New York in the value and variety of its manufactures. You will remember that manufacturing calls for (1) raw materials, (2) power, (3) labor, and (4) markets. By rail and steamer any raw materials needed can be brought to New York for use in its factories.

Coal from the mines of Pennsylvania, fuel oil and gasoline from tanks and refineries in New Jersey, and electric generating stations all furnish power. The population is large enough to furnish the needed labor and a great market. By rail and steamer the manufactures can be sent to markets in all parts of the world. 

The chief manufacturing industry is the making of clothing. New York makes far more clothing than any other city in our country. The industry in 

New York City includes the making of suits, overcoats, dresses, hats, underclothing, and fur coats and neckpieces.

The industry second in importance in 

New York City is the preparation of foodstuffs. This includes the refining of sugar; the making of flour, bakery products, and candy; the slaughtering of animals and the preparation of meats; the grinding of spices; and the roasting of coffee. 

Printing is another great industry of 

New York. More books, magazines, and newspapers are printed there than in any other city. Among the

Among the many other manufactures of New York are cigars, cigarettes, and sheet metal work.

This avenue is nearly always crowded with A.-Fifth Avenue, New York, looking north toward Central Park. taxicabs, busses, and other automobiles. 

Business and Buildings. Two other facts should be kept in mind as helping to explain why there are so many people in New York. (1) Nearly every great business in the world has an office or agent in New York to look after the distribution of its products or the purchase of supplies. (2) Where many people are gathered together there must be stores, office buildings, schools and colleges, churches, theaters, and hotels. There must be ways for people to travel quickly.

There must be banks where money can be deposited and borrowed. 

To carry on any business there must be managers, clerks, salesmen, stenographers, and bookkeepers. For the larger buildings there must be renting agents, elevator men, machinists, and cleaners.

Schools and colleges must have teachers and caretakers. There must be men to operate taxicabs, busses, street cars, and the electric trains that run on tracks above the street or in the tunnels, or subways, under the streets and under the rivers. 

There must be firemen, policemen, postmen, doctors, lawyers, and engineers. 

As the business of the great city grows, so does its population. In former days many workers came from foreign countries as immigrants and settled in the city near others who spoke their language. 

There are parts of New York City where nearly all the buildings are occupied by groups of foreign people. As the population of the city grows, land becomes more and more valuable and small buildings are torn down and larger ones put in their places.

Many great office buildings more than twenty stories in height have been built. 

Some are more than forty stories high.

The largest of these are in the southern and midtown sections of Manhattan (174 A). The great banks also have their main offices in southern Manhattan, but several of them have branches scattered throughout the city.

Ewing Galloway 

A.-Looking north from Washington Square, New York.

At the left is the Washington Arch, at the southern end of Fifth Avenue. At the right are several old residences, contrasting with the modern apartment hotel behind them. 

There are stores and warehouses in New 

York where goods are sold wholesale; that is, to dealers, and usually in large quantities at a time. There are thousands of small stores, especially in residential districts, where goods are sold at retail; that is, to consumers, usually in small quantities. Other retail stores are very much larger in size, some of the largest and finest being in the midtown district of Manhattan. Each of these department stores, as they are called, is really many separate stores under one roof and under one management. 

New York is a center of education and culture. It has famous museums, art galleries, and many colleges. Here, too, are Columbia University, New York 

University, and Fordham University. 

Where do the millions who work in the city live? Many thousands live in the suburbs that is, in the smaller cities and villages in or near the Metropolitan District. They go to the city each workday morning and back to their homes at night. Traveling for so long a distance between home and work is called commuting. Those who do so are commuters. 

Those who live in the city may live in large or small apartment houses. Apartment buildings are usually from five to thirty or more stories high. An apartment may have from one to twenty or more rooms, and the rooms may be large or small. In the larger buildings (A) there are usually several separate apartments on each floor. Each apartment opens on a public hall, but it is entirely separated from all the others.

Many apartment houses, especially the poorer ones, are called tenement houses.

In the high apartments, hotels, and office buildings, elevators take people up and down, and leave them or take them on at any floor. In the very tall buildings the higher floors are reached by express elevators that do not stop at any of the lower floors except the ground floor.

2. New York leads all other cities as a trading and manufacturing center. 

3. One of New York's great problems is that of transporting people quickly between their homes and the places where they work.

THE PIEDMONT 

Turn again to the map on page 80, and trace the boundaries of the Piedmont 

Plateau. Make a list of the states, from 

New York to Alabama, through which this region extends. 

The Piedmont is higher toward the south and lower toward the north. On the east is the Fall Line about which you have been reading. On the west is the Appalachian Mountain and Valley region which you are soon to study. 

Your map shows the Piedmont extending into New England; but you will study New England as a separate region. 

The Piedmont of the Middle Atlantic

States. North of Manhattan Island is the part of New York City called the 

Bronx (page 174). Much of the Bronx and a small part of New York state to the north is a region of low, well-rounded hills. It is a part of the Piedmont

Plateau (map, page 80) which extends south across northern New Jersey and southeastern Pennsylvania. In both of these states the country is higher and more hilly than the Atlantic Coastal 

Plain. One of the New Jersey ridges forms a high rocky wall on the west side of the Hudson River. The ridge is called the Palisades (183 A).

Cities of the Northern Piedmont. 

Just north of New York is the large city of Yonkers. Many people who live there work in the city of New York, but many others work in their own city. Among the goods manufactured in Yonkers are carpets and rugs, felt hats, elevators, and rubber goods. There are also sugar refineries and machine shops. The New 

Jersey cities nearest New York City are 

Hoboken, Jersey City, and Bayonne 

National Jewelers Publicity Ass'n, Newark, N. J.

A. In a factory where watch cases are made. Each case is carefully inspected before shipment. (map, page 175). They lie along the lower southern part of the Palisades ridge. 

Here, on the west bank of the Hudson 

River, most of the railroads from the southern, western, and northern parts of our country have their Atlantic coast terminals. Several of the great Atlantic steamship lines have their docks, piers, and storage warehouses on the New 

Jersey side of the Hudson. 

More crude oil, or petroleum, is refined in Bayonne than in any other place in the world. The oil comes to it through pipe lines from most of the principal oil fields of the United States and by tank ships from other oil regions. Jersey City has slaughtering and meat-packing plants and soap factories. At Hoboken ships and ships' engines are built and repaired. 

Newark, the largest city in New Jersey, is a great manufacturing center with many different industries. Among its important manufactures are electrical machinery and supplies, paints and varnishes, leather, jewelry, chemicals, and meat products.

It is a railroad center, and is one of the great airports of the eastern United

States. Newark is developing as a seaport by deepening and widening a channel to the sea and building piers. 

Paterson's great industry is the manufacture of silk, including the steps of weaving, dyeing, and finishing. Passaic also manufactures textiles. Perth Amboy has a good harbor and shipyards. There are drydocks where ships can be cleaned or repaired. Elizabeth manufactures half of the sewing machines made in this country, and other articles of iron and steel, including wire. Both Elizabeth and Perth Amboy smelt and refine copper, and through these cities much coal from Pennsylvania is shipped. In the forested upland region of northern New 

Jersey the cities of the Piedmont find a source of pure water. 

Southeastern Pennsylvania. — On the map (page 80) find the Piedmont region of southeastern Pennsylvania. Locate the same region on the larger map on page 154. The country is quite hilly, but it has an excellent soil and is one of the richest farm regions in the United States.

Some of the farmers there are descendants of the early Quaker settlers, and many are descendants of Germans who first settled in that region. They are noted for their industry and thrift, and for their good houses and barns, well-cultivated fields, and fine herds of cattle.

Among the crops grown are wheat, corn, vegetables, fruits, and tobacco.

Most of the corn is used as food for hogs and cattle. Some fruits and vegetables are sold fresh in the markets, and others are sold to canneries. There are many dairy farms, especially near the large cities, and on other farms tobacco is the one great crop. Most of the farmers, however, carry on mixed farming. 

Lancaster, west of Philadelphia and almost in the center of this Pennsylvania section of the Piedmont, is one of the chief trading centers. It has stockyards that are among the largest in the country. Its many factories get electric power from a great power plant on the 

Susquehanna River, 25 miles to the south. 

Farming from Maryland to Georgia.

The mixed-farming region of southeastern Pennsylvania is continued through the Piedmont in other states southward to Georgia; but the farms are not all as productive, the farmers are not always as prosperous, and the crops are not always the same. As in other mixedfarming regions, there are usually several market crops, and other crops for home use and farm animals. In the Piedmont region of Maryland and Virginia, as well as in Pennsylvania, there are large, wellkept apple orchards from which fine fruit is sent to our own and foreign markets.

The tobacco district of Pennsylvania is continued southward through Maryland, southern Virginia, and the Carolinas.

Tobacco is a crop that takes from the soil a large amount of plant food that must be put back again by the use of fertilizers. In the southern part of the 

Piedmont the money crop is cotton. 

Farming as a Business. You have learned much about the crops grown in different parts of our country, and there is much more to learn. This is a good time for you to think of farming farmers think of it - as a business. as 

To be successful, the farmer must know many things about his farm and about the crops and live stock that he raises. He must choose the crops that will grow well on the soils of his farm. 

They must be the kind that will ripen

THE PIEDMONT 

181 

A. Plowing with a tractor-drawn plow that turns two furrows at once. Many farmers use a single plow drawn by a team of horses. during the growing season. There must be enough but not too much rainfall, and it must come at the right time of the year. 

In planning and caring for the crops the farmer must know the kind of fertilizer to use, how much to use, and when to use it.

He must know when fruit trees or crops must be sprayed, and what sprays to use. In selecting his crops he will take those that are planted at different times and harvested at different times so as to spread his work as much as possible. 

He must know what foods his farm animals need, when they should be fed, and how to care for them.

When there is no work to be done in the fields, there will be roads, buildings, and fences to repair, and trees to cut for lumber, fence posts, and firewood. The housewife is as busy in the house as the farmer in his fields. Farming is not a life for the idler.

The farmer has his rewards. In the more prosperous farming regions many of the homes are fine old mansions. They are set in the midst of great trees whose spreading branches give a welcome shade when the summer sun is high and hot.

There are outbuildings and barns for housing the animals and farm machinery, and for the storage of crops. All about are the meadows, the pastures, and the cultivated fields.

In the spring time there is the fresh green of trees and meadows and pastures, the brown earth of plowed fields (A), and the pink and white of blossoming orchards. Later each growing crop will take on its own color and over all there will rest the haze of the summer. Then comes autumn, when the crops are gathered. Once more the fields are bare and, with the frost, the foliage becomes golden and russet and red.

There are also smaller, less stately homes, with smaller farms and fewer outbuildings. But there also you will find the patterns of the fields and the same beauty of coloring. For every one, rich and poor alike, there are the beautiful views from high places and the lovely streams winding among the hills.

Cities of the Southern Piedmont. 

All of the Piedmont cities are trading centers in which the farmers of the surrounding country can buy their supplies and sell their farm products. For many years the cities of the Piedmont from

southeastern Pennsylvania to Georgia | Appalachian Mountains. Because it is had little manufacturing. The tobacco and cotton crops furnished some raw materials, but these were sent to factories in cities more favorably located, especially to those along the Fall Line at the head of tidewater. 

In recent years manufacturing in the Piedmont cities has been increasing.

Throughout this region there are many waterfalls, large and small. Plants for generating electricity have been built at a number of the larger falls. As you know, electrical power can be sent over wires from the generating stations to places many miles away. In the Piedmont cities it is used for electric lighting, transportation, and running the machinery of cotton mills, aluminum plants, furniture factories, and ice-making plants. 

Great power lines unite these generating stations with each other, and the combined flow of power can be distributed to cities and factories as needed. 

One of the interesting new industries that is developing in the Piedmont is the use of electricity to get aluminum from its ore. 

Some bauxite (page 146) is found in Georgia, Alabama, and Tennessee, but most of the aluminum is made from the bauxite from Arkansas.

The manufacture of tobacco is carried on in Winston-Salem and Durham, North

Carolina. There are cotton mills in 

Charlotte and other Piedmont cities, especially in the Carolinas and Georgia. 

The great Piedmont city of the South is Atlanta, the capital of Georgia. It is sometimes called the "Gate City." Let us see why. It is located where the railroads from the northeastern, southern, and central parts of our country meet as they swing round the southern end of the so well located to control the trade of the eastern section of our southern states, many northern business houses have offices in Atlanta. To Atlanta come such raw materials as cotton and lumber, and among its manufactures are cotton goods, furniture, fertilizers, paper, and machinery. Coal from mines in the southern Appalachians, and electricity from a great power plant only 15 miles from the city, furnish light and power.

THE APPALACHIAN HIGHLANDS

After this has been done you will study the industries of the two regions combined. That is because several of the great industries are carried on in different parts of the highland and it is easier to study them all at the same time. This is especially true of coal and iron mining and the manufacture of iron and steel.

Conservation Department, State of New York 

A. Mount Marcy, the highest peak in the state of New York. It is 5344 feet high- just a little over a mile. 

Mt. Mitchell in North Carolina is considerably higher. 

The hills were once much higher, but they have been worn down by the work of heat and cold, and the work of winds, and rains, and running water. They are a very old part of the Appalachians. 

From Carlisle, Pennsylvania, a single low mountain range, called South Mountain, extends southward into Maryland. Before studying the higher mountains in 

Maryland, Virginia, and farther south, some mention should be made of the 

Adirondack Mountains of northern New York. 

The Adirondacks. This mass of highlands is a region of many peaks but with no ranges. The rivers flow out from the central and higher parts to the surrounding lowlands, and across these to lakes and larger streams. This kind of drainage is called radial drainage.

The rocks that form the Adirondacks are among the oldest of our continent, and they are very hard. The rock layers are not horizontal as you will find them in some parts of the Appalachian Highlands. They have been twisted and crumpled and broken. The highest peak is Mt. Marcy (A), 5344 feet high. The slopes of this and other peaks are steep and rough and covered with forests. 

There are hundreds of lakes in the 

Adirondacks, many of which are not shown on the map (page 154). You will remember that the ice sheet covered most of New York state. It ground off the tops of the hills, making them lower and more smoothly rounded. When the ice melted, some of the valleys were partly filled with sand, gravel, and clay, washed into them from the melting ice.

At the same time many stones called bowlders were left scattered over the fields. 

Lake basins were formed because the moving ice dug parts of some old valleys deeper, and blocked other valleys with earth and stones. After the ice melted

away, many old stream valleys were so blocked that the streams flowed in new channels. In doing so they cut gorges and formed waterfalls. 

Most of the lakes of the Adirondacks are there because of the hollows dug by the ice sheet and because the material left by the melting ice made dams across the many stream channels. 

You have read about the summer and winter resorts of the Atlantic Coastal

Plain (pages 163-166). Many people like to go to the mountains instead of to the seashore. There are a large number of resorts in the Adirondacks as well as in other parts of the Appalachian Highlands.

From the tops of the higher mountains you can get wonderful views. On a clear day you can look down into near-by valleys, or a long, long way across the forestcovered peaks and slopes.

A climb to the top of a mountain may take hours of hard work. The state of 

New York is building a road to the top of Whiteface," one of the Adirondack peaks (A), as a memorial to the men of the state who died in the World War. On this road those who have never been able to climb mountains may ride to the top to see the views. Hunters and campers make their way deep into the forests or to the shores of some small lake hidden away far from road or trail. There, where the air is cool and sweet, they find delight in boating, swimming, fishing, and hunting; and they get restful sleep. 

One reason why the Adirondack Mountains are a delightful region in which to spend a vacation is because they are cool even in the summer. This is largely because of their height. You have learned that there is a decrease in tem- 

Courtesy New York Central Lines 

A.- Whiteface Mountain, as seen from Lake Placid. This mountain is almost entirely covered by forest. perature of about one degree for every 

300 feet increase in elevation (page 158).

When people in the lowlands are suffering from heat those in the mountains are comfortably cool.

The short, heavy rains of the summer help to keep the air fresh, the trees growing, the streams flowing, and the lakes full. But there are long dry periods, so that much of the time the climate is not only cool, but dry. This has led many people who have lung trouble to go to the 

Adirondacks to recover their strength and health.

In the winter time, snow gathers on the mountains and in the valleys. More and more people are going to these mountains each winter to enjoy such sports as skating, snowshoeing, tobogganing, bobsledding, and skiing. Winter temperatures often go to zero or below zero. 

Waterfalls furnish power for the development of electricity. There is some lumbering, and a little mining of iron ore. 

There are large mills in several places engaged in the manufacture of wood pulp and paper.

A.-Part of the Pisgah National Forest, south of Asheville, North Carolina. These mountains are a part of one of the ranges of the Blue Ridge and are largely covered with a fine growth of trees. 

Blue Ridge. After this study of the 

Adirondacks turn to the map on page 131 and find the mountain range called the 

Blue Ridge. It is 1000 feet high in 

Maryland and rises to 4000 feet as it crosses the northern boundary of North

Carolina. It gets its name from the color it has when seen from a distance. 

For much of its length this ridge of mountains is a barrier to trade between the Piedmont on the southeast and the Great Valley to the northwest. The

Potomac River has cut a gap across the ridge at Harpers Ferry, which is just west of the point where the states of Maryland, Virginia, and West Virginia meet.

Farther south other rivers have cut gaps across the Blue Ridge near Lynchburg and near Roanoke. These gaps are used by the railroads and the highways which connect the coastal plain with the Valley of Virginia.

It is in North Carolina that these old mountains have their greatest height and width, and their finest forests and mountain peaks. Find Mt. Mitchell, and compare its height with that of Mt. 

Marcy in New York (184 A). Here the 

Blue Ridge widens out into several ranges (A). Among these are the Great Smoky 

Mountains, the Unakas, and the mountains surrounding the Asheville basin, of which Mt. Mitchell is one of the peaks.

The hardwood forests are most extensive in North Carolina, but are found in all the states that reach into the mountains. The common kinds of trees in these forests are oak, ash, maple, chestnut, hickory, black walnut, and birch.

A large amount of lumber is produced. 

In some places the forests have been entirely cut away. On the slopes thus left bare, the heavy rains have cut gullies and washed away much soil (187 B).

Courtesy Norfolk and Western Railway Co. 

A. Part of the Great Valley of Virginia, west of Roanoke. Notice the railroad, the fields, and the forests. 

In such regions, after each rainstorm, the water flows into the streams very quickly, making destructive floods down the river valleys. The states of North Carolina and Tennessee have joined with the national government in buying land for a forest reservation in order to save some of the forests, protect valleys from floods, and maintain water power. 

Asheville, in North Carolina, is 2200 feet above the sea. This means cooler summers for Asheville than for most of the South, and this city is a great summer resort.

In Virginia you are in the Valley of Virginia, sometimes called the Shenandoah Valley. Look on the map, page 131, for the Shenandoah River. It flows from the southwest to join the Potomac in the gap at Harpers Ferry. Broad and beautiful farm lands border the river for a long distance. Here are seen fine apple 

Here are seen fine apple orchards and wheat fields. This valley has become a popular route of travel, both by train and by automobile. 

In the valley there are thick beds of limestone. Water slowly dissolves them, and this is one reason why there is a valley. Water follows cracks down into this underground limestone, where it dissolves the rock and forms great caverns.

You learned about one of these in Kentucky (page 153). In Virginia also there are such caverns at Luray and many other places. Water with lime in it dripping from the roof of a cavern may evaporate and leave the lime as a stalactite. 

In places a stream may flow for some distance underground in a cavern, wearing away the bottom and sides. When most of the roof falls in, leaving only a little standing, there is a natural bridge.

The best-known bridge of this kind in our country is the Natural Bridge in the 

Great Valley of Virginia (A).

This region of valleys which you have been studying is continued toward the northeast by the Cumberland Valley of 

Maryland and the Lebanon-Cumberland 

Valley of Pennsylvania.

The entire valley region is a part of the newer Appalachian Mountains, as the Blue Ridge and the hill lands of 

Pennsylvania (pages 186-187) are parts of the old Appalachians. The other part of the newer Appalachian Mountains, west of the Great Valley in Virginia,

Maryland, and Pennsylvania, is a region made up of long ridges, with narrow valleys between. The ridges and large parts of the valleys are covered with forests.

Allegheny Plateau. - 

The Allegheny

Plateau occupies much of northern Pennsylvania and of southern New York. It extends eastward in New York to the Hudson River where a higher and rougher part of the plateau is called the Catskill 

Mountains. The average elevation in southwestern New York and in neighboring parts of Pennsylvania is about 2000 feet, but it is a little higher in West Virginia.

The plateau was once an almost horizontal surface underlain by nearly horizontal rocks. It has been cut by a network of streams into a maze of hills and valleys. Most of the travel across it, both by roads and by railroads (B), follows the river valleys, and passes from one valley to another through cuts or gaps. 

On the north a part of the plateau ends in the steep slopes that form the southern side of the Mohawk River Valley. The 

Mohawk is an important tributary of the 

Hudson River and flows into the Hudson from the west, a short distance north of 

Albany. Find it on the map, page 154.

In the western part of New York state, and in northwestern Pennsylvania, the northern edge of the plateau slopes more gently to the Lake Plains. B. 

Railroad in a valley of the Allegheny Plateauthe Chemung Valley. Notice the ridge at the right.

A. 

Looking across the tops of ridges in the Allegheny Plateau. See how even they are.

How the Plateau Landscape Looks. 

From the picture (A) on this page you can get a good idea of how some parts of the Allegheny Plateau look. Imagine that you have climbed to the top of one of the ridges. From it you can look across the top of other ridges and see that they are all nearly the same height. 

If the valleys between them were filled in again, the country across which you are looking would seem to be a gently rolling plain.

The tops of the ridges are about 2000 feet above sea level. The bottoms of the larger valleys are from 800 to 1200 feet above the level of the sea. There are flat lands close beside the main rivers. 

These valley bottoms are, in many places, a half mile or even a mile wide. When you are looking from a valley bottom, the ridges on each side seem high and rugged. It is only when you are on top of a ridge that you see how even the top of the Allegheny Plateau is.

Effect of Land Forms on Man's Work. 

Most of the people whose homes are in the Allegheny Plateau live on the flat lands of the valley bottoms. That is partly because the soils there are deeper, better, and easier to plow, and grow better crops. It is also because roads and railroads are built more easily along the valley bottoms from one place to another (189 B). It is in the valley bottoms and on the flood plains of the streams that villages and cities have grown up.

On the tops of the ridges and on their steeper slopes there is not much soil.

Some of it was scraped away by the ice. sheet, and some is washed away by rains and melting snow. 

If these ridge tops and steep slopes were plowed, the rain might wash away more and more of the soil. Many regions of thin soils and steep slopes have been left as forests. 

On hill lands of gentler slope many farms are to be found. Each farm generally has a part where trees have been left

to grow. This wood lot, as it is called, furnishes the farmer each year with some timber, fence posts, and firewood. Other parts of the farm have been cleared and are used as pasture land for cattle, horses, and sheep, or as fields for crops.

Among the crops that these hillside farmers grow are hay, oats, corn, wheat, and garden vegetables. The hay and oats and much of the corn are used to feed the animals in the winter time. 

Most of the vegetables and wheat are for use on the farm.

On the hillside farms of the Allegheny Plateau the climate is cool and moist. 

The grass of the pasture lands and fields is not killed by the late frosts in spring or the early frosts in autumn. The cool A. - Chopping corn. nights in the plateau do not stop its growth. Cattle can get much food from pasture land that is too steep to be cultivated. 

Dairy Farming. Many of the farms of the Allegheny Plateau are dairy farms; that is, their chief product is milk. Dairy farming has become a great industry because the people in cities need so much milk and cream and butter.

A farmer must do much hard work to make a success of dairy farming or any other farming. He is busy with his crops in the spring, summer, and fall; and all the year round he must care for his animals and keep his buildings and fences in repair.

Think of the important things that must be done on one of the large dairy farms. Hay must be raised. It is needed for both horses and cattle, and some, too, may be sold to get money to buy other things. Corn must be grown, Corn must be grown, and much of it must be stored in a silo to feed the cattle in the winter time.

XX 

The pieces chopped by the machine are blown up the pipe into the silo. 

Corn for the silo is cut in September.

The whole plants - stalks, leaves, and green ears are chopped into small pieces by a machine, and packed into the silo (A). Here this food keeps green and juicy. In the winter it takes the place of the green grass of the pasture. In addition many farmers buy ground corn and other feed for their herds in the winter.

On the better dairy farms the stock barns are well built. Fresh, pure water is supplied for the cattle to drink. The barn and the cows are kept clean. Milk and cream to be sold in cities must be pure and pass certain tests.

In the old days the women on the farm made butter to sell. They had to let the milk stand in open pans a long time while the cream was rising to the top. The cream was then skimmed from the milk, put into a churn, and stirred or beaten until the small particles of butter-fat collected together.

A.- Well-kept modern dairy barn. The cows are being milked by machinery; and also are being fed. 

Now on many farms the cream is quickly separated from the milk by a machine (the separator) and is sent to a creamery to be made into butter. From some farms, too, the milk is taken to factories where it is used for making cheese. 

Other Farm Products in the Allegheny

Plateau. Poultry raising is an important branch of farming in the plateau region; for a supply of eggs and fowls, as well as of milk, is needed both for home use and for sale in the great cities. 

Potatoes and other vegetables are grown on the farms for home use; but on many farms they are grown also for sale. 

Some tobacco is raised and there are fine vineyards and apple orchards along the shores of the lakes in central New York. 

These lakes (map, page 154) are called the "Finger Lakes." Can you tell why?

The Catskill Mountains. - The part of New York that has been given the name of the Catskill Mountains is really the eastern part of the great Allegheny Plateau. Its hills are higher, the valleys are deeper, and the slopes steeper than in most other parts of the plateau. There are also fewer farms and people, more forests, and more wild life. 

Not far from the Catskills are the millions of people in and near the city of New York. In the summertime when city people take their vacations, many of them think of the cool air and shade of the Catskills. There they know that they will find hotels, boarding houses, or camping places, whichever they may prefer. A large part of the Catskill forest, with its cool valleys, springs of fresh water, lakes, and waterfalls, has been purchased by the state of New York, and is now the Catskill State Park. 

Binghamton. Roads and railroads follow the valleys of the larger rivers, and cross from valley to valley across low places between them. Many small villages have grown up in the valleys at points where it is convenient for farmers to come and trade. Some of the larger villages are important trade centers and

There is little or no flat land on the hill tops or in the valley bottoms along the streams. Railroads, roads, and trails follow the valleys. There are but few bridges in the higher regions. Its other industries include the making of cameras, films, calculating machines, and cigars. 

Cumberland Plateau. western continuation of the region you have been studying as the Allegheny Plateau is the Cumberland Plateau (page 189). In the higher parts of eastern 

Kentucky, western Virginia, and Tennessee the Cumberland Plateau in some places reaches heights of from 3000 to 4000 feet. It is even more cut up by 

The Cumberland Plateau is a region of great forests, but because of the few railroads through this part of the country, many of the forest areas cannot be profitably cut. Where lumbering is carried on, streams are used to float the logs to the lowlands or to the railroads.

What about its people? There are some farms and orchards whose owners can get to villages to sell their produce and buy what they need and can afford. 

There are other regions in the higher valleys and among the hills where life is very difficult. The people 

The people who live there have few things that they can sell and they are a long way from any market. 

Their roads are poor and they cannot take long journeys. There are no near-by villages where the people can go to school or church or where they can find libraries, museums, or theaters. Yet, in spite of all difficulties, with the help of friends and by their own efforts, some of them are able to get some education themselves and to send their children to school. highlands and followed an Indian trail to the Ohio River. MINERAL PRODUCTS OF THE APPA- LACHIAN HIGHLANDS 

In all the mountain belt of Pennsylvania the most important product is coal. There is coal in many states in our country, but Pennsylvania has more than any other. No other coal beds are so near the great manufacturing centers as are the coal beds of Pennsylvania. 

How Coal Was Formed. Years and years ago in the history of the earth there was much swamp land. Forests grew in these swamps and on their borders. The trunks, branches, and leaves of the trees fell into the swamps, and mosses grew there. The water kept this woody material from decaying. From time to time the level of the land would change. The swamp would sink and be covered with water. Sand and dirt would be deposited on top of the woody material. Once again the region would be lifted above sea level and become a swamp in which trees would begin to grow. With other changes of level, layers of sand and mud and layers of woody material would be piled on top of one another. This would take a long, long time. 

At last the land remained below sea level so long that great layers of earthy material were piled on top of all the materials that had been accumulating for so long a time. Their weight was enormous and under this pressure the woody material began to change into coal. The land was again lifted above sea level and in some places it was wrinkled into great folds. The earthy material was changed into rock, and the woody material into coal. One proof of this is the fact that fossils of leaves, roots, fruits, and trunks of trees have been found in coal beds. A. 

Ewing Galloway 

Loading a car with hard coal or anthracite in the mine.

At the right you see one of the supports of the roof. 

Fossils are deposits of stone that have taken the place of leaves, tree trunks, and other remains of plants and animals, but have kept their shape. 

Where the rocks were folded and the pressure was greatest the coal gave off part of its gas and became a hard coal called anthracite. Where the rock layers and coal layers were not folded but remained horizontal not so much gas was driven off. The coal is not so hard and is called soft or bituminous coal. 

Coal Regions. Almost all the hard coal in this country is found in eastern 

Pennsylvania. Much of the soft coal is found in western Pennsylvania and elsewhere in the Appalachian Plateau. 

In mining the anthracite, holes called shafts are dug into the earth through layers of coal and rock. The coal in each layer, if it is thick enough, is dug out by miners. These miners drill holes into the mass of coal with electric drills. 

The drill holes are loaded with dynamite and the dynamite exploded to break up the coal. The miners then loosen it with picks and load it on little cars (A) that take it to the shaft. There it is loaded into buckets and lifted to the surface.

Keystone-Underwood

A. 

Coal breaker. Here the coal is sorted and graded after being brought in from the mine. The slate and other waste material is run off up the incline at the right. See the huge pile it has made. 

Not all of the coal can be taken out of each layer. Enough must be left in pillars to hold up the roof, or other supports must be used. In some coal regions so much coal has been taken out that the surface of the earth has begun to sink. 

When the miners are at work water that gets into the mine must be pumped out, or the mine would be flooded. The mine must also be ventilated so that gases will not gather and explode, injuring the miners or shutting them off from the shaft so they cannot get out of the mine. 

When the anthracite is brought to the surface, it goes to the breakers. These are buildings in which the coal is washed, broken into pieces, sorted into different sizes, and loaded into railroad cars (A). 

A number of cities have grown up about the anthracite mines. The largest of these are Scranton and Wilkes-Barre. 

When anthracite was discovered, over a hundred and twenty-five years ago, people thought it had no value, because they did not know how to make it burn. Today there is an enormous demand for anthracite, especially for heating and cooking. 

The railroads that run into the anthracite regions have a great business in hauling the coal. The cities of New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore are the chief markets on the eastern seaboard. Great quantities of coal are taken in barges from the railroad terminals to many Atlantic seaports. 

Several railroads distribute the coal through New York state and New England. Large amounts go to the ports of the Great Lakes and the cities of Canada. 

Other railroads carry it to the west beyond the Mississippi.

In the plateau region of western Pennsylvania there are great beds of bituminous coal. Remember that the rocks of this region are almost flat, so that the coal layers between the rocks will be flat. It is for this reason that it is easier to mine the bituminous coal than it is to mine the anthracite. The rivers in the plateau region, in cutting their deep valleys, cut through rock layers and coal layers near the surface. The edges of some of the coal layers show along the sides of the valleys. In such places it

MINERAL PRODUCTS 

197. is not necessary to sink a shaft down through the earth to reach the coal. 

It can be mined by digging back into the coal layers from the valleys.

From some of the West Virginia mines coal loaded on barges is sent down the rivers to the Ohio. There the barges are bound together and pushed down the river to supply western and southern markets (page 149).

The beds of soft coal extend south along the plateau to Alabama. This coal is much used in the smelting of iron ore and the manufacture of iron and steel. 

Petroleum and Natural Gas. - You have already learned much about the great petroleum industry of our country and about natural gas (pages 141-144). 

For many years western Pennsylvania and southwestern New York were leading petroleum-producing regions and natural gas was abundant and cheap.

Some oil and much natural gas are still found there. Pipe lines have been built so that the natural gas can be supplied to many of the cities of western New York as well as those in Pennsylvania.

Iron Ore. Iron is mined in the 

Adirondacks, in the highlands of New

Jersey, and in various parts of Pennsylvania. Large deposits are found also in other parts of the Appalachian 

Highlands, especially near Birmingham, Alabama. In recent years most of the ores used in the iron furnaces in western 

New York and Pennsylvania have come from the large deposits that are found in the Lake Superior region. 

Pittsburgh and the Iron Industry.

The point of land lying between the Monongahela and Allegheny rivers, where their waters join to form the Ohio, was once in the possession of France. To protect this territory, the French had built Fort Duquesne. It was taken by the English in 1758 and renamed Fort

Pitt. The city that grew up around this fort was called Pittsburgh. The iron and steel industry developed at Pittsburgh because it was near great deposits of bituminous coal and near beds of iron ore and limestone rock. All of these are necessary in the manufacture of iron and steel. 

The iron deposits of Pennsylvania no longer furnish enough ore for the mills of 

Pittsburgh and other cities. A part must come from other states. Vast deposits are found not far from Lake Superior in Minnesota, Michigan, and Wisconsin (map, page 141). This ore is mined and taken to the near-by lake ports. It is then brought down the lakes in ore boats to Cleveland, Erie, and Lackawanna, or to other ports on Lake Erie. From these ports some of the ore is carried by rail to 

Pittsburgh and other cities for smelting and manufacture. At the smelting cen- 

8. Why is soft coal easier to mine than ters it meets the coal and limestone needed hard coal? for separating the iron from the ore.

Society for Visual Education 

A. - Steel mill at Pittsburgh. At the left of the center are seen the B. One step in steel making-pouring rounded tops of three ovens for heating the air used in blast furnaces. 

Iron ore is a mixture of iron with other | matter. The coal used for fuel is first turned into coke. To make coke, coal is heated in large ovens until some of its gases are driven off. Coke, iron ore, and broken limestone are put together into great furnaces. The furnace has a tall stack or chimney, and a blast of very hot air (A) is forced through the burning mass to increase the heat. The limestone is put in to make the impurities separate from the iron. 

When ready, the melted iron is run off from the furnace into molds in sand, making pig iron, or it may be run directly into another furnace called a converter or into an open basin or hearth. Here it is purified still more and certain other materials are added to it, so that it becomes steel (B). 

Pig iron is brittle, and so are many objects made from it, such as stoves. ingots. 

Steel is tougher and harder, and can be used for many different things, such as tools, rails, boiler plates, steel for tall buildings, and watch springs. Wrought iron, made by a different process, can be bent or hammered into any desired form.

The making of glass has become an important industry of Pittsburgh, because of the supplies of cheap fuel and of glass sand near at hand. Much of the American plate glass is manufactured here. 

Other Cities. In the neighborhood of 

Pittsburgh are a number of other cities that are centers for the manufacture of iron and steel.

The population in the plateau section of Pennsylvania and West Virginia is largely in the mining and manufacturing centers. In these regions, as their industries develop, the population increases. 

Wheeling in West Virginia, like Pittsburgh, manufactures iron, steel, and glass.

NORTHERN LOWLANDS 

199 

Charleston, the capital of West Virginia, has become the trading center of a great coal, oil, and gas region.

The manufacture of iron and steel and of many articles made from them is carried on in other Pennsylvania cities.

Each one of these cities usually has other manufactures as well. In eastern Pennsylvania, Bethlehem, Reading, and Lebanon manufacture iron and steel. Bethlehem and Allentown make use of the limestone of the Great Valley in manufacturing cement. 

Roanoke in Virginia and Chattanooga and Knoxville in Tennessee are near deposits of coal, iron, and limestone rock. 

All three cities manufacture iron and steel, but the great iron and steel center of the 

South is Birmingham, Alabama.

Birmingham is close to the southern end of the great Appalachian Valley.

Here and to the north there is more iron ore left underground than there is in the whole Lake Superior region. Close to 

Birmingham are beds of coal and beds of limestone. There is one place where you can see an iron mine, a coal mine, and a limestone quarry all at once all the things needed to make iron. Birmingham is sometimes called the "Pittsburgh of the South." It has grown very rapidly. Before 1870 there was no blast furnace and there was no city. Now many thousands of people in Birmingham are at work in the steel, cement, and other manufacturing industries. 

NORTHERN LOWLANDS 

Lake Plains. On the map, page 80, find the Lake Plains. A small part of this natural region is in northwestern 

Pennsylvania and in New York, north of the Allegheny Plateau. These plains were at one time the bed of a large glacial lake. You know that a great ice sheet once covered a large part of North 

America (page 12). It was very thick at one time, but the time finally came when it began to melt so rapidly that its southern edge retreated toward the north. 

With this retreat of the ice front the 

Allegheny Plateau was at last free from ice. The front of the ice sheet, its southern edge, was then somewhere over the Lake Plains in western New York.

Water from the melting ice made a lake between the ice front and the highlands to the south. This glacial lake slowly increased in size and depth until it found a low point on the shore where it could overflow. 

As the ice kept on melting, one after another lower places were uncovered where the waters of the lake could escape. For a long time they flowed to the Hudson River and helped to cut the 

Mohawk Valley. At last, when the ice was melted, Lake Ontario, Lake Erie, and the other Great Lakes farther west (map, page 10) came to have their present size.

The glacial lake built beaches much as our Great Lakes are now building beaches. There are such old lake beaches to be seen on the Lake Plains of 

New York state and in other states farther west. The early settlers used them as roadways, and some of them, much improved, are still in use.

The Lake Plains have an uneven, gently-rolling surface, and as a whole slope toward the Great Lakes and drain toward these lakes. In central New 

York, lying partly within the Appalachian Plateau and partly within the 

Lake Plains, are the Finger Lakes.

WP-12

Farther west along ward to Lake Ontario, as does much of this great trade route are the cities of the Allegheny Plateau of western New Utica, Syracuse, Rochester, and Buffalo. York. 

The Soils and Farming on the Lake Plains. The soils of the Lake Plains are fine and fertile and much of the land is under cultivation. The cities and villages of the Lake Plains furnish a market for much of the farm products, and the surplus is sent to more distant markets. In the southwestern corner of the state, bordering Lake Erie, is a grape-growing region from which grapes are shipped to many city markets. Some are used, however, in the manufacture of grape juice. Peaches, apples, pears, and cherries are grown in the region south of Lake Ontario.

A Great Trade Route. One of the most important trade routes in our country is the one connecting New York City with the Middle West by way of the Hudson and Mohawk valleys and the Lake 

Plains. Railroads, highways, and the canal that connects the Great Lakes with the Hudson River follow this route. 

Along this route there are many cities and important manufacturing industries. 

Find Albany on the Hudson River (map, page 154). It is the state capital and is now a port that can be reached by ocean-going freight steamers. A deep channel has been dredged in the Hudson for thirty miles below the city so steamers can reach the wharves of the port. 

North of Albany is Troy, famous as a center for the manufacture of shirts and collars. A short distance west of 

Albany is the manufacturing city of 

Schenectady. There you will find great factories engaged in the making of all Utica is the trade center of a rich farming country, and has large textile mills. 

Syracuse manufactures electric lighting apparatus, typewriters, and automobiles. Rochester leads the world in the manufacture of cameras and other photographic materials. Here also are made fine optical glass, men's clothing, and scientific instruments. 

Buffalo is a port at the eastern end of 

Lake Erie, and there are only twelve larger cities in the United States. It is at the western end of the Barge Canal, and at Buffalo much of the freight that comes east by steamers on the Great 

Lakes is landed. 

There are grain elevators, stock yards, and smelters. Flour and cereal foods are made, meat-packing establishments prepare meats for market, and there are many metal industries in or near the city. A modern industry that is increasing in size and importance is the manufacture of airplanes.

Niagara Escarpment and the Falls. - 

In the western part of New York state a high bluff called the Niagara Escarpment separates the part of the Lake 

Plains bordering Lake Erie from the part bordering Lake Ontario. North of 

Buffalo, where this bluff crosses the 

Niagara River into Canada, the land on one side of the bluff is over 300 feet higher than on the other side. 

The Niagara River connects the two lakes of Erie and Ontario. Between them there is a drop of 327 feet, half of which is where the river tumbles over a cliff 160 feet high to form Niagara Falls.

Niagara Falls are famous all over the world for their beauty and grandeur.

They are visited by many thousands of people every year. You know that falling water is used to turn machinery to generate electricity (page 185). Think of the electricity that might be generated if all the power of falling water at Niagara could be used in that way. But to do this would destroy the beauty of the falls. 

The American and Canadian governments have made an agreement upon the amount of water that can be taken by each country. 

Much of the electricity generated at 

Niagara Falls on the American side is used in the city of Niagara Falls for the manufacture of aluminum and other products. Some is sent over wires to other cities, to be used for light and power. 

Lowlands of Northern New York. 

The lowlands that surround the Adirondacks in northern New York are narrow, but they are important as trade routes.

The Mohawk Valley has already been mentioned. North of Albany a lowland route follows the Hudson River for a short distance, then crosses a low divide into the valley of Lake Champlain. This is a direct route from New York City to 

Canada and the city of Montreal. West of the Adirondacks another route follows a river valley north of Utica to the lowlands bordering the St. Lawrence River. 

Railroads and highways follow both of these routes and a branch of the Barge 

Canal connects the Hudson with Lake

Champlain. In these valleys and in that of the St. Lawrence there are some fine farms and large dairies.

A Subject to Talk Over. - Farming in different parts of the Appalachian Highlands. 

Extra Reading. In studying geography, you should form the habit of being constantly on the lookout for more information about subjects in which you are interested. 

Since you cannot get all this information at first hand, the best way to find it is to read books and other printed material-newspapers, magazines, and pamphlets. Your encyclopedia is always a handy reference book. Refer to it frequently in your study. Turn to the names of states, cities, rivers, industries, etc., and you will find accurate and interesting information. Look up the articles on "Mississippi River, Cotton, ""Rice," 99 66 and "Coal," and see what a wealth of facts they contain.

Periodicals such as the National Geographic Magazine and the Home Geographic Monthly are filled with well-chosen pictures as well as readable articles on geographic subjects. 

In your newspaper you will often find facts that will help to keep your knowledge up to date. Clippings on the subjects you are studying will make interesting additions to your scrapbook. 

Carpenter's North America (American 

Book) will take you on sight-seeing trips through all the Atlantic and South Central 

States. You may thus visit Washington, Baltimore, Philadelphia, and New York

City. Your visits will include cotton fields and cotton mills (pages 139-151), Florida and its orange groves (pages 160–168), New

Orleans and the lower Mississippi (pages 

171-179), a sugar plantation, a salt mine, the sulphur mines, and the petroleum fields of the Southwest (pages 179-209). There are chapters on cotton, sugar, fruit, coal, and iron in Allen's United States (Ginn).

If you want a whole book about cotton, try Turpin's Cotton (American Book) or 

Brooks's The Story of Cotton (Rand McNally).

In these books you will find accounts of Eli Whitney and the invention of the cotton gin, as you will also in Bachman's Great Inventors and Their Inventions (American Book). 

There are accounts of the cotton industry in Carpenter's How the World Is Clothed 

(American Book), and in Chamberlain's 

How We Are Clothed (Macmillan). 

Brigham's From Trail to Railway through the Appalachians (Ginn) tells of the development of transportation in the East. Dakin's 

Great Rivers of the World (Macmillan) has a chapter on the Mississippi.

NEW ENGLAND 

New England is the name commonly given to the group of six states in the northeastern part of our country (map, page 204). Five of these states border on waters of the Atlantic Ocean. Name them. The other state in the group shares a large lake with New York. 

What is the name of this state? What is the name of the lake? Notice that three of the states in the New England group border New York on the east. 

All of New England is farther north than the most southerly part of New 

York. A part of New Hampshire and more than half of Maine are farther north than the most northerly part of 

New York. Keeping these facts in mind, you can tell about what the winter climate and the summer climate will be in different parts of New England. 

Natural Regions of New England. 

Southern Connecticut, eastern Massachusetts, southeastern New Hampshire, and most of Maine are hilly lands. On the map, page 80, this region is shown as a part of the Piedmont Plateau. It is not very high above sea level, and you might almost think of it as a lowland, but you must not think of it as a coastal plain.

Long, long ago, there were mountains where these hill lands are now. These old mountains were worn away by wind and rain, by frost and sunshine, by the slow grinding of ice, and by the work of running water and dashing waves. The average width of this hilly region is about 60 miles, and, except in Maine, it is one of the most densely settled parts of the

You will notice two long belts of highland.

One of these extends from northern Connecticut across central Massachusetts, through New Hampshire into northwestern Maine. The other extends from 

Massachusetts to western Connecticut northward through 

Massachusetts to northern Vermont. 

Between them is the Connecticut River 

Valley. The two highlands are parts of the natural region called Appalachian Mountains and Valleys (map, page 80). 

In northern Connecticut and western

Massachusetts this highland region is a hilly plateau with a surface from 900 to 

2000 feet above sea level. In it the streams have cut deep valleys. One of these is the valley of the Connecticut River, the bottom of which is only a little above sea level. 

The Berkshire Hills in western Massachusetts are more like mountains. 

Find them on the map, page 204. Farther north, in Vermont, find the Green

Mountains. They are a region of many ridges and peaks. On the west they slope down to the level of the Champlain Valley, a part of the Great Valley of the 

Appalachian Highlands.

Connecticut River, are the White Mountains of New Hampshire. Some of the peaks of the White Mountains have been 8. Climate of New England. Northern 

New England and southern New England differ in climate because of differences in altitude, in latitude, and in nearness to the sea. The southern part is warmer because it is low and near the ocean. 

The northern part is colder because it is mountainous and a long way from the ocean. What other reason is there?

In the woods of Maine and in the uplands of New Hampshire and Vermont the winters are long and there is a heavy cover of snow. Thick ice is formed on lakes and streams. In regions with heavy winter snows, vehicles with runners instead of wheels are used during the winter (A). These sleds, as they are called, slide over the snow as it packs in the roads. Along the coast, especially in the south, there is far less snow.

Winter winds from the southeast come from the warmer sea and usually bring much moisture that falls as rain or snow. 

A winter storm from the northeast, called a northeaster," " is dreaded because it comes from colder seas and has crossed the cold ocean current flowing south along the coast of Labrador. 66 

Work of the Ice Sheet. Your study of the work of the ice sheet will help you to understand many things about New England. The ice sheet covered all of 

New England (map, page 12). The moving ice ground off the tops of the hills and lower mountains over which it passed, and left them smoothly rounded (page 

14). It swept away the loose material from hill tops and steeper slopes, leaving bare rock or a thin layer of rocky soil. It deepened some valleys and filled others. 

When the ice sheet melted away it left a new surface. Some of the old stream channels were blocked so that the water gathered into lakes, many of which later became swamps. Rivers were forced to find new courses where they have since cut gorges and formed waterfalls and rapids. Glacial waste was left in many places as deposits of clay, sand, or gravel, and as moraines, drumlins, and scattered bowlders. All of these things happened in New England as they did in other regions covered by the ice, and they have greatly influenced the lives of the people.

EARLY HISTORY 

207 

Early Settlements in New England. 

Notice that New England has a long coast line. It was natural that when our country was being explored and settled some of the explorers and later some of the settlers would land on these shores. One of the early explorers was an

Englishman who made a map of this part of our country and gave it the name New England.

On the map, page 204, find the state of 

Massachusetts. Notice the long arm of land extending into the Atlantic Ocean from the southeastern part of the state. 

Find Cape Cod Bay. On the western shore of that bay find Plymouth. 

It was at Plymouth (A) that a shipload of men, women, and children landed late in December, 1620. How many years ago was that? They had come from England on the other side of the Atlantic Ocean. 

They had faced the dangers of the voyage across a stormy sea to make new homes in a new land. The ships of that time were small. There were only a hundred in the little band of people who landed at Plymouth that winter day. But they were full of courage, and faced the future with high hopes.

Most of the land round about was covered with forest. Winter had already come. Nothing could be planted until the next warm season. They could and did cut down trees for the logs with which to build homes.

For food during that first winter they had to depend upon the supplies they had brought with them and the fish and game they were able to catch. There were 

Indians not far away, but not all of them were friendly. Once in a while, however, the settlers were able to get some food from them. A. 

Plymouth harbor today. The monument marks the spot where the first settlers landed. 

That first winter was a time of terrible hardship, but the story is too long to tell here. The thing for you to remember now is that Plymouth was the first permanent settlement in this part of our country.

As the years went by, more people came to Plymouth, and settlements were made also at other points along the New 

England coast. To live in this new land it was necessary for the settlers to have homes in which to live, food to eat, and clothing to wear. How did they get these things?

The raw materials for their buildings came from the forest and the cleared fields or rocky shore. The trees furnished logs for houses and lumber for furniture. 

The stones from the fields or shore were used for foundations and fireplaces and chimneys. In the building of the first homes many of the men joined together to help each other. Some were more skillful in shaping the logs for use, others were more skillful in laying the foundation walls or in building a fireplace or oven.

NEW ENGLAND 

In those early days every one learned to there were lowlands where the soil was work with his hands.

For food the settlers had fish and game, and from the Indians they learned how to grow corn and how to use it as food. The settlers wanted to raise larger crops of corn, and to raise other kinds of grain. 

They wanted to grow vegetables and fruits that could be stored for winter use. 

They wanted also to keep such domestic animals as sheep, hogs, cattle, and horses.

The clearing of the land for crops went on as the settlers made their way inland. 

Trees were cut down and their stumps pulled up or burned. Stony lands were cleared and the larger stones piled into fences between the fields. Even when all of these things had been done there were many places where the soil was thin, stony, and hard to work (A). 

 fine and fertile. It was because of the level fields and fertile soil that the Connecticut River Valley was settled early. 

It is still one of the best farming regions in New England.

All through the early years the growth of population away from the coast was slow. On many of the hill farms the thin stony soils yielded only poor crops, and the farmers were barely able to get along. 

When the fertile lands of the Lake Plains of New York and farther west were opened for settlement many New England farmers left their hillside farms and moved west. There they made new homes where the land was rich and easily worked. 

All during the time New England was being settled there were other settlements being made farther south along the Atlantic coast. Those who settled in 

Virginia found a milder climate than that of New England, and finer and more fertile soils. In each of the new settlements there was at first more or less trouble with the Indians. After these troubles were over the Virginia settlers spread out over the land and cultivated large farms or plantations. They found a good market for their tobacco in England and became prosperous. They were able to live well with less labor and with fewer worries than the farmers and fishermen of New England. 

Early Shipbuilding, Fishing, and Trade. 

Many of those who settled along the shores of New England stayed there. A.-New England hill-land pasture. The soil here is They knew that there were many fish in thin and is partly covered with many bowlders.

In the valleys of the larger rivers and at the heads of some bays (especially in the Boston and Narragansett basins) the waters of the Atlantic not far from the New England coast. They knew also that there was a market for salt fish at home, in England, and elsewhere.

EARLY MANUFACTURES 

209 A. 

Whaling ship built in New England. 

Men learned to build ships, using lumber from trees in the New England forests. Shipbuilding soon became an important industry at many points along the coast. As the fishing industry increased, more and larger ships were built. 

The New England fishermen became very brave and skillful sailors. Later when they began to hunt for whales and when trade with China developed, they would be away from home for months or years at a time. It was not long before the shipbuilders of New England were building the swiftest sailing ships of the world (A). Salt fish, lumber, and furs were carried across the ocean to England, and lumber to the West Indies. 

The Beginnings of Manufacture. 

Among the things that the settlers in this new land needed were shoes and clothing. 

At first these were made by the settlers themselves. The women spun the wool from sheep into yarn. This they wove into cloth or knit into stockings or other garments. The hides of cattle were tanned as leather, and during the winter or at night this was made into boots and shoes for the family or into harness for the horses. Furs bought from the Indians, or taken from animals that the settlers killed, were made into caps and coats.

After a time the more skillful dressmakers and shoemakers traveled from farm to farm or set up shops in the villages.

AGRICULTURE OF TODAY 

You have learned that for many years after the settlement of New England began, its population increased slowly (page 208). This was because the chief industry was agriculture. The land was hard to clear and hard to cultivate. 

You have also learned that some of the

New England farmers left their stony hillside farms and moved to the more fertile regions in western New York and beyond (page 208). Because of this a number of the poorer farms of New England were left to grow up to forest again. 

When people began to invent machines that would spin, weave, and do other work, they needed power to run them.

They found this power in the waterfalls of New England streams. Factories and mills were built. Workers were needed, and the population began to increase rapidly. You will soon learn more about these great manufacturing industries.

Just now it is important for you to know that southern New England is one of the most densely settled parts of our country. How did the farmers of New 

England profit from its increase in population?

Farming in New England. - When the rich soil of our western states was brought under cultivation general farming in 

New England was no longer profitable.

This does not mean that there was no longer any farming at all, but it does mean that the New England farmers turned to special kinds of agriculture. 

In doing this they had to select their crops with care. This was true not only because of the thin and stony soil but also because of the climate. The climate is even more important than the soil in determining what crops will grow.

AGRICULTURE OF TODAY 

211 The growing season in the southern lowland is only about six months long. 

There the last killing frost in the spring may come in late April, and the first killing frost in the fall in the middle of 

November. Along the Canadian border the growing season may be only four months long. The last frost in the spring usually comes late in May; the first frost in the fall, in the middle of September. 

Turn back to page 157 and read again about the climate of Florida. Why are the winters in Florida so much warmer than those in New England? Compare the length of the growing season in southern Florida with that in southern 

New England. Explain the difference.

As the population of the New England cities and villages increased with the growth of manufacturing, there was need of more vegetables and fruit. Market gardening and truck farming became important industries and are carried on near all the larger villages and cities.

Many of the hillside farms where it is too cool or too moist for raising many food crops have become dairy farms as in the Appalachian Plateau. Railroads and trucks bring milk and cream every day to Boston and other cities from nearly all parts of New England. It is distributed to stores and homes by wagons and automobiles. Some of the milk and cream from the fine dairy farms of 

Vermont is sent by fast train to New York

City. In New England, as in other dairying regions, vegetables are raised for home use, and such crops as hay, oats, and corn are raised for the animals. Some farmers raise poultry for city markets, and also raise a part of the food for the poultry. 

Raising Cranberries in New England.

The greatest cranberry-growing region 

A. Machine for picking cranberries. The finger-like teeth pull the berries from the vines without crushing them. in our country is in Massachusetts. 

Some cranberries are also raised in certain places along the coast of New Jersey, especially in the southern part. 

But cranberries are grown chiefly on

Cape Cod and in the land to the west of the cape. The dark red berries grow on low vines in swamp lands or bogs.

In growing them for market the vines must be flooded from time to time, and they must also be sanded. The level bog lands among the sand hills in southeastern Massachusetts make ideal places for growing this marsh-land fruit. 

In gathering the crop a scoop is used with fingers through which the vines but not the berries will slip. At times a single sweep of the scoop will gather a quart or more of the berries. A machine may be used for doing this work (A).

FORESTS AND LUMBERING

Underwood & Underwood

THE FORESTS AND LUMBERING 

The early settlers found forests almost everywhere. Naturally they cleared the lowlands, the smoother and gentler slopes, and the lower hilltops. The rougher and higher lands were left in forest, and form the wood lots of today. You will still find large forested areas in the Green 

Mountains, the White Mountains, and stretching across Maine. 

Why Forests Are Valuable. The people are now learning the value of the forests, not only for lumber, but also for preventing floods and for keeping a steady flow in streams used for water power. 

The leaves, mosses, and black soil of the woodlands hold much rain water and allow it to flow slowly into the lakes and streams. If the trees were all cut away, the rains and melting snows would rush down the steep slopes. There would be floods in the lower river valleys, causing great damage, and in dry spells there would be a failure of the water supply. 

Pine, spruce, and hemlock are important kinds of soft wood found in northern

New England. Among the hardwoods are oak, ash, maple, and birch. Lumbering is much less important here than in former times. It is, however, still an active industry in Maine and in parts of 

New Hampshire and Vermont. With care the New England forests will always furnish lumber for local use, supplies of firewood, and some pulp wood. Part of the White Mountain forest has been made a national forest reserve.

The forests furnish raw material for many small manufactures in New England. Among these are bobbins, spools, crates and boxes, pails, rakes, clothespins, toys, and many novelties. Large amounts of lumber are used for building or made into furniture.

In the lumbering district, most of the trees are cut in the winter time (206 A). 

The lumber camp in the deep snow is a place of hard toil and rough and ready fun. When the snows melt and the logs are driven down the flooded streams of spring (A) the work is both hard and dangerous. Downstream at some good place the logs are collected in a pond. 

One by one they are pulled into a mill to be cut into lumber or made into wood pulp.

NEW ENGLAND 

By a waterfall on some small stream a factory may be built to make spools or clothespins. In some large town or city the lumber may be worked up into moldings, window frames, doors, or flooring. 

These will be sent to Boston or some other city for sale. Bangor and Augusta in

Maine are downstream from great forests and have water power for sawmills and factories. They can ship lumber and its products both by water and by railroad. 

Maple Sugar. More maple sugar is made in Vermont than in any other state. During March the sap of the maples begins to flow through the trees. 

The farmers bore small holes in the trees and put spouts into them. The sap comes through the spouts and drips slowly into buckets that hang from the spouts. The sap, collected once or twice a day, is boiled in large kettles or pans until it forms sirup or sugar.
