A Topic to Discuss. - Uses made of the forests in New England.

MANUFACTURING 

Several causes have combined to make 

New England a great manufacturing region. (1) There is much water power, the cheapest kind of power that man uses. (2) New England is on the sea border.

It has many fine harbors and an extensive railroad system. These help in the collection of raw materials and their distribution to factories, as well as in the marketing of the manufactured goods. (3) Labor-saving machinery came into use early in New England and led to the building of mills and factories and to the training of skillful workers. (4) The industries that prospered grew larger, and other industries of the same kind were started in New England. (5) Because of the success of industries already there, new industries using other raw materials located in New England. 

There they were sure to find trained factory workers. More workers came, some from the farms, others from more distant places, and in time they also became skillful. (6) Factories making the same kinds but not always the same quality of goods began to locate in the same city. This made it possible for buyers to visit many factories in a short time. 

An excellent example of this concentration of an industry in a city is found in Troy, New York (page 200), where many shirts and collars are made. The felt hat industry at Danbury, Connecticut, is an example in New England.

In any large city the wholesale, retail, and factory districts are usually in different parts of the city. The larger and finer houses and apartments are near each other on a few of the better streets.

MANUFACTURING 

215 

A. In a New England woolen mill. These machines are combing the wool and making it into yarns ready for spinning. 

More modest apartments will be found on other streets. If you wish to look at automobiles in the retail district, you will find most of the salesrooms along some main street and within easy walking distance of one another.

Where the Raw Materials Come From. 

Very few of the raw materials used in the mills and factories of New England are found or grown in New England itself. Chief among the manufacturing industries of New England are the weaving of cotton and woolen cloth (A), the tanning of hides and the manufacture of shoes, the making of small metal goods and jewelry, and paper making.

Most of the cotton used in New England is raised in our southern states. The wool and hides come from our western states and from foreign countries. The metals and jewels used in New England factories come from other places. Pulp wood for paper making is the only one of the chief raw materials of which New 

England produces a large quantity. 

The Cotton Industry. More than a hundred years ago skillful men in England had invented machinery for spinning and weaving cotton. England did not allow any of these machines, or even any patterns of them, to be taken out of the country. She wished to keep the business for herself. ness for herself. In 1789 Samuel Slater came to this country and settled in Rhode 

Island. Remembering how the English machines were made, he built some new ones. In 1790 he began to make cotton goods in Pawtucket, Rhode Island, a town which now has great cotton mills. In this way our cotton manufacture began.

NEW ENGLAND 

Cotton, pressed into bales of about 

500 pounds each, comes to New England ports by ship from Savannah, Mobile, New Orleans, and Galveston. Some of the New England ports where it is received are also cotton-manufacturing centers. This is true of New Bedford and Fall River, and in those cities many of the bales are unloaded close to the mills. When cotton is sent to inland mills, it must be carried a short distance by rail.

Many people are employed in the cotton industry. A single mill may employ several thousand workers. People from Canada and people from various European countries have come in large numbers to work in the mills. Schools in New Bedford, Fall River, and Lowell train workers for this industry. 

Calicoes, ginghams, and other cotton fabrics are made; also knit underwear, stockings, towels, handkerchiefs, and thread. From the mills the finished goods are sent to the wholesale and retail merchants in all parts of the United States, and to many foreign countries. 

Among the great cotton-manufacturing centers of New England are New Bedford, Fall River, and Lowell in Massachusetts; Manchester in New Hampshire; and Pawtucket in Rhode Island. There are large mills also in Taunton, Massachusetts; in Nashua, New Hampshire; in

Lewiston, Maine; and in many other cities and towns. In recent years cotton manufacturing has developed largely in the South (pages 134-135), and New

England factories find themselves in competition with southern mills. 

The Woolen Industry. The manu facture of woolen goods in New England began long ago on the home farms. Later small mills were built in villages along the streams. Now much of the industry is centered in great mills in a few cities. 

Lawrence in Massachusetts, and Providence and Woonsocket in Rhode Island, are large centers of woolen manufacture. 

Many of the cities that make cotton goods make woolen goods also. The weaving of carpets is an important branch of the woolen industry, and special attention is given to making the finer kinds, such as Brussels, Axminster, and Wilton.

Boots and Shoes. The tanning of skins is an old industry in eastern Massachusetts. Men who knew how to make leather were among the first settlers who came from England. Tanning is still done in towns near Boston, but much leather is brought to New England from other states. Boston is the principal 

New England market for hides and leather. 

Many common articles, such as traveling bags, pocketbooks, and harnesses, are made from leather. The greatest use of leather, however, is for boots and shoes.

In early days these were all made by hand, but now they are turned out very rapidly by machinery. The region about 

Boston is the leading part of the United States for shoe manufacturing. This region extends to southern Maine and 

New Hampshire, notably in Manchester.

The more important centers of the shoe industry in Massachusetts are Brockton, Lynn, Haverhill, and Boston. This industry alone is more than three fourths of all the business of Brockton and 

Haverhill. Shoes are made also in New 

York, Rochester, and many other cities in our country. Because of the excellence of American shoes, they are sold all over the world.

Metal Manufacture. Firearms and manufactured in Meriden, and almost other smaller articles that do not require all kinds of hardware are made in various very much iron are made in great quanti- cities of the state, especially in New ties both in Massachusetts and in Con- Haven, Bridgeport, Hartford, and New necticut. The watchmakers in the fac- Britain. tories at Waltham (A), in Massachusetts, turn a small piece of steel into so many small screws or fine watch springs that it becomes worth hundreds of dollars. 

With the steel they use brass and silver or gold, and turn out, ready for use, several thousand watches a day.

In Providence, Rhode Island, and at several places in Massachusetts, jewelry and silverware are made. In many New 

England cities, especially in Worcester and Springfield, looms and other heavy machinery are manufactured. Wire making also is one of the industries of Worcester. In all these products, -the watches, the jewelry, and the textile machines, the amount of material used is small, but the amount of time and skill required is very great. 

The state of Connecticut has many factories in which small but valuable things are made of metals. Waterbury is a center for brass work, and for watches and clocks. Cutlery and silverware are 

Wood Pulp and Paper. In the old days paper was made from rags gathered from homes and factories. For more than fifty years, however, much paper has been made from wood, and now newspapers and most books and magazines are printed on such paper. The softer kinds of wood are used, especially spruce. 

Maine, New Hampshire, Vermont, and 

New York have much spruce, and these states have become important in the wood-pulp industries. Much pulp wood is also imported from Canada.

The trees are cut down, the bark removed, and the trunks cut into blocks about two feet long. The mills, which often make both the wood pulp and the paper, are in or near the forests, usually where a waterfall supplies power. The pieces of wood are ground by heavy machinery or boiled in acid to make the pulp. The acid process leaves the fibers longer and makes the paper stronger than the method of grinding the wood. WP-13

NEW ENGLAND 

Holyoke, on the Connecticut River in

Massachusetts, has great paper mills (A) and other industries, as the river here provides a large amount of water power. 

The city is not near the forests, and the best grades of Holyoke paper are made from rags. Some of the rags come from 

New England mills, and some are brought in from elsewhere.

Manufacturing and Raw Materials. - A 

large number of manufactured articles are mentioned in the section which you have just read. Take a large sheet of paper and write in a column down the left-hand side the names of all these manufactured articles. 

Then in a second column, opposite each article, write the name of the raw material from which it is made. In this second column you will notice that you have used many fewer names than in the first column. 

How do you explain this fact? 

Now see if you can reduce your second column to not more than six raw materials. 

You may have given leather as the raw material from which shoes are made, but leather is all made from hides and skins. Iron, copper, gold, etc., may all be included under the name of metals. If you have given rags as one of the sources of paper, you may include them under the name of cotton. 

Perhaps these are the six raw materials you will now have in your shortened list: skins, cotton, wool, metals, pulp wood, jewels. Two of these are of animal origin, two of vegetable origin, and two of mineral origin. Name the two in each group.

THE FISHING INDUSTRY 

Some of the New England ports have sent men and ships to sea to fish ever since the days of their first settlement. Fish are to be found in great numbers in the waters along the coast and in shallow waters a few hundred miles to the northeast. Europeans have fished in the shallow sea to the south and east of Newfoundland (map, page 6) for more than four hundred years. 

Since the trade between our country and Europe is now so large, fishing on these "Grand Banks" of Newfoundland is at times very dangerous. This region is crossed by the great Atlantic steamers. 

Fogs and icebergs are very common, and when a fog forms the sailors cannot see far. Steamships and boats caught in a fog may run into an iceberg or into another boat. Even though each steamship gives warning to other ships by means of a foghorn or the ringing of a bell, there are still many accidents due to fog.

THE FISHING INDUSTRY 

219 

A. - Drying codfish at Gloucester. On the two racks in the foreground the covering has been turned back and the men are sorting out the fish that are dry enough for packing. 

Although fishing is still an important New England industry, some shore towns have changed their business from catching and packing fish to manufacturing.

Sailors from New Bedford and Nantucket 

Island at one time went out on sailing voyages of three or four years to distant oceans to catch whales. Now New Bedford is best known for cotton mills, and Nantucket as a summer resort. 

How the Fish Are Caught. Boston and Gloucester are the great fishing centers of New England, and are among the greatest in the world. Hundreds of schooners sail from New England ports to the Grand Banks of Newfoundland, where cod and halibut are found. The fishing is done from small boats called dories that put off from the schooner with one or two men in each. The fish are dressed and packed in ice, or salted, and the schooner, when loaded, returns to 

Boston, Gloucester (A), or it may be to

Portland or some other fishing center. 

Cod and halibut live in waters of some depth and must be caught with hooks.

Mackerel swim in schools through the surface waters and are caught with huge nets. Lobsters are caught in traps near the shore, especially along the coast of 

Maine. Oysters are common on the south shores, and many are shipped from shore towns. Along the shores of Maine many small herring are canned and sold as sardines. The canning of fish and other sea foods is an important industry at several of the fishing ports. 

QUARRYING 

In all settled countries where good building stones are found, many public buildings and fine homes are built of stone or of brick with stone trimmings.

Many of the rocks of New England are so hard that they can be cut into blocks and slabs for building and other purposes. 

Granite. The most widespread and important rock in this region is granite.

NEW ENGLAND 

It is hard and is used for buildings, for monuments and tombstones, and for paving blocks and curbstones. New

England granites are usually of a gray color. There are large granite quarries in Quincy, Massachusetts; Barre, Vermont; Concord, New Hampshire; and in Maine. 

Marble. Marble is quarried in the 

Green Mountains in Vermont, in the 

Berkshire Hills in western Massachusetts, and in Connecticut. The industry is largest in Vermont, which ranks first among the states in this product. Rutland and Proctor are the chief centers.

Here great pits have been made where the marble has been taken out, and yards and sheds are filled with the blocks and slabs to be made ready for shipment. Marble is used for the walls. of buildings, for interior decoration, and for monuments. 

Slate and Other Stones. Slate is a fine-grained rock that splits into thin sheets or slabs, and is used in roofing, for school blackboards, and for floors, stair treads, and other uses in building. Slate is quarried in Vermont and in Maine. 

Red and brown sandstones are found in the Connecticut Valley.

The granite, marble, and slate are shipped not only to towns and cities in New England, but also to other states. 

Much of the granite is quarried near the sea and goes by ship. This is usually the cheapest way for any freight that is as heavy and bulky as stone. Nearness to large markets and good transportation facilities make quarrying a large industry in New England. As in other states, beds of sand and gravel furnish materials for concrete which is used in roads and buildings.

SUMMER VACATION LANDS 

Many people who are able to do so take a vacation during all or a part of the summer. Popular vacation places in

New England are to be found along its seashore, among its hills and mountains, deep in its forests, and along the shores of its lakes. 

Resorts in Mountains and Forests. — 

Mountains are cooler than lowlands (page 185), and the New England mountains have forests and clear air far away from the smoke of villages and cities.

They also offer wonderful views of woods, fields, lakes, and sky. Hence, in summer, thousands of people go to these mountains for rest and pleasure.

In the wide forests of Maine are many lakes, joined by streams. Here the tourist can canoe for long distances, and can camp, hunt, and fish.

Others find work in making and sailing pleasure boats, and carrying the tourists and their baggage in trains, automobiles, and wagons.

Seaside Resorts. Along the New the summer residents.

England shores there are many rocky cliffs as well as some smooth sandy beaches. Many thousands of people go to the seashore in the heat of summer, because of the cool ocean breezes and the bathing, fishing, and boating. Newport in Rhode Island is one of the best-known seaside resorts. It has an excellent harbor frequently visited by ships of the 

United States Navy. Bar Harbor on

Mt. Desert Island is also a very well known summer resort (A). 

Vacation Business. The value of all the farm products of Maine is less than the amount of money which comes into the state through tourists and summer residents. The New England shores, mountains, streams, lakes, and forests attract people from the cities and villages near at hand and also from places far to the westward. 

A great number of New Englanders find work in managing hotels and boarding houses, building and renting cottages, and raising vegetables and fruit needed by 

The growth of manufacturing and trade has led to the growth of many large cities in New England. It will not be possible to mention all of them here, and it is not necessary to know exactly what is made in each of these cities. Some cities have been named already in connection with great industries. A few others are mentioned here because of their size and general importance. 

Boston. The chief port and largest city of New England is Boston. Many other large cities and towns are within a few miles of Boston, and " greater Boston," or the Metropolitan District, as the region is sometimes called, has more than two million people. At Cambridge, just north of Boston, is Harvard, the oldest of American universities. It was founded in 1636, by John Harvard, and

Boston is the capital of Massachusetts and one of the most important seaports of the United States. Coasting steamers run to all points on the New England coast, to the ports of Canada, and to 

New York, the West Indies, and the 

Gulf of Mexico. Several lines have regular sailings to Great Britain and other foreign countries. countries. Cotton from the southern states, fruit from the tropics, and hides from many countries are among the products landed on Boston wharves. 

From Boston are sent many of the manufactured products of New England. 

The city is also a shipping point for grain from the western states. 

Salem, like Boston, is an old seaport, and many years ago ships from all parts of the world brought rich cargoes to its wharves. Boston grew faster and gained more suitable for modern ocean steamships. Boston is also at the most western point on Massachusetts Bay, and from

Boston railroads were built across the 

Berkshire Hills to Albany and the West. 

Railroads run from Boston to the city of New York, and to all important points in New England and eastern Canada. 

Southern New England is covered by a close network of railroads. One of the roads passes through the Hoosac Mountain, a part of the Berkshires, by a tunnel about five miles long.

The canal across Cape Cod from 

Buzzards Bay to Cape Cod Bay shortens by several hours the sea route from 

Boston to southern points. 

Boston is the business and banking center of New England. Many companies that own mills and factories in the smaller cities have their main offices in 

Boston. Through these offices they buy their raw materials, such as wool, cotton, or leather, and also make sales of cotton cloth, worsteds, shoes, and other products.

Other Seaports of New England. 

Look at your map and see how the shore curves in and out in deep bays and many headlands. On some of the rivers in drowned valleys boats can sail far inland. Bangor, Providence, and Hartford can trade on water routes, yet notice how far each is from the sea.

Portland is not a large city, but it is a great seaport. It is the chief city of

Maine. It is a trade center for the manufacturing cities of the state, has an excellent harbor, and sends steamships to 

Boston, to other American ports, and to

Liverpool. The Grand Trunk Railway of Canada reaches the sea at Portland, and thus has an outlet in the winter months, when most of the Canadian ports are closed by ice. Thus Portland is a seaport for the country to the north, sending across the Atlantic some of the grain and live-stock products of Canada. 

Other New England coast cities have much coastal trade, but they have grown partly from other causes. Providence has a large trade by water, but it has become the second city of New England largely by reason of many manufactures. 

It is the capital of the state of Rhode 

Island, and is also the seat of Brown 

University. New Haven and Bridgeport (A), in Connecticut, are large cities on the sea. Because the sea road was open to them, they could get the raw

A Few Inland Cities. - Many cities are where they are chiefly because of the things they make. They are places of one or two kinds of factories. But some of the large cities have more than one reason for their growth. Worcester is next in size to Boston and Providence.

It is not on the sea, but has long had a railroad from Boston and its wharves.

This railroad also extends west to Albany. 

Worcester has a great number of mills and factories of many kinds, especially in iron work and machinery. 

Springfield is a crossroads city, with railroads running north and south in the 

Connecticut Valley, and between Boston and the West. Several smaller cities, many towns, and a large, rich farming region depend on Springfield for their chief trade. 

Hartford (A) is another great city of the 

Connecticut Valley, a center of trade and of many kinds of manufactures. It is the home of insurance companies. 

There are smaller inland cities which, like the "big three" just named, are well known. Pittsfield has factories, railroads extending in four directions, beautiful homes, and is the chief trading center of the Berkshire region. Bennington, in 

Vermont, is an old town, near a battlefield of the Revolution (225 A). Burlington, which looks out across Lake Champlain to the Adirondack Mountains, is the chief city of Vermont, and the seat of the state university.

Population. Turn to the table near the end of the book giving the population of the different states. Make a list of the 

New England states and the population of each. You will find that the total population of New England is more than 

8,000,000. An interesting comparison can be made with Texas, as that state is four times as large as all New England and has only about three fourths as many people. The distribution of the New

England people is also interesting. In the northern parts of Maine, New Hampshire, and Vermont there are no more people than you would find in other mountain regions. It is in southern New

England that most of the great manufacturing and trading centers are found.

That region has become one of the most densely settled parts of the United States. Richardson 

Commerce. - Before leaving the study of New England, think again of some of the things you have learned as they influence the commerce of this part of our country. You will remember that most of the raw materials used in New England factories must be imported. Some of these come from distant parts of our own country by railroad and by sea. Others are imported from foreign lands in nearly every continent.

To pay for these imports, manufactured goods must be sent to market and sold. Again the railroads and ships are called into use. This means, of course, that more cloth, shoes, paper, and metal goods are manufactured in New England than can be used in those states. It is one of the great manufacturing centers of our country and of the world.

 EARLY HISTORY 

Some time ago you studied the southern part of the great central lowland of our country. What group of states includes that region? Among its chief crops are cotton, sugar cane, and rice. In it are found petroleum, natural gas, sulphur, salt, iron, coal, and great forests. It has fine soils, fertile fields, and a long growing Over most of the region there is season. an abundant rainfall. 

You remember, too, that the great central lowland of North America extends northward through our country and Canada to the Arctic Ocean (map, page 

10). In the northern part of our country these Central Plains (page 80) are occupied by the North Central States, shown on the map on pages 228-229. This is the group of states you are now to study. 

Early Exploration. Turn to page 7 and read what is said there about French 

explorers. Then turn back to the map of the North Central States. Notice the rivers that flow into the Great Lakes. 

From several points on the Great Lakes 

French explorers made their way to the west and south along streams flowing into the lakes. into the lakes. From some of these streams they crossed low divides to eastern tributaries of the Mississippi, and followed these until they reached the 

Mississippi itself. One of the French explorers, La Salle, made his way along the great river to its mouth in the Gulf of Mexico.

Some of the explorers were missionaries who wished to convert the Indians to Christianity. Others were traders who bought furs from the Indians, and set up trading posts or places for trade. There were not many of these posts and they were widely scattered. They did not help much in the settling of this part of our country. 227

THER THOU - Bronze statue of the Pioneer Woman, in Kansas City, Missouri. 

The Coming of Settlers. It was not until much of the best land of New York, Pennsylvania, and other Atlantic states was occupied that the real settlement of the North Central States began.

Many people in the East had heard stories about the fertile plains south of the 

Great Lakes and beyond the Appalach- 'ian Mountains. Except for the Indians it was an empty land. It was known that those who went there to make new homes would face great hardships. 

They would have to depend upon supplies they could take with them and upon whatever they found in the new land. 

To leave the older settlements behind and seek new homes farther west was to establish a new front of settlement a new frontier. Only men and women of courage would do this. But there were those who were willing to try because they could find fertile lands that would be all their own, and that is what they wanted. 

From many places in the East brave men and women with their children and household goods and farm animals set out on the great journey. The march of settlement toward the West did not end until it pushed beyond the Western Highlands to the shores of the Pacific Ocean.

In the north the early settlers followed the south shore of Lake Erie. Others made their way across the highlands of 

Pennsylvania to Pittsburgh. From that place they floated down the Ohio River to some point in Ohio, Indiana, or 

Illinois. Somewhere they would land and make a home. Settlers from the South 

Atlantic States and from Kentucky likewise made their way to the Ohio River and the states north of it.

Some of the new settlers traveled by wagons drawn by horses or oxen, some rode horseback, and others walked (A).

EARLY HISTORY 

231 

Most of the women and younger children rode in the wagons with the household goods. The farm animals were led or driven along with the wagons. There were no roads for these first settlers to follow through the wilderness. None were built until much later. They waded through small streams and built flatboats to cross or to float down the larger ones.

Michigan and Wisconsin were easily reached by way of the Great Lakes, or across the plains that lay about them. 

Missouri was settled by men from both 

North and South. In the other North 

Central States west of the Mississippi most of the early settlers were from the North. Many of the later settlers came directly from Europe. 

How the Land Was Brought under Cultivation. In southern Ohio there are hilly uplands that are a part of the Appalachian Plateau (page 80). There are also some hill lands in southern Indiana.

This hill country varies in elevation from about 1000 feet above sea level in parts of Ohio to a few hundred feet in Indiana. There are likewise upland areas in northern Michigan and in northern and southwestern Wisconsin. These upland areas and the larger part of the lowlands of Ohio and Indiana were originally covered with forests. Even in the treeless regions called prairies there were some trees along the rivers and on their bordering bluffs.

The first settlers selected their homes where there were trees even if it meant much hard work to clear the fields. They needed logs and lumber for their houses and furniture and for shelter for their animals. Another reason why the first settlers did not like the prairies was that they were afraid that no crops would

A. 

Sod house built by early settlers on the prairie. grow where trees were not growing. 

Some time later they learned that the treeless plains had a rich deep soil and they began to cultivate it. They put up buildings the walls of which were made of sods piled on top of one another (A). 

The early years of settlement were years of hardship, great danger, and loneliness. They were years of hardship because of the lack of many things needed, and because of the hard work necessary to clear the soil and to cultivate it with the poor tools of that day. They were years of great danger because of fights and wars with the Indians, who wanted to stop the white settlers from turning the hunting grounds into farms. 

They were years of loneliness because in this new land the settlers were far away from their friends. They could see them only by taking the long, long journey back to the place from which they had come. Even their nearest neighbors might be miles away- - too far to travel, except when it was necessary. 

With the passing of the years things grew better. More settlers came. Settlements grew larger and there were more farms. Roads were built, though they

had no hard surface like the macadam or concrete roads of today. When the winter snows were melting, or after heavy rains, the roads were very muddy. Where a road crossed a boggy place, however, it was covered with logs laid down side by side so that the horses and wagons would not sink into it. Later some canals were built, and finally the railroads. People were friendly and lived close enough to each other so they were able to visit back and forth. After the railroads were built (maps, pages 254, 259), the farmers could sell their surplus produce in the cities or ship it to eastern markets. The people became prosperous. 

In the North Central States you now find a large population and ten or more cities of great size. Millions of people have come there from foreign lands.

Other millions have been born there. 

There are many farms, mines, oil wells, mills, factories, and packing houses.

The farms are large and are cultivated by the use of the most modern machinery.

These states have become one of the great food-producing regions of the world. 

Giving Reasons. State reasons that explain why 

1. The Frenchmen who first came to the central section of our country did not help much toward its permanent settlement. 

2. The first settlers of this region required a great deal of courage. 

3. Settlers, at first, selected lands on which there were trees. 

4. The first roads built were very different from those of the present day.

THE LAND AND ITS CLIMATE

THE LAND AND ITS CLIMATE 

When settlers first began moving into the region you are now studying, the land belonged to the national government. 

Later it was divided into one-mile squares, called sections, and these into quarter sections, each having 160 acres. When the states were formed many of the boundaries followed the straight lines between squares. It was natural that when roads were laid out in such regions, many followed these boundary lines. 

At first the government sold the land to the settlers for $2 an acre, later for $1.25 an acre. Still later the government gave a quarter section to a settler who would make a home on it and farm the land for five years. As a result most farms were larger than those in the East. 

The Highlands. You have read about the forested uplands in Ohio, Indiana, Michigan, and Wisconsin. In northern

Michigan there are places that are more than 2000 feet above sea level, but the highest elevation in Wisconsin is some- A. 

Harrows, drawn by a tractor. Can you tell why harrowing is done?

B.- 

Grain drill, drawn by a tractor. This machine plants the seed in rows close together. what less. The same hilly upland extends into central and northern Minnesota. It is a region of very old rocks like those in the northern Appalachians. It is covered with glacial material, and dotted with thousands of lakes. 

Much of the southern half of Missouri, south of the Missouri River, belongs to the Ozark Plateau. Its surface is from 

800 to 1800 feet above the sea, and it is largely a region of forests and mines, although there are also many small farms. 

The Prairies. The prairies, or treeless parts of the Central Plains, are usually free from stones, and are covered with a black, rich soil which is in places ten feet in depth. As the land is nearly level, or only gently rolling, large fields may be laid out and planting and harvesting machinery can be used. Where single crops such as corn or wheat are grown the most modern machinery will be found in use. Gang plows, harrows (A), grain drills (B), reapers (227 A), headers, threshing machines, and great tractors are part of the equipment of these farms.

Climate of the North Central States. 

The North Central States are far from the sea, and have hot summers and cold winters. The annual rainfall varies from about 45 inches in the southeast to less than 20 inches in the west (84 B). Winds from the west and storms from the Gulf of

Mexico pass over this part of our country. 

The average length of the growing season is 200 days in the very southern parts of Illinois and Missouri, on the lowland along the Ohio and Mississippi rivers. It is 110 days in the northern parts of Minnesota and North Dakota.

In the heart of the prairie country it varies from 140 to 170 days, allowing enough time to mature or ripen the chief crops of temperate latitudes. It must be remembered, also, that crops get most of their growth by daylight, and that the summer days are longer in the north than in the south. The 110 crop-growing days in northern Minnesota have more daylight hours than 120 days in southern Illinois.

The water of the Great Lakes is warmed during the summer and stores up heat. 

As water does not cool so quickly as land, the autumn winds are warmed in passing across the lakes. As a result the growing season is longer on the side of a lake toward which the wind is blowing than in regions in the same latitude that are not near large bodies of water.

In general, therefore, the 

North Central States have warm summers for the growth of crops. The winters are cold but clear, and very bracing. The soils are rich, many of the rivers and lakes are navigable, and the plains are level so that railroads have been easily constructed. 

These facts help to explain the rapid increase of population and the growth of many cities. 

The Corn Belt and the Wheat Belt.

Study the wheat map (A). A great deal of wheat is grown in central Kansas, and much wheat in the far West, especially in 

Washington and Oregon. Turn to the corn map (235 A). Very little corn is grown west of the central region of the 

United States. Much of the West is either mountainous or dry, and corn needs rain and warm days.

Notice that corn grows south to the Gulf Coast and is found all through the cotton-growing region, while there is hardly any wheat in the cotton belt. 

Corn grows well in the heat and moisture of the South; wheat grows best in the dry, clear air of higher and cooler places. 

Look north toward the Canadian border.

Much spring wheat is grown in Minnesota and the Dakotas. Spring wheat, as its name shows, is planted in the spring and ripens in the fall. Winter wheat is

THE CORN BELT 

The corn map shows you a region or belt of heavy production. The dots make some places black, because they are so thick. Look from Ohio across Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, and eastern 

Nebraska. The belt of heavy corn production reaches over into Missouri, Kansas, South Dakota, and 

Minnesota. This is the corn belt, but see how much sown in the autumn farther south where | wheat is grown in the same region (234 A). the winters are not so cold and where it is not killed by winter cold or by spring thawing and freezing. The northern parts of Wisconsin and Michigan are too rough and full of forests for either corn or wheat. Eastern New York and New 

England grow more corn than wheat because corn is needed as food for stock. 

The farmer in the East could raise more wheat, but it is cheaper to buy flour made from wheat grown in the central or western parts of our country.

Almost every farmer there raises some oats, too. All that is meant by corn belt is that corn is the biggest crop. 

Farming in the Corn Belt. You must not get the idea that corn and wheat are the only crops grown in the corn belt or that the whole farm is used for crops.

Oats and hay are raised chiefly for the horses used on the farm, although some of each may be sold if there is a surplus. 

Oats will ripen where it is quite cool, so they are raised chiefly in the northern part of the region and in the highlands. 

However, most farmers grow some. Hay, including timothy, clover, and alfalfa, can be grown throughout the North Central 

States wherever there is enough rain. 

Most farmers in the corn belt keep some dairy cattle, as well as beef cattle and hogs to fatten for market. This means that a part of the land must be used for pasture. Farming in such regions as this where many crops and farm animals are raised is called mixed farming. 

Usually mixed farming is much safer for the farmer than the raising of a single crop would be. If a farmer raises only

A. 

Cultivating young corn, four rows at a time, with an auto-driven cultivator. one crop to sell and that crop is a failure he has done his work for nothing. If he raises several crops, one may fail and he will still have something to show for his work. A wise farmer, in most places, does not limit himself to one crop.

In mixed farming the crops are rotated. 

That means that the same crop is not always planted in the same field. This is because different crops use different plant foods or different amounts of the same plant food. By changing the crops every year or every two or three years no single plant food in the soil will all be used up. 

Another advantage in mixed farming is that the farmer can spread his work through the year. His plowing may be done in the fall if he is planting winter wheat, or in the early spring if he is planting oats. During the growing season the wheat and oats do not need cultivating, but the corn must be kept free from weeds (A). The hay is cut late in June or early in July. When dried it is stored in the barns or in large stacks.

After the hay has been gathered the wheat and oats are cut, tied into bundles, and allowed to stand in shocks in the field so the seeds can dry. When the grain is dry enough it is threshed and the grain stored or sold.

Even after the ears of corn are ripe they are left on the stalks until the kernels are dry. The ears are then pulled from the stalks, husked, and stored in large cribs. 

The Corn Crop. - Corn is also known as maize and is often called Indian corn, because the Indians raised it. It is a native of the Western Hemisphere, but was introduced into Europe soon after the discovery of America. Although it is now raised in many parts of the world that have a suitable climate, the crop in the United States is far larger than the crops of all other countries together.

STOCK RAISING AND MEAT PACKING 

237 

Nearly 3,000,000,000 bushels of corn are raised each year in our country. 

This is about two thirds of the number of bushels of corn raised in all the countries of the world.

Some of the corn is ground for corn meal and is used for making cornstarch and breakfast foods, sirup, oil, and other corn products. 

The greater part of the corn, however, is fed to hogs and cattle. This is the reason why the corn belt is also the chief live-stock region in the United States. 

STOCK RAISING AND MEAT PACKING 

In the North Central States many domestic animals are raised on farms and on ranches. The farms are numerous in all the states of the group. The ranches are in the drier districts farther west.

A farmer in one of the North Central

States may plant a quarter or a third of his farm in corn. Much of it will be used to fatten the cattle and hogs. The farmers who make butter and cheese will have left-over skim milk, buttermilk, and whey to feed to their hogs. Many farmers in the prairie states get their money mainly from the sale of the cattle and hogs, and from milk, butter, cheese, and poultry. Iowa, Missouri, Illinois, and

Ohio are the leading states in the poultry industry of our country. 

Sheep. Large numbers of sheep are raised in several of the North Central

States. Among these are Ohio, Michigan, Iowa, and Missouri. There is also a large sheep-raising region in Texas. But more than half the sheep of our country are raised in the Western Highlands. Do you remember what you learned about the sheep industry when you read about the 

Great Plains (pages 122-123)? 

Beef Cattle. The map on this page shows where most of the beef cattle of our country are raised. The region of greatest production includes Iowa, and extends from that state southward into market. Thousands of cattle are sent from the ranches to the farmers of the prairie states farther east to be fattened. 

Meat Packing. In former times the people of a city were supplied with meat by farmers near at hand, who drove in their cattle, sheep, or hogs and sold them to the local butcher. Today, beef and to pork enough to supply New York or 

Philadelphia or any other large city cannot be raised near these cities. They must often be brought from a great distance. Modern trains and ships make this possible. Some animals are shipped alive, but the greater number are slaughtered in cities that are not far from the stock farms and ranges. 

Such cities are known as packing centers. Chicago is the greatest of these.

It is not only in the midst of the prairie stock farms, but is easily reached by railroads from the stock ranges west and southwest. Still nearer the ranges are the packing houses of Omaha in Nebraska, Kansas City (239 A) and Wichita in Kansas, Sioux City in Iowa, St. Joseph and St. Louis in Missouri, and East St. 

Louis in Illinois.

STOCK RAISING AND MEAT PACKING 

239 A. 

Stockyards at Kansas City, Kansas. The animals are kept in different pens, so that those of one owner may be kept separated from those of other owners. These yards can hold 70,000 cattle, 50,000 hogs, and 50,000 sheep. 

The horns and bones are turned into buttons, hairpins, combs, brushbacks, and handles of many kinds. Other parts serve for making glue, soap, glycerin, poultry food, gelatin, and for various medical preparations. Nothing is wasted.

Things to Tell.-1. The names of the states in which there is the heaviest corn production.

The stockyards receive the animals as they come from the trains. Thousands of cattle and hogs and sheep are butchered every day, and the families of those who work in the packing houses would people a good-sized city. Most of the meat is sent in halves or in whole carcasses, by refrigerator cars, to cities and towns in all parts of the country; and in stores there it is cut up and sold in the form of steaks, roasts, or other "cuts." Some of the meat goes abroad in cold storage. Some is frozen, salted, smoked, or cooked and sealed in air-tight cans. Meats prepared in these ways will keep for a long time and can be shipped to distant markets.

THE WHEAT BELT 

Now, what about the wheat belt? 

Look again at the map, page 234. The winter wheat is grown in the corn area, only there is more, much more, wheat in 

Kansas, and very much less in Iowa.

Wheat grows well in Iowa soil, but it pays better to raise corn there. Notice that wheat grows also in northern Texas. 

Follow the chief regions of wheat production to the north and east through the 

North Central States and on through 

Pennsylvania to Maryland and Virginia. 

The Red River of the North. When the glacial ice was melting in the region that is now Minnesota and North Dakota, there was a very large lake where these states join each other. Much fine mud settled in the bottom of the lake. When the water finally drained away a very flat lake plain was left. A sluggish stream, the Red River of the North, flows across this plain. Here are some of the richest wheat lands in the whole world. 

Spring and Winter Wheat. - The northern part of the wheat belt is suited to growing spring wheat. In Minnesota and the Dakotas and some other northern states the winters are cold and dry. 

These conditions are poorly suited to the growth of winter wheat. Winter wheat must be sown in the autumn of one year and harvested in the early summer of the following year. After it is planted in the autumn it grows to several inches in height before cold weather stops its growth. It will live through the winter under a light blanket of snow. It will live also in regions where there is no snow if the winter cold is not severe. 

With the warm weather of spring it begins to grow again. 

Spring wheat is sown in the spring as soon as the soil can be worked. It is harvested in the late summer of the same year. The spring-wheat-growing region extends into Saskatchewan, Alberta, and 

Manitoba in Canada (map, page 284). 

Winnipeg is the trading center of the 

Canadian wheat region. The growing season in this wheat area is short compared with the growing season of the cotton or corn belts. However, the springwheat region has very long days, and the long period of sunlight makes up for the shorter length of growing period. 

Wheat requires less rain than corn.

It grows best in a region having about 

20 inches of rain a year. 

The great wheat states in the northern part of the central farming region have dry summers. This helps the ripening and the harvesting of the crop. Threshing can be done out of doors, and large barns to house the harvested wheat are not needed. 

Many of the spring-wheat farmers keep cattle. The manure from the cattle barns helps to keep up the fertility of the soil, and caring for the cattle keeps the men busy when there is little other work to do. The climate and soil of the springwheat-growing region is suited to the raising of potatoes. They are not a large crop, however, because the demand at home is small and the cost of shipping the potatoes long distances to market is too great. 

The Use of Machines. In the early days wheat was sown by hand, reaped with a sickle, and threshed with a flail. 

By these simple means wheat bread could be produced only for the few. Now gang plows turn over the soil, and drills are used for seeding (233 B). The ripe

A. - Elevator at Minneapolis, in which wheat is stored. Compare its size with that of a freight car. wheat is cut and bound by harvesting machines (227 A), and the grain is separated from the straw by threshing machines, or a combined harvester and thresher may do all the work (103 A).

The use of such machinery in the cultivation of the wheat lands, and the use of railroads and steamships in the transportation of wheat and flour, make it possible for hundreds of millions of people to have bread at a moderate price. 

Wheat Markets. - After the wheat is raised it must be taken to market. The farmer hauls it to the railroad station. 

If it does not go at once into a freight car, it is lifted into a grain elevator where it is stored until needed. When it is to be shipped, the grain is loaded into freight cars through chutes. The railroad takes it to some milling center such as Minneapolis, or to a Great Lakes port such as 

Chicago, Superior, or Duluth. In such places are elevators (A) that hold millions of bushels of wheat ready for the mills or awaiting shipment on steamers to the east or across the ocean. 

Flour Mills. Much wheat is taken to Minneapolis, the chief wheat-milling center in the United States. The city is on the Mississippi River at the Falls of 

St. Anthony, in a vast wheat region; and trains on many railroads bring in the crop. Large Minneapolis mills make many thousands of barrels of flour in a day. Barrels and sacks are needed, in which to pack the flour. People are kept busy in the lumber and paper mills making these barrels and sacks. The railroads that handle the crop and the manufactured flour must have steel for rails and engines, wood for ties, and both iron and wood for freight cars, stations, and elevators. Thus the people of the farm, the forest, the mine, the mill, and the railroad all help to supply the loaf of wheat bread - but we do not really get the loaf until the wholesale and retail dealers in flour and the baker have done their parts. 

A List to Make. -Name the states in the central part of our country which are heavy producers of wheat. (Use the map, 234 A.)

OTHER FARM PRODUCTS 

Special crops are raised where soil and climate favor them or where great markets call for them. You remember the cranberries and tobacco and Aroostook potatoes of New England, the truck gardens of Long Island, the grapes of western 

New York, the peanuts of Virginia, and the sugar cane of Louisiana. So in parts of the North Central States, as markets and transportation have improved, the farmers have raised more special crops.

Students of farming often tell farmers of special crops suited to their land. 

Special Crops of the North Central 

States. Much flax is grown in North 

Dakota, South Dakota, and Minnesota.

It is not grown for its fiber, but for the seed, which is used for making linseed oil. After the oil has been pressed out, the cake that remains is a valuable food for stock. The oil is used in mixing paints. Most of the flax fiber used for making linen is raised in Europe. 

Other special crops are celery, beans, and sugar beets. Kalamazoo, Michigan, is the shipping center of one of the districts where fine celery is grown. Michigan and Wisconsin raise more sugar beets than any other states east of the Mississippi River, and have a number of factories for making the sugar. Tobacco is raised in parts of Ohio and Wisconsin. 

Grains. Three fourths of the oats -― raised in the United States are produced in this group of states. Illinois and Iowa, which have the largest proportion of prairie land, are the leaders in raising this grain. Most of the oats are fed to horses and poultry, but some are used for the food of man. Rye and barley are raised in some parts of this region. 

Forage Crops. Hay is a very important crop of the North Central States.

Besides hay, the forage plants include corn, sorghum, and kafir corn. This lastnamed forage plant grows well in dry regions. 

Fruit. -The North Central States pro duce much fruit. Many grapes are grown in southern Michigan, near Lake Michigan; and in northern Ohio, near Lake 

Erie. You will find few grapes on the 

Wisconsin side of Lake Michigan because the prevailing, or most frequent, winds blow from west to east. In autumn, frosts come early in Wisconsin, but the wind passing over the lake to the Michigan side warms the air over the vineyards and keeps off the frosts till later. 

Apples, peaches, and other orchard fruits are grown in abundance in Michigan and 

Ohio and also in Missouri, Illinois, Kansas, and other states.

MINING AND LUMBERING 

The North Central States are well provided with useful minerals. Most of these states have coal beds (map, page 

141). Minnesota, Michigan, Wisconsin, and Missouri have deposits of iron ore. 

Copper is found in northern Michigan, and lead and zinc in Missouri, Kansas, and Wisconsin. Petroleum and natural gas, building stones, and clays are found in many states of the group. 

Iron. Minnesota produces more iron ore than any other state. Most of the ore which is used in the Pittsburgh district comes from the three states of the Lake Superior region. Much of it is an earthy ore that is easily dug in open pits. Trains of ore cars are run into these broad pits, and the cars are filled by steam shovels. 

When filled, the ore cars go to Duluth, Superior, or other ports on Lake Superior or Lake Michigan. They are run out on long piers, and the ore is dumped into great pockets and from these into the holds of ore vessels, to be taken down the lakes. Most of the ore is unloaded (A) at Detroit, or at Toledo, Cleveland, Erie, Buffalo, or some other port of Lake Erie, or at Chicago or Gary on Lake Michigan.

Some of it is smelted in these cities, but much goes on to Pittsburgh. 

Thus in most cases the iron ore is brought near the coal beds, or near large markets. But the big freight steamers that bring iron ore from Lake Superior to the lower lake ports do not have enough freight to carry on the return trip.

Therefore, they can afford to carry coal very cheaply. Some of the coal is shipped to cities farther west. A steel plant at Superior, Wisconsin, uses coal brought from the lower lakes in this way.

In the North Central group the manufacture of iron and steel, and of iron and steel castings and machinery, is important not only in the cities already mentioned, but also in many others. It is, for example, the chief industry of Youngstown and 

Canton in Ohio; and of Joliet in Illinois.

A.-Sawmill. The band saw passes around two large wheels, one above and one below. A slab or a board is cut from the log on each trip of the car past the saw. There are sawmills like this in many lumbering regions. 

Coal. In this group of states there are extensive beds of soft or bituminous coal, especially in Illinois, Indiana, Ohio, Iowa, Kansas, and Missouri. There are coal beds under about two thirds of the state of Illinois. With so much coal to supply power, manufacturing has grown very rapidly in many of the cities in the 

North Central States. 

Copper. Michigan is one of the four leading states of our country in the production of copper; the other three are Arizona, Utah, and Montana. The

Michigan copper is found on the Keweenaw Peninsula, and has been longest known. Pieces of the metal were broken from ledges of rock and carried southward by the ice sheet. The Indians sometimes picked them up and hammered them into implements. Now the copper ore, consisting of small particles of metal scattered through the rock, is mined far below the surface.

The United States produces about half of the world's supply of copper. This metal is much used for making electrical apparatus and machinery, and electric and telephone wires, because copper is a good conductor of electricity. Copper is also the chief metal in brass and bronze. 

Much copper is exported.

Other Mineral Products.- Much pe troleum and natural gas are found in 

Kansas, and smaller amounts in Illinois, Indiana, Ohio, and Michigan.

The limestone and clay needed in the manufacture of cement are found in many places in the North Central States.

There are large cement factories in Michigan, Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, Missouri, and Kansas.

Missouri ranks first among the states in the production of lead and zinc. Gold is mined in the Black Hills in South 

Dakota, and Michigan produces more salt than any other state in the country. 

Lumbering. Lumbering (A) is still carried on in the forests of Michigan, Wisconsin, and Minnesota, though most of the white pine and much of the other 

---

timber have been cut. The industry is no longer so important as it once was in the North Central States, or as it is now in some states of the South and on the 

Pacific coast. 

Duluth and Superior, at the head of

Lake Superior, are large lumber markets, as is also Minneapolis in Minnesota. The cities of Grand Rapids, Michigan, and 

Rockford, Illinois, are especially known as furniture-manufacturing centers (A).

The making of farming implements has become a very important industry in this group of states, for here are found (1) supplies of lumber, (2) supplies of iron, and (3) farms that offer a market for the product. Illinois makes nearly half of the agricultural implements of the country, and Chicago makes far more than any other city. Chicago is near the lumber and iron of the Lake Superior region, and has supplies of coal for her factories, and a ready market in all the prairie states. Among the many other centers of this manufacture are Moline, Peoria, and Rock Island, Illinois; Springfield, Ohio; and South Bend, Indiana.

1. Extensive soft coal fields. 

2. Enormous supplies of iron ore. 

3. Plentiful supplies of lumber.

CITIES AND TRANSPORTATION 

The population of the North Central

States has grown rapidly in recent years, especially in the states bordering the 

Great Lakes and the Ohio and Mississippi rivers. Only a few of the many cities of the North Central States have so far been mentioned. You should know more about the chief cities of this region and about the transportation routes that serve them.

The Ohio Basin.The Ohio River basin reaches from New York to Alabama (map, page 87). Find two streams that come together at Pittsburgh. One of these comes from New York. Find the

Tennessee River where it flows out of 

Alabama. A large part of the Ohio basin is in the great Appalachian Plateau. 

Much of Ohio and parts of Kentucky and 

Tennessee belong to the plateau. In the lowlands of the Ohio basin in Kentucky is the rich Blue Grass region, and in the lowlands of Tennessee is the Nashville basin.

A. 

-Making automobile tires in Akron.

The Ohio River, as you know, was a great gateway for the early settlers. settlers.

They came from the East to the place where Pittsburgh is, and went down the 

Ohio in rough houseboats called "arks."

They also came through the Great Valley from Virginia and through the rough plateau to settle the Ohio lands on both sides of the river. The river is now a great freight route for coal and other products, and has three very large cities on its banks - Pittsburgh (pages 197198), Cincinnati, and Louisville (page 153). 

Cities in and near the Ohio Basin. 

Cincinnati is the largest port on the 

Ohio River except Pittsburgh, and is an important railroad center. It is a gateway to the South, the city having built its own railroad to Chattanooga. It is a manufacturing center for boots and shoes, clothing, pottery, machinery, tools, and ornamental iron. It is a large market for fruits and vegetables, as well as for hardwood lumber. Many residents of near-by cities in Ohio and Kentucky carry on business in Cincinnati. 

On the Ohio River in Indiana is Evansville, a hardwood lumber market. It has flour and grist mills and furniture factories.

In Ohio, Indiana, and Illinois there are many important cities not on lakes or on navigable rivers. Among these the largest is Indianapolis. It is an important railroad center, and is the center of trade for the state. It has large pork-packing establishments, machine shops, and automobile and other factories. In Indiana, also, Fort Wayne has large machine shops and other factories; and South Bend makes automobiles, wagons, and plows.

Columbus, the capital of Ohio, is near the coal fields of the state, and has many manufactures, especially of machinery, cars, and shoes. At Dayton, another city of Ohio, are manufactured railroad cars, airplanes, and most of the world's supply of cash registers. Akron makes more rubber goods than any other city in the world. This industry was established there long before automobiles were made. 

But when the cities of the North Central

States began the manufacture of automobiles and trucks it was natural that the 

Akron rubber factories should manufacture tires and tubes (A). It leads all other cities in this work. 

The Mississippi Basin as a Whole. 

You have already studied about the southern part of the Mississippi River.

The fertile soil of its delta and flood plains is used for growing sugar cane and cotton. The river has a very large basin because the main stream and its branches reach to about half of the United States. 

It is a very sluggish stream and is useful for power only in the upper part of its course. The elevation at its source is

New York, for example, has an average fall of more than twenty feet a mile. 

Cities of the Mississippi River. - St.

Louis, the chief city of Missouri, is the largest city in the basin of the Mississippi

River. It is on the west side of the 

Mississippi, a little below the mouth of the Missouri River. It is midway between the traffic of the upper and that of the lower Mississippi. Railroads from 

Chicago, Pittsburgh, New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore center here. 

St. Louis handles a great variety of products (A), including tobacco and cotton from the South, meat from the West, wheat from the North, coal from Illinois, and the lead, zinc, coal, glass, and iron of Missouri. It was one of the early trading posts in the Mississippi Valley and is still a great fur market. It is a great lumber market, and its manufactures include shoes, clothing, tobacco, chemicals, and railroad and street cars. 

It is also a meat-packing center. 

Several bridges cross the Mississippi 

River at St. Louis. On the Illinois side of the river is East St. Louis, with flour mills and other factories (A), besides some large meat-packing establishments just outside the city limits. 

At Keokuk, Iowa, 140 miles northwest of St. Louis, is a concrete dam across the 

Mississippi River, 31 feet high and nearly a mile long, one of the greatest power dams in the world. In the power house the water turns immense water wheels, called turbines, and generates electricity.

Most of this power is used in driving machinery and street cars in St. Louis and other cities. Steamboats on the river pass the dam by means of a lock. 

At the head of navigation on the Mississippi River are St. Paul and Minneapolis in Minnesota. St. Paul is the capital of the state, and Minneapolis is the largest city. The two cities are so close to each other as to be one center of population, having together more than 700,000 people. They are often called the Twin 

Cities. Minneapolis owes its development as a manufacturing city to the water power of the Falls of St. Anthony in the 

Mississippi River. The great fields of the spring-wheat belt are near, and 

Minneapolis has become the largest center in the world for making flour (A). It also has large linseed oil mills. St. Paul has a large wholesale and retail trade, and is a printing and publishing center. Many railroads center in the two cities, with lines running eastward to Milwaukee and 

Chicago, northward to Winnipeg, westward to Seattle and Portland, and southward to St. Louis.

In central Iowa, on a tributary of the 

Mississippi, is Des Moines, the capital, largest city, and chief trading center of that state. It is a manufacturing and railroad center.

Cities of the Missouri Basin. - On the 

Missouri River in Missouri is Kansas 

City, with a large trade in live stock, grain, and agricultural implements. 

Across the state line is Kansas City, Kansas, which, although much smaller than the sister city in Missouri, is the largest city in Kansas. Meat packing is the leading industry, and Kansas City is one of the largest live-stock markets in the world (239 A). From this city and from A. - 

Ewing Galloway 

Sacking flour in a Minneapolis mill. 

Omaha, Nebraska, cattle and sheep from the western grazing regions are sold to corn-belt farmers to be fattened. 

Up the river is St. Joseph, Missouri, which is also a trade and meat-packing center. On the Missouri in Nebraska is Omaha, a great railroad center with lead and silver smelters and refining works, and meat-packing houses. On the 

Iowa side of the river, opposite Omaha, is the smaller city of Council Bluffs, a trading center for the surrounding region, particularly in agricultural implements, and the eastern terminus of the Union 

Pacific Railroad. 

Some distance west of the Missouri 

River are Topeka in Kansas and Lincoln in Nebraska, each the state capital and the trading center of a fertile and productive farming region. The cities of North 

Dakota and South Dakota are still small because the natural resources of these states have not been fully developed. 

Sioux Falls is the leading railroad center and the largest city of South Dakota; and Fargo, on the Red River, is the largest city of North Dakota.

A. 

The Great Lakes. - On the map, look at the five great lakes in the heart of the North American continent. Their waters reach the ocean through the St. Lawrence 

River. Along their shores stretch vast inland plains. Notice how large the lakes are. Lake Superior is the largest freshwater lake in the world. It is almost as large as the state of Indiana. Named in the order of size, the others are Lakes 

Huron, Michigan, Erie, and Ontario.

Lake Ontario is less than one fourth as large as Lake Superior.

Through these "Soo Canals mous amount of freight passes. Iron ore goes south to ports on Lake Erie and Lake Michigan, and wheat and lumber also are shipped in large amounts. Coal is the largest item of freight shipped up the lakes.

Cities of the Great Lakes. - Of the thirteen largest cities in the United States, five are counted as cities of the Great 

Lakes. They are Chicago and Milwaukee on Lake Michigan, Cleveland and 

Buffalo on Lake Erie, and Detroit. Find them on the map. Detroit is on a short river between two of the lakes. Which two? Find also, on the map, Toledo and Duluth. Many of the shops and markets of the prairie states are in these seven cities, which are railroad centers as well as lake ports. Here the grain, cattle, and hogs are sold. From these cities supplies are sent to the homes and farms in the rich agricultural regions. 

Chicago. Chicago is second in size among the cities of our country and fourth in size among the cities of the world. It has a population of about 

3,500,000. It is near the head of Lake 

Michigan, the only one of the Great Lakes that lies wholly within the United States.

The rich fields, mines, and forests of 

Michigan, Wisconsin, Indiana, and Illinois are not far from Chicago, and the city has naturally become the center of great business activity. The Central

Plains are so nearly level over large areas that many railroads have been easily built from the city. These railroads, the shipping on the lakes, the vast and rich prairies, and the coal, iron, copper, and lumber that are not far away, have all helped to make Chicago the chief city of the central lowland of North America.

The University of Chicago is one of the great universities of our country. 

A small stream called the Chicago

River has been deepened and forms part of the harbor of the city. The outer harbor is protected by long breakwaters.

The water supply of Chicago comes from Lake Michigan. It is taken from the bottom of the lake at a point some miles out from the shore. To avoid the pollution of the water with sewage the city dug the Drainage Canal. This canal is nearly 30 miles long and not less than 

22 feet deep. It conducts the waste of the city, mingled with water from Lake 

Michigan, to the Illinois River. This canal is designed for use as the connecting link between the Great Lakes and the Mississippi River system and therefore as part of an important route for commerce when the channel of the Mississippi is deepened enough to permit the use of larger vessels. 

Chicago has many lofty business buildings, wide boulevards (250 A) and parks, and the means of transportation common to great cities, such as surface and elevated railroads. There are also subways for carrying freight from one part of the city to another. Iron, coal, and grain are the chief products carried by its ships. 

Its stockyards and meat-packing business (A) are the largest in the world. 

Courtesy Swift & Co.

A. At work in a Chicago meat-packing plant. These men are slicing bacon for packing and shipment.

In the making of agricultural implements, as you have learned (page 245), it is the first city in the world, and in the manufacture of clothing it is second only to 

New York. It has the leading grain exchange of the country, and its elevators have a capacity of many millions of bushels. It manufactures much iron and steel. The parlor, sleeping, and dining cars on many of the American railroads are made in Pullman, a part of Chicago, and many street and freight cars also are built there. 

Other Lake Cities. Milwaukee is on the shore of Lake Michigan, about 85 miles north of Chicago, and is the chief city of Wisconsin. Like Chicago, it has the advantage of sending and receiving goods both by lake and by railroad. Its manufactures include leather, machinery, iron and steel, flour, knit goods, packed meats, and shoes. Milwaukee is an important grain-shipping port and has enormous grain elevators. It is also a distributing point for the coal brought in by lake steamers from Michigan, Ohio, and Pennsylvania, and shipped throughout the Northwest.

Among other cities on Lake Michigan are Racine in Wisconsin, a manufacturing center for agricultural implements, automobiles, and carriages; and Gary in Indiana, with immense steel works, tin-plate mills, and cement works. A. In an automobile factory near Detroit. Here the engines are tested.

Aerial Surveys, Inc. 

A. Part of the Cleveland harbor. Point out the breakwater; docks for freight steamers at the left; passenger steamers and docks near the right; and the fine stadium between. The tallest building is the chief railroad station of the city.

At the head of Lake Superior are Du luth in Minnesota and Superior in Wisconsin, which have iron furnaces, steel mills, and large grain elevators and ore docks. These cities ship iron ore, lumber, and grain eastward, and coal to the west.

All traffic from the upper to the lower lakes passes the city of Detroit. This city is an important railroad center. It is connected with Canada by a bridge and by railroad and vehicular tunnels under the Detroit River. Detroit is fourth in size among the cities of the

United States. It has manufactures of drugs, machinery, stoves, and, most important of all, automobiles (252 A), motor trucks, and airplanes, and their parts.

Other Michigan cities in which the manufacture of motor cars is an important industry are Flint, Pontiac, and Lansing, the capital of the state. 

Several important cities in Ohio are on 

Lake Erie. Cleveland is the greatest of these. Like Chicago, it has a river harbor and has increased its size by building a long breakwater to inclose a part of the lake (A). Cleveland receives iron ore that is on the way from Lake Superior to 

Pittsburgh, and ships coal to all points on the upper lakes. Much of the coal and the iron ore received in Cleveland are not shipped out of the city, for Cleveland has vast iron industries, including many foundries. It also refines petroleum and builds ships for lake traffic.

Among its many other manufactures are automobiles and clothing. 

Toledo, at the west end of Lake Erie, on a river harbor, has a large trade by lake and rail. Among its manufactures are iron and steel goods, automobiles, ships, electrical machinery, glass, and clothing.

A. 

Manufacturing districts and chief lines of transportation in the United States. 

Transportation in the North Central | was much traffic down the Ohio and Mis- 

States. No other part of the United 

States is so well adapted to the development of transportation routes to all parts of the country as the North Central region. The country is nearly level, or at least is not so hilly as to interfere with the making of good roads. Plenty of stone suitable for making excellent roads can generally be supplied from quarries near at hand.

It costs less to build railroads in a level country than in a mountainous region, because there are no expensive cuts and embankments. Several railroads join 

Cleveland, Detroit, and Cincinnati with 

Chicago, Milwaukee, and Indianapolis.

Others have been built westward to 

St. Paul, Minneapolis, St. Louis, Kansas 

City, Denver, and other cities farther west and south. In the older days there sissippi rivers from Pittsburgh, Cincinnati, Louisville, and St. Louis, to Memphis and New Orleans. In later years railroads have been built from north to south along the Mississippi Valley, and there is not so much traffic on the rivers, except that vast amounts of coal are carried down the Ohio River from Pennsylvania and West Virginia.

Thus in the North Central region transportation is easy. The region is mainly a lowland, and it has a network of roads, like those on the Lake Plains of New York. It is crossed by great rivers, and has on the north the greatest system of fresh-water navigation in the world. 

There are many motor busses and trucks in use for carrying passengers and freight for short distances within the cities or for longer distances between cities.

NC 13306 NC13306 A. Eastbound and westbound planes on the runway of the Chicago Municipal Airport. 

A business man of Chicago can go to and from his suburban home by rail, by motor bus, or in his own automobile. 

By means of the telephone he can carry on business with men in Milwaukee, Minneapolis, St. Louis, or, if he wishes, with business men in Europe or South America. He can go by train to New Orleans or New York in a day, and in less than three days to San Francisco. By using an airplane (A) he can make any of these journeys in less than half the time taken by fast trains (map, page 259). 

Great educational institutions have helped him and his children to business success and to a knowledge of the world and its affairs. Every state in this group supports a university. All but three of these universities have more than 5000 students each.

A. 

Farming. In the hill and valley region of the Ozark Plateau much of the soil is stony and poorly suited to ordinary farming. The stones are flinty material once included in the limestone rock but left on the surface when the limestone was dissolved or worn away. Some crops can be raised with profit even in such soils as these. Orchard trees grow well, and this region has become famous for its fine apples and peaches. In certain regions excellent strawberries also are grown.

On the map, page 128, find where the 

Arkansas River crosses the southern part of the Interior Highlands. The lowlands bordering this stream have a fine, deep, rich soil. They are an excellent farming region; the chief crop grown is cotton. 

To the north and south of the valley of the Arkansas there are highlands somewhat like the older Appalachians. The 

Ouachita Mountains are a region of

In a Missouri lead mine. Two men are shoveling ore into a small car. Two others, above, are drilling in preparation for breaking up more ore by means of an explosive. ridges separated by broad, flat valleys, THE INTERIOR HIGHLANDS 

Find the Interior Highlands on the map, page 80. This region, surrounded by the lowlands of central United States, lies partly in the North Central States and partly in the South Central States.

It occupies a large part of Missouri, the northwestern half of Arkansas, and a part of eastern Oklahoma. These highlands have folded mountains and a plateau region like those of the Appalachians and are sometimes spoken of as a western extension of the Appalachian Highlands.

The Ozark Plateau in Missouri and northern Arkansas is a true plateau only in the west. In its eastern part the rivers of the region have cut deeply into the underlying limestone rocks, and the whole region is a maze of hills and valleys. but the soil is poor, the surface rough, and farming difficult. Some of the land that cannot be cultivated is used for pastures for cattle and sheep. The farmers depend upon corn for a part of their own food as well as food for their live stock and poultry. Corn-fattened beef cattle and hogs are raised for sale. 

Lumbering. Highlands that cannot be cultivated or used as pasture are covered with a growth of oak, ash, hickory, and other hardwoods. Many a farmer in Missouri or Arkansas makes money in winter by lumbering on his own land or for some company. 

Mineral Wealth. Among the mineral resources of the Interior Highlands are lead (A), zinc, bauxite, and coal. While lead and zinc are found in several parts of this area, the chief region of production is

A. The business district of Hot Springs, as seen from a high tower. near Joplin, Missouri, where there are large smelters. Most of the bauxite comes from Arkansas. The mines are along the southeastern edge of the Interior Highland, not far from Little Rock.

Coal is found in widely scattered mines in both Oklahoma and Arkansas. Little

Rock is the capital of Arkansas, and a trading and manufacturing center. Since the mining of bauxite has become so large an industry, Little Rock has increased rapidly in size. 

Southwest of Little Rock in the Ouachita Mountains is a region of mineral springs that has been made into a national park. The springs of this region have been found helpful in the treatment of certain forms of illness, and Hot 

Springs has become a famous resort for health and pleasure (A).

or snow on the mountain slopes and on the plain. Some winds blow inland from the Atlantic Ocean, but these are not the prevailing winds.

When you were studying the geography | eastern coastal lowlands, and there is rain of the United States you learned about the winds that cross our country (page 123) and about the distribution of rainfall (map, page 84). The winds that blow across the country are usually from the southwest. These southwest winds, therefore, are the prevailing winds and are called westerlies. In the western part of our country they blow from the 

Pacific Ocean, where they have picked up moisture as water vapor, and move toward the land as moisture-bearing winds. In crossing the mountains the wind is cooled and the moisture falls as rain or snow on the western slopes.

In the eastern part of the country the winds blow inland from the Gulf of 

Mexico and move toward the northeast.

Even in the central lowland they are moving from the warm southland toward the cooler north, and so rain or snow falls on the plains. The mountains in this part of our country also run southwest and northeast. The winds blow northeast in the Great Valley and along the 

In the southern part of North America the prevailing winds are from the northeast. These winds grow warmer as they blow over the ocean, and they pick up more and more moisture. This moisture falls as rain on the lands when mountain slopes or plateaus force these winds from the northeast into higher and higher altitudes. This happens where the northeast winds cross Mexico and Central 

America, and they leave much rain and some snow on the eastern slopes and on mountain peaks.

The map on page 263 shows the prevailing wind belts that girdle our earth, and the calms between them. There is some shifting of these wind and calm belts toward the north in our summer and toward the south in our winter. On the average, however, they are as shown on the map.

NATURAL REGIONS OF NORTH AMERICA
