Between each wind belt and the one next to it there is a belt of calms where there is hardly any wind blowing across the sea or land. The calm belt between the two belts of trade winds is called the equatorial calms or doldrums. The calm belts at and near 30° north and 30° south are the horse latitudes. 

You will notice that north of the horse latitudes in the northern part of the map the winds are from the southwest. South of the horse latitudes in the southern part of the earth the prevailing winds are from the northwest. They are the westerlies. 

South of the horse latitudes in the north the winds blow toward the Equator from the northeast and are called the northeast trade winds. North of the horse latitudes in the south the winds blow toward the Equator from the southeast and are the southeast trade winds.

In the equatorial calms the air is moving upward, but you cannot feel it. At the surface of the earth the northeast and southeast trade winds are blowing into this belt along its edges. In the horse latitudes the air is settling. It is moving downward, and from the outer edges of this calm belt the air is blowing outward over the surface of the earth.

A.— The arrows within the large circle show the direction of prevailing winds on the earth's surface. The arrows outside, at the right and at the left, show movement in vertical sections of the atmosphere on a much larger scale. 

The short arrows show the movement of the air upward in the equatorial calms and downward in the horse latitudes. 

The outermost arrows show the movement of the air at some distance above the earth's surface. 

When you study the other continents you will always want to know their north and south latitude. This will help you to know what prevailing winds blow across them, and that will help you to understand how the rainfall is distributed.

There are other things that influence the direction of winds and the distribution of rainfall, but the map on this page shows the most important prevailing conditions.

NATURAL REGIONS OF NORTH AMERICA 

The map of North America on page 262 will give you a very clear idea of the larger natural regions of our continent. By comparing it with the map of the United 

States on page 80, you will see which of the natural regions of our country extend beyond its borders. To the north some of them will be seen in Canada and 

Alaska, and to the south some in Mexico and Central America. 

In your study of the United States you learned how important the natural regions of our country are in relation to the people and their industries. As you now turn your attention to the countries to the north and to the south of our own you will study the natural regions of each.

Remember that the surface, climate, and natural resources of any country have a great influence on its population.

ALASKA 

Many years ago Alaska belonged to Russia, one of the countries of Europe. 

In 1867 it was purchased from Russia by the United States for $7,200,000. Some of our people thought at that time that our government was paying more for 

Alaska than it was worth. Because the purchase had been arranged by William 

H. Seward, then Secretary of State, people spoke of it as "Seward's Folly." 

Later you will be able to decide for yourself whether the money was wisely or foolishly spent. Alaska has been made a territory of the United States, with its own laws. It elects a legislature and has a delegate in Congress. 

Position and Size. On the facing page you will find a map of Alaska. Before studying it, learn something about the position of Alaska as it is shown on a globe. You will remember that the lines drawn on the surface of the globe from the 

North Pole to the South Pole are meridians. They are north-and-south lines. 

The lines on the globe crossing the meridians at right angles are parallels and are east-and-west lines. Look on the globe at the map of the United States. You will see that many of the state boundary lines are meridians or parallels. 

On the globe find the boundary between 

Alaska and Canada. Notice that the northern part of this boundary is a meridian and therefore a north-and-south line. Follow with your eye the irregular and winding southern part of this boundary until it turns toward the Pacific 

Ocean at Dixon Entrance. This southern part of Alaska along the Pacific coast is sometimes called the "Panhandle."

Study the map on page 264. On this map trace the boundaries that you traced on the globe. You will notice that the meridian lines on the map are straight. 

They get nearer to each other at the north and if they were made longer they would all meet at the North Pole. They get farther away from each other toward the south and would keep on getting farther apart as far south as the Equator. 

From there toward the south they would get closer to each other until they met at the South Pole. 

Notice that the parallels curve. They are drawn in that way on the map so they will cross all meridians at right angles as they do on the globe. It is not possible to show any large part of the earth's curved surface on a flat map without making some parts larger and other parts smaller than they ought to be. Remember in studying any map that meridians are north-and-south lines and parallels are east-and-west lines. 

You will see on your map a long narrow strip of land with some islands beyond it extending in a curving line southwest into the Pacific Ocean. The narrow land strip is the Alaska Peninsula, and the islands are the Aleutian Islands. All of these are part of Alaska and so belong to the United 

States. North of the Alaska Peninsula and the Aleutian Islands is Bering Sea. 

It connects with the Arctic Ocean through 

Bering Strait, where Alaska is only 36 miles from the continent of Asia. The northern shores of Alaska border the cold waters of the Arctic Ocean. About one 265

third of the main part of Alaska is north | there is much rain, with heavy snowfall in of the Arctic Circle.

Alaska is twelve times as large as New 

York state, and about one fifth as large as the main part of the United States.

Point Barrow, its most northerly point, is in almost the same latitude as the most northerly point of Europe. Notice this on the globe. The most southerly island in the Aleutian chain is in almost the same latitude as London, the largest city in the world. The difference in latitude between Point Barrow and the southernmost of the Aleutians is about 20°. is nearly the same as the difference in latitude between Galveston on the Gulf of Mexico and the western part of the boundary between Canada and the 

United States.

This 

Although Alaska is much smaller than the main or continental part of the United States, the distance through which it stretches east and west is about the same.

The distance from its southern point at 

Dixon Entrance to the westernmost of the

Aleutians is about the same as from 

Plattsburg in New York state to Portland in Oregon. 

Climate. Alaska has a great variety of climate. This is due to the difference in latitude between its northern and southern parts, to differences in elevation above sea level, and to the effect of winds from bordering oceans. In the Aleutians and Alaska Peninsula and south along the Panhandle the winds are from the

Pacific and have been warmed by its warm currents. Farther north the winds are from colder waters.

The Aleutians have a moderate temperature throughout the year, with heavy rains and almost continuous fogs. Along the Panhandle the weather is cloudy and the mountains. The warm winds from the Pacific are cooled by the mountains and part with their moisture, as on the 

Pacific coast farther south (page 123).

This part of the coast has the heaviest rainfall of all Alaska. In some places in the mountains 100 feet of snow may fall in a single year. This heavy snowfall helps to account for the large number of glaciers in this part of Alaska. Summer temperatures at sea level are high and winter temperatures are moderate. The thermometer has been known to show 87° at Sitka in August, and the lowest temperature recorded there in winter is 4° below zero. Our northern states all show lower temperatures than this.

In the interior the rainfall and snowfall are much lighter. The summers are short and hot and the winters long and cold. 

Such a climate is called continental. 

Winter temperatures of 75° to 80° below zero have been known. 

At Point Barrow the sun cannot be seen for about forty days during the winter. The temperature averages below zero from November to April. East and west along this Arctic shore the average annual rainfall is less than 7 inches. 

Questions to Answer.-1. How did Alaska become a part of the United States?

3. How far is Alaska from Asia at the narrowest part of Bering Strait? 

4. How far does Alaska stretch from east to west? From north to south? 

5. What causes the great variety of climate found in Alaska? Compare the extremes of temperature at Sitka with those of the place where you live.

- Mt. McKinley, the highest peak in North America. Its summit is always covered with snow many feet deep. 

The land about it has been set aside as a national park in which much wild life is protected. 

Natural Regions. - On the map show-| Many other glaciers are found farther Going the natural regions of North America (page 262) find what ones extend north and west from Canada into Alaska. ing inland from the Pacific you will find a region of mountains bordering the coast for more than 1200 miles and extending inland for 200 miles. Next inland is a region of plateaus, bordered on the north and east by an extension of the Rocky 

Mountains. Beyond them, at the north, is the Arctic Coastal Plain.

Pacific Region. - The Pacific region of Alaska is a continuation of the western mountains of the United States and

Canada. The Coast Range of the Pan handle is rather uniform in elevation, with no well-marked crest. The mountains slope down steeply to the sea. Many long and deep inlets, or fiords, extend from the Pacific Ocean into these mountains of southern Alaska. Great glaciers move slowly down the valleys into the sea at the heads and along the sides of the fiords. back in the mountain valleys. There are more than a hundred large glaciers and several thousand small ones in Alaska.

Mt. St. Elias is the highest of the mountains near the ocean, and Mt. McKinley, in the interior, is the highest peak in 

North America (A). It has a height of almost four miles. 

Notice the many islands lying off shore from the Panhandle country. They are the tops of a mountain range most of which is below sea level. Between these islands and the coast are deep quiet waters forming an inland passage, the route of steamers for the north. the north. Along the mountains that look down upon these protected waters you can see some beautiful forests, snow-covered peaks, and cascades and waterfalls without number.

It is this part of Alaska that is most visited by tourists. Its beauty and grandeur can never be forgotten by those who have been fortunate enough to see it.

A. - Juneau, the capital of Alaska. It is in the Panhandle, on the Inland Passage. Find it on the map. 

On the Alaska Peninsula near where it joins the main part of Alaska, find Katmai 

Volcano. Until early June, 1912, this mountain did not show any sign of volcanic action. At that time a great explosion took place and the whole top of the mountain was blown off. At the same time in a valley to the northwest many volcanic vents were opened. It has been named the "Valley of Ten Thousand 

Smokes." The mountain and valley have been set aside as the " Katmai National Monument." 

Yukon River, however, which crosses 

Alaska from east to west, is useful for transportation in the summer. In winter, travel is by dog sled. Airplanes are now being used frequently. The summer days are very long, but the summer season is short. The long hours of sunshine make farming possible, and hardy crops such as oats, rye, barley, and potatoes are grown. Mining and trapping also are carried on. 

Juneau is the capital of Alaska and the leading mining center of this part of the territory. It is built partly on the side of a mountain (A). 

Central Region. Middle Alaska, along the Yukon and its branches, is a rolling country with no high mountains. 

The mountains to the south are a barrier to transportation because it is difficult to build roads through such a region. The 

Fairbanks is the chief trading center of this region. It is connected by railroad with the coast and by a government-built highway across the Alaska Range to

Chitina on the Copper River and on to 

Valdez. Fairbanks is at the head of navigation on the Tanana River, a tributary of the Yukon. 

Polar Alaska. Northern Alaska, along the Arctic Ocean, is a low and treeless plain. On its southern boundary are the Brooks Mountains, an extension of

Population. None of the towns or villages of Alaska are large. Juneau, the capital and largest town, has a population of only 4000. The total population of Alaska is about 60,000, much less than that of such cities as Niagara Falls or Binghamton, New York. Less than half of the population are white people, the rest being made up of Eskimos (A)

Mining. Since the year 1867 about $700,000,000 worth of minerals have been taken from Alaskan mines. Chief in value among these are gold, copper, and silver. Copper mining did not begin until 1901, but by 1927 its annual value was greater than the combined value of all other Alaskan minerals. The most productive copper mines are in southern and southeastern Alaska. 

Since 1880, gold to the value of about $400,000,000 has been taken from Alaskan mines, and silver to the value of over $12,000,000. Much of the gold was obtained by placer mining (page 97) along the streams and in the sands and gravels of the Nome coast lands. Gold is still produced at Nome, although the

Hatcheries have been established to keep the waters well stocked with fish. 

Potato field in Alaska, near the Pacific coast. Potatoes grow well in cold countries. Remember that the cool 

Aroostook region of northeastern Maine is noted for the excellent potatoes grown there. short summer season, season, poor shipping facilities, and the great distance from markets and supplies, make mining expensive. Fairbanks on the Tanana 

River and Juneau in the southeast are other important mining centers. Some of the miners spend the long winter in 

Alaska, but many of them leave the territory before winter shuts them in. 

There are large deposits of coal in 

Alaska, but little of it is mined, as most of the deposits are so located that transportation costs would be high. Tin, lead, and petroleum are also found. 

Fisheries and Furs. The fishing industry is the most valuable of all Alaskan industries. Among the fish taken are salmon, halibut, cod, and herring, but the salmon are of far greater value than all of the others combined. Many kinds of salmon live in the waters of Alaska. 

Read again what was said about this industry on page 115. Most of the salmon are canned, and the canneries may be seen all along the Pacific coast. The canned salmon is sent to the United

States and may be found on the shelves of nearly every grocery store in our country.

In addition to the canneries there are 

Cod are found on "banks" in Bering

Sea in great numbers, but cod fishing has not been developed into as important an industry as it has on the Grand Banks of Newfoundland.

Many fur seals are taken in Alaskan waters. Laws are now in force to regulate the number of seals to be taken each year and to prevent the killing of the mother seals. Since these laws were put in force, and especially in recent years, seals have greatly increased in number.

The Pribilof Islands in Bering Sea are the breeding ground of the seals. Walruses and whales also are taken in the seas about Alaska. 

Wild foxes and those raised on fox farms furnish valuable furs for export to the United States. These include the red, white, blue, and black foxes. Minks, beavers, otters, and muskrats also are trapped for their skins.

Forests and Farms. There extensive forests along the mountains and in many valleys of southern Alaska and along the Yukon and its branches.

A. 

Part of the 371-mile Richardson Highway between Valdez and Fairbanks. Here it winds through an evergreen forest at the foot of a mountain range. 

The trees are, as a rule, not large. They are chiefly poplar, spruce, and hemlock, and will be more useful for wood pulp than for timber. Timber for building is brought in from the Puget Sound ports. 

Because of the cool climate and the frozen and rocky soils, Alaska is not a good agricultural country. Some hardy grains will grow, but much of the grain is cut green for forage. Potatoes (270 A) and some other vegetables are successfully grown. Some dairy cattle are kept and are pastured on native grasses during the summer. They must be fed during a winter of about eight months. 

Years ago reindeer were imported from 

Siberia in northern Asia. Because of the similarity of the climates these useful animals have done well in their new home and their numbers have increased greatly. 

There are now many large herds in the territory. Many people believe that that reindeer and moose raised on the tundra lands of Alaska and Canada will furnish a valuable meat supply in future years. 

Transportation. - A good road (A) runs from the seaport Valdez to Fairbanks on a branch of the Yukon River. The

United States government has built and operates the principal railroad of Alaska, reaching 467 miles from Seward, on the south coast, to Fairbanks. Passengers and freight can thus be transported to the heart of the territory, and various mining regions are opened to traffic. Another railroad leads from the seaport Skagway to White Horse on the Yukon; from there one may travel by steamboat down the Yukon to the sea. Coast steamers run along the south shore, and northward to Seward Peninsula and Nome. 

Along winter trails, small flags are placed every few hundred feet to guide and save the traveler during the dreadful storms. Travel is difficult and dangerous also because streams that must be crossed are without bridges. 

There are several thousand miles of telegraph and telephone lines. A submarine cable connects Alaska with

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

273 

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

Turn to the map on page 10 and find the narrow neck of land that connects the continents of North America and South

America. It is the Isthmus of Panama. 

The map shows you that there is a part of the isthmus which is not very high above sea level. One of the lowest routes across it is followed by a railroad. The 

United States has built also a canal across the isthmus, following about the same route. Is this canal of much value to our country or to other countries?

Discovery of the Pacific Ocean. - It was in 1513 that Balboa, a Spanish explorer then living in a colony not far away, crossed the isthmus. There were with him a small company of Spaniards and many natives. He had learned from a native chief that there was a large body of water beyond the land. The expedition made its way through forests and swamps until it came to the ocean that we now call the Pacific. Balboa called it the "Great 

South Sea" and claimed it for Spain. 

The Panama Canal. — Although some earlier explorations and settlements had been made, the Spaniards now extended their explorations in the western part of 

South America and in southern North 

America. To make it easier to get from

Spain to the western parts of the two continents it was planned to dig a canal across the isthmus. The Spanish government at first did not want such a canal, but many years later the government favored the plan. No work was done by the Spanish, and the canal was not completed until nearly 400 years after it was first talked about.

In 1881 the canal was begun by a French company with a famous French NCS- - 16 engineer in charge. It was planned to dig a sea-level canal so that ships could pass through without being lifted or lowered in locks. The French companies spent about $250,000,000 in the work and lost the lives of many hundreds of their workmen. They finally gave up the attempt and sold their property and all their rights to the United States for $40,000,000. In 1903 the Republic of 

Panama sold to the United States a strip of land ten miles wide across the isthmus. 

The canal was to run through the middle of this strip. After the United States began work the canal was completed in about ten years. 

Getting Ready to Build the Canal.

While the French were working on the canal many of their workmen died of fever, especially of yellow fever, a very dangerous disease. Before the United

States started to build the canal, Dr. 

Gorgas and many helpers were sent to the Canal Zone to clean up the region so that it would be healthful. Swamps were drained. The breeding places of the mosquitoes that carry yellow fever were covered with oil to kill the larvae hatched from the mosquito eggs. Houses, screened to keep out insects, were built. Pure drinking water was provided. After all this was done to protect the health and furnish the necessary comforts for the workers, the canal work was begun. Its construction was not interrupted by fever or illness of any serious kind among the workers. Today the Canal Zone is a very healthful region in which to live. 

Building the Canal. The American plan called for the use of locks. It meant that the work could be done in a shorter time, at less cost, and that all practical needs would be met. The Gatun Dam

A. 

Courtesy Canadian Pacific Railway 

A huge steamship in a lock of the Panama Canal on its way from the Pacific to the Atlantic Ocean. The gates of the next lock are being opened to admit the ship. Point to each gate. See also the electric tow cars used for hauling the ship into the lock. was built to turn the valley of the Chagres River into a large lake. This in itself was a great piece of work. By using locks the largest ocean ships are raised to the | level of this lake. They sail on the lake part way across the isthmus, and are lowered again to the level of the ocean by locks near the other end of the canal. 

Ships are towed through the locks by electric locomotives running on tracks along the sides of the locks (A).

At the Gatun Dam a hydroelectric plant has been built that uses water from the lake to run its generators. Enough electricity is generated to furnish the power for the electric locomotives, to operate the gates, and for other purposes. 

Use of the Canal. The Atlantic end of the canal is farther west than the 

Pacific end, so that a ship sails southeast in going toward the Pacific Ocean (map, page 272). The canal is fortified by the

United States but in times of peace it is open to the ships of all nations. Ships must pay toll for the privilege of using the canal. Traffic through the canal is very great and is increasing yearly. It is interesting to know that many ocean routes have been greatly shortened by the building of this canal. In the old days the distance from New York by water through the Strait of Magellan to 

San Francisco was 15,126 miles. By the 

Panama Canal route it is 6059 miles - a

PUERTO RICO AND VIRGIN ISLANDS 

275 saving of 9067 miles. Such a saving in distance is also a saving in time and in all the costs that the length of a voyage increases. These include the coal or oil used for fuel, the wages paid to the sailors, the food for their use, and other items. From these savings toll charges must of course be deducted. 

Making a Great Canal. 

Tell the story of the digging of the Panama Canal. Emphasize (1) the difficulties, (2) why locks were used, and (3) the advantages of the canal.

PUERTO RICO AND VIRGIN ISLANDS 

Climate. Puerto Rico is the most eastern of the four large islands of the 

West Indies. It lies at about 18° north latitude, and has an even, tropical climate.

It is not excessively hot, because it is surrounded by ocean waters and is in the course of the northeast trade winds (page 

263). As the island has mountains of moderate height the trade winds are forced to higher altitudes in crossing them. 

This causes more rain to fall on the northeast side than on the southwest. In parts of this island irrigation is needed at some period of the growing season. 

People and Industries. The area of 

Puerto Rico is about three fourths that of

Connecticut. The population is nearly equal to that of Connecticut. About three fifths of the population are white and two fifths are Negro. The island was long under the rule of Spain, but was ceded to the United States in 1898. The A. 1998 

E. M. Newman, 

A truckload of sugar cane on its way from the field to the mill on one of the good roads in Puerto Rico. governor and some other officers are appointed by the President of the United

States. The people elect the legislature, so that Puerto Rico is now largely selfgoverning.

A modern school system is being developed. Roadways, including railroads, are being constructed, along with telegraph and telephone lines. Sugar is the chief product, and the amount produced has grown much larger under American rule (A). Coffee, tobacco, and a variety of tropical fruits also are raised. Vegetables are now sent in large quantities to the winter markets of New York and other northern cities. Such vegetables may be purchased in these cities within a week after being gathered from the truck farms of Puerto Rico. San Juan, the capital, and Ponce are the largest cities.

A. — The main harbor of St. Thomas, one of the Virgin Islands. What flag do you see on the flagstaff at the right?

The waters of the harbor are deep and well protected. 

Virgin Islands. East of Puerto Rico are the Virgin Islands. Three of them were bought from Denmark by the 

United States for $25,000,000 in 1916. 

They are St. Thomas, St. John, and St. 

Croix. They are chiefly valuable to our country because of the excellent harbor in 

St. Thomas (A). Most of the people are 

Negroes. Since the Panama Canal was built it has become very important for its protection that our government should have bases of supplies in the West Indies. 

It was this reason among others that led to the purchase of these islands.

Give Reasons for the Statements. 

1. Although Puerto Rico is in the tropics, it is not excessively hot, because 2. There is more rainfall on the northeast side than on the southwest side of Puerto 

Rico because 

3. Truck farming is important in Puerto

4. The United States purchased some of the Virgin Islands from Denmark because

Out in the Pacific Ocean, about 2100 miles southwest from San Francisco, lie the Hawaiian Islands. Many of the ships crossing the Pacific between North American ports and those of Asia, Australia, and 

New Zealand stop at Honolulu, the chief port of the islands. There they are able to get fuel and supplies and to take on or land passengers and freight. It is because of the central position of these islands that they are said to be " at the crossroads of the Pacific." In 1898 the 

Hawaiian Islands were annexed by the 

United States. As you read about them make up your mind as to whether these islands are of much value to our country. 

Size and Location. -The area of the group is 6449 square miles, of which the one island Hawaii has nearly two thirds (map, page 278). The area of the group is much less than that of Massa chusetts or New Jersey. The state of 

Ohio is more than six times as large.

The more northerly islands are just to the south of the Tropic of Cancer, and the whole group is between 18° and 23° north latitude. 

Formation. The islands have been built chiefly by volcanoes that have poured out lava from the interior of the earth. They are really volcanic peaks that rise above the level of the sea, but they rest upon the ocean bed 15,000 to 

18,000 feet below the surface of the ocean. 

The highest peaks are those of Hawaii, which reach nearly 14,000 feet above the sea. Mauna Loa, on this island, is an active volcano. At its top, and at another point on the east side of the mountain, are the largest craters of active volcanoes in the world (A). These craters are several hundred feet deep and two or

U. S. Geological Survey 

A. Part of the crater of a volcano in Hawaii. three miles wide. When the lava in the craters cools, as it sometimes does, there are places hard enough to walk on, but they contain lakelets of spluttering lava. 

At certain times the lava rises in the higher crater until it runs out of some break in the side of the mountain and flows slowly down the slope. These streams of melted rock sometimes flow across the island for fifty miles or more to fall into the sea. The period between two such eruptions may be several years. 

Climate. The islands are in the belt of the northeast trade winds (page 263). 

These winds blow over the Pacific as they do over the Atlantic. As these winds are forced up into cooler heights when they cross the mountains of the islands, the rainfall is heavy on the northern and eastern sides. Not much rain falls on the southern and western parts of the islands. 

The climate is mild and even, because the islands are in the tropical part of the earth where the temperature changes very little at sea level. There are no wellmarked rainy and dry seasons, although there is usually more rain in winter than in summer. The temperature decreases about one degree with each 300 feet of elevation and there is usually snow on 

Mauna Loa and on Mauna Kea, the other great peak of Hawaii. There is little or no vegetation above 11,000 feet.

Agriculture. The chief industry of the Hawaiian Islands is agriculture. The soil is fertile, being formed from decayed lava. The climate is warm the year round, so plant growth is not interrupted by frosts or snows anywhere near sea level. Breezes from the ocean make the temperature much cooler than it is in tropical regions of the same elevation that are far inland.

A. 

Pineapple plantation near Honolulu, in the island of Oahu. One pineapple grows at the top of each plant, only a short distance above the ground. New plants grow from shoots that spring up from the base of the old plant. 

Sugar. The greatest crop of the island is sugar cane. 

Soil and climate are both suited to this crop. There are great plantations in the valleys and along the coasts. Where rainfall is not abundant enough for the crop, irrigation works have been constructed. Many Americans have invested in the sugar industries of the islands, and much of the raw sugar is sent to San Francisco to be refined.

Because the growing season is continuous you can see at one time cane that is just starting to grow, cane that has been growing for some time, and cane that is ready to cut. It is not necessary to plant the fields each year, and some fields are not plowed and replanted for several years. 

Other Crops. Among the other crops grown on the islands are pineapples and other tropical fruits, rice, coffee, tobacco, figs, nuts, and dates. Great fields of pineapples (A) are found in many places in the islands, and the industry is increasing in importance. Some of the pineapples are shipped fresh to market, but most of them are canned. The tobacco is made into cigars and cigarettes. The rice is grown chiefly for home use as a food for the poorer people and field workers. 

Manufacturing and Commerce. 

There is but little manufacturing except the making of raw sugar, the canning of pineapples, and the making of cigars.

Sugar mills and canning factories have been built in the regions where the cane and pineapples are grown.

The greater part of the trade of the islands is with the United States. Only a small part is with other countries, chiefly the importing of foodstuffs from 

Japan, China, and Australia, and of fertilizers from Chile and Germany.

Government and People. --The islands were under native rulers until 1893, when a republic was established. Five years. later they were annexed by the United States, and were made a territory in 1900. 

They have their own legislature and send a delegate to Congress at Washington.

A. 

One of the fine Hawaiian hotels, on Waikiki Beach, Honolulu. Do you think the outrigger canoe shown in the foreground would upset as easily as our canoes? 

The original Hawaiians belong to the brown race, but they form only a small part of the present population. The people are now of many nationalities. 

In addition to the natives there are 

Japanese, Chinese, Filipinos, and Americans, as well as some Portuguese and other people from Europe, besides many of mixed descent. The English language is used in schools and in business. 

Cities. Honolulu, on the island of 

Oahu, is the largest city, chief seaport, and the capital. It is a beautiful and modern city, and many of our people go there to spend a winter in its warm and sunny climate. There are fine business blocks, many of them owned by Americans, and large hotels for travelers and visitors to the islands (A). At Pearl Harbor, near by, there is a United States naval station where vessels of the United States Navy go for supplies and repairs. Honolulu is important as a refueling place for Pacific 

Ocean steamers. It takes three weeks or more for steamers to cross the Pacific on some routes, and it would be difficult for a ship to carry all the coal or oil needed for so long a voyage. 

Hilo is the chief city of the island of Hawaii.

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS 

281 

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS, GUAM, AND SAMOA 

The Philippine Islands are southeast of 

China. They lie wholly in the Torrid 

Zone, between 4° and 22° north latitude. 

Of what value are they to our country? 

Physical Features. There are over 

7000 of the islands, with a total area of about 115,000 square miles. Of the larger islands, Luzon in the north is the largest, with 41,000 square miles of surface, and Mindanao in the south has 

37,000 square miles. Much of the land is hilly or mountainous, and there are many volcanic peaks. Earthquakes are not uncommon. The higher peaks are from 8000 to 10,000 feet in altitude. 

Climate. -The climate is warm, and the rainfall is heavy, especially on the northern and eastern mountain slopes.

The prevailing winds are from the northeast. In crossing the warm waters of the ocean they have picked up water vapor which falls as rain when the winds cool as they cross the mountains. The winters are dry, and the summers wet. The soil is deep, and the ground is shaded with a dense growth of plants. In many regions the ground is usually damp and often flooded. Houses are raised above the ground to be above the mud. They are small and light and built chiefly of bamboo (A). Fierce storms called typhoons blow across the islands at times and do much damage. Many of the small native houses may be blown down by such storms. Roads and trails are often impassable, but some good roads are being built. From the United States government weather bureau at Manila, storm warnings are sent out and daily weather reports are issued.

L. Green from  

A. - Native houses in the Philippines, made of bamboo and roofed with thatch. Compare them with houses of our country and account for the differences. 

History and Government. These islands were discovered by Magellan in 

1521, in the first exploring expedition that went around the world. Magellan himself was killed here, but the islands soon came under Spanish rule.

The islands were transferred to the control of the United States in 1898 as a result of a war with Spain. They contain over 12,000,000 people. Of these, a small number are Americans and Europeans, 50,000 are Chinese, and a smaller number are rather tiny black men known as Negritos; but most of the people are of the brown race, and are called Filipinos especially the majority who are Christians.

Chinese immigration is now forbidden, as it is in Hawaii and in the main part of the United States. 

Much exploration and surveying have been done by the American army and by men of science since 1898. The United

States Coast Survey has charted the dangerous seas in and about the islands.

The governor general and a few other officers are appointed by the President of the United States. A Philippine legislature is elected by the people. An excellent system of education has been provided. Most of the teachers are natives, but the English language is generally used in the schools. 

Forests and Minerals. As in most - As in most warm and wet countries, the forests of these islands are very dense. Many kinds of trees are found, including more than fifty kinds of hardwoods. Some of these are heavy and take a fine polish, and are therefore used for fine woodwork. Some are so hard that an ordinary saw will not cut them. The top of a large dining table is sometimes made from a single slab of native wood. There are forest districts and stations, all in charge of expert foresters. In this new care of the forests, as well as in the agricultural experiment stations, in the educational system, and in the charting of the coasts, the good work of the American government is seen.

The bamboo and rattan are put to a variety of uses by the natives, especially in making tools, domestic utensils, and the houses of the poor. The well-to-do 

Filipinos have homes of comfort and elegance. The poorer people and the savage tribes often live in huts of poles, roofed with a thatch of boughs or of grass. The abaca plant is grown for its fibers, called Manila hemp. In preparing the hemp for market, the plants are cut down, the layers that form the main stalk are taken off one by one, and the pulp removed by scraping. The fibers remain.

These are washed and dried and are used in making rope or are shipped to other countries for that use.

Soft coal is found, and nearly all the important metals are known to exist in the Philippines, but as yet little mining has been done. 

Some Things to Do. 

1. Locate the Philippines and mention some of their physical features. 

2. Tell how large the population is and of what races it is made up. 

3. Tell why many of the houses must be raised above the ground. 

4. Make what seems to you a true statement about the value of the forests. 

5. Tell why typhoons are dreaded.

Agriculture. The resources of the islands are very large, but are not yet well known and are little developed. The soils are very deep and rich; in many places they are from volcanic rocks that have decayed and have been fertilized by the decaying plant growth of which there is so much in a moist, warm region. 

Very important industries are connected with the raising of coconuts. The white meat of the coconut is crushed and pressed to get an oil used in food products and in making soap. When the coconut meat is dried it is called copra, and much A. 

Ewing Galloway 

Opening coconuts to get the meat ready for making copra or coconut oil.

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS, GUAM, AND SAMOA 

283 

A. Plowing with a water buffalo. of it is shipped out of the islands to our own and other countries. Coconut meat is shredded and used in the manufacture of candy and in puddings and cakes. 

Raw cane sugar and tobacco, as well as refined sugar and cigars, are among the exports sent to the United States. 

Rice is the main food crop, but it is not sent to other countries. At times not enough is grown for home use. More and more of the forest is being cleared for crops, and in some valleys narrow terraces on which rice is grown have been built on the hillsides almost to their very tops. 

Corn, bananas, coffee, spices, and small fruits are raised. Farming methods are crude. The water buffalo, used in tilling the soil (A), is slow and unruly, and requires a frequent plunge in mud and water. 

This habit fits it, however, for use in the rice fields, which must often be flooded. 

Commerce and Cities. Much of the surface is covered with a jungle, but the muddy trails are giving way to good roads, several thousand miles of which have been built. These roads make it possible to reach the soils, forests, and minerals of the lands in the interior. 

Several railroads have been built during the recent years of American occupation, and there are now in the islands more than 6000 miles of telegraph lines. There is ocean cable service to Asia and to the 

United States.

The chief city is Manila, the capital, which has a population of about 330,000. 

The second city in size and importance is Iloilo, on the island of Panay. 

Guam.- A few hundred miles east of the Philippines is the small island of Guam.

The United States took Guam from Spain in the war of 1898. It is useful as a fueling station for vessels visiting the United 

States, Hawaii, and the Philippine Islands. 

Samoa Islands. The island of Tu tuila and a few smaller islands of the 

Samoan group belong to the United 

States; the others are controlled by 

New Zealand. Tutuila is a mountainous island, with the excellent harbor of Pago 

Pago. A United States naval station is maintained there.

The American flag has also been raised over a few other small islands in the 

Pacific Ocean (map, page 278).

Canada is one of a group of countries that make up the British Commonwealth of Nations. It is under the British flag but it is in most ways entirely independent. 

You remember that you read earlier in this book about the finding of Puget Sound by Vancouver and of the Columbia 

River by Captain Gray. You remember, too, that for many years England and the United States claimed that part of our continent. People of both countries made settlements there. They did not go to war to settle the question, but finally agreed in treaties that the parallel of 49° north latitude should be the boundary from the Pacific Ocean almost to the 

Great Lakes. In the same peaceful way, by arbitration, or the decision of a committee, the boundary between our state of Maine and Canada was fixed, and also the line between Alaska and Canada.

We have much trade with the country that is so close to us on the north, and many of you who have never seen any other foreign land have been in 

Canada. You will now want to learn about the country itself, its people, and their resources and industries. Make a list of the lakes and rivers that are on the boundary between Canada and the United 

States. What parallel of latitude is used as the boundary between Canada and our states of New York and Vermont? 

Regions and Provinces. The most southern point in Canada is in about 42° north latitude, on the shores of Lake Erie, not far from Detroit. The northern parts are on the Arctic Ocean, only a few hundred miles from the North Pole. 

Like the United States, Canada has low mountains in the east, high mountains in the west, and broad plains lying between the two highlands. 

Southern Canada may be divided into four regions. On the east are Nova 

Scotia, New Brunswick, and Prince

Edward Island, which are often called the Maritime Provinces. An old name for the region is Acadia. Here are the only ports of eastern Canada that are free from ice throughout the year. The most important ports are Halifax, St. 

John, Sydney, and Charlottetown. The surface is low and nearly flat in Prince Edward Island. The surface in the two other provinces is higher and much more hilly. It is much like the surface of parts of New England.

The second region consists of the provinces of Quebec and Ontario. These lie along the St. Lawrence River and the

Great Lakes, and extend northward into a wilderness of rocks, hill lands, and forest. Northern Ontario and Quebec border Hudson Bay, and Quebec has a long shore line on the Gulf of St. Lawrence. 

The lowlands along the St. Lawrence and the Great Lakes have rich farms and the largest cities and densest population of the country. Some of this region is hilly, but in southern Ontario there are lake plains like those of western New York. 

North of the St. Lawrence Valley and the lake plains is the region known as the Laurentian Plateau. It includes most 285

of Quebec, crosses Ontario, extends into | Keewatin. In the southern part of this 

Manitoba and Saskatchewan, borders region are forests, and in the north are Hudson Bay on the west, and reaches the treeless tundras (page 269). In this re- 

Arctic Ocean. Only a part of this area is gion are Great Slave Lake and Great Bear in Quebec and Ontario.

Lake, and many other large lakes whose outlets lead to Hudson Bay or the Arctic

Ocean. The Yukon Territory is moun tainous and continues the highlands of 

British Columbia northward. A part of northern Canada is often called the

Barren Lands. Some of these lands are free from snow in summer and are brilliant with flowers. They are visited by countless numbers of wild fowl.

The third region includes the provinces of Manitoba, Saskatchewan, and Alberta, whose northern boundary is the parallel of 60° north latitude. It consists of prairie and dry plains, and, at the south, is like the plains of North Dakota, Nebraska, and Montana. This region has in it Lake Winnipeg, and much of it drains by the Red River, the Saskatchewan, and other streams, through the 

Nelson River into Hudson Bay. It is the most important part of Canada for the raising of grain. A part of the province of 

Manitoba borders on Hudson Bay and is similar to northern Quebec and Ontario.

The fourth region is the province of British Columbia, which also has as its northern boundary the parallel of 60° north latitude. Through it the Western

Highlands extend from the United States on the south. They also reach over into western Alberta. On the east are the "Canadian Rockies," and on the west is the northern continuation of the Cascade 

Range of Washington. The glaciers in these mountains of Canada are larger and there are more of them than in western 

United States; but there are not so many of them, nor are they so large, as those of 

Alaska. The southern parts of British 

Columbia are drained by the Columbia and Fraser rivers, and the northern parts by branches of the Mackenzie, which flows into the Arctic Ocean, and by smaller streams flowing to the Pacific Ocean. 

The northern plains of Canada extend to the Arctic Ocean and include the provisional districts of Mackenzie and North of the main body of the continent are many large and many small islands. 

They have much snow, and are inhabited only by a few groups of Eskimos. 

Shore Line and Scenic Beauty. - 

Nearly all the shore lines of Canada are rugged and broken, with deep inlets or fiords, bold headlands, and many mountainous islands. For this reason there are good harbors in British Columbia and in the Maritime Provinces. The St.

Lawrence River is at tide level as far as 

Montreal. Along the western shore, vessels go from Victoria or Vancouver northward to Alaska in quiet waters behind the fringe of islands that border the coast. Many bays reach far into the land between high mountain spurs, or promontories. 

Hudson Bay is an inland sea, 900 miles long, lying in the interior of Canada.

It was named for Henry Hudson, whose sailors set him adrift there to perish about three years after he had sailed up the Hudson River.

Both the eastern and western coasts of 

Canada are important for their commerce.

The coast of Hudson Bay may become important; but the Arctic shores will

Visitors to Canada delight in the grandeur of the rugged mountains and the charm of the beautiful lakes (A).

Climate. -The climate of the Mari time Provinces, and of the region along the St. Lawrence and the Great Lakes, is like that of New England, New York, and 

Michigan. The storms of the westerly winds pass over this part of Canada. 

Moisture from the Atlantic that comes with the east and southeast winds gives heavy rains in summer and heavy snows in winter. Southern Ontario, in the latitude of southern Michigan, is almost surrounded by lakes, and has a mild climate. Here grapes and peaches flourish, as in western New York and southern 

Michigan. Northern Ontario and Quebec are much colder.

The summers of Manitoba, Saskatche wan, and Alberta are much like those of the western plains of the United States, the western plains of the United States, but as these provinces are farther north, the winters are longer and colder. Saskatchewan and Alberta, however, are warmer than the lands eastward about 

Hudson Bay. One reason is that westerly winds, called chinooks, blow across the

mountains in those provinces as well as southward in the United States. After crossing the mountains the air warms as it comes down upon the plains, and makes them warmer than they would be without this wind. The climate of western British

Columbia is moist and mild like that of western Washington. 

Indians. Canada, with its abundant forests, rivers, and lakes, was a natural home for many Indian tribes. They could hunt in the forest, fish in the waters, and gather an abundance of wild fruits. 

Their canoes enabled them to make long journeys by water. These canoes were made of strips of birch bark sewn together with split roots of spruce, lined with thin strips of cedar, and made strong by cedar ribs. The gum of some evergreen tree was used to make the seams watertight. 

The finished canoe was so light that it could move in shallow water and easily be carried across the portages between lakes and streams. 

Explorations. The first explorations up the St. Lawrence and along the Great

Lakes were made by Frenchmen, and the land was long under the French flag (page 7). The missionaries and the fur hunters went through all parts of the great wilderness. They found nothing so useful to them, for travel and for carrying their packs of skins to market, as the Indians' birch-bark canoe. The search for fur-bearing animals really was more important than any other circumstance in the early history of Canada.

After the English took possession of 

Canada, the hunting went on. The 

Hudson's Bay Company, which had been formed long before, spread over most of the country. It held many fortified posts, called "factories," to which the pelts or skins of animals were brought by the hunters and trappers. 

Large parts of the vast Canadian wilderness are little known even yet. For many years explorers have sought knowledge of the rocks, minerals, forests, rivers, and soils, making long and toilsome journeys with canoes no larger than those used by the red men centuries ago. In this way maps are prepared, information about the country is provided, and immigrants and settlers learn where there is good land for farms. They also learn where rich masses of minerals have been found, and where to build their roads. 

People. The total population of 

Canada is over 10,000,000. This is about four fifths as many people as live in the state of New York. Canada, however, is about 80 times as large as New York state. Taken as a whole, the country is very thinly populated. 

Most of the people live in the southern part (316 A). There are more people in the provinces of Ontario and Quebec than in other parts of the country. The western farming area has few people for every square mile of area. Why? 

A large number of French-speaking people live in the province of Quebec, where A.

A. - Horse-drawn reaper and binder harvesting oats in a field in Alberta. This machine cuts the grain, binds it into sheaves, and drops them off in piles. The sheaves are then set up in shocks until the grain is dry enough to be threshed. the customs of the early settlers are still found. Most of the English-speaking people of Canada are of English or Scotch descent, and several hundred thousand of them were born in the British Isles.

Many immigrants in Canada have come from the United States, and smaller numbers from continental Europe and from 

China and Japan. 

The people of the United States and 

Canada have always been very friendly. 

There are no forts anywhere on the boundary between the two countries. 

The Peace Bridge (9 B), built across the 

Niagara River, celebrates the long period of peace which has existed between the two countries. 

Agriculture. In the Maritime Provinces mixed farming is carried on. Grain, fruits, and vegetables are raised and live stock is kept. Fine apples are produced in the Annapolis Valley of Nova Scotia. 

The most valuable kind of live stock in 

Canada is the silver fox which is bred for its beautiful fur in fox "farms" on 

Prince Edward Island (288 A). 

Southern Ontario, especially the socalled Niagara peninsula partly surrounded by three of the Great Lakes, is the chief farming region of eastern 

Canada. It has excellent soils and a mild climate, and produces wheat, apples, grapes, peaches, and small fruits. Much of the butter and cheese shipped to Great 

Britain comes from Ontario. 

Manitoba, Saskatchewan, and Alberta are wheat-growing regions. The surface, soil, and methods of raising the crop are like those in the great wheat region of the North Central States. Winnipeg, the chief wheat market for the district, has a grain exchange similar to that at 

Chicago. Many cities and villages on all the railroad lines have elevators to which the farmers take their wheat for transfer to the railroads. Oats are raised (A); but as the summers are short and cool, corn does not do very well. Wheat is raised, though not in large quantities, in the valley of the Peace River. Cattle and horses are raised on the dry plains, especially in Alberta.

Logging railroad on Vancouver Island. Of what are the ties and roadbed made? For what use is such a railroad constructed? 

British Columbia has rich agricultural regions, though only partly developed.

They are suitable for grain and general farming, and the mild climate of the coasts and valleys especially fits these areas for fruit culture. One of the best of the fruit-growing regions is the valley of the Fraser River. 

Manufactures. The manufactures of 

Canada include agricultural machinery, vehicles, and other iron and steel goods. 

The weaving of textiles and the preparation of food products and of lumber and wood pulp are also important.

Forests. The forests of Canada cover about 800,000 square miles, or more than one fifth of the country. In the eastern provinces there are hardwoods and pines, as well as spruces, aspens, and larches. 

More than a third of the area of Nova

Scotia is covered with forest. The great lumbering region of the Maritime Provinces is New Brunswick, which has large mills at Fredericton and St. John.

The eastern forests extend through 

Quebec and Ontario. The Ottawa Valley is important for its white pine, which, with other timber, is floated down the river to Ottawa. river to Ottawa. There large mills work it up into lumber. Much lumber is also manufactured at Montreal and Toronto.

Many of the soft woods, such as spruce, especially the smaller and imperfect trees, are used for making wood pulp. The forests of northern Quebec have not many large trees, but the timber is suitable for pulp, railroad ties, telegraph poles, fences, mining timber, and firewood. Tree growth in the cold north is very slow, and a small tree may have required hundreds of years for its development.

The forests of central Canada extend between Lake Superior and Hudson Bay to the northwest, across the southern parts of the Mackenzie basin, and join with the forests of British Columbia. The valleys and lower slopes of this province are covered with wonderful forests of cedar, spruce, and Douglas fir, similar to the trees of Washington and Oregon. Here is the third great center for lumbering in 

Canada (A). Much of the product goes to

Vancouver and is shipped to foreign lands. 

Forest fires are very destructive, and they are started not only through carelessness of hunters and by sparks from locomotives, but also by lightning. In a

Courtesy Canadian National Railways

A. 

Asbestos mine in eastern Quebec. Although asbestos can be used in the making of a coarse cloth it is really a mineral. It will not burn and is used for fire curtains in theaters and to cover steam pipes. It dry time the mossy floor of the forest, the thick undergrowth, the dry, fallen timber, and the pitchy character of many of the trees, cause a fire to spread quickly. It sometimes outruns and destroys the swiftest animals of the forest. Half of the timber wealth of Ontario is said to have been destroyed by fires. While the forests are one of the main resources of 

Canada, there is danger of their exhaustion, and measures are being taken for their care and preservation. On 

Two regions have few trees or none. 

These are the plains west of Winnipeg and the Barren Lands of the north. the western plains many farmers are planting trees, for windbreaks, for fuel, and for other uses. 

Mining. One of the most important minerals of Canada is coal. There are three principal regions for coal mining in the Dominion. The first is Nova Scotia, where mining has been carried on for many years. Nova Scotia furnishes most of the coal used for household purposes and for manufacturing in eastern Canada.

The second locality is in the plains east of the Canadian Rocky Mountains. In this region the greater part of the coal is soft, and has not yet been extensively mined.

The large region between Winnipeg and the St. Lawrence River has no coal. It either imports coal from the United States, or brings it from Nova Scotia. The third region in which coal is found is British 

Columbia, which has large supplies like 

Washington on the south. 

Petroleum has been found in the provinces of Alberta and British Columbia, and in the Mackenzie district. Large beds of oil-bearing shales from which oil can be obtained are found in New 

Brunswick and Nova Scotia. Not much use has been made of these shales up to the present time.

Some iron is found in various parts of Canada, but in many cases the mines are far from coal, and the iron-smelting industry of the Dominion is not yet very large. However, iron mined beneath the sea in Newfoundland is near enough to the coking coal of Nova Scotia to make possible a large steel industry at Sydney. 

Asbestos is found in eastern Quebec, and that region furnishes most of the world's supply (A).

A. Fishing fleet at Prince Rupert, British Columbia. 

In the province of Ontario, north of Lake Huron, are several mining centers where nickel, copper, silver, and gold are produced. Ontario is now by far the largest producer of gold in Canada, and in the Sudbury district it produces nine tenths of the nickel of the world.

The Klondike region in Yukon territory, on the borders of Alaska, also yields much gold. The mining center is Dawson. 

Another important mineral region of

Canada is in the province of British 

Columbia. Mountain ranges are found in much of this region. They contain deposits of gold, silver, lead, and copper.

The value of the mineral output of Canada has increased greatly in recent years. 

Some mines, such as the silver mines of 

Cobalt, are being exhausted, but there are vast unexplored areas in both eastern and western Canada in which other large mineral areas will no doubt be found. 

Fisheries. Canada has a broken shore line, and in its numerous bays there are found great numbers of fish. On the east, off the shores of Nova Scotia, is the 

Courtesy Canadian National Railways shallow water of the Grand Banks of 

Newfoundland. Here and in the Gulf of St. Lawrence many fish are caught.

In all the Maritime Provinces fishing is an important industry. Cod, herring, and halibut are especially valuable. Many lobsters also are taken. In recent years the value of the lobster fisheries has been greater than that of the cod fisheries, which had always been the most important. 

Nova Scotia has the largest fishing industry in the eastern part of Canada, carried on from Halifax, Yarmouth, and other ports. Some of the geographical advantages of Nova Scotia in this industry are (1) its many protected harbors, where small fishing villages have grown up, (2) the nearness of the fishing grounds, and (3) the fact that ships to carry the dried, salt, or canned fish to market can come direct to the shipping centers. 

Canada has thousands of lakes and rivers in which fresh-water fish flourish. 

Fishing is a large business on the lakes of 

Manitoba, and many men are employed in it. The "catch" includes salmon,

trout, and whitefish. The salmon and other fisheries of British Columbia are like those of Washington and Alaska. Most of the salmon are canned, and the annual product amounts to several million dollars. 

Halibut and herring also are numerous, and shellfish are gathered near the shores. 

On both the Atlantic and the Pacific sides of Canada, seals, walruses, and whales are still captured in considerable numbers. This branch of the fishing industry is smaller than it formerly was. 

Codfish from the Labrador coast are marketed in Spain, in Greece, and especially in Italy. Codfish has been called the "bread of the sea," because it is abundant, nutritious, and is easily preserved by salting, smoking, or drying. 

Parts of these fish are used for making glue, oil, and fertilizer, and in Arctic lands they are used even for fuel. One medicine, or specially valuable food, is made from this fish, namely, cod-liver oil. 

The ordinary codfish weighs only a few pounds, but large specimens weigh more than 100 pounds each. 

Furs. In 1670 the Hudson's Bay

Company was formed to trade in the furs produced in America, and particularly in Canada. The Dominion is still one of the leading fur-producing regions of the world. Hunting and trapping are carried on in the large areas where it is not suitable for many people to live. There are also many "farms" where fur-bearing animals are raised for their skins. Foxes, raccoons, minks, muskrats, and other animals are bred for this purpose. Montreal is an important market for furs in eastern Canada. Winnipeg and Edmonton also are fur markets. 

Transportation by Water. The prin cipal eastern seaports are Halifax, St. 

John, Quebec, and Montreal. Ships of several lines sail from Montreal to European ports, except during the winter months, when the St. Lawrence River is blocked with ice. From Montreal ships of considerable size can go to the head of the Great Lakes at Duluth or Chicago. 

They pass the rapids of the St. Lawrence

River and enter Lake Ontario by means of canals. By the Welland Canal they can reach Lake Erie. reach Lake Erie. Between Lake Huron and Lake Superior the Soo Canal takes them past rapids in the St. Marys River. 

The Red River and the Saskatchewan are navigable for small boats for hundreds of miles, to Lake Winnipeg. From the lake to Hudson Bay the Nelson River is broken by difficult rapids.

The Mackenzie River and its branches are useful for navigation in northern 

Canada, but not for foreign trade, as it flows into the Arctic Ocean. It serves the fur-trading posts along the river, and these have been called the outposts of civilization. For much of its length of 

2000 miles, measured to the source of Peace River, it goes through a very sparsely settled country inhabited chiefly by hunters and trappers, most of whom are Indians.

The Hudson Bay is not much used by ships, except those carrying grain of the western plains from the port of Churchill to Europe.

Water Power. — Both transportation and manufactures will be favored by the water power which can be made available in Canada, amounting to more than 18,000,000 horse power at ordinary minimum flow of. the streams. Many of 

Canada's lakes will be very useful in developing water power, for they act as storage reservoirs and regulate the flow of WP-17

Railroads. Canada has built many railroads. The 

Canadian Pacific Railway joins Halifax, St. John, Quebec, and Montreal in the east with Winnipeg, Vancouver, and Victoria in the west. It has a network of branches that reach nearly every town and city in the Dominion. Its lines carry to market much of the products of the farms and ranches of Manitoba, Saskatchewan, and Alberta.

The Canadian Pacific Rail way Company owns and operates more than 15,000 miles of railroad in Canada and controls 5000 miles in the streams. The hydroelectric plants | the United States. It operates passenger already built in Quebec, Ontario (A), Manitoba, and British Columbia use only a small part of the available power. The power developed is used to operate street cars and machinery, and to furnish light to homes and cities.

Cities. Ottawa is the capital of the 

Dominion of Canada, and is an important industrial center, especially for the timber products of Ontario. 

Quebec is an old city standing on a bold promontory north of the St. Lawrence

River. It was here that Wolfe won his victory over Montcalm in 1759; and the result of the battle was that this part of America passed from the French to British rule. The dredging of the St. Lawrence has made it possible for ocean vessels to go up to Montreal, and consequently 

Quebec, though prosperous, is not relatively so important a port as in an earlier period.

Montreal, with over 800,000 people, is the metropolis of Canada (A). It is served by the transcontinental railroads, and on the south is connected by rail with 

New York, by way of the Champlain and 

Hudson valleys. 

Toronto is the largest city of Ontario, and the second in size among the cities of Canada. It has much manufacturing, is in the midst of a productive agricultural region, has many fine buildings, and is the seat of Toronto University. Kingston is at the foot of Lake Ontario.

Hamilton is at the head of the lake, and is in the midst of the fruit-growing region.

There is an extensive development of electrical power at Niagara Falls on the

Canadian side of the river as well as on the American side (294 A).

Photo by Royal Canadian Air Force 

A. Part of the city of Vancouver. Find it on the maps (pages 284 and 92). The body of water that you see is the Strait of Georgia. Point out a ship; a dock; a pier.

Winnipeg is the metropolis of central Canada. It has grown rapidly on the site of one of the forts of the Hudson's Bay

Company, where the Assiniboine joins the Red River. All the great railroads that cross Canada center at this market city of the prairies. Southward, railroads run to Minneapolis and Chicago. Regina and Moosejaw in Saskatchewan, and 

Calgary and Edmonton in Alberta, are railroad centers and are important and growing cities. 

Vancouver is the great Canadian seaport on the Pacific coast. Its site was a forest until 1885, and it is now a large and splendid city, the terminus of the Canadian Pacific Railway, and the point of departure for ships running to all countries on the Pacific Ocean (A). Victoria, at the south end of Vancouver Island, has a beautiful situation. It is an important port and the capital of British Columbia. 

Prince Rupert, a little south of Alaska, is another seaport on the Pacific coast.

NEWFOUNDLAND, GREENLAND 

297 

NEWFOUNDLAND 

Newfoundland is a colony of Great Britain, but did not, like other colonies of this region, join the Dominion of Canada. 

It is nearly as large as the state of New York but has not many more than a quarter of a million people. 

Perhaps you think of Newfoundland as far north. St. John's, its capital, is in about the same latitude as Seattle, where roses may bloom until Christmas. You have been told in this book that some places are warm because they are by the sea or the borders of a large lake. Why is not Newfoundland warm? It is partly because a current of cold water from the Arctic seas flows southward along the east coast of the continent to New England. 

Steamships from Canada bound for some British ports find their shortest course north of Newfoundland. Through what strait must they go? Outside of this narrow passage, you may see icebergs which have broken loose from Greenland glaciers and set out on a long voyage southward with the Arctic current. 

The climate of Newfoundland is too cold for most crops. Oats, hay, and some vegetables can be raised, but most of the food must be imported. Much of the land is rocky or too wet for cultivation. 

There is some forest, and the trees, which are not large, are of spruce and other kinds suited for wood pulp and paper. English firms have built large mills for making both pulp and paper. 

Some useful minerals are found. 

Among them are iron, copper, gold, silver, lead, and coal.

As the sea is all about, the main work of the people is in fishing. Not far away are the Grand Banks, where the waters are but a few hundred feet deep. The meeting of the Gulf Stream from the south and the Labrador Current from the north makes the region of the Grand Banks a feeding place for fish, particularly cod, halibut, and and mackerel. Here many nations have fished for centuries. St. 

John's is the center of the fishing industry. 

Newfoundland is not far from the route taken by steamers on their way from New 

York to Europe. If from mid-ocean a passenger sends a radio message home, it may be picked up at St. John's and go the rest of the way by land wire.

Labrador belongs to Newfoundland (map, page 284). Fishing is almost the only means of getting a living there. 

What mountain in Alaska is in the same latitude as the north end of Labrador?

GREENLAND 

Greenland is the largest island in the world. It has over 800,000 square miles of surface, most of which is covered by an ice sheet. There are several towns and villages on the coast, inhabited by Danes and the native Eskimos. The island was discovered more than nine hundred years ago by the daring sailors of northern

Europe. It now belongs to Denmark. 

Furs, ivory, eider down, and seal oil are the chief products.

2. Farming is not carried on extensively in Newfoundland. 

3. There are large paper mills in Newfoundland. 

4. The main industry in Newfoundland is fishing. 

5. Greenland has a very small population.
