In the parts of North America you have already studied, except Greenland, most of the people speak the English language. 

In the province of Quebec in Canada, however, many of the people use the 

French language instead. In all the 

North American countries south of the

United States, except in a few West Indian islands, the main language is Spanish. You have already learned about several of these lands because they belong to our country. You are now to study the southern lands of North America that do not belong to the United States.

Be- 

The American Mediterranean. tween the continents of North America and South America there are two bodies of water largely separated from the 

Atlantic Ocean and from each other. 

These are the Gulf of Mexico and the 

Caribbean Sea (map, page 298). They are often called the "American Mediterranean.' They have been given this name because they lie between the continents of North America and South 

America somewhat as the Mediterranean 

Sea of the Old World lies between the continents of Europe and Africa. You will get a clear idea of the outline of these two bodies of water by a study of the map. 

Follow with your eyes the Gulf coast from the southern end of Florida to the eastern tip of Yucatan. Look across 

Yucatan Strait to Cuba and follow its northern shore until you can look northward to the southern end of Florida. shores that your eyes have followed are those of the Gulf of Mexico.

The 

From Yucatan let your eyes follow along the coast to the south and east until you see the island of Trinidad. Then let your eyes travel along the chain of islands to the north and west until you reach the western end of Cuba. You have followed the shores that surround the 

Caribbean Sea. 

Whenever you use the names of these two water bodies remember how they are separated, in part, from each other, and from the Atlantic Ocean. 

Latin America. In nearly all of the countries on our continent south of the 

United States, in the West Indies, and in 

South America, the explorers and settlers were either Spanish or Portuguese or 

French. Through hundreds of years these languages have been used by most of the people. All three of these languages are much like the ancient Latin language, and people have therefore for many years called these parts of the New World "Latin America." 

Until recently the Spanish language was little known in our country. Now it is taught in many schools. There is much need of it for those who travel in these southern countries or trade with their people. For many years most of this trade went to countries in Europe. Now more and more is coming to our country and there are direct steamship lines between their ports and our own. 

A number of air routes have been established between our country and the various Latin-American countries. This has been done to speed up the mail service and to help business. 299

MEXICO 

South of the United States our continent is less than half as broad as in our country; and, in general, it gets narrower and narrower toward the south. It is largely a land of mountains and plateaus. 

They are a continuation of the highlands in the western part of our own country. 

These highlands continue to the Isthmus of Panama, where they sink to much lower levels, but they rise again to great heights in South America.

The country just to the south of our own is Mexico. A part of the boundary between Mexico and the United States follows the course of the Rio Grande.

West of El Paso in Texas the boundary between the two countries crosses the land to the Pacific Ocean.

Size and Surface. -The area of Mex ico is about one fourth that of the United States. Its most northern point is a little north of 32° north latitude, and its most southern point is a little south of 15° north latitude. The Tropic of Cancer crosses the country about midway between these two points. The northern part, therefore, is in the Temperate Zone, and the southern part is in the Torrid Zone. 

Using the scale of miles on the map (page 298), find the length of Mexico.

Measure from the point where the Colorado River crosses the northern boundary to the point where the southern boundary reaches the Pacific Ocean. What is the width of the main part of Mexico along the parallel of 25°?

The eastern shores of Mexico are washed by the waters of the Gulf of 

Mexico and the Caribbean Sea, and its western shores by the Pacific Ocean. On the south it is bounded by two of the countries of Central America. On the map find the peninsula of Lower California. This part of Mexico is almost completely separated from the main part of the country by the Gulf of California. 

Notice that the Colorado River flows into the northern end of this gulf. North of the lands at the mouth of the Colorado there is a region in southern California that is below sea level. Why is it not flooded by the waters of the Gulf of 

California? Read again what is said. about the lands at the mouth of the 

Colorado on pages 105 and 106 if you need help in answering this question. 

Above the coastal lowlands on each side of Mexico rise steep, rugged mountain ranges. These ranges come together south of the city of Mexico. Where the two mountain systems join, volcanic peaks reach great heights. Their upper parts are covered with ice and snow even though they are in the Torrid Zone. The highest of these is Orizaba, which rises 18,314 feet above sea level. It is nearly as high as Mt. McKinley. Mt. Popocatepetl is 17,888 feet high, and several others have altitudes of from 12,000 to 

16,000 feet.

Between the mountains of eastern and western Mexico is a vast plateau opening toward the United States at the north, where it has an altitude of about 4000 feet. It rises toward the south, where it reaches a height of 8000 feet. As a whole, the elevation of the plateau increases from north to south and from east to west. The city of Mexico is near the southern narrow end of this high basin. 

Southeastern Mexico is a land of mountains and hills.

Most of the rivers of Mexico flow down the mountain slopes on the east and west.

MEXICO 

301 

A. In the dry part of northern Mexico, near Monterey. See the desert plants. What is the horse carrying?

They are short, and offer little chance for | lands. They are are the region of the navigation. These rivers might be a source of water power, but they have not yet been developed. The coast line of 

Mexico is about 6000 miles long, but has few bays and few good harbors. 

Climate and Products. -Mexico has many kinds of climate. It extends over seventeen degrees of latitude. It is bordered by the sea on both sides. It has 

It has great differences in altitude. In most of 

Mexico there is a dry season from November to June, and a rainy season during the rest of the year. The northeast trade winds (page 263) blow across Mexico, and so the mountain slopes facing the Gulf of 

Mexico are wet. The coastal lowland up to 2000 or 3000 feet is known as the hot country. These hot, moist regions are very unhealthful places in which to live, especially for white people from cooler dreaded yellow fever. A large part of this region is jungle. There are no good roads or railroads, and those who wish to enter the forests must cut a pathway through the tangled undergrowth.

Farther south is the hot, dry peninsula of Yucatan. In parts of the peninsula there is enough moisture so that there are large forests. Here, too, grows the plant called henequen or "sisal hemp," valuable for its fiber.

The higher slopes of the mountains that lead up to the plateau are temperate, but warm rather than cool. The cool zone is on the plateau. It has a temperate climate, and is so dry (A) that, in many places, irrigation is necessary for growing crops. It is in this part of Mexico that most of the people live. The southern part of the plateau is in the Torrid Zone,

A. Pyramid of the Sun, built by the Aztecs about 6000 years ago. It is 219 feet high, 761 feet long at its base, and 721 feet wide. It is the largest artificial mound in the western hemisphere. but on account of its elevation it has an enjoyable climate. Midday is usually hot, and at that time Mexicans close their places of business and stay indoors to avoid the hot sun. The early morning and late afternoon hours are much pleasanter, and it is then that the people are most active. The nights are cool. 

The northern part of the plateau is warmer because it is much lower. It is also dry because the eastern mountains shut out the rain. It is a region of cactus and sagebrush (301 A). There are areas where cattle can be grazed, but agriculture without irrigation is impossible.

The west coast, including the peninsula of Lower California, is hot and dry like southern California and Arizona. So little can be grown in Lower California that the Mexicans call it the "fleshless arm of Mexico."

The southern and southeastern hills and lowlands are very hot, but since the distance from ocean to ocean is short there is moisture almost everywhere. The hills are covered with dense forests. 

The result of all this variety in climate is that Mexico produces the fruits, grains, and forest trees of both temperate and hot countries. — - 

Tell Why. Give reasons for each of the following facts: 

1. Most activities in Mexico are carried on during the early morning and late afternoon hours rather than in the midday period. 

2. Most of the people of Mexico live in the great central plateau. 

3. The peninsula of Lower California is called the "fleshless arm of Mexico." 

4. Mexican rivers offer little chance for navigation. 

5. The Spanish language is taught in many of our high schools and colleges. 

People of Yesterday and Today. - For hundreds and perhaps for thousands of years before the discoveries of Columbus there were people in Mexico who lived an orderly and settled life. These native people, later called Indians, were divided into many tribes or nations. The strongest of these was the Aztec nation. The 

Aztecs built fine temples (A) and other

MEXICO 

303 public buildings, made roads, irrigated their fields, and carried on trade. They made articles of clay, cotton, and silk, and mined gold and silver that they used for ornaments and temple decorations.

The country was conquered by the Spanish general Cortes, and was under 

Spanish control for three hundred years.

Spain was never successful in its control of other lands and people. In the years that have passed since its early explorations and settlements, Spain has lost one by one all of its foreign possessions. 

Many of them have declared themselves independent and set up their own governments. Some have passed to other nations as a result of war, treaty, or sale. 

A little more than a hundred years ago the Mexican people set up a government of their own. For most of the time since then Mexico has been a republic with a government somewhat like that of the United States. The country is made up of twenty-eight states, four territories, and a Federal District. 

There are about 16,500,000 people in

Mexico, including the natives or Indians, the descendants of the Spanish settlers, and the half-breeds or mestizos. The 

Indians are descended from the Aztecs and other tribes. Most of them belong to the working or serving class. They are often called peons. They buy a cotton suit for a dollar, a pair of sandals for a dime, and a blanket for a dollar and a half, and they have all the clothing that they need. They are poor, polite, and usually lazy. Although schools have increased in number in recent years, the peon class is still largely without schooling. The mestizos are descended from 

Spanish people who married natives. 

They call themselves the real Mexicans. A. 

A home-made Mexican plow. 

Ewing Galloway 

Rude and simple ways of living are found side by side with those of modern life. The houses of the poor are only one story in height and are built of sun-dried brick or adobe. Straw is mixed with the clay in these mud bricks, as in ancient 

Egypt. Some of the farmers still use wooden plows (A), and peons carry heavy loads on mountain trails. The burro, or donkey, is much used as a beast of burden. There are, however, a number of well-equipped railroads, and the wealthier people have beautiful homes and make use of automobiles.

Beans and corn are common articles of food. The poor soak the corn, grind it, and make it into round cakes. These are baked in an open iron pan and are called tortillas. A kind of century plant, or agave, which, like many desert plants, has much juice, supplies a common drink. 

Mining. There are mines in almost every state, and the value of the silver produced has in some years amounted to over $100,000,000. There are many gold mines and many copper mines, and near Durango is a great deposit of iron known as 

Iron Mountain. Quicksilver, lead, zinc, and sulphur also are found., Among the precious stones found in Mexico are onyx, opals, turquoises, topazes, emeralds, agates, and garnets.

A. 

Silver mine near Pachuca, Mexico. It is believed that silver was mined here by the Indians before the discovery of America. 

Hundreds of millions of dollars are invested by Americans and other foreigners in Mexican mines (A) and oil wells. 

Mexico was once second only to the

United States in the production of petroleum. Old wells no longer yield as much as they did, and the new wells also are small producers. There are now several countries that produce more oil than Mexico. Recent laws have made the operation of oil wells by foreigners less profitable, and the production has decreased as a result. Most of the oil is sent in pipe lines to the coast at Tampico and shipped from there to 

American refineries. 

Agriculture. Corn, wheat, beans, and tobacco are raised in the cooler parts of the country. Corn is the chief crop and is the staff of life to many Mexicans. Almost all tropical fruits are grown, as well 

as sugar, coffee, cotton, indigo, rice, and the vanilla bean. Henequen, or sisal hemp, is extensively raised in Yucatan. It is sent to the United States to be used in making rope, twine, bagging, rugs, and carpets. 

Some kinds of cactus contain fibers that can be used in making paper. Many hammocks are woven in 

Yucatan, and some are very beautiful and costly. Mahogany, rosewood, logwood, and other tropical trees abound in the hot parts of 

Mexico, and lumber forms one of the valuable exports. 

Some rubber is produced. 

Grazing. -The dry plateaus of northern Mexico, like those of western United States, furnish pasturage for thousands of cattle. 

The life on the ranches, the ranches, with their cowboys and round-ups, is like that on the Great Plains of the United States.

Some estates are very large and contain many villages and even private railroads.

Irrigation is increasing in the states of northern Mexico. 

Cities and Routes of Trade. Mexico city is the capital and the largest city, with about 1,000,000 people. Much of it is well built, and its cathedral is one of the largest churches of the New World (305 A). The oldest railroad of Mexico is the one between the capital and Veracruz. The climb from the coast to the plateau is accomplished by skillful engineering. Main lines of railroad extend northward and connect with lines in the United States. Other lines reach

Tampico, Veracruz, and Progreso are important Gulf ports. Tampico exports petroleum; Veracruz exports bananas, hides, gold and silver, and coffee; and

Progreso exports sisal hemp. Among the larger interior cities are Guadalajara, Puebla, and San Luis Potosí.

The people of our country carry on a large trade with the people of Mexico. 

We send them iron and steel goods, textiles, and food products, and get from them ores, metals, hides, petroleum, cabinet woods and dyewoods, and other tropical products.

CENTRAL AMERICA 

How many countries are there in Central America (map, page 298)? British

Honduras is a British colony. The 

Panama Canal Zone, as you know, belongs to the United States. The rest of 

Central America is divided into six small republics Guatemala, Honduras, Salvador, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, and Panama. 

The Republics of Central America. 

All of Central America is less than twenty degrees from the Equator, and the climate is hot. As the land is narrow and in the region of the trade winds, there is a heavy rainfall nearly everywhere. The lowlands of the coast are swampy and unhealthful. The climate of the mountains and hills on the west and southwest is more favorable for human occupation. 

There are several active volcanoes, and earthquakes are frequent.

Few of the people are educated, and the conditions of life are unsettled. As in other tropical lands, many of the houses 

Ewing Galloway

The 

B. Part of a banana plant. bananas grow pointing upward. are primitive. Coffee, sugar, rubber, and tropical fruits, especially bananas, are extensively raised and are the chief exports. All these countries are rich in cabinet woods and dyewoods.

Like Mexico, these countries were long ruled by Spain, but became independent early in the last century. With education, with sanitary ways of living, and with the development of commerce on the isthmus, they will no doubt become prosperous. Some of the cities are well built. The largest city of Central America is Guatemala. 

Bananas. Bananas are now a common fruit which can be bought in almost any grocery or fruit store. Fifty years ago it was hardly possible to buy bananas in the United States; it is only since 1900 that they have been easily obtainable. 

The hot, wet lowlands bordering the 

Caribbean Sea in Central America produce bananas in large quantities (A). 

The thick vegetation along the coast has been cleared to make room for the banana plantations. Banana plants grow to a

CENTRAL AMERICA 

307 height of twenty feet or more in a little. over a year. Then they begin to bear fruit. The bananas that are to be exported are not allowed to ripen on the plants but are picked while still green. 

The big bunches of hard, green bananas are taken by railroad or by other means to the coast, where ships built especially for transporting perishable fruit are waiting. These fruit liners have holds which can be cooled, or refrigerated, so that the fruit will not ripen too rapidly on its trip to northern markets. Great seaports, such as New Orleans, New York, Philadelphia, and Boston, receive the fruit and distribute it to inland markets. 

Coffee. Coffee is raised in the uplands of the various Central American countries. Coffee requires a hot climate, but the young trees must be shaded from the very hot rays of the sun. For this reason banana plants are usually set out for shade among the little coffee trees. The trees require a great deal of moisture, and the soil must be rich and well drained.

All these conditions of temperature, rainfall, and soil are found in the hilly regions of Central America. The coffee is harvested in the dry season, which comes during our northern winter from November to April. In preparing the coffee for market the red fleshy covering of the coffee bean must be removed and the beans dried (A) before they are sent to the world markets.

The rugged hilly surface of the coffeegrowing region makes transportation of the sacks of coffee very difficult. They are sometimes carried on the backs of natives or by mule packs to the nearest shipping point, and finish the trip by railroad or motor truck to the seacoast city which exports the coffee. The coffee

United States and must be roasted before being used.

THE WEST INDIES 

On the map, page 298, find the long chain of islands that shuts in the Caribbean Sea from the Atlantic Ocean. You will see that it is made up of two groups. 

One group is known as the Greater 

Antilles and consists of four large islands: 

Cuba, Hispaniola (often called Haiti),

Jamaica, and Puerto Rico. The other group, called the Lesser Antilles, is made up of many islands which are much smaller. They lie in a curve from a point near the eastern end of Puerto Rico almost to the northern coast of South America. 

North of Cuba and Hispaniola, and east of Florida, is another group of small islands, called the Bahamas.

Position and Climate. - - The West 

Indies are in the belt of the northeast trade winds between 10° and 23° north latitude. The larger islands are in the northern part of this region and have in general an east-west direction. The highlands of these larger islands also have an east-west direction, and it is upon their northern slopes that the rainfall is heaviest. The southern and western parts have much less rain and at times need irrigation. As all the islands are south of the Tropic of Cancer, the climate is hot the year round, except as cooled by the sea. Some parts of the year are dry and some very rainy, and the changes in seasons depend upon rainfall instead of upon temperature. Contrast such seasonal changes with the different kinds of seasons in our home state. The rainy season in the West Indies is from May to 

October, and during this time fierce whirling storms, known as tropical hurricanes, often sweep the region and do damage on sea and land. 

A. A tobacco field in Cuba. 

Ewing Galloway 

Industries. The leading industry of the West Indies is agriculture, and the chief crops are sugar and tobacco (A). 

Coffee and spices, bananas, pineapples, grapefruit, oranges, lemons, coconuts, and other tropical fruits are grown. Another product is cacao seeds, from which chocolate and cocoa are made. In the forests many valuable trees are found, including cedar, rosewood, and mahogany. Lines of steamers ply regularly to many ports of these islands to carry the fruit and other products to the market cities of the United States.

Many of the islands were long under Spanish rule, though none are so governed today. Spaniards, half-breeds, and Negroes constitute most of the population of the West Indies.

THE WEST INDIES 

309 The island of Puerto Rico and three of the small Virgin Islands are possessions of the United States about which you have already learned. 

Population. The white population of the West Indies is not large, but it is the whites who own most of the plantations, orchards, mines, and factories. The vast majority of the people are Negroes who are laborers. For these Negroes, life is rather simple. They must do a certain amount of work in order to live, but they do no more than they must. Their homes are small, very cheaply built, and used chiefly as places in which to sleep and as a protection from rain and the heat of the midday sun. Fires are not needed, so there is no coal to purchase. Clothes are not necessary to keep them warm, and so a few very simple garments are all they need. Shoes they do not wear. They are happy in their care-free life, although most of them have no schooling and know little about what is going on in the world. The younger generation, however, benefits from the schools that have been built in some of the islands, especially in Cuba and Puerto Rico. have come from other countries as government officials or as managers of estates, find life in the islands very lonely. They look forward eagerly to the coming of the steamers that bring mail and supplies from the outside world. Three things that help to keep these lonely people in closer touch with the world are (1) wireless telegraphy, (2) radio broadcasting, and (3) airplanes.

The Greater Antilles. Cuba is the largest island in the West Indies. It is about 730 miles long, and for most of its length it is from 50 to 100 miles wide. It has plains, hills, and even high mountains, The white people, especially those who and has rich forests and productive fields. 

Orient & Occident Photos 

A. 

-Loading an ox-drawn cart with sugar cane, Cuba. 

It gained its independence with the aid of the United States in 1898, after three centuries of Spanish control. Under the rule of Spain the resources and industries of the island were largely undeveloped. 

Afterward much foreign capital was invested in building railroads, in mining and manufacturing industries, and in the sugar and tobacco plantations. Sugar and tobacco (308 A) of fine quality are the leading products of the soil, and the trade in these and other things is mainly with the United States. Sugar is the largest item, but 

Havana cigars are even better known.

Jamaica is a colony of Great Britain, but its people are nearly all Negroes. 

Sugar cane is the most important crop, and raw sugar is the leading export.

Among the other products and exports are logwood and logwood extract, coconuts, and tropical fruits, especially bananas. The main trade is with the 

United States. Ginger, the underground stem of a plant, is produced here. Kingston is the capital and principal seaport. 

The Lesser Antilles. - The Lesser Antilles belong to several different nations.

Under British rule are many islands, including Dominica, St. Lucia, St. Vincent, A. - Native house in Jamaica. See the tobacco plants Grenada, Barbados, and Trinidad. On at the right. 

Iron and copper are found in eastern

Cuba, near the chief eastern port, Santiago de Cuba. Havana at 23° north latitude is only about 100 miles from the southern tip of Florida. It is the capital and the largest city, with a very large and well protected harbor. It was cleaned and freed from yellow fever by sanitary engineers employed by the United States.

A good road was built through the island from west to east. Havana is a popular winter resort and is easily reached by steamer from Key West or by airplane from Miami.

Hispaniola is a rugged island east of Cuba. It is divided into two republics: 

Haiti, with a Negro population speaking 

French, and the Dominican Republic, whose people are of Spanish and Negro ancestry and speak Spanish. Santo

Domingo is the capital and chief city of the Dominican Republic, and Port-au- Prince is the capital and main city of 

Haiti. Coffee and sugar are the chief crops of both countries, but some cotton, sisal hemp, and tobacco are grown.

Trinidad is Pitch Lake, about half a mile wide, filled with "pitch" or bitumen the substances left over from evaporated petroleum. This pitch (311 A) supplies the material for many of the asphalt pavements in the United States. 

Guadeloupe and Martinique are French colonies. On Martinique is the volcano

Mont Pelée, which became active in 1902. 

At that time an outburst of suffocating gases swept down upon the city of St.

Pierre and destroyed its entire population of more than 25,000 people. 

Curaçao and several smaller islands form a Dutch colony. 

Relations with the United States.

For many years our country has carried on trade with Cuba and other islands of the West Indies. They have found in the United States markets for the products of their fields and mines and forests. 

We have imported a vast amount of sugar, much tobacco, many cigars, and great quantities of winter vegetables and tropical fruits, especially bananas. We have taken much of their iron and copper and such timber as cedar and mahogany.

THE WEST INDIES 

311 A. 

Ewing Galloway

Pitch Lake, Trinidad. The cars take the pitch to the wharves where it is loaded on ships. 

To them we have sent cotton goods, machinery, furniture, and a great variety of manufactures. We have sent them such foodstuffs as cereals; dried, salted, and canned meats; and codfish. All of this is in the way of ordinary trade. Our relations with the people of the West Indies have been much more important than as buyers and sellers of goods. 

We have helped them in their problems of government and in their problems of sanitation. We have invested money in their industries and helped them to increase the yield of their crops and the quality of their products. 

Bahama Islands. The Bahamas are a group of many small islands north of 

Cuba. They belong to Great Britain.

They are low islands, largely of coral origin. It was on one of them that 

Columbus made his first landing in 1492.

Sponge fishing is one of the industries of these islands, and Nassau is the principal city. Most of the people are Negroes.

The Bermuda Islands. - One other group of islands may be mentioned here, HAW- 18 although they are not really a part of the 

West Indies. They are in the Atlantic 

Ocean east of Charleston, South Carolina (map, page 298). They belong to Great

Britain, and a naval and coaling station is maintained there. They are far enough south to have a warm winter climate, and many of our people go there for the winter. 

The chief crops are onions, potatoes, and winter vegetables, most of which are sent to the United States or Canada.

NORTH AMERICA AS A WHOLE 

And so you come to the end of the story of the countries, large and small, that occupy the continent of North 

America and the islands near it. North 

America is the larger of the two Americas and the third in size among the continents of the world.

Most of it is in the temperate and cooler parts of the earth. It is in such temperate and cooler regions that the great nations of our modern world are found. 

Surface. The relief and drainage of 

North America are easy to remember.

Along the west is a great mass of mountains and plateaus. The eastern coast is low, but a short distance inland there are other mountains. They are lower, shorter, narrower, and less rugged than those of the west, and they are a less serious trade barrier. The great heart of the continent is a lowland, with some minor elevations, that extends from the 

Gulf of Mexico to the Arctic Ocean.

The drainage of the continent follows the slopes of the land. In the southern part of the central lowland the plains slope to the Mississippi and with that river from north to south. In the northern part of the central lowland (A) the 

Mackenzie basin drains to the Arctic, and its drainage area slopes as the river flows. 

The lands around Hudson Bay drain into it and slope toward it. Another great drainage system of the continent is that of the St. Lawrence. This includes the 

Great Lakes. Parts of Canada and the

United States slope to its waters. The remainder of the continent is drained by shorter streams except the basins of three great rivers of the west that flow out of its plateaus and mountains. The Yukon flows into Bering Sea, the Columbia into the Pacific Ocean, and the Colorado into the Gulf of California.

Countries. - How many countries of 

North America can you name? Remember that Canada is the largest but that the United States with Alaska is almost as large. Mexico is much smaller than either, but it is also much larger than the group of small countries to the south of it that make up Central America. 312

CLIMATE 

313 The islands to the north of the conti- | a chill to the lands across which they blow. nent are but little known. On the north- What examples of the effect of large bodies east, however, the great land mass of of water on climate can you give? 

Greenland is largely buried under a vast ice sheet and is of but little use to man. 

On the southeast there are tropical islands, some large and many small, but all set like green jewels in a deep-blue sea. 

They send us tropical products, furnish a market for our exports, are useful as coaling or naval stations, and are guide posts on airplane routes to Central and 

South America. 

Climate. Differences in latitude in part explain such differences as you found between different regions of the continent; for example: (1) tropical jungles of 

Central America (A); (2) cotton fields with a seven months' growing season in our southland; (3) corn and wheat belts in northern United States and southern 

Canada; and (4) the barren tundras of the Arctic coast lands (312 A). 

Because of the differences in altitude you can understand how it is possible to stand in a warm and sunny orange grove in southern California and at the same time lift your eyes to snow-clad mountains that look down upon you.

On the north of the continent there are Arctic seas- - icy cold from year's end to year's end. At the south the waters are always warm, sometimes almost hot.

The warm waters of the Gulf Stream flow from Florida to the northeast, off the east coast. This warm east-coast current is matched in the northern Pacific off the

Aleutians and the Panhandle of Alaska by the warm drift of the Japan current. 

Winds blowing across these currents bring their comforting warmth to the land. In a similar way winds blowing across the cold Labrador current bring A. 

Winds blowing inland from the Pacific, the Atlantic, or the Gulf are cooled as they cross the mountains or as they get into higher latitudes. As they cool they drop their moisture on the slopes against which they blow or over the cooler northern plains. The rainfall map of our own country (page 84) shows these effects very clearly. 

Thomas F. Lee from Ewing Galloway 

The largest known mahogany tree in North America.

It has a diameter of more than seven feet.

NORTH AMERICA AS A WHOLE 

Industries. 

You should remember | in the southeastern and southern part of the United States, is raised also in irrigated regions farther west. Sugar cane is grown in Louisiana and Florida, and rice in Louisiana, Arkansas, Texas, and California. the chief industries of the people in the different parts of the continent and think of them as connected with one or more of the larger natural regions.

There is fishing in coastal waters, in the deeper seas, and also in the Great Lakes, in smaller lakes, and in many rivers. 

Cod, salmon, halibut, mackerel, bluefish, weakfish, and many others are caught for sale. The chief cod-fishing grounds are the Grand Banks of Newfoundland, and the chief salmon fisheries are in the waters of the northwest from Oregon to Alaska. 

Shellfish such as lobsters, crabs, and shrimps are taken in large numbers. 

Agriculture is one of the greatest industries. Fruit raising, dairying, and grazing are found in many parts of the continent. In the east there is truck farming and market gardening along the coastal lowland from Florida to Maine. 

Oranges, grapefruit, and lemons are raised in Florida, southern Texas, Arizona, and California. Peaches are grown in 

Georgia, Maryland, New Jersey, New

York, and Michigan. Among the famous apple-growing regions are those in Virginia, Maryland, Pennsylvania, New 

York, Missouri, Oregon, Washington, and 

Nova Scotia.

Mixed farming has long been followed in the northern part of the Atlantic 

Coastal Plain, on the Lake Plains, and in

New England. It is now becoming more and more common farther south. Special crops also are grown in the eastern region, such as potatoes in Maine, cranberries in Massachusetts and New Jersey, and peanuts in Virginia and farther south.

Among the chief crops of the central lowland are cotton and food crops for men and animals. Cotton, grown chiefly 

Wheat and corn are grown in many parts of the United States and to some extent in Canada and Mexico. Unless you remember where these producing regions of our country are, you should study again the maps in this book showing where these crops are raised. In studying the wheat map, remember that it is spring wheat that is grown in northern United States. Winter wheat is grown farther south. Don't forget the wheat-growing region of eastern Oregon and Washington.

In the corn belt and farther south the corn is raised as a grain, and the ears or kernels are fed to hogs, cattle, sheep, and poultry to fatten them for market.

In the dairying regions the whole plant is cut before the ears ripen. It is then chopped up and stored in silos for use as a winter food.

Much hay of different kinds is raised, and many cattle and sheep feed upon the native grasses in various parts of our continent. Among the hay crops there is one that grows so rapidly that it is cut several times a year. This is alfalfa, which will grow in regions of less rainfall than many other hay crops. 

Remember the two special methods of farming that are in use in dry regions. 

These are dry farming and farming by irrigation. The map on page 125 will help you in reviewing this.

The pastures of the Great Plains and the valleys of the Western Highlands furnish food for great herds of cattle and

There are forests along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts, in the Appalachian and 

Laurentian highlands, and in Canada from Hudson Bay to the west. The western slopes of the mountains of the Western Highlands also are covered with a forest growth up to the timber line in regions where there is enough moisture. 

There are other forests in the Mississippi Valley and in southern Mexico and 

Central America, especially on the eastern mountain slopes. In many of them lumbering is an important industry. 

Metals such as iron, copper, nickel, lead, zinc, and gold and silver are found either in mountain regions or in regions where old mountains have been worn away. These regions include not only the great highlands in the western part of the continent but also the Appalachian 

Because of the great wealth of natural resources it is reasonable that many people of our continent should engage in manufacture. More or less change must be made in most natural resources to fit them for man's use. The development of manufacturing has led to the growth of cities and to the building of a vast network of railroads and a greater network of highways. In more recent times these transportation routes have been supplemented by air routes carrying some freight but chiefly for the transportation of mail and passengers. Such agencies bring together the raw materials for factories, and whatever is needed by factory and other workers. They also distribute the manufactured goods through the continent or take them to ports for export.

NORTH AMERICA AS A WHOLE 

Great cities have grown up along the coasts at harbors which are well located for carrying on trade with foreign countries or with the interior. To and from these ports sail the ships that handle a large part of the world's commerce.

This you can usually do if you will ask yourself the question "What can they do?" and then find the answer. each case? What season of the year do you think would be best for each visit?

It is easier now than ever before to visit Niagara Falls, Yellowstone

National Park, the forests of redwoods, and the Grand Canyon. Many tourists, however, are content with the more quiet beauties of hills, lakes, and woodlands reached by shorter journeys.

Population Problems. — Use the map on this page. 

1. What large section of North America has very little or no population? Why? 

2. Where do you find large areas which are sparsely inhabited? How do you explain the fact that there is such an area in the western part of the United States? 

3. Where are the greatest areas of dense and very dense population? What reasons can you think of that account for this 'bunching" of people in certain sections of the continent? 

4. In the future do you think the unoccupied and sparsely inhabited sections will be filled up rapidly? Or, will the sections now densely inhabited become even more dense?
